Incarnate: Book Two
The Salt and the Seam
A novel of the Corruption Saga

Trigger warning: This book contains severe PTSD, Dissociative Identity Disorder (DID), dissociative amnesia and fugue states, graphic violence, self-harm and suicidal ideation, addiction and withdrawal, survivor's guilt, panic attacks, body horror, eye and limb trauma, religious trauma, drowning, and heavy adult language.
Read at your own risk.



Chapter One: The Ledger and the Tide

I lost four hours between the low mark and the high.

That isn't a new record. When we first washed up on the black basalt pilings of Salt-Reach, after the highway went out and the bottom of Purgatory opened like a rotten net and dropped whatever didn't burn into the water, I lost eleven days in a row. I went to sleep in a slick of green Nursery fluid on a salt-shelf and woke up in an ironwood stilt-house with a shaved head, three cracked ribs, and a gutting knife in my boot that smelled like old eel blood.

Four hours is practically a blink. Four hours is polite.

The first thing that came back was the cold. It always starts in the sternum, right where the white ridge of scar tissue pulls the skin tight over the breastbone, and then it spreads down my left arm into the black slag-cracks between my elbow and my wrist. The water on Pelagia doesn't get warm. There's no real sun above the cloud shelf, only a flat pewter smear that turns the swells the color of boiled cabbage at noon and wet slate at dusk.

I was sitting on the lowest landing of the outer pier, four steps below the door of the stilt-house, with my legs hanging off the ironwood planks into the marsh muck. The tide had come up over my calves and soaked my wool trousers to the knees. Gray salt-silt had dried in a crust higher up on my thighs, which meant I'd been down in the mud flats under the pilings while the water was out, digging or hauling or walking in circles until the sea came back and chased whoever was wearing my body up the ladder.

My left hand was resting on my knee. The knuckles were split open across the third and fourth fingers, deep enough that when I flexed them, the white shine of the tendon showed through the pink salt-rinsed meat. No blood was running anymore. Cold brine stops bleeding fast, though it stings like vinegar poured straight into the joint.

"Fuck," I said out loud.

My throat felt scraped raw, the way it gets when Harumi has been screaming into the wind off the trench, or when Kio has been drinking marsh-water without boiling the grit out of it first.

Nobody answered from inside my skull. That's the worst ten minutes after a switch. The room behind my eyes feels like a house where somebody just slammed all the doors and went down the back stairs, leaving the kettle boiling on the stove and a chair knocked over on the floorboards. You can smell the smoke of whoever was just there, you can feel the shape of their teeth in the way your jaw is clenched, and if you call out, the walls just hum back at you.

I looked down at my belt.

The ledger was untied from its rawhide loop and lying open across my wet lap. We made it three months ago out of dried skate-skin, pressed kelp paper, and tarred hemp twine, because Eri refused to play messenger for four people who won't look at each other in the mirror. Eri has one working eye left, and it's clouded across the bottom half where Siren's power burned the cornea in the fourth ring, so making her read our bullshit out loud was cruel even by our standards.

A piece of charred driftwood charcoal and a flat tallow chalk-stub were wedged into the spine crease.

I held the book closer to my face to catch the gray drizzle-light coming off the water. The top of the right-hand page was mine. I recognized my own cramped, crooked letters from this morning, written right after I checked the fresh-water barrel by the rain gutter.

LOW WATER. SIX POTS ON THE SOUTH PILING. NEED GREASE FOR THE WINCH. DO NOT GO DOWN TO THE TRENCH LINE. ERI SLEPT THREE HOURS. LEAVE HER ALONE UNTIL NOON.

Underneath that, in block capitals pressed so hard the charcoal had torn through the kelp fibers into the page below, was Kio.

KNOTS ON THREE AND FOUR FRAYED. RETIE. DOUBLE BECKET. HANDS DIRTY. WASHED. HANDS DIRTY. WASHED. SALT CASK LOW. NORTH PILING SHAKING IN THE SWELL.

Kio doesn't use pronouns. Kio barely uses verbs. When Kio takes the body, we don't talk to Eri, we don't eat unless food is put directly into our palm, and we check every splice on the pier forty times until our fingertips split from the tarred hemp. Then Kio scrubs the split fingers with coarse salt and pumice because the black slag in our left arm smells like the Nursery tank when it gets wet, and Kio thinks if you scrub hard enough you can get down past the corruption to clean bone.

Below Kio's block letters, slashing diagonally across the middle of the page in a tall, jagged slant that took up half the leaf, was Harumi.

The charcoal had snapped twice while she was writing; black smudges were ground into the paper with the heel of a wet thumb.

YOU FORGOT TO LOCK THE BAIT BOX, PRINCESS. GULLS GOT THE EEL HEADS. FIXED YOUR STUPID WINCH WITH A ROCK AND MY FIST BECAUSE THE PIN WAS RUSTED SOLID AND SOMEBODY WAS TOO BUSY CRYING OVER HER MORNING TEA TO OIL IT. ALSO YOUR ONE-EYED GIRLFRIEND WAS STANDING AT THE WINDOW WATCHING ME LIKE A DEAD FISH FOR TWENTY MINUTES. TELL HER IF SHE GOES IN OUR HEAD WHILE I'M OUT I'LL THROW HER CRUTCH IN THE CHANNEL AND WATCH HER CRAWL HOME.

And down in the bottom right corner, squeezed into the margin where the kelp paper curled from the damp, drawn in white tallow chalk so the rain wouldn't wash it away:

Four little round circles with triangle ears. Cats.

Next to the fourth cat, in round, careful seven-year-old print with the S turned backward:

TIM WAS COLD UNDER THE HOUSE. I MADE A NEST IN THE NETS.

I closed my eyes. The wind came off the open water to the west, smelling of dead bladderwrack, wet basalt, and the cold iron stink that never leaves Pelagia. There are no continents here. The fishermen down on the lower reaches call this whole drowned shelf the Leeward Sump, and they say if you sail far enough south you hit the white water where the world just falls off into the dark, though half of them are lying and the other half were dead before they got here and don't remember how boats work. All I know is that when the highway collapsed at the end of everything, when Twenty-two's song broke inside my chest and Himo looked back with red in her eyes and everything tore along the seam, the ocean at the bottom of Purgatory caught the pieces that were too heavy to float and too wet to burn.

Salt-Reach is six hundred black stone pilings sticking forty feet out of the tide, connected by rotting ironwood catwalks, hemp rope bridges, and shacks patched together out of whale ribs, driftwood, and sheet metal pulled up from the sunken ruins a hundred fathoms down. People live here because you can't drown twice, or because you can, and they haven't decided which one they're hoping for yet.

Sunny had been out.

My stomach knotted hard enough to bring bile up the back of my tongue. I can handle losing time to Kio. Kio leaves me with raw hands, aching shoulders, and thirty fathoms of neatly coiled line. I can handle losing time to Harumi, even when she picks fights with the reef-dredgers down at the gutting sheds or punches rusted iron winch pins until my metacarpals swell up like poisoned plums. Harumi was built for the ruin. Harumi is the part of me that grew around the red hum like scar tissue around shrapnel, the part that laughed when the six hounds came through the laundromat window on Fifth, the part that still thinks the prophecy was a promise somebody cheated us out of.

Sunny is seven.

Sunny thinks it's a Sunday afternoon in late October in Illinois, before the kitchen window broke, before our mother Asa stopped singing at the sink, before Himo climbed over the sill with grease on her boots and a ledger in her coat pocket to make a prophecy come true. Sunny doesn't know what corruption is. She doesn't know why our left arm looks like burned asphalt with red sparks dead under the crust. When she sees the black skin, she thinks we fell in the coal bin behind the garage, and she hides it under her sleeve so Tim won't tell Mom we got our Sunday shirt dirty.

And she was down under the stilt-house in the rising tide, making a nest in the gill nets for an orange cat with half an ear who died or got left behind in a world that doesn't exist anymore.

"Harumi," I muttered against my teeth, pressing the heel of my good hand into my forehead. "Why the fuck did you let her out on the pilings?"

Silence. Just the slap of the gray water against the ironwood steps, four inches below my boots now, rising fast.

Then, from the back of my skull, a dry, tinny scrape of a laugh. It isn't a voice you hear with your ears. It's the feeling of your own tongue moving in the dark before you speak, a thought arriving with somebody else's posture and somebody else's teeth.

I didn't let her out, baby. Your soldier boy dropped the rope when a gull screamed behind his ear, and I was busy pulling the winch out of the mud before the tide took it. Kid slipped right through the gap when I hit my knuckles. She wanted to look for the cat. Try keeping a seven-year-old out of the mud when your head's ringing like a cracked bell.

"She could have drowned," I whispered.

We don't drown, Harumi said, rolling over in the dark behind my eyes like a dog settling on cold stones. We've tried. Remember? First week here. You walked right off the south reef with two anchor stones in your pockets and sat on the bottom for forty minutes until Kio got bored and walked us back up the slope. Go inside. Your legs are blue and you're giving me a headache.

I tucked the charcoal and the tallow stub back into the crease of the ledger, tied the rawhide cord around the skate-skin cover, and hooked it back onto my belt. When I stood up, my knees popped like dry twigs. The salt mud had stiffened the wool around my shins into gray plaster, and the cold had gotten deep into the old fractures in my ribs. Across my shoulder blades, where the two ragged vertical trenches of scar tissue run from the base of my neck down to the mid-back, the skin pulled tight and hot. Torn wing-scars never stop itching in the salt air. Every time a storm front builds out over the trench, the nerve endings along the ridges light up like somebody is dragging hot wire through the muscle, reminding the body of a shape it hasn't worn since Galactix fell.

I climbed the four wet steps to the deck of the stilt-house, hooked my good right thumb under the iron latch, and pushed the plank door open with my shoulder.

The air inside smelled of peat smoke, boiled dulse, tallow grease, and whale-oil camphor.

It was a one-room shack, twelve feet by fourteen, nailed together out of black salt-cured ironwood that never rots and never dries all the way through. The floorboards had eighth-inch gaps between them where you could look straight down and see the gray swells surging and sucking around the basalt pilings thirty feet below. When the wind came from the west, cold spray blew up through the cracks like breath from a cellar. We'd caulked the worst of the seams under the sleeping pallets with tarred oakum and shredded kelp, and hung dried skate skins over the windward wall to cut the draft, though the room still stayed wet enough that salt crystals grew like white moss along the iron nail heads every night.

A three-legged iron brazier sat in the center of the floor on a slab of flat basalt, burning dried peat bricks and driftwood roots with a low, oily orange flame.

Eri was sitting on the low bench by the grease-paper window, her back braced against the wall framing and both legs stretched out straight in front of her on an upturned crab crate.

"Four hours and twenty minutes," Eri said without turning her head.

Her voice sounded like gravel rolling in a tin pan. The wet air on Pelagia stays in her chest, and she smokes dried marsh-reed when the pain in her shins gets bad at night, which doesn't help.

"Three," I lied automatically, before I could stop myself. Lying about numbers is so deep in my spine that my mouth does it while my brain is still reaching for the latch. I caught myself, bit the inside of my lip hard enough to taste copper, and let my forehead drop against the inside of the door plank. "Four. Sorry. The ledger says four."

Eri turned her face toward me then.

The left side of her face is a star-shaped cave of white, puckered scar tissue pulling the brow down into the cheek, the empty socket sewn shut years ago after Duke Vasselgrave took the eye in his cellar in the third ring. The right eye, the only one she has left, used to be bright, sharp dark brown, the kind of eye that could look through a liar's ribs and count the stolen coins in his stomach. Now a milky gray cataract scar runs diagonally across the lower half of the iris, left over from the three weeks Siren wore Eri's body like a stolen coat and burned her nerve pathways out from the inside. She has to tilt her chin down and look up from under her eyebrow to see anything clearly through the top half of the pupil.

She looked at my mud-caked trousers, then at the split knuckles on my left hand, then at the skate-skin ledger hanging from my belt.

She was wearing her gloves. Smooth, dark eel-skin leather, boiled in wax and salt until they were stiff as horn, laced tight around both wrists with rawhide thongs. She sleeps in them. She eats in them. The only time she takes them off is when she locks herself behind the sailcloth curtain in the corner to wash, and even then she checks to make sure I'm on the other side of the room near the brazier before she unties a single knot.

"Kio fixed the south lines," Eri said, tilting her head down so the clear crescent of her eye tracked my face. "Then Harumi beat the hell out of the iron winch with a piece of basalt because the cotter pin wouldn't draw. Then she stood on the rail and yelled at Old Maren's skiff until Maren threw a dead dogfish at her and rowed around the point."

"I saw the ledger," I said. My voice was flat and small. I stayed by the door, dripping gray mud onto the scrub-mat Kio had woven out of marsh grass. "And after Harumi?"

Eri's jaw tightened. Her gloved fingers rested on the heavy ironwood crutch leaning against the crate beside her thigh.

"She went down the under-ladder," Eri said quietly. "About an hour before the slack water turned. I heard her humming that river song your mother used to sing. The one with the flat seventh."

"Why didn't you call her back up?"

"I did." Eri looked down at her legs. Both of her shins, from just below the knee to the ankle bone, were wrapped in layers of tarred linen and bound tight between four-inch slats of carved whalebone and ironwood. When the Nursery babies carried her out of Himo's vault by her ankles, they crushed the tibias into gravel. By the time we fell through the seam into the water and dragged ourselves onto the basalt reef at Salt-Reach, the bones had already started trying to knit wrong in the cold. We had no surgeon, no clean plaster, and no morphine. Harumi held Eri down on the floorboards with a knee on her chest and a rag between her teeth while Kio broke both legs back apart with a drift-pin and set them in whalebone splints. They're knitting now, five months later, though the left one has a visible dog-leg curve two inches above the ankle and swells up like a bladder whenever she stands on the crutch for more than ten minutes. "I banged the crutch on the floorboards three times. That's the signal Kio gave me. She looked up through the cracks and waved at me and said she saw an orange tail by the kelp racks."

"You could have pulled her up," I said, and my voice cracked on the word pulled. "Eri, the tide comes in six feet an hour through those pilings. If she got her foot wedged in the basalt cracks while she was looking for a dead cat..."

"Pull her up with what, babe?" Eri asked, very softly.

She didn't mean her legs. We both knew she didn't mean her legs.

If Eri reaches into my head with her mind, she can switch us. She did it once, during the second week on Pelagia, when I was stuck in a flashback to the laundromat on Fifth, clawing the skin off my own collarbone because I thought the black glass on the tiles was sticking to my throat. Eri took off her right glove, pressed two bare fingers to my temple, and reached into the wet dark behind my eyes to pull Ena back to the front.

Instead of just finding me, she fell straight through the floor of my skull into four people at once. She hit Sunny hiding under the kitchen table in Illinois while our father's blood dripped through the oak leaves of the dining room table; she hit Kio drowning in the green Nursery wave; she hit Harumi laughing with thirteen shards burning a hole through our left palm; and at the bottom of it all, she tasted the residual rot of Twenty-two's god-throat and the red prophecy hum that still sits in our bone marrow like lead.

And worse than what she saw in me was what it did to her. For three weeks during the war, Siren had lived inside Eri's brain, folding Eri down into the dark under her own tongue while Siren used Eri's mouth and Eri's hands to pull people apart. Earlier than that, in Vasselgrave's cellar, they'd strapped Eri to an iron chair and forced her to reach into Lute's head and strip every song he knew out of his memory until she left him sitting in his own piss humming two broken notes into an empty jar.

Now, touching anybody's mind makes Eri vomit until her ribs cramp. She calls her own power the hook. She wears boiled eel-skin gloves in a damp house so she never accidentally brushes my wrist while handing me a tin cup of tea and slips inside my head by reflex.

"I'm sorry," I said, rubbing my good hand hard over my face. My short blonde hair was stiff with dried salt, plastered to my skull in greasy spikes. "I know. I'm sorry. I didn't mean use the hook. I just. She's seven, Eri. When she's out, she doesn't know where we are. She thinks if she walks far enough down the pier she'll find the gravel road behind our house and Tim will be waiting by the mailbox with his bike."

"She didn't go down the pier," Eri said. Her clouded eye softened a fraction. She picked up the ironwood crutch, planted the rubber-wrapped tip between her boots, and levered herself upright off the bench with a grunt that she bit in half before it could turn into a groan. Her left leg hung an inch shorter than the right, the whalebone splint clicking against the floorboards as she swung her weight onto her good hip. "I watched her through the floor seam the whole time. She sat on the third horizontal cross-beam above the water mark, braided three scraps of blue twine into a collar, tied it to the piling, and sang to it. When the water touched the beam below her boots, Kio snapped back in, climbed the ladder in four seconds flat, and sat on the steps outside until you came back."

I let out a breath that tasted like dead kelp and ash.

"She left a chalk drawing in the book," I said.

"Yeah. She asked me through the floor if we had any sardines in the tin with the key on the side. I told her we only had salted rock-cod, and she said Tim doesn't like rock-cod because it has too many prickles in the tail." Eri hobbled three steps to the brazier, leaning heavily on the crutch under her left armpit, and used a hooked iron rod to lift the lid off the black clay pot simmering on the grate. Steam rolled up, thick with the smell of boiled sea-kale, vinegar, and smoked skate wing. "Get out of those trousers before your skin splits. Kio left dry wool by the hearth-stone. And let me look at your hand."

"My hand's fine."

"Your hand looks like Harumi tried to punch through a barnacle bed, which she did, because I heard the rock break from in here. Sit down, Ena."

I peeled the stiff, mud-heavy trousers down over my hips and kicked them onto the grass mat by the door, keeping my long linen undershirt pulled down over my thighs. My legs were mottled purple and white from the cold, crisscrossed with old pale lines across the outsides of both thighs from the months in the ruined city when the red hum would go quiet for three days and the skin felt so thick and dead I had to check with a piece of broken window glass to see if there was still blood under it. Nobody talks about that part of getting clean after you've been an apocalyptic weapon. When you spend two years glowing like a furnace, burning whole brigades into cinder with your pulse, coming back down into a regular human body feels like being buried alive in five feet of wet lard. You can't feel air on your skin. You can't feel heat. You cut just to prove the meat still belongs to a person instead of a corpse.

I pulled on the dry gray wool trousers Kio had folded into a neat square by the basalt slab, tied the hemp drawstring at my waist, and sat down on the low three-legged stool across the brazier from Eri.

Eri lowered herself back onto the bench, using both gloved hands to lift her splinted left leg back onto the crab crate so the blood wouldn't pool in the ankle. A bright sheen of sweat stood out along her hairline and above her upper lip, though she kept her face blank. Five months of constant bone-ache had carved two deep parentheses around her mouth that never went away, even when she slept.

"Put it on the knee," she said, nodding at my left arm.

I hesitated.

Even with the boiled eel-skin gloves on her hands, I don't like anyone looking closely at my left arm. From the shoulder to just below the elbow, it's pale, freckled human skin, thin and corded with muscle from hauling crab pots up thirty feet of tide. Two inches below the elbow joint, the skin turns the color of charred pitch.

It isn't a burn scar. When I tipped those thirteen shards from the red-and-white mint tin into my palm on the roof before running back to the laundromat, and then twenty-two more from the blue button tin three weeks later before I walked into the Nursery and put my fist through Twenty-two's glass tank, the corruption didn't wash out when the world broke. The glass melted into the meat and stayed there, dead and cold, like slag left at the bottom of an iron smelter after the fire goes out. The black surface is cracked into tiny hexagonal plates like dried river mud. When I bend my wrist or clench my fist, the cracks widen and show a dull, dead-coal gray underneath that never bleeds red, only weeps a clear, salty serum that smells faintly of ozone and pennies.

Across the third and fourth knuckles, where normal pink flesh still survived in ragged islands between the black plates, the skin was burst open to the tendon from Harumi's fight with the winch pin.

Eri reached into the wooden box under her bench and pulled out a strip of boiled linen, a bone needle threaded with gut, and a small clay jar of rendered seal fat mixed with crushed yarrow and salt.

"Needs two stitches on the fourth knuckle," she said, squinting through the upper clear half of her right eye. "Or it'll split every time Kio hauls a pot line."

"Do it without the grease first," I said, laying my black wrist across my knee. "The fat makes the needle slip."

"You want the stick?" she asked, nodding toward the thumb-thick piece of leather-wrapped drift-oak sitting on the shelf above the salt barrel.

"For two stitches? Fuck off."

Actually, she could use the stick, Harumi's voice floated up from the warm dark behind my ears, lazy and mean. She whines like a kicked spaniel every time a needle goes in. Let me take the arm if you're going to flinch and make the cripple drop her thread.

"Shut up," I muttered under my breath.

"Talking to me or the gallery?" Eri asked, gripping the bone needle between her stiff leather thumb and forefinger. She does everything slower with the gloves on, her bruised mind-demon reflexes fighting the thick boiled hide, and she hates every second of the clumsiness.

"Harumi," I said.

"Tell her if she breaks another iron pin on that winch, she's the one swimming down to the wreck-beds with a pry bar to dig up a replacement. I'm not trading two weeks of dried cod to Old Maren for a ten-cent piece of scrap iron."

Eri braced my black forearm with her gloved left palm and drove the triangular tip of the bone needle through the torn skin of my fourth knuckle.

The pain went up my arm like a white wire.

Wait. No. It wasn't just pain.

That's the trap with the left hand. Anywhere else on my body, a cut feels like a cut. When something sharp goes into the black slag-skin of my left hand, the dead nerve endings don't send regular pain up the radial nerve to my brain. They vibrate. A low, hollow, metallic twang travels up the bone of the forearm, hits the white knife-scar in the center of my sternum, and for half a second, my mouth fills with the taste of cold peppermint and rusted tin.

The hum.

Just the ghost of it. Like hitting a dead tuning fork against an iron stove and feeling the tooth-ache of the note without hearing the sound. For half a beat of my heart, the gray room around the brazier went red at the corners, and a voice that wasn't Harumi and wasn't Kio and wasn't me whispered right behind my sternum:

You're right. Whatever you do next, you're right.

I jerked my hand back half an inch before I caught my wrist with my own right hand and slammed it back down flat onto my knee, pinning it there until my fingernails dug crescents into my black forearm. My breath came out in a thin, wet whistle through my teeth.

Eri froze with the gut thread pulled halfway through the skin. Her one clear half-eye came up to my face.

"Pupils stayed black," she said, watching me with that terrible, clinical stillness she learned in hell by the river that isn't water. "You still with me, Ena?"

"Yeah," I breathed. Sweat had broken out cold under my arms and down the twin wing-trenches on my back. "Yeah. Just pulled a nerve. Finish the knot."

"Liar," Eri said softly. There wasn't any anger in it. We ran out of room for anger about lies somewhere around the third month on the pilings, after we finished telling each other every awful thing we'd done before the highway broke. Now when she calls me a liar, it sounds the way a carpenter taps a rotten beam with a hammer just to mark where the soft wood is. "It hummed when the needle went through the black part."

"It doesn't hum anymore," I said, staring at the peat fire in the brazier. "It's dead slag, Eri. Everything went out when the highway went out. There's nothing left in the world to hum."

"A tuning fork doesn't stop being shaped like a tuning fork just because the choir died, babe." She pulled the gut thread tight with her gloved fingers, looped it twice around the bone needle, and bit the tag end off with her own teeth because the blunt iron shears couldn't cut wet gut cleanly. She spat the half-inch of thread into the brazier, where it curled and hissed in the red peat ash, then drove the needle into the third knuckle for the second stitch. "Hold still. Almost done."

I clamped my teeth together until my molars ached, watching the orange spark of the burned gut thread die in the ash.

I remembered the last time I saw her in the Nursery.

Not the highway after. Nobody remembers the highway clearly; when Twenty-two's song met Himo on the asphalt, memory itself started unspooling like wet film in a projector that caught fire. I remember before that. I remember walking through the vault door of the Nursery with red heat coming off my skin like an open blast furnace, thirty-five shards of dead boy and dead hounds boiling in my blood, and seeing Eri lying on the concrete floor in the dark next to six smiling gray ushers.

Her shins were bent at angles legs don't have. She'd crawled six hours on her elbows through the drainage pipes just to lay her hand on that skyscraper-tall green glass tank and wait for somebody strong enough to break it.

Tell me not to, I'd begged her. I remember the exact taste of the air in that vault: warm salt water, copper, and the sweet, heavy ozone smell of a hundred eaten gods floating in green fluid. Tell me not to and I'll walk out. You're the only one I'd listen to.

And Eri had looked up at me from the floor with her one good eye, having just reached into my skull and seen the red-and-white tin behind the water tank, the blue button tin from the rubble, the twenty-two shards, and nineteen-year-old Dov coming apart into black sugar behind the laundromat dryers because I couldn't stand for Eri to know I'd relapsed.

She saw all of it. Every Tuesday I'd sat on the edge of the roof with my legs dangling over Fifth and told her forty-one days, day one, day two, while she bumped her shoulder into mine and said she was proud of me.

And instead of calling me a monster, instead of using her mind-demon hook to reach into the soft part of my brain and make me drop the corruption, she looked at my black smoking hand and said:

Break it. Make her sorry.

We don't talk about who enabled who in that vault. We don't talk about whether Himo ever said the two words Eri traded the universe to hear before the road gave way under all of us. Most mornings we just boil the dulse, haul the six crab pots before the current turns around the south basalt reef, grease the winch, and check the ledger to see which version of me survived the tide.

"There," Eri said, tying off the second stitch and smearing a thick dab of the gray seal-fat and yarrow salve across the bloody seams on my knuckles. She wrapped the boiled linen strip twice around my palm, split the end with her teeth, and knotted it behind my wrist. "Keep it out of the bait bucket until tomorrow. If Kio tries to scrub the stitches out with pumice tonight, I'm tying your left wrist to the bedpost."

He wouldn't have to scrub if she didn't punch rocks, a quiet, tight pressure shifted behind my breastbone. Not Harumi's drawl. Not a voice at all, really; Kio communicates in sudden flashes of muscle memory and visual inventory: the image of the frayed third pot-line, the gray slime on the bait box lid that could breed rot if it isn't scraped with lye, the loose knot on the rain-tarp corner flapping in the west wind.

"Tell Kio I'll scrape the bait box myself after we eat," I said out loud, flexing my bandaged fingers. The linen pulled tight across the stitches, dulling the metallic throb in the slag-cracks.

Eri reached for the wooden ladle sitting in the notch of the clay pot and filled two dented tin bowls with the dark green broth. She handed one across the brazier to me, keeping her leather-gloved fingers strictly on the rim away from my thumb.

"Maren came by before you went down in the mud," Eri said, blowing across the top of her bowl.

I paused with my spoon halfway to my mouth. Old Maren lives four hundred yards east along the catwalks, in the hull of an overturned iron lifeboat lashed across three basalt stacks. She trades smoked eel, lamp oil, and salvage iron to the outer pilings, and she usually stays as far away from our shack as the rope bridges allow. During our first month here, when one of Maren's nephews came over drunk with a gaff hook to claim our rain-catch barrels, Harumi took the gaff hook out of his hand, broke his collarbone in two places with the shaft, and held him by one ankle over the thirty-foot drop into the barnacle surge until he pissed down his own shirt.

"I thought you said Harumi yelled at her skiff and Maren threw a dogfish at her," I said.

"That was at slack water, when Maren was rowing back from the inner traps," Eri said. "Before that, around dawn, while you were still asleep and before Kio took the body to check the lines, she tied up at the lower ladder and knocked on the piling with her oar. I went out on the door-step to talk to her."

My chest went tight. Right over the white sternum scar, my heart gave a hard, double thud against my ribs. "What did she want?"

"The dredgers down on the Long Reef pulled up a sunken boiler room from the seventy-fathom shelf yesterday afternoon," Eri said. She kept her clouded eye fixed on her tin bowl, stirring a piece of pale skate meat around in the green kale broth. "Old world salvage. Concrete blocks and twisted steel rebar still stuck together from when the seam dropped them. Maren said they cracked one of the concrete slabs open to get at the copper piping inside."

The broth in my mouth turned to wet sawdust. I forced myself to swallow so I wouldn't spit it back into the bowl.

"And?" I asked.

"And two of the boys who cracked the slab didn't come back up to the catwalks last night," Eri said quietly. "Maren went down to their float-shack at first light with a lantern. Said the wood around their stove was scorched black without catching fire. Said both boys were sitting against the wall with their eyes wide open and red rims around their corneas, laughing at a corner of the ceiling where nothing was."

The room got very quiet. Even the wind whistling up through the eighth-inch gaps in the floorboards sounded far away, muffled like sound underwater.

Below my elbow, under the boiled linen bandage and the dead hexagonal plates of black slag, something twitched.

"They found a vein," I whispered.

And behind my eyes, where the house had been dark and empty five minutes ago, Harumi sat all the way up in the doorway and smiled with every tooth in our head.

Oh, baby, she purred, soft as oil sliding over hot iron. Looks like we didn't get all of it after all. Looks like the bottom of the sea kept a little stash just for us.



Chapter Two: Splints and Pumice

By noon the drizzle had thickened into the steady, grease-gray slant of Pelagian rain that smells like cold rust and boiled bladderwrack.

Eri went first down the catwalk toward the gutting shed on the middle pier, swinging her ironwood crutch out in a wide, deliberate arc so the rubber-wrapped tip wouldn't slip in the green slime that grows along the plank edges. Thump, click-scrape, drag. Thump, click-scrape, drag. The whalebone splints on her shins knocked against the wet timber with every second step, a hollow wooden rattle like dry oars knocking in a skiff.

I walked three paces behind her with two wooden buckets of salt-marsh bait-fish hanging from a pine yoke across my shoulders and the tarred wicker basket of gutting knives tied to my belt.

Walking behind Eri when her legs are swelling takes a kind of discipline that cuts against every normal human reflex. If she slips on a slick plank and her crutch skids out toward the frayed hemp guard-rope, I am not allowed to catch her arm. We made that rule in the third week after Kio set her tibias, when she lost her footing on the water-barrel step and I grabbed her bare forearm above the glove cuff to keep her from going down on her broken shins. My thumb hit the radial pulse inside her wrist. Before either of us could let go, the mind-demon reflex snapped open like a bear trap in the dark; her pupil dilated until the brown disappeared, and we both went down on the wet boards screaming while she swallowed ten seconds of Tim's blood on the kitchen linoleum and I swallowed ten seconds of Siren folding Eri's consciousness into a box under her own ribs like dirty laundry.

So I walked three paces back with my thumbs hooked into the hemp straps of the shoulder yoke, watching the gray rain soak into the back of her woolen oilskin coat. Every eight or ten steps she had to pause, plant the crutch tip against a spiked plank cleat, and lean her weight entirely onto her left armpit for three seconds while the blood surged through the crooked calluses forming around the shattered bone in her shins. She never looked back at me when she stopped. Looking back meant asking me to slow down, and asking anybody to wait for her made Eri's jaw lock so hard you could see the muscle bunching under the star-shaped scar where her left eye used to be.

The gutting shed sat fifty yards down the pier from our stilt-house, built on four thick basalt needles where the current swept fast enough through the gorge below to carry the offal straight out toward the deep trench before the reef-sharks could follow the blood up under the houses. It had three walls of bleached whale-rib planking and a leaky roof of flattened iron drums, open on the leeward side to the gray swell. Two long tables made of split drift-logs stood in the middle of the floor, black and shiny with years of fish oil, scored deep by ten thousand knife strokes and crusted along the runoff grooves with dried silver scales.

Nobody else was in the shed today. Old Maren and the four families on the eastern catwalks had pulled their skiffs up onto the high rock shelf after those two salvage boys were found laughing with red eyes in their float-shack at dawn. In a place made out of survivors who crawled out of the seams of three broken worlds, people recognize the smell of residual corruption the way barn swallows recognize hail clouds. Nobody wanted to be out over open water until they saw whether the boys in the float-shack died quiet or turned into something with too many joints that needed six men with harpoons and weighted nets to sink in the trench.

Eri hooked her crutch over the corner brace of the south table, eased herself down onto the high gutting stool so her splinted legs hung straight without touching the cross-rung, and blew a wet strand of dark hair out of her good eye.

"Dump the small ones in the trough," she said, reaching into the wicker basket with her gloved hands for the short, wide-bellied bone scaling blade. "Save the rock-eel heads in the tub for the deep pots. If Kio's going to drop three and four back down off the south piling before dark, we need bait that won't wash out of the wire in the undertow."

I slid the pine yoke off my shoulders, set the buckets down on the wet planks, and tipped the first load of silver-bellied marsh-cod and spotted mud-eels into the wooden trough between us. They were stiff and cold from the morning traps, their gills clotted with gray silt, smelling of iodine and bitter weed.

I reached into the basket with my right hand and pulled out the long gutting knife.

It was eight inches of ground whale-rib, scraped down on basalt whetstones until the edge was thin and translucent as horn, fitted into a handle of wrapped dogfish skin. My left hand, stiff inside the boiled linen bandage Eri had stitched over my split knuckles, was only good for pinning a fish by the tail against the wood while my right hand did the cutting.

We worked without talking for twenty minutes.

That's usually the best part of the day on Pelagia. When there's a trough full of seventy dead fish between your hands, the rules are simple and physical. Pin the tail with the black hand. Drive the bone point in at the vent under the belly fins. Draw the blade forward through the pale skin to the gill arch. Hook two fingers under the cold purple ribbon of liver and gut, rip it free in one wet pull, drop the offal through the square hole in the floorboards into the racing tide thirty feet below, toss the gutted carcass into the salt-barrel on the right.

Pin. Slit. Draw. Rip. Drop.

Behind my eyes, the house was quiet. Kio likes repetitive knife-work because you can count it in sets of ten and check the edge for nicks after every twentieth fish. Harumi had curled up somewhere down near the base of my spine, still warm and twitchy from Maren's news about the sunken boiler room on Long Reef, humming a rhythm under my heartbeat that didn't have words yet. And Sunny was asleep or tucked way back behind the pantry door where she goes when the big people are using sharp things and smelling like blood.

Fish thirty-four was a heavy, thick-skinned bull-cod with a spine twisted from some old reef net it had torn out of years ago.

When I pinned its tail with my bandaged left palm, the slimy gray skin rolled over the slick grease on the drift-log table. I was pressing down hard with the whale-rib knife in my right hand, driving the tip toward the tough cartilage between the pectoral fins, when the cod's body slid two inches sideways out from under my stiff black fingers.

The wet bone handle twisted in my greasy right palm.

The blade skidded off the fish's rib cage, hit a knot in the oiled wood of the table with a sharp, wet *crack*, and the heel of my hand slammed down over the hilt as the tip buried itself half an inch into the timber right between the cod's silver eyes.

A thick red bead of warm arterial blood from the fish's torn gill-sac splashed straight up across my right thumb and the white skin inside my wrist.

Look at that. Just one drop of red on pale skin, and the wet *crack* of a knife hitting wood after it goes through meat.

The gutting shed didn't disappear all at once. That's what people get wrong about flashbacks when they haven't lived inside a broken nervous system. You don't go somewhere else like a movie cutting to another scene. Your body drops through the floor of right now while your eyes are still looking at the table, and the old room comes up through your boots like sewage backing up through a basement drain.

First my hearing went tinny and narrow, like somebody had clapped two greased hands over my ears. The roar of the gray tide rushing between the basalt pilings thirty feet below turned into the hum of a white Frigidaire refrigerator with a bad compressor in a kitchen in Illinois.

Then the smell of raw marsh-cod and wet ironwood in the shed curdled at the back of my nose into burnt cream-of-mushroom soup, cheap tin-can tuna, and the dry, chemical vinegar reek of permanent black marker ink on linoleum.

Eleven dead Sharpies on the kitchen floor by the radiator.

We'd gone through every drawer in the house after my eyes turned red and the backward words started coming out of my mouth like black bile. Tim had tried to write down what I was saying on the backs of grocery receipts and old electric bills so we could figure out how to stop it, and every marker he picked up ran dry after three words because the prophecy was burning the ink out of the felt tips while he held them.

I stared down at my right wrist where the red speck of fish blood sat on my skin.

Except I couldn't feel the bone knife in my right hand anymore. I could feel the kitchen knife in my chest.

Right in the center of the sternum, where the bone is flat and hard like a saucer under the skin. Tim hadn't swung it like a killer in a movie. Tim was nineteen and he worked four days a week stocking shelves at the hardware store off Route 14 and he cried when our neighbor's beagle got hit by the county snowplow. He'd held the eight-inch wooden-handled carving knife from the walnut block by the toaster with both hands, his knuckles white as lard, tears and snot running into his open mouth while I stood against the yellow wallpaper screaming a language that made the kitchen floorboards smoke.

*I'm sorry, Ena. Oh god, Ena, I'm sorry, I don't know what else to do, please don't hate me, please.*

And then the sound. Not a slice. A dull, hollow *thock*, like dropping a heavy iron skillet onto a wooden cutting board, when the steel point split the cartilage of my breastbone and jammed hard between the fourth and fifth ribs before it slid into the pericardium.

"Ena," somebody said from the end of a long concrete tunnel.

My knees unlocked. I didn't feel myself hit the planks of the gutting shed, only the sudden jarring shock in my patellas and the cold slime of fish scales soaking through the wool at my shins. My right hand let go of the whale-rib knife and went straight to the center of my shirt, fingers clawing at the coarse gray wool over my sternum, trying to get two fingers around the wooden handle that had been pulled out of my corpse three years and two worlds ago.

I couldn't get air past my throat. When a blade goes into the chest cavity, the lung collapses inward like a wet paper bag and every time your diaphragm pulls down to breathe, nothing goes into the windpipe; the air just whistles in and out through the slit in the ribs like a broken bellows. My chest heaved, ribs straining against my collarbone, and only a thin, dry click came out of my open mouth.

"Ena, look at the table. Look at the wood."

The voice was rough and low, coming from somewhere above my left shoulder, followed by the rapid wooden *thump-clack* of whalebone splints hitting the floorboards as Eri dropped down off the gutting stool onto her good right knee. She couldn't kneel on the left one without re-breaking the crooked callus, so she dragged the splinted left leg out sideways in the fish scales and braced her gloved hand on the edge of the bait bucket three inches from my boot.

She didn't touch me. Even with the boiled eel-skin gloves on, she kept six inches of cold wet air between her leather fingers and my shaking shoulders. When you're back in the kitchen with the knife in your heart, any hand that touches you is Tim's hand holding you against the yellow wallpaper so you don't slide down onto the linoleum before the blood stops pumping.

"There's no linoleum, babe," Eri said, keeping her voice flat, low, and loud enough to cut through the refrigerator hum in my ears. "Smell the bucket. It's salt and dead cod. Your chest is closed. Tim isn't here. The kitchen burned down three years ago."

My fingernails tore through the gray wool of my shirt and raked across the thick white ridge of keloid scar tissue on my breastbone. I couldn't feel the scar. My fingertips were numb, buzzing like they were pressed against a live battery terminal, and behind the refrigerator hum came the other sound, the hot red drain-laugh rising up out of the black slag in my left arm:

*He stuck a knife in you, baby. After you braided his hair when he had the flu, after you hid him in the cellar when Himo came through the window, your own brother stuck an eight-inch carving knife through your heart because a book told him you were going to be bad.*

"Get it out," I gasped, my forehead slamming down against the scaly rim of the wooden bait bucket. "Tim, take it out, it's stuck in the bone, please take it out, I'll be good, I won't say the words anymore..."

And then the light behind my eyes didn't turn red.

It clicked off.

Like an iron shutter dropping down across a shop window when a riot starts in the street. One second I was drowning in hot blood on yellow kitchen linoleum in Illinois, my lungs collapsing inside my ribs, and the next second I was watching my own body from forty feet away through a thick pane of smoked bottle-glass.

My chest stopped heaving. The crying cut off in the middle of a breath, leaving my face blank and flat as a wet rock.

Kio was in the front.

I couldn't move my mouth or my fingers anymore; I was pushed back into the cold corner behind the cellar stairs where the rest of us sit when Kio takes the wheel, watching through the crack under the door while the soldier runs the machine.

Kio didn't look at Eri. Kio stood straight up off the slimy floorboards in one silent, mechanical lift, ignoring the bruised knees and the torn wool over our sternum. Our left hand was covered in gray fish slime where the linen bandage had dragged through the cod bucket during the fall, and a smear of dark red gill-blood had soaked through the cloth over the two new gut-stitches Eri had put in an hour ago.

*Dirty,* the thought registered across the front of our skull, square and black as the charcoal block letters in the ledger. *Contaminated. Slag leaking through the seam. Clean the hull before it rots.*

"Kio," Eri said from the floor, leaning heavily on her crutch handle as she watched our eyes go flat and focus-less. "Kio, don't touch the bucket. The stitches are fresh. Let me wrap clean linen on it."

Kio stepped past her splinted leg without looking down, walked four paces to the barrel of brine and coarse sea-salt by the leeward rail, and picked up the fist-sized block of gray volcanic pumice we use for scouring dried blood out of the drift-log tables.

With the right hand, Kio caught the knotted end of Eri's linen bandage, ripped it off our left palm with one jerk that tore the scab straight off the gut-thread, and plunged our blackened left forearm up to the elbow into the freezing saturated salt-brine.

Back behind the smoked glass in the dark, I screamed at the sudden white-hot agony of raw brine hitting the torn knuckles, though Kio's face didn't twitch. Kio doesn't register pain the way a person does; Kio registers pain the way a gauge registers boiler pressure, just a needle moving across a dial while the hands do their job.

Kio lifted the wet black forearm out of the barrel, grabbed a handful of coarse crystalline rock-salt from the sack beside it, packed the salt across the hexagonal slag-cracks between our wrist and our elbow, and brought the jagged gray pumice stone down across our skin like a plane shaving oak planks.

*Scrape. Scrape. Scrape.*

"Kio, stop!" Eri hauled herself halfway up the gutting table with one hand on the log and the other clamping her crutch under her arm, her splinted left leg swinging behind her like a dead weight. "Goddammit, soldier, put the rock down! That isn't dirt, that's her arm!"

*Scrape. Scrape. Scrape.*

Kio didn't speed up and didn't slow down. Long, methodical six-inch strokes from the wrist bone to the elbow crease, grinding the volcanic stone and the sharp salt crystals into the black coal-crust of our forearm. Gray foam turned pink, then bright arterial red where the pumice tore the narrow strips of living human skin between the slag plates, mixed with the clear, oily ozone-smelling serum weeping out of the dead corruption cracks. One of the gut stitches on the fourth knuckle snapped under the edge of the stone; the torn skin gaped back open to the white tendon, spilling fresh dark blood down our thumb into the brine barrel.

*Hands dirty,* Kio's mind repeated, calm and rhythmic as an oar stroke. *Wash. Thirteen in the palm. Twenty-two in the palm. Boy in the palm. Wash down to the white. Keep the watch clean.*

Eri reached the barrel in three wrenching hops on her crutch, her face chalk-white under the rain-sweat as the jagged movement jarred her crooked left tibia inside the whalebone slats. She didn't grab Kio's arm. She knew if she grabbed Kio while the soldier was locked in a scrubbing loop, Kio would break her collarbone by reflex before recognizing her face. Instead, she swung the heavy ironwood shaft of her crutch sideways and knocked the brine barrel itself off its wooden chocks.

Thirty gallons of blood-streaked salt water, floating ice-slush, and cod scales crashed across the floorboards of the shed and poured through the offal chute into the sea.

Kio's right hand kept moving the pumice stone across our bleeding black forearm twice more over the empty air where the barrel had been, then stopped.

Without the support of the crutch, Eri's right boot slipped in the rush of greasy brine. She went down hard on her hip against the base of the gutting table, the whalebone splints on her left shin slamming squarely into the iron cleat spiked to the floorbeam.

A sound came out of Eri's throat that wasn't a word: a short, animal bark of pure bone-shock that ended with her teeth clamping down through her own lower lip until blood ran down her chin. She rolled onto her side in the wet fish scales, both gloved hands clutching her left knee while her whole body shook like a wet dog.

Inside my head, the iron shutter didn't just lift.

It blew off its hinges in a spray of hot red sparks.

*Oh, for the love of fuck,* Harumi snarled, shoving Kio back into the dark behind the stairs hard enough to make my teeth snap together and seizing the motor cortex with both hands.

The pumice stone dropped out of our right fist and bounced off the planks into the sea.

Suddenly I was right behind the steering wheel alongside Harumi, close enough that I could feel every raw nerve on our scraped, bleeding left forearm screaming in the cold rain air, while Harumi operated our legs and our mouth. That's what co-consciousness with Harumi feels like: sitting tied into the passenger seat of a burning truck while your meanest older sister drives it through a fence and yells out the window at the cops.

"Look what you did, you stupid Jarhead!" Harumi shouted into the empty shed, shaking our dripping red-and-black left arm at the roof beams so blood splattered across the gutting trough. "Every fucking time! Every time the princess has a cry about her brother's butter knife, you gotta try and sand our fucking ulna off with a piece of reef rock! You know who has to live with the scabs all week while you two take a nap in the basement? Me! That's who!"

She dropped to both knees on the wet planks beside Eri, sliding through the spilled brine and silver scales without caring that the salt was biting into the raw, pumice-shredded meat of our left forearm like battery acid.

"Get your hands off your knee, one-eye," Harumi snapped at Eri, reaching for the hem of Eri's oilskin coat with our good right hand while holding our bleeding left arm tucked tight against our own ribs out of reach. "Let me see the splint before you vomit on my boots."

Eri was breathing through her nose in short, ragged jerks, her single clouded eye squeezed shut and fresh red blood pooling in the cleft of her chin from her bitten lip. "Did not... break," she forced out through clenched teeth. "Callus... held. Just... hit the iron cleat."

"Yeah, well, you're a real fucking acrobat, kicking thirty-gallon barrels over on one leg like a circus seal."

For all the venom in her voice, Harumi's right hand moved with a terrifying, practiced gentleness that neither Kio nor I ever manage when we're scared. Harumi spent two years tearing armored tank battalions apart with her bare hands, so she knows down to the ounce how much pressure human bone and swollen meat can take before it gives. With just her right thumb, forefinger, and teeth, she unknotted the three rawhide thongs binding the whalebone slats around Eri's left shin, peeled back the tarred outer canvas, and lifted the inner layer of grease-soaked wool away from the skin.

I looked through our eyes at Eri's leg and felt my stomach turn over.

The shin was purple-black and swollen tight as an overboiled sausage from the knee cap down to the ankle bone, with a thick, jagged lump of healing bone-callus jutting out under the skin two inches above the joint where the Nursery babies had crushed the tibia into fragments. Where the iron floor-cleat had struck the whalebone slat, an angry red welt was already rising across the ridge of the shin, hot enough that steam lifted off the skin in the cold rain air of the shed.

"Didn't break," Harumi muttered, brushing two clean right-hand knuckles lightly along the sides of the bone ridge to check for crepitus while Eri hissed through her bloody teeth and dug her gloved nails into the floorboards. "Swelled up like a dead pig in a ditch, though. If I put those wood slats back on tight right now, you won't be able to piss standing up for three days."

"Don't piss... standing up... anyway," Eri panted, opening her half-clouded eye to glare up at our face. Even gray-faced and shaking on the fish-house floor, she looked at our torn, bleeding left arm tucked against our ribs before she looked at her own leg. "Your arm's bleeding all over your shirt, Harumi."

"Good. Saves me washing the shirt," Harumi shot back. She pivoted on her knees, grabbed the iron hook beside the small brazier we keep lit in the corner of the shed to warm the gutting hands, and dragged the flat stone slab of simmering kelp-poultice out of the ash. We keep a pot of crushed bull-kelp leaves boiled down with willow bark, marsh-camphor, and tallow going every day when we work the pier, because Eri's shins seize up in the damp after two hours whether she falls or not.

Harumi tore a clean yard of dry unbleached linen off the bolt hanging from the rafter nail, scooped a thick green-brown layer of the steaming kelp mash onto the cloth with a flat wooden paddle, and tested the heat against the inside of our own right wrist before laying the hot compress gently around Eri's swollen purple shin.

Eri let out a long, shuddering groan as the willow-bark and hot kelp grease sank into the bruised periosteum, the tight lines around her mouth loosening by a fraction of an inch.

"You're an asshole, you know that?" Harumi said conversationally, winding the dry linen tail around Eri's calf with one-handed loops and tucking the whalebone slats back in place a notch looser so the swelling had room to breathe. "You could've just yelled at Kio to drop the rock. Or used your little brain-hook for half a second to tell him to sit on his hands."

"I don't use the hook on you," Eri said quietly, her head dropping back against the base of the drift-log table while the rain drummed on the flattened oil-drums overhead. "Not on any of you. Never again."

"Yeah, well, now you got a shin the size of a fender and I got a left arm that looks like somebody dragged it behind a gravel truck for four miles." Harumi finished tying the middle thong in a clean square knot with her right fingers and her teeth, sat back on her heels in the wet scales, and finally looked down at our left forearm.

Now that the adrenaline of the switch was draining out of our bloodstream, the raw red trenches Kio had gouged between the black slag-plates with the pumice stone were throbbing in time with our pulse. Blood and clear yellow serum dripped slowly off our fourth knuckle onto the gray wool of our trousers.

Harumi reached for the pot of remaining hot kelp-and-willow mash, slapped a brutal, stinging handful straight across the raw meat of our forearm without blowing on it to cool it first, and grinned when I flinched inside our skull at the burn.

*Feel that, princess?* she asked me privately in the dark behind our eyes, wrapping a dirty strip of linen over the green mash and tying it tight enough around our wrist to cut the throb in half. *Still got red blood under the coal. You're not dead in your brother's kitchen yet. Come on back out here and help the cripple up the pier before the tide washes our fucking fish away.*



Chapter Three: The Red-and-White Tin

We hauled the deep pots off the south piling at dusk, right before the black squall line came over the trench from the west.

Harumi had gone back down inside around four o'clock, leaving me stiff, hollow-boned, and shaking in the knees the way I always am after a three-way switch. My left forearm burned under the kelp poultice where Kio's pumice stone had ripped the skin between the slag plates, and my sternum still felt bruised from clawing at my own shirt on the gutting-shed floor. Every breath tasted slightly of raw cod and willow-bark steam.

Eri couldn't stand on her left leg anymore after knocking the brine barrel over, so she sat on an upturned tar bucket by the iron winch with a slicker pulled up to her chin, working the ratchet pawl with her gloved right hand while I leaned out over the thirty-foot drop and hauled the heavy tarred hemp line up hand over hand.

Pots one through five came up light: six spider-crabs with pale, blind eyes from the mid-water shelf, four rock-eels twisted into knots around the bait cages, and a bucket's worth of gray sea-snails.

Pot six was the deep trap. Kio had weighted it with two bars of salvaged window-lead and dropped it forty fathoms straight down into the basalt fissure below our pilings where the drowned city rubble washed back and forth in the undertow.

Halfway up, the hemp line snagged against an outcropping of rock below the surface, hummed like a bowstring under my wet palms, and then came free with a heavy, dead-weight jerk that almost pulled my right shoulder out of its socket.

"Got ballast in it," I grunted, bracing both boots against the lower rail beam and heaving the dripping willow-and-wire cage over the roller pin onto the deck planks. "Current must've rolled it into the concrete drift at the bottom of the gorge."

Two big black-clawed trench crabs were wedged in the back corner of the wire mesh, snapping furiously at the empty air. Except they weren't snapping at us. They were backed as far away as their shells could get from the front corner of the trap, where a chunk of broken concrete masonry the size of a loaf of bread had tumbled through the funnel throat when the frame dragged across the trench floor.

Even through the gray dusk rain and the green beard of weed clinging to the aggregate, I knew what was sticking out of that concrete before the pot finished settling on the planks.

You don't forget the color of it. It isn't the black of basalt or wet coal or deep water. It looks like a hole cut straight through the air into a room where all the lights are dead, shiny and wet-looking even when it's dry, with razor-sharp conchoidal fractures along the edges like broken bottle glass.

A shard of residual corruption, the size of my thumb, wedged half-in and half-out of the gray cement mortar.

And the second it cleared the salt water and hit the open air of the pier, my left arm stopped hurting.

All the raw, torn trenches Kio had scoured across my forearm with the pumice stone, the ripped stitches on my fourth knuckle, the cold ache in my ribs, the burning itch along the twin wing-scars on my back: gone. Switched off like somebody had blown out a candle.

In their place, rising up through the soles of my boots and the fillings in my back teeth, came a warm, thick, velvet vibration.

*Hmmmmmmmm.*

Low at first. Like a refrigerator in the next room, or a diesel engine idling three blocks away on a snowy night. And in the center of the hum, coiled tight and sweet as hot honey poured over a tongue that hasn't tasted sugar in five months, the voice that isn't a voice:

*There you are. Oh, look how tired you are. Look how cold they made you. You don't have to be tired anymore.*

Eri leaned forward off the tar bucket, tilting her head down so the clear upper crescent of her single eye caught the front corner of the crab cage.

I heard her breath stop.

"Ena," Eri said. Her hand went down to the ironwood crutch lying across the planks by her boots. "Step back from the rail."

I didn't step back. I couldn't feel my feet on the boards. My right hand was still holding the wet tarred haul-line, and my left hand, wrapped in its bloody linen and green kelp mash, was hanging six inches above the wire latch of the trap door. Through the cloth, the hexagonal plates of dead black slag along my forearm were warming up, ticking faintly like cooling stove-iron as the shard in the concrete sang to the thirty-five shards buried in my meat.

It wasn't even a big piece. Three ounces, maybe. A flake off some dead hound or some collapsed pillar from when the highway broke and dropped the ruins of Fifth Street down through the bottom of Purgatory into the sea.

My brain didn't show me the highway, though. Addiction doesn't show you the end of the movie when it wants you to open your hand; it shows you the quiet three seconds right after the first hit, before anybody screaming gets through the door.

I saw the loose brick behind the wooden water tank on my roof on Fifth.

I saw the red-and-white mint tin. Dented on the left corner where I'd dropped it down the fire escape in the second week. Fourteen shards inside, rattling against the painted tin with a dry *tink-tink* when my shaking fingers pulled the brick out of the mortar wall. Every Tuesday after Eri climbed down the iron ladder into the street, right after she bumped her shoulder into mine and said *Proud of you, babe,* I was on my knees in the pigeon dirt behind that water tank with the red-and-white lid off before her boots hit the third-floor landing.

You press one shard into the center of your palm. Just one. It feels like dry ice for half a second, a sharp sting that makes your breath catch, and then the glass softens into warm black grease and sinks straight through the skin without leaving a mark.

And then the noise stops.

The memory of Tim's face when the carving knife jammed in my breastbone goes away. The memory of the cities I turned to ash when I was Corruption-027 goes away. The blue button tin Marguerite left sitting on a pile of broken concrete two blocks over from the laundromat so I'd find it on my walk home, because Marguerite needed her weapon loaded when the hound packs came down Fifth: that goes away too.

Even Dov goes away.

Nineteen years old, skinny wrists covered in black runner-scars, patchy beard he was trying to grow so he looked older than a kid at the meetings. Standing behind the dead commercial dryers in the laundromat while the six hounds lay around my boots in piles of wet black sugar. Looking at my glowing red eyes and my smoking black left palm with the exact same horrified, heartbroken face Tim wore in our kitchen in Illinois.

*You're using,* Dov said.

And I didn't even think about whether he was going to die. I thought: *Eri can't know.*

One twitch of my left hand. He didn't even scream. He just looked surprised, his mouth open in a little round O, and then he came apart from the collarbone down into thirty shiny black pieces on the cracked white linoleum tile, mixed in with the hounds so nobody at his funeral even knew whether the jar Marguerite buried in the churchyard had boy in it or dogs.

And at three in the morning, with the orange cat watching me from the broken front window, I crawled around on my hands and knees on that laundromat floor in the dark and picked up the twenty-two shards Marguerite missed, put them in the blue button tin, and took them home to use so I could stop remembering how Dov looked when he came apart.

That's what the shard in the crab pot smelled like. It smelled like not having to remember Dov for one single hour.

*Take it,* Harumi whispered right against the inside of my eardrums. She wasn't laughing now. Her voice was shaking as hard as my knees were, greedy and sick and starving, her fingers twitching inside my left hand under the bandage. *Take it before the cripple kicks it through the boards. Just one hour, Ena. Look at this shithole. Rain and dead fish and your brother's ghost in every bucket. Nobody's left to save. Nobody's left to lie to. Put it in our hand.*

"Ena," Eri said again.

She hadn't reached for the trap. She knew she couldn't beat me to it on one good leg and a crutch. Instead, she reached down with her gloved left hand, pulled the flat iron winch-bar out of the ratchet housing, and laid the heavy squared tip across the edge of the wire cage.

"If you open that latch," Eri said, her voice dropping below the rattle of the rising wind in the oil-drum roof, "I'm going to smash the rock through the bottom of the pot into the trench. And if Harumi throws me off the pier for doing it, we both drown tonight and we're done."

My fingers curled on the wet hemp line.

"It's thirty fathoms down on a basalt ledge, Eri," I said, and my voice didn't sound like mine; it had that double-toned flatness it gets when Harumi and I are speaking through the same vocal cords at once. "Even if you knock it through the planks, I can swim down there and get it out of the mud without breathing."

"I know you can," Eri said. Her single clouded eye held mine in the gray twilight, unblinking, wet with rain. "And when you come back up the ladder with red in your eyes, I won't be here. I did it once, Ena. In the vault. I looked at you full of dead boy and I told you to punch the glass because I wanted my mother to turn around and say two words to me. I handed my best friend a loaded gun because I was tired of hurting on the floor."

Her gloved hand tightened on the iron winch bar until the boiled leather creaked.

"She never turned around," Eri said. "And the gun didn't kill you. It just left us here in the rain with our legs broken and four people living in your skull. I'm not handing it to you twice."

A gust of cold wind hit the south face of the stilt-house behind us, carrying the first heavy drops of the night squall and making the thirty-foot basalt pilings groan under the deck like a green glass tank taking weight in the dark.

*Take her bar away,* Harumi snarled in my head, shoving hard against the back of my sternum to get at my right arm. *We don't have to hurt her, just hold her down by the shoulder and open the wire, it'll melt in before she can yell!*

*No,* I screamed back inside my skull, slamming both heels down onto the wet ironwood planks so hard my teeth rattled. *No, get off my hands, Harumi, get off!*

And behind both of us, Kio rose up out of the dark like a stone wall, locking every muscle in our shoulders, neck, and knees into rigid iron paralysis so neither Harumi nor I could bend our elbow toward the cage.

Three people pulling on the same five tendons in opposite directions at the same time while a chunk of black glass two feet away sang into our bone marrow.

My vision split down the middle into double images of the crab pot, the gray sea, and Eri's scarred face. A red hot spike drove itself straight through my temples from ear to ear. Saliva and sour kale-broth came up my throat; I doubled over the wooden guard-rail and vomited into the thirty-foot surge below, my left hand spasming open and closed in the air like a dying crab claw while Kio locked the shoulder and Harumi tried to wrench the wrist down toward the wire latch.

"Knock it off!" I screamed at Eri, blood running down my chin where I'd bitten my tongue against the cramp. "Eri, hit it! Hit the fucking cage before I drop Kio!"

Eri didn't hesitate.

She braced her good right boot against the winch post, swung the five-pound iron bar with both gloved hands, and brought it down like a sledgehammer straight onto the rusted wire bottom of the crab pot.

The mesh tore open with a screech of snapped iron. The chunk of gray concrete and the thumb-sized black glass shard dropped straight through the hole in the cage, bounced once off the edge of the lower pier plank with a bright, musical *chink* that went through my teeth like a dentist's drill, and fell thirty feet into the white foam churning over the basalt trench.

The hum cut out instantly.

It felt like having a fishhook ripped out of the back of my throat on a dry line. Every nerve ending on my pumice-scraped left forearm lit back up at once, screaming raw salt-fire into my shoulder. The sky above the pilings went utterly black as the squall hit the reef in earnest, driving horizontal sheets of freezing rain across the deck and making the whole stilt-house lurch three inches to leeward on its ironwood braces.

Inside my skull, the rope holding the three of us together snapped.

Harumi went backward into the dark swearing at the top of her lungs; Kio's iron lock on my spine gave way all at once; and I fell forward off the rail onto the wet deck planks with my hands over my ears while the thunder cracked right above the roof beams like the sky splitting down the seam.

And when I opened my eyes against the streaming floorboards, the deck looked huge.

The iron winch post looked ten feet tall. My wool trousers were way too long, bunched up around my ankles in wet gray folds, and the sleeves of my shirt hung down over my wrists and smelled like dead fish and medicine and scary smoke.

Thunder boomed again, so loud the wood under my stomach buzzed against my ribs.

"Mom?" a small, high voice whispered out of my mouth.

Wait. No. That wasn't me talking. I was way back behind the glass wall at the top of the stairs, too tired to lift my head off the floor, watching while seven-year-old Sunny pushed herself up on our hands and knees on the freezing pier planks in the dark.

Sunny looked down at our left hand. The dirty linen bandage Harumi had tied over the green kelp mash had come loose when I threw up over the rail, and the black, cracked skin of our forearm showed in the lightning flash, streaked with fresh red blood from Kio's scrubbing.

Sunny's breath hitched in a tiny, terrified gasp. She jerked the wet wool sleeve of our shirt down over the black arm as fast as she could, tucking the thumb inside her fist and hiding the whole left arm tight against her belly so nobody would see she'd fallen in the coal bin behind the garage again.

"Tim?" Sunny called out, her voice trembling and high against the roar of the sea under the pilings. She scrambled backward away from the scary black drop at the edge of the planks until her back hit the wall of the stilt-house beside the door. "Tim, the basement's full of water! Why did you turn the lights off?"

Ten feet away by the winch, Eri was already moving.

She didn't try to stand up on her crutch in the gale; the wind would have knocked her straight off the wet boards into the channel. She dropped flat onto her hip, hooked the crutch through the belt loop of her oilskin coat so it dragged behind her, and pulled herself hand over hand along the spiked deck cleats until she reached the plank door of the stilt-house and shoved it open out of the rain.

"Hey," Eri called out softly over the wind, sitting just inside the doorway on the dry marsh-grass mat where the orange glow of the peat brazier reached her boots. She kept her voice high and clear, stripping all the rough third-ring gravel out of it the way she always does when Sunny comes out in the dark. "Hey, Sunny. Look inside where the lamp is. It's raining too hard out there on the porch."

Sunny blinked rain out of our eyes, peering into the warm orange doorway with her chin tucked down into our collar and our left arm hidden tight under our ribs.

"Where's Tim?" Sunny asked, her lower lip wobbling as another clap of thunder shook the ironwood walls. "I made him a bed in the string downstairs because his ear was cold, and then somebody yelled real loud and the floor broke."

"Tim went inside before the rain started," Eri lied gently, shifting back three feet across the floorboards so the doorway was wide open and clear. "He's dry. Come on in out of the wet and pull the door shut so the wind doesn't blow the fire out. You can sit by the stove."

Sunny crept over the threshold on her knees, still keeping our ruined left hand balled up inside the wet sleeve against her stomach, and pushed the heavy plank door shut with her right shoulder until the iron latch clicked into the staple.

Inside the stilt-house, the storm sounded even louder, like being inside a wooden drum while somebody beat on the roof with wet ropes. Every ten seconds, when a twenty-foot swell rolled in from the trench and slammed against the black basalt pilings thirty feet below, the whole room groaned and shuddered, a deep, hollow *whoomp-creak* that came up through the soles of our feet.

To me, pinned back behind the glass wall in the dark of our head, that sound was the green glass tank in the Nursery coming apart under my fist.

To Sunny, it was the sound of the oak tree branches scraping the kitchen window on Route 14 right before the glass broke and the lady with grease on her boots climbed over the sink.

Sunny backed into the far corner by the fresh-water barrel, pulled both knees up to her chest, wrapped her good right arm around our shins, and squeezed her eyes shut while her teeth chattered so hard they clicked together.

"Don't let her come in the window," Sunny whispered, rocking forward and back against the salt-crusted ironwood planks. "I didn't take the markers, I promise I only took the blue one to draw on my shoe, don't let her touch Daddy..."

Across the room by the door, Eri looked at the child shivering in the corner.

Then Eri looked down at her own legs.

To cross the room on the crutch meant standing up over Sunny with a heavy four-foot club of black ironwood thumping and clattering on the floorboards, and Sunny always flinches when tall people walk toward her carrying sticks.

Eri didn't pick up the crutch.

She unbuckled the wet oilskin coat and dropped it by the door. Then, leaning forward over her knees with a low groan she swallowed into her collar, she used her gloved fingers to untie the rawhide thongs on both of her shins, pulled the heavy whalebone and ironwood slats off her swollen purple legs, and laid the splints beside the door mat so nothing hard or rattling would touch the wood.

Without the splints or the crutch, she couldn't even crawl on her knees. She turned onto her right hip, planted her gloved palms flat on the floorboards, and dragged herself across the twelve feet of rough ironwood plank by her arms, pulling her crooked, swollen shins behind her inches at a time the exact way she'd crawled for six hours through the drainage pipes of the Nursery.

When she reached the corner by the water barrel, she didn't touch Sunny. She stopped two feet away, hauled her back up against the same wall, and stretched her swollen, un-splinted legs out flat on the floorboards with a sharp hiss of breath through her nose.

"Windows are nailed shut, kid," Eri said quietly, letting her head rest back against the black timber three feet from Sunny's shoulder. Her gloved hands lay palms-down on her own thighs, in plain sight. "Iron nails all the way around. Nobody's climbing in here tonight."

Sunny opened our eyes a crack, looking sideways at Eri's sewn-shut left eye socket and her wet, white face in the orange peat-light.

"Your leg has a bump on it," Sunny said in a tiny voice.

"Yeah," Eri said. "Fell off my bike when I was running away from a mean dog. Looks ugly, hurts when it rains, works fine by morning. What was that song you were singing under the house today when you were making the collar for the cat?"

Sunny sniffled, rubbing our nose on the wet shoulder of our shirt while keeping the left arm hidden against her tummy. "The water song. Mom sings it when she hangs the sheets on the line behind the porch."

"How does it go?"

Sunny hummed four notes under her breath, wavering and thin against the crash of the waves below the floor: a simple country pentatonic rise that dropped down to a flat seventh at the end of the bar, the kind of tune women in the central river valleys sang before the red star came down. Then her voice caught on a hiccup as a wave shook the pilings, and she stopped. "I forgot the middle part. When the thunder makes a noise I forget the middle."

Eri stared across the dim room at the red peat coals in the brazier.

For a long time, the only sound in the shack was the rain lashing the skate-skin window cover and the groan of the ironwood braces holding us above the trench. Back inside our head, the storm was starting to go quiet too; Harumi had curled up in a tight, exhausted ball near the back of our ribs, her rage burned out into cold ash, and Kio was sitting motionless at the top of the cellar stairs with his hands folded across his knees, listening to the child breathe.

"I used to know a guy who sang all the middle parts," Eri said softly to the wall. "His name was Lute. He lived on a bridge in a really big cellar where nobody ever saw the sun, and he had a lute he made out of an old barrel and five pieces of wire. Whenever the bridge shook and people got scared in the dark, he'd sing this really stupid song about a baker who dropped his spectacles in a pie."

Sunny turned her head an inch toward Eri. "Why did he put his glasses in a pie?"
