"Because he couldn't see without his glasses to find where he dropped them," Eri said, the corner of her mouth pulling up by a fraction of an inch under the white star-scar on her cheek. "Come on. You hold your knees, I'll hold mine, and I'll sing you the pie one until the rain stops."

And there in the dark of the stilt-house, thirty feet above a drowned world full of broken concrete and black glass, Eri tipped her head back against the wet ironwood wall and began to sing.

Her voice was terrible. It was cracked and hoarse from marsh-smoke and ten thousand years in hell, and she missed half the high notes because when she'd pulled Lute's songs out of his skull in Vasselgrave's cellar and stuffed them into a glass jar, she'd broken the edges off the melody without meaning to.

She sang it anyway. Verse after verse of a dead boy's bridge song from the fourth ring, keeping her gloved hands flat on her own bruised thighs two feet away so she wouldn't scare the seven-year-old hiding in my body.

Little by little, Sunny's fingers uncurled from the wet wool over our shins. Her chin dropped down onto our collarbone; our breathing slowed into the long, heavy rhythm of a child falling asleep in the back seat of a car while somebody else drives through the storm.

And way back in the quiet dark behind our closed eyes, for the first time since the tide went out at dawn, all four of us slept at the same time.



Chapter Four: Wren of the Drift-Weir

Four hours on the oars and the ocean still looked like dirty dishwater.

I don't row. I want that on the record. Even if my shins weren't two bundles of poorly knitted gravel held together by driftwood slats and boiled linen, I don't have the shoulders for it. Mind demons aren't built for manual labor. Himo built me out of peeled nerves and old cellar dark and the part of a headache that sits behind your left eyebrow. When you put me in a twelve-foot flat-bottomed skiff in the middle of a grey chop that smells like dead kelp and wet iron, what I do is sit in the bow with my knees wedged against a salt-crusted bailer bucket, pull my wool coat up over my horns, and try very hard not to vomit into my own lap.

Ena rowed.

She'd been rowing since the Salt-Reach pier went down behind the fog at seven in the morning. Her gray wool coat lay folded under my ruined calves to keep the bilge water off the bandages, so she was down to her canvas work shirt, the sleeves rolled past her elbows, the grey cloth dark with sweat under the arms and stiff with dried brine around the cuffs. Every time she leaned back on the ash handles, the pale muscle of her left forearm bunched and flattened, and every time the muscle shifted, the thirteen horizontal white scars across the underside of her wrist caught the flat noon glare. Twelve from before the collapse. One newer, pinker, slightly puckered at the thumb side from a sharpened tin lid three weeks after the water came up.

Above her elbow, hidden under the rolled wet cuff, was the blue button tin. Strapped straight to the meat of her upper arm with two turns of greasy leather cord.

It had one shard left in it. One fingernail-sized flake of dead black glass that used to be the end of the world.

I didn't have to go into her head to know it was there. It had stopped humming the way the fourteen shards in the red-and-white mint tin used to hum behind the water tank on her roof on Fifth; even dead and cold, it still had weight. When you're a demon of the mind and you've spent ten thousand years listening to the soft wet machinery inside people's skulls, residual corruption feels like somebody scraping a fork across a dry skillet in the next apartment. Every time Ena pulled the oars back, her bicep brushed the inside of her ribs, the tin pressed against her skin, and a faint, sour, metallic itch went up the back of my teeth.

"Your lip's bleeding," she said.

She didn't look up when she said it. She was watching the stern wake, her silver hair tied back with a piece of tarred twine, the cropped uneven ends whipping across her cheekbones in the cold wind. Her eyes were brown today. Flat, tired, muddy brown, with dark purple hollows under them that looked like somebody had pressed two dirty thumbs into her face.

I took my bottom lip out from between my teeth. I tasted copper and salt.

"My lip's fine," I said. "Your left oar's feathering short. You're pulling us south."

"The current runs south around the shoal," she said. Her voice had that scraped, careful flatness it always got on day four or five of a withdrawal fever, when the sweat had mostly stopped smelling like burnt copper and started smelling like sour milk, and her joints ached so badly she had to count to three before she closed her fingers around anything. "If I don't pull into it, we miss the cables. And if we miss the cables, I have to row us back against the tide with your dead weight in the bow."

"I weigh ninety pounds, babe. Ninety-four with the splints."

"The splints are waterlogged oak," said Ena. "You weigh a hundred and ten."

She pulled again. Her jaw clicked on the back swing. Across the bridge of her nose and down her left cheek, where the residual red used to crack open her skin when she was full of Twenty-two's song, the flesh had healed into a lattice of faint, shiny, sunken lines, like mud that had dried in the sun and never quite gone back together.

I shifted my weight on the bow thwart, and a white-hot spike went up through my right tibia so fast my vision went black at the edges.

Only one eye to go black in, these days. That makes it faster.

I grabbed the gunwale with both gloved hands and locked my jaw until the grease in my stomach settled back down. The leather of my gloves was stiff with salt. I hadn't taken them off in eleven days. Not since we traded a broken brass compass to a deaf crabber on the Reach for two pairs of horsehide work mitts that came up past my wrists. They were too big for me. The fingertips flopped when I tried to button my coat, and the seams rubbed raw blisters across the web of my thumbs, and they smelled like rancid tallow and somebody else's sweat.

I slept in them. I pissed in them. If Ena tried to pull them off when I was shivering in the night, I bit her hand until she bled.

"Stop moving your legs," Ena said. She hadn't missed the hitch in my breathing. She never missed anything when she was sober enough to see straight, which was the most exhausting thing about her. "You're twisting the right board."

"It's already twisted," I said through my teeth. "The bastard who set it on the barge had one eye and the shakes. Both of my feet point northeast. When I stand up I look like a crab that got stepped on."

"That's why we're going to the Weir."

"We're going to the Weir because you think somebody out here has willow bark that doesn't taste like bilge."

"We're going to the Weir," Ena said, pulling hard on the right oar to bring the blunt nose of the skiff around a drifting tangle of drowned pine roots and bloated yellow bladderwrack, "because the woman on the fish pier said they have a medic who used to fix bones before the water came. And because your right shin smells like hot garbage through three layers of boiled rag, Eri."

I looked down at my lap.

She wasn't lying. A yellow-gray stain the size of an egg had soaked through the outer linen wrap halfway down my right calf, right where the nursery usher's gray fingers had crushed the bone into five pieces before dumping me in the drainage pipe. Under the wool coat, a thick, sweetish heat was coming off the meat. I knew that smell. I'd smelled it on my own knuckles during the second week Siren wore me, when my body was rejecting her like a bad tooth and the teal skin started sloughing off in wet strips.

"It's fine," I said. "Demon meat doesn't go gangrenous. It just sulks."

"You were feverish all night. You kept talking to a bridge."

I shut my mouth.

The fog was thinning out ahead of us, torn into long greasy rags by a wind that came straight down off the northern shelf of Pelagia's sea. When the worlds folded in on each other after the highway collapsed, the ocean hadn't swallowed everything cleanly. It had choked on the big pieces. Three miles out from the drowned coast of Salt-Reach, where the water dropped off from brown shallows into a black trench that smelled of cold sulfur, two pre-war concrete bridge pilings stuck up out of the swell like rotten molars.

Around those two pilings, fifty or sixty survivors had lashed together everything that floated and several things that didn't.

They called it the Drift-Weir.

It came out of the mist piecemeal, hearing before seeing. First the sound: a vast, wet, rhythmic groaning, timber rubbing against concrete, rusted iron chain grinding in hawseholes, fifty thousand gallons of grey chop slapping into the bellies of overturned river barges and sucking back out through gaps in stone breakwater blocks. Then the smell: woodsmoke from green driftwood fires, rendering fish guts, wet wool, creosote, and human shit dropping straight through plank privies into the current.

Then the shape of it.

It looked like a junkyard that had learned to swim. On the windward side, facing the open grey reach where the big swells rolled in, someone had sunk three rusted ore-hopper hulls on top of a submerged reef of broken highway slabs, forming a crescent breakwater forty feet high that took the whitecaps and spat them back as dirty foam. Inside that crescent, floating on rows of tarred oil drums, waterlogged cedar logs, and chained-together shipping pallets, was a three-acre tangle of shanties. Some of them were built from the wheelhouses of old tugs. One was the top half of a yellow school bus bolted to the deck of a shrimp boat, its windows boarded over with flattened biscuit tins. Catwalks made of highway guardrails and frayed hemp net swung between the platforms, dipping two feet into the slop every time a wave came through the outer gap.

And sitting on the surface of that whole creaking, stinking web of wet wood, like grease on soup, were seventy-three human minds.

I felt them hit me at two hundred yards.

I tucked my chin hard into the collar of my coat and squeezed my one eye shut.

Before the collapse, before Siren, before the Nursery, seventy-three minds wouldn't have made me blink. I used to walk through the Fourth Ring of hell with six thousand damned souls screaming about their dead kids and their burned houses right against my eardrums, and I'd just tune them out like traffic noise while I looked for a customer with a pearl worth pulling.

Now my skull had no skin on it.

That's what it feels like after someone else has lived in your head for two weeks and peeled your eye out with your own fingers. Every wall I'd spent ten thousand years building inside my brain had been knocked flat when Siren folded me down under the floor. The thoughts of those seventy-three people on the Weir didn't knock at the door; they came straight through the wet bone behind my empty left socket like cold salt water pouring into an open blister.

*Net's torn on the south drum again, if I don't tar that seam before dark the whole fucking frame goes under...*

*Cold, cold, my toes haven't been warm since Tuesday, if he eats the last dried herring I swear to God I'll push him off the plank...*

*Where's the blue bucket, who took the blue bucket, nobody leaves anything where it belongs in this place...*

I gagged, Dry and silent, my stomach folding in on itself. My gloved nails dug into the salt-rotted oak of the skiff's gunwale until the wood splintered under the leather.

"Eri," Ena said. The oars stopped. Water dripped from the blades onto the grey chop: *pat, pat, pat*.

"Row," I said. My voice sounded like a boot scraping gravel. "Just row, babe. Don't look at me. They're loud."

"You want to wait outside the breakwater? I can tie up to the chain buoy and go in alone."

"And carry ninety pounds of willow bark and bone-splints back in your teeth? Or let Harumi take the body and pick a fight with the first guy who looks at your hair wrong?" I opened my eye. The water was forty feet from the outer landing stage now, dark green and swirling with fish scales and grey pumice pebbles. "Pull in. I'll shut the window. I know how to shut a fucking window."

I didn't know how anymore. The hinge was rusted off.

Ena pulled three more strokes, shipped the left oar with a clatter that went straight through my teeth, and caught a barnacle-crusted hemp Painter hanging from the lowest landing raft with an iron boat hook.

A boy was sitting on the corner of the raft, six feet above our heads.

He was nine, maybe ten. Skinny in the way kids get when they've spent two winters eating boiled kelp stems and salted dogfish: big knobby knees sticking out of cut-off canvas trousers, a ribcage you could play like a washboard under a grey wool jersey three sizes too wide in the neck, dark hair hacked short with a dull knife so it stood up in greasy tufts around his ears.

He wasn't watching the boat hook. He was lying flat on his stomach on the wet cedar planks, his tongue clamped between his teeth, drawing on a sun-bleached piece of hatch-cover with a lump of black willow charcoal.

It was a dinosaur. A big, fat-bellied one with plates down its back like broken dinner dishes and four spikes on the end of its tail.

My heart stopped.

It didn't slow down. It didn't skip. It stopped dead against my breastbone like a bird hitting a closed window.

*Pip was asleep on the concrete by the pillar when I came back in, curled around his charcoal and a half-drawn stegosaurus.*

That wasn't my memory. I didn't have eyes when that happened. I'd been lying in the dirt outside the parking garage by the tin cans, blind, my teal skin peeling off in the weeds, my black finger twitching on the gravel while my empty eye sockets leaked pink water into the dust.

I knew that stegosaurus because I'd watched it through Siren's clouded blue eyes for fourteen days from under the floor of my own skull.

"Hey," the boy said, without looking up from the tail spikes. "You're leaking. Your boat's got a hog in the bottom plank. Three buckets an hour, easy."

Ena looped the painter twice around a rusted iron cleat and stood up in the rocking skiff, keeping her knees bent to take the roll. "We're only staying an hour," she said. "Is the medic here?"

The boy blew charcoal dust off the wood. "Which one? Old Marten died in the sleet storm last month. His lungs filled up. Wren's up by the cistern tarp boiling rags."

*Wren.*

The name went down my throat like boiling lye.

I tried to get my legs under me to push off the bow thwart, to shove the skiff back out into the channel, to tip the whole boat over into the forty-foot sulfur trench and sink like a stone until the cold water filled my mouth and drowned out every mind on the sea. My shins didn't bend. The driftwood slats caught on the bailer bucket, my heel skidded in the greasy bilge slop, and the white spike came back up through my right leg so hard I bit straight through the inside of my cheek.

Blood filled my mouth, hot and salty. I didn't make a sound. Himo broke me of screaming when I was four days old. When you scream on the steel table, she writes it down on the clipboard and does the same nerve again to see if the pitch changes.

"Pip!" a voice called from the upper catwalk. "Who's on the chain?"

She came down the companionway ladder from the shrimp-boat platform with one hand on the rusted pipe rail and a canvas bucket full of steaming grey water in the other.

Twenty-five. Maybe twenty-six now. Brown skin gone rough and wind-burned across the cheekbones from six months on the open water. Her head was still shaved close to the skull, though the dark stubble had grown in uneven around a three-inch silvery dent above her right ear where a concrete beam had grazed her when the garage came down. The white scar across her upper lip pulled her mouth slightly crooked on the left side. Over her greased wool vest she wore a canvas shoulder bag stuffed with boiled grey linen rolls, and tucked through the belt at her hip, dragging her trousers down an inch on the right, was a fourteen-inch iron pipe wrench with old brown blood dried into the teeth of the jaw.

Her eyes were brown.

All the way brown. Dark, tired, clear at the edges. No blue. Not a speck of glowing Nursery blue anywhere in the iris.

Siren wasn't in her.

I knew what had happened to Siren before Wren's boots hit the bottom rung of the ladder, because the shape of the dent was still sitting in the front of Wren's mind like a burned-out fuse box. When Ena broke the tank in the Nursery and swallowed Twenty-two's throat, every scrap of borrowed anti-god meat that Himo had cooked down to make the Nursery babies had been yanked backward toward the highway like iron filings to a magnet. Siren hadn't walked through the door on her own. She'd been ripped out of Wren's skull through the nose and mouth like wet smoke while Wren lay screaming on the garage floor, leaving Wren alone in her own meat with four months of somebody else's lies rotting in the basement.

Wren stepped off the ladder onto the cedar raft. She set the steaming bucket down by her boot. She wiped her wet hands on her thighs and looked down into the skiff.

She looked at Ena first: the cropped silver hair, the scarred nose, the grey shirt clinging to thin ribs.

Then she looked into the bow. At the curled horns sticking out of the wool hood. At the old flame-shaped scar running down the right side of my face. At the sunken, puckered skin of my empty left socket, and the one yellow-brown demon eye staring up at her from the dark.

At my face.

The face that had come stumbling into her parking garage through the tin-can alarm on day five, teal and peeling, and looked up at her with two stolen blue eyes and said, *You know me. From before. I helped your people get out of the city, remember? I held the door.*

The face she had washed with grey gauze every night by the cracked skylight while she talked about her dead mother and an iguana with a bladder stone.

The face that had looked at her on the good mattress in the dark and said, *Wren. You'd do anything for me. Right? Even let me in?*

Wren's right hand didn't drop to the pipe wrench. It was already there. Her brown fingers closed around the oiled iron handle so hard the tendons across the back of her wrist stood up like fence wire.

Every drop of blood left her face. The scar on her upper lip went white as chalk.

And in her mind, loud enough to shatter every glass window in a three-mile radius, came the sound of a woman screaming *Pip, Pip, Pip* from the dark under a concrete floor while her own mouth hummed a river hymn out of tune.

"Get up," I told Ena.

It didn't come out right. My tongue was thick with blood from my cheek.

"Eri?" Ena turned her head on the landing raft, one hand still holding the painter. She looked from Wren's white face and the iron wrench to me, wedged into the bow of the skiff like a sack of wet salt. "What is it? Do you know her?"

"Untie the boat," I said.

Wren took one step forward on the cedar planks. The raft dipped two inches under her boot. The wrench came clear of her belt. It didn't come up fast; it came up level, heavy, the way somebody lifts a slaughterhouse maul when the hog is already tied by the hind leg.

"You're dead," Wren whispered. Her voice was rough, low, scraped raw at the bottom, the exact voice that used to sing about the far shore against the concrete pillar while the dead anti-gods inside Siren went quiet to listen. "I watched you fall off me on the mattress. You had no eyes. Your skin was black. When the sky broke and that thing got sucked out of my mouth, the garage came down on top of you. I went back with a shovel. You weren't there."

"Wren," I said.

My throat closed. I couldn't get air past my collarbone. The seventy-three minds on the Weir were still pressing against the back of my skull, and right in front of me, six feet away, Wren's memory of two months locked in the dark under her own ribs was pouring straight into my face like open sewage. I smelled the parking garage. I smelled the starchy pale tubers growing in the shopping cart. I felt the terror of trying to move your own right hand to push your eight-year-old brother away from a monster wearing your skin, and watching your hand pat his hair instead.

I grabbed the sides of the skiff and tried to stand.

It was the stupidest thing I could have done. You don't stand up in a flat-bottomed boat on a chop when neither of your tibias is connected in the middle. I got my boots onto the wet floorboards, hauled myself halfway over the gunwale toward the raft so Ena wouldn't be between me and the wrench, and the linen binding on my right calf slipped two inches down the wet oak slat.

The bone ends grated together inside the meat.

I heard it. A wet, chalky crunch, like somebody stepping on a snail in gravel.

My legs folded backward under me. I hit the cedar planks of the landing raft on my knees and my elbows, the exact way I'd crawled for six hours through the drainage pipe out of Himo's vault, my chin slamming into a salt-slimed bolt head.

"Eri!" Ena dropped the painter and dropped to one knee beside me, her hand coming down toward my shoulder.

"Don't touch me!" I screamed at her. I jerked away so hard the driftwood splint caught on a plank gap and wrenched my ankle sideways. Black stars burst across my right eye. I curled on my side in the grey puddle around the steaming bucket, my gloved hands clamped over my face, my knees drawn up toward my stomach, shaking so hard my horns knocked against the cedar wood. "Don't fucking touch me, Ena, keep your hands off me!"

Pip had scrambled backward across the raft on his hands and heels, his lump of charcoal rolling into a crack between the boards.

Above me, the shadow of the pipe wrench fell across my knuckles.

"Step back," Ena told Wren.

Ena's voice wasn't flat anymore. It was very quiet, thirty degrees colder than the sea water, and even with my face buried in my horsehide gloves I felt the faint, sickening prickle of the black shard under her left sleeve waking up to the adrenaline in her blood. Ena shifted her weight between my head and Wren's boots. She didn't have a weapon. She didn't need one. Even clean, even starving, even four days out from a withdrawal shake, Ena moved like a woman who had killed nine hundred people with her bare hands and remembered the weight of every skull.

"I don't know what happened in your garage," Ena said, her eyes locked on the fourteen-inch iron wrench in Wren's fist, "and if she owes you blood, we can talk about it when she's not passing out on the deck. Put the iron down."

"You don't know what she is," Wren said. Her chest was heaving under the greased wool vest. Her thumb was white on the knurled adjusting nut of the wrench. "She came to us in the ruins. She looked at me and she reached into my head and she made me remember a door. A door that never existed. She made me love her. She made me carry her inside and wash her hands and feed her my brother's rations while she rotted, and when that body gave out, she looked me in the eyes and she climbed inside me and locked me in the dark for fifty-eight days."

Wren's voice broke in the middle, jagged as a snapped bone.

"Fifty-eight days," Wren said, taking half a step closer, her eyes wild and wet and fixed on my curled back. "I watched my own hands touch my brother through a sheet of dirty glass. I screamed until I didn't have a voice left down there, and she just hummed my mother's song wrong over my head so she wouldn't have to hear me. And you brought her here? To where he sleeps?"

Ena froze.

Slowly, Ena looked down over her shoulder at me.

She knew about Siren. A little. She'd seen the teal thing wearing my body in the Nursery hallway right after she relapsed, when Siren laughed with my mouth and shouted *Thanks for the vessel!* while Ena ran for her life. She'd found me two months later in the wreckage of the parking garage after the collapse, blind, half-starved, with one new eye grown back wrong from demon spit and spite, and two shins smashed to paste by the ushers before the end.

We had never talked about the fifty-eight days in between.

We had a rule on the skiff, Ena and I. The same rule we used to have on the roof on Fifth Street on Tuesdays when I brought her cans of peaches and she told me it was day forty-one. Don't ask where the mud on the hem came from. Don't ask why you wake up screaming about a blue light or a boy behind the dryers.

I lay on the wet wood with my cheek pressed against a rusted bolt and tasted the blood in my mouth.

I could fix this.

The thought came up so fast and slick it didn't even feel like a choice; it felt like muscle memory. Like sitting on my stool in the Fourth Ring by the river that wasn't water.

Wren wasn't an anti-god. She wasn't a duke. She was a twenty-five-year-old human vet tech whose brain had already been cracked wide open by a mind-pusher for two months. The hinge was right there. I could see it glowing in the dark behind her brown eyes, raw and bruised and wide open. All I had to do was pull my right glove off with my teeth, lay two fingers against her ankle, and push.

*It wasn't me, Wren. You remember, don't you? You saw the teal thing leave me on the mattress. I was trapped under the floor just like you. We were in the basement together. You don't hate me. You feel sorry for me. Put the wrench down and fix my leg.*

One push. Half a second. She'd blink, and her shoulders would drop, and she'd kneel down in the puddle and start cutting the dirty linen off my shin with her little bandage scissors, and she'd never know why her stomach felt hollow whenever she looked at my face.

Just like the realm I fixed ten thousand years ago, when I was an anti-god and a master left a polite little thing in my head.

Just like Lute sitting on my stool while I pulled my own name out of his skull so he wouldn't cry by the river.

Just like Ena kneeling in the Nursery vault while I didn't push her away from the glass because I wanted Himo to turn around and say two words.

I kept my gloves on.

I reached up with both horsehide mitts, grabbed the collar of my wool coat, and hauled myself backward until my spine hit the rusted iron cleat on the corner piling. I didn't let Ena help me. I dragged my broken right leg across the cedar planks by the knee of my trousers and let the pain white out my eye until the nausea burned through the back of my throat.

"Get out of the way, Ena," I croaked.

"Eri, shut up, your bone's sticking against the cloth."

"Move," I said. I shoved at the back of Ena's calf with my gloved fist. "Get out from between us. She's not swinging at you."

Ena didn't move out of reach, but she took half a step sideways so Wren could see my face.

I looked up at Wren. One yellow-brown eye, blood running out of the corner of my mouth into the salt grime on my chin, both gloved hands held out flat in front of my chest where she could see every finger.

"Look at my hands," I told her. "The leather's tied at the wrist. I won't take them off. And look at my eye. It isn't blue. If I go into your head, my scar lights up red and you feel cold grease behind your ears. If you feel cold grease behind your ears, hit me in the temple with that wrench as hard as you can. You'll crack the horn root and I'll be dead before I hit the water."

Wren didn't lower the wrench an inch. "You were in me," she said. "I heard your voice under the floor before you took me. You talked to yourself. Two voices in one mouth."

"That wasn't me talking to myself," I said. My voice was shaking so badly I had to bite my tongue to make the words hold their shape. "My name is Eri. Before that it was Number Nine. The thing that walked into your garage on day five was named Siren. She was three days old. She came out of a tank in the Nursery while I was chasing my friend through the dark, and she pushed my mind down into the dirt, and she climbed inside my skin, and because I kept trying to look out of my own eye to scream, she took a piece of broken glass and she cut my eye out with my own right hand."

Pip made a small, wet sound behind the cistern barrel. Wren didn't look back at him, though her left hand twitched toward her hip, the old reflex of checking where he was.

"I was under the floor," I said. The wet wind off the channel hit the sweat on my neck and turned it to ice. "For fourteen days. While she walked around in my meat and rotted it out from the inside. I was down there in the dark where you went later. Every night when you sat her on that overturned bucket under the skylight and peeled the dead skin off my knuckles with your gray gauze, I was screaming at the bottom of my own skull. I watched her push you. I watched her put that lie about the door into your head because she was terrified of dying and going to the grey waiting room, and she needed somebody with a song in them to take care of her."

Wren's jaw worked. The white scar on her lip stretched tight. "You didn't stop her."

"I couldn't even move my own pinkie finger!" I shouted, and then my breath caught on a wet rattle in my ribs, and I dropped my voice back down into the mud where it belonged. "I couldn't stop her. I told her she was a parasite. I told her when we both rotted out I'd find her in hell and put her in the ground. And on the fourteenth night, when she limped back from the Nursery after trying to wear an empty body, and you carried her in on your back and put her on the mattress and said you'd do anything for her, I knew what she was going to do. I screamed at her to tell you the truth. I screamed until whatever part of a mind can bleed was bleeding."

Wren's knuckles loosened a fraction of an inch on the iron wrench. A tiny, raw flicker went through the front of her thoughts: not forgiveness, not even belief, just the desperate, sick urge every decent person has to find out that the thing that hurt them wasn't the thing sitting in front of them crying.

I smelled that urge. I'd made a forty-year living off urges like that in the Fourth Ring.

And because I smelled it, and because my hands were wrapped in stinking horsehide so I couldn't touch anyone anymore, I kept going.

"Wait," I said, holding up one gloved palm when her wrench dipped half an inch. "Don't put that down yet. You haven't heard the rest."

Ena looked at me sharply. "Eri."

"Shut up, babe. You can hear it too." I wiped my bloody chin against my shoulder without taking my eye off Wren's face. "I want to be very clear with you, Wren, because you've had a liar in your head for two months and you don't know which thoughts in there belong to you anymore. I was under the floor. I told her not to take you. And do you know why I told her not to take you?"

Wren said nothing.

"Because I wanted her to die in my body so I could watch her go into the grey room," I said. "And when she climbed out of me and went into you instead, and dropped my blind, rotting body on your mattress like a dirty coat, do you know what the first thing I felt was? When I heard you down under the floor screaming for your little brother in the dark?"

My throat closed up again. I forced it open. It felt like swallowing broken oyster shells.

"Relief," I said. "I lay there in the dirt by your tin cans with no eyes and a black finger, listening to her walk back inside wearing your legs, and I thought: *thank God it's the vet tech and not me.* And when I crawled out of that garage into the ruins to wait for my eye to grow back, I didn't come back for you. I didn't get a rock and wait by the door to smash her skull in so you could have your body back. I left you in the basement with her for fifty-eight days because my shins hurt and I was tired of being in the dark."

On the landing raft, the only sound was the grey chop slapping against the oil drums under our feet and the creak of the hemp painter against the iron cleat.

Ena was staring down at the top of my wool hood. Her face had gone completely still.

"She's not the only one who does it," I told Wren, my voice dropping into a flat, ugly singsong that sounded way too much like Himo reading numbers off a jar. "That's what we do. Things built out of minds. Before the wars, in hell, there was a man named Lute who sang on a bridge for forty years. He was the only person in any world who ever looked at me and asked if I was tired. He gave me my name. And when a duke offered me a key out of hell for his soul, I sat him down on my stool, and I told him to close his eyes, and I pulled every song and every memory and my own name out of his skull and put them in a jar and sold him, and left him standing on the river bank like an empty coat."

I turned my head an inch and looked up at Ena's canvas knee.

"And in the Nursery," I said, "when the ushers broke my legs and left me by the tank, and Ena came in glowing red with her dead friend's blood still under her fingernails, she knelt down on the floor and asked me to tell her to walk out. She said I was the only one she'd listen to. And I could have reached into her head with one finger and turned the red off. I didn't even have to push hard. Instead I told her to punch the glass and eat the end of the world, because I wanted the bitch who made me to turn around and say she was sorry for throwing me in a pit ten thousand years ago."

I let my head fall back against the rusted iron cleat. The cold metal bit through my wool hood right between my horns. My right shin felt like somebody was turning a hot auger inside the marrow, round and round, slow and steady.

"So don't look at me like I'm the girl who got stolen," I told Wren. "Siren was three days old and made out of my leftovers. The only difference between her and me is that I've had ten thousand years to get better at making people think I'm their friend before I use them."

Nobody spoke.

Above us, a gull with three wings and a patch of grey scales across its crop landed on the roof of the yellow school bus, clacked its beak once, and took off again into the salt fog.

Ena didn't walk away from me. I waited for her to. I waited for her boots to turn around on the wet cedar planks and step back into the skiff and cast off the painter, the way Himo walked away from the pit, the way Chimera stepped over me in the rubble, the way Ena herself had walked out of the Nursery vault in the green water without looking back.

Instead, Ena slowly sat down on an overturned tar bucket three feet to my left, rested her elbows on her knees, and stared down at the thirteen white scars on her left wrist.

"I didn't want you to tell me not to," Ena said to the planks. Her voice was barely loud enough to carry over the slap of the waves. "When I knelt down by the tank. I already had twenty-two shards in my blood. If you'd told me to walk out, Harumi would have broken your neck and punched the glass anyway. I just asked you so that afterward I could tell myself it was your fault too."

I closed my eye. A single hot drop of water ran out from under my right lid and lost itself in the salt crust on my cheek.

Across the raft, the fourteen-inch pipe wrench slid back through the leather loop on Wren's belt with a dull iron clink.

Wren didn't say *I forgive you.* If she'd said she forgave me, I think I would have rolled sideways off the raft into the sulfur trench and held my breath until my lungs split.

She knelt down in the grey puddle in front of me. Her knees hit the cedar boards hard. She unbuckled her canvas shoulder bag, took out a pair of curved iron shears with rust-pitted handles, and grabbed the ankle of my right boot.

"If I feel cold grease behind my ears," Wren said, her voice flat and shook-out and hard as a quarry stone, "I don't need the wrench. I'll push my thumb through your soft palate while you're on your back. Hold her shoulders down, silver-hair. This bone set crooked and the pus has been sitting in the pocket for four days. I have to break the callus over my knee and drain it before I put the fresh wood on."

"Wait," I said.

Wren didn't wait.

She slit the wet wool trouser leg from ankle to knee with three crunches of the iron shears, peeled back the stinking, yellow-crusted linen wrap in one dry jerk that tore the scab straight out of the hole in my skin, braced her brown boot against my hip, and pulled my crooked tibia across her knee with both hands.

The dry crunch echoed off the oil drums.

I didn't go into anybody's head. I kept my horsehide gloves wrapped around Ena's ankles so tight the leather split at the thumb seam, and I screamed out loud with my own mouth into the open grey air until my throat tasted like rust and my eye went dark.



Chapter Five: Voices Behind the Eyes

The headache always starts two inches behind my left temple, right where the bone thins out above the hinge of the jaw.

It doesn't feel like a normal headache. A normal headache is a dull weight, like a sack of wet grain sitting on your forehead after you've rowed six miles against a tide. This is a carpenter's awl being tapped through the skull plate with a small wooden mallet: *tink, tink, tink*. Every tap lets a little bit of cold air into the dark behind my eyes, and with the air comes the noise.

We were in the net-loft on the second tier of the Weir, thirty feet above the oil drums, in a slant-roofed shed cobbled together from corrugated zinc sheets and the split plank sides of an old grain scow. Outside, it was raining: a thick, sideways Pelagian sleet that rattled on the zinc like handfuls of gravel and dripped through the tar-paper seams into five rusted lard tins Wren had set along the floorboards.

Eri was asleep on a pile of dry cork floats in the corner. Her calves were wrapped from ankle to knee in clean grey linen and straight ash slats bound with tarred cod-line. Her face was the color of tallow, and even in her sleep her horsehide gloves were crossed tightly over her breastbone like a dead bishop's hands. Wren had poured half a cup of boiled willow-bark tea and raw kelp spirits down her throat after lancing the green pocket in her leg, and Eri had gone out like a candle dropped in a bucket.

I was sitting on an upturned herring barrel by the stove-pipe hole, trying to count my fingers.

One. Two. Three. Four. Five.

Left hand. Thirteen white lines across the wrist. A blue button tin tied above the elbow under the wet canvas sleeve.

I blinked.

The lard tin third from the door had been empty when I looked at my thumb. Now it was overflowing, brown water spilling down its rusted sides onto the scow planks, and the grey light coming through the oiled sheepskin window had gone from iron-dark to the flat, greasy pearl of mid-morning.

I had lost fourteen hours.

My boots were wet. They hadn't been wet when Eri fell asleep; I'd taken them off by the stove-pipe to dry the wool socks. Now they were laced up to the top hooks, tied in double square knots that I never use because my fingers are too stiff in the mornings to pick them out, and there was fresh gray concrete dust from the outer breakwater ground into the welt of the left sole.

My knuckles were raw. Across the back of my right hand, the skin over the middle two joints was split and oozing clear yellow serum, and there was a smell on my tongue that made my stomach turn over: cold rusted iron and raw garlic.

I don't eat raw garlic. It makes me gag.

*Harumi,* I thought.

Nothing answered.

Instead, the awl behind my left temple tapped twice, very fast, and the room went long.

That's the only way to describe what happens when the front starts to slide. The herring barrel was still under my thighs, and the zinc roof was still four feet above my crown, yet my eyes felt as if they had been pulled three yards backward into the dark behind my own skull. I was looking out through my own pupils the way you look through the wrong end of a pair of field glasses down a dark stairwell. My arms looked tiny and far away, resting on the knees of a pair of canvas trousers that belonged to somebody else.

And the kitchen smelled like burnt pancake batter.

*Don't,* I whispered. My mouth didn't move on the outside. Or maybe it did, and I couldn't feel the lips because my face had gone numb from the cheekbones down. *Not here. We're on a raft. There's water under the floorboards.*

In the dark behind my left ear, someone was counting.

*One orange. Two red. One blue with the cap chewed. One green that doesn't work unless you lick the felt.*

Seven years old. Linoleum with little yellow daisies worn down to the black tar backing in front of the sink. The radio on top of the refrigerator humming because the dial knob broke off in Tim's pocket when he tried to tune in the night game from Cleveland, and Mom hasn't come down the stairs yet, and there's a dark wet spot on the ceiling plaster above the toaster that wasn't there when we went to bed, and if you keep all the Sharpies lined up by size along the metal rim of the Formica table and don't look up at the stairs, nobody has climbed in through the window and Tim won't have to go up there with his baseball bat.

*Sunny,* I thought, reaching backward into the hot grease-smell with hands that didn't have scars on the wrists yet. *Sunny, close the drawer. Put the markers in the coffee can. We aren't on Elm Street.*

*He broke the yellow one,* Sunny said. Her voice wasn't out loud. It was small and sticky with maple syrup, sitting right in the hollow under my tongue. *He pressed too hard on the dinosaur tail and the tip went back inside the plastic. If Mom sees the tip pushed in she'll say we can't have nice things.*

She meant Pip. She'd been watching Pip draw on the hatch cover through my eyes yesterday morning, before the wrench came out, and now the willow charcoal on the cedar raft was mixed up with the coffee can of markers on the Formica table thirty-four years and three dead universes ago.

Before I could pull her back from the table, a heavy iron door slammed shut across the kitchen doorway, and the smell of burnt batter vanished under three feet of freezing brown cellar water.

*Twelve,* a man's voice counted in the dark. Not a voice, really; Kio doesn't use words with sound on them. He thinks in chalk marks on wet stone and the click of a rifle bolt being greased in a cellar when the resistance runners haven't come back from the north bridge for three days. *Twelve breaths. Hold four. Out twelve. Keep your back to the brick. The floorboards above the cistern have a three-inch gap. If they come up the ladder, take the first one in the throat with the bailer handle before the second one clears the hatch.*

He was the one who had tied my boots in double square knots during the night.

I looked down at the laces through the wrong end of the field glasses. Kio had walked the perimeter of the Drift-Weir in the sleet while I was gone. I could feel the map of it sitting in the muscle of my calves, neat and cold and measured in paces: forty-two steps from the net-loft ladder to the school-bus shanty, sixteen planks missing on the south catwalk, three men with fish-spears sleeping by the kelp vats, one blind spot behind the sunken ore-hopper where a skiff could come in under the spray without the boy on the raft hearing the oars.

*Kio,* I pleaded, pressing the heels of my palms into my eye sockets until red and green geometry bloomed in the dark. *Stop pacing. My knees are swollen. We walked four miles on the beach before we rowed out here.*

He didn't answer. Kio never answers me. He was born during the second winter of the first war, when the anti-gods of the eastern quadrant pinned me in a flooded lime-kiln for eleven days while Tim was still dead the first time and my skin was peeling off from the early corruption fever. When you're nineteen years old and you haven't slept in two hundred hours because everything that breathes outside the kiln door wants to cut your head off and put it on a spike to stop a prophecy, your brain doesn't ask your permission. It builds a guy out of wet limestone and silence who doesn't know what a brother is and doesn't care about prophecies, and it hands him the legs whenever you fall down.

Then the guardrail rang like a struck bell, and Harumi laughed.

"Oh, look at the whole fucking family," my mouth said out loud.

I didn't say it. My jaw unhinged on its own, wider and looser than I ever hold it, and my shoulders dropped two inches away from my ears, and my spine rolled back against the zinc wall of the net-loft with a loud metal *clonk*.

My right hand came up, reached into the breast pocket of my damp canvas shirt, and pulled out half a clove of raw, sprouted garlic wrapped in a piece of dried kelp leaf.

My teeth bit into it.

The taste exploded across my tongue, sharp and hot and rank enough to make my eyes water. I gagged inside the back of my own throat, trying to spit it out, trying to get my fingers back from her, and Harumi just chewed harder, grinning with my cracked lips while the bitter juice burned all the way down into my empty stomach.

"Quit kicking the dashboard, Ena," Harumi said to the empty shed. Her voice is lower than mine. She pitches it down in the chest where the red hum used to sit, raspy and lazy, dragging the vowels out like she's got all the time in the world and a pocket full of somebody else's money. "The kid wanted to look at the dinosaur. The soldier wanted to count the boats. And you left us sitting on a barrel in a wet shack with a sleeping mind-demon whose leg smells like an old tooth. Somebody had to go out in the rain and steal breakfast."

*Put the body down,* I shouted at her from the back of the stairwell. *Give me my hands back. You split my knuckles.*

"I split our knuckles on a rusty nail pulling a slat off the cabbage crate behind the school bus," Harumi said, licking a flake of garlic skin off my lower lip. "You hadn't eaten since Tuesday morning on the beach. You were shaking so bad at the oars yesterday I thought you were gonna drop us both in the trench, and if you drown us before I get to see what's on the far side of this puddle, I'll never let you hear the end of it."

She reached down with my left hand and pressed two fingers hard against the outside of my wet sleeve, right over the blue button tin strapped to the meat of my bicep.

Through the cloth and the tin lid, the last black shard gave a tiny, dead click.

A wave of nausea so hot it tasted like penny-rust washed up from my groin to my teeth. My skin prickled into gooseflesh across both collarbones. God help me, even from three yards back in the dark inside my own head, part of me leaned toward those two fingers. Just one fingernail of black glass. Press it into the meat of the palm until the skin splits and the dark melts into the blood, and within ten seconds the awl behind the left temple would pull right out. The kitchen with the yellow daisies would go quiet. The flooded cellar stairs would dry up. The guardrail would vanish. There wouldn't be four of us fighting over ten fingers and one pair of muddy boots; there would just be the big, warm, red hum saying *you're whole, you're right, weapons don't have headaches.*

"Yeah," Harumi whispered, her thumb rubbing the round edge of the tin through the canvas in a slow, dirty circle. "You feel that too, don't you, saint? Four days clean and you're already falling into pieces so small the kid with the markers had to come out and hold your face together."

"Take your hand off her arm," a voice said from the doorway.

Harumi didn't jump. Kio took the spine for half a second, bringing our heels under the barrel rim and shifting our weight onto the balls of the feet, our right hand dropping toward the rusted gaff hook leaning against the lard tins, before Harumi shoved him back down the cellar stairs with a snort.

"Jesus Christ," Harumi said, glaring at the narrow plank door of the net-loft. "Do all you mind-freaks walk without boots, or did they take your heels off in the first war?"

Esme stood on the threshold with the sleet blowing into the dark hair around her shoulders.

She looked older than she had at the hermitage when Galactix fell, and older than she'd looked in my kitchen at the edge of the restored world when my table used to glitch at the corners. She wore a long oilskin coat patched at the elbows with grey seal leather, and her boots were caked with dried fish-scale meal from the lower curing tables. The brown eyes in her thin, sharp face didn't glow gold anymore, though around the very outer rim of the iris there was a faint, permanent ring of dusty blue, like chimney smoke trapped under glass.

She was carrying a tin mug of hot chicory water in each hand.

She stepped inside, kicked the plank door shut against the sleet with her heel, and looked past me into the corner where Eri was snoring lightly on the cork floats.

"She's still got the horsehide tied on," Esme said mildly. "I wondered why I couldn't hear her complaining from the curing sheds. Usually when Eri has a fever, half the hemisphere has to listen to her opinions about her mother."

"Wren broke her leg over a boot and reset it yesterday," Harumi said, not taking her hand off the blue tin on our bicep. "If you're here to peek under the lid and tell the fish-people that Corrupt the world-eater is sitting in their net-loft, save your breath. I'll push this whole tin shack into the channel before your eyes turn gold."

Esme looked at our face for a long moment. She didn't look angry, and she didn't look frightened. She looked the way she used to look at Tim when he asked her to explain master-time for the fourth time before breakfast: deeply, bone-wearyingly annoyed.

"First of all," Esme said, walking over and setting one of the tin mugs down on the floorboards by my wet boots, "everybody on the Weir already knows who you are. You have silver hair, scars across your nose from when the red burned out, and you rowed in with a one-eyed demon with curled horns. Nobody out here cares. Half the people on the third raft used to work in Himo's supply yards, and the other half watched their towns turn into salt marshes when the highway broke. If you aren't eating their dried cod or poking holes in the oil drums, you can be the Queen of Hell for all they care."

"I ate three of their cabbages last night," Harumi said, grinning with my teeth.

"They were rotten turnips from the bait crate," said Esme, sitting down on a coiled pile of tarred hemp line across from the herring barrel. "And second of all, I didn't peek. I haven't gone into a mind without asking since the month after my house came back together. I don't need to go into your head to hear you, Harumi. You're both shouting so loud my teeth have been buzzing since I woke up at four."

In the corner, the cork floats rustled.

Eri pushed herself up on one elbow, grimacing as her splinted right leg dragged across the canvas tarp. Her yellow-brown eye was bloodshot and crusty with sleep, and her wool hood had slipped back, showing the grey stubble between her horns. She looked at Esme, then at me sitting on the barrel with the half-chewed garlic clove between my fingers, and let out a long, rattling groan.

"Oh, great," Eri croaked. "The librarian's here. Does your polite little sister still want to make us all tea, Esme, or did you finally drown her in a puddle?"

"She told me to bring you two chicory," Esme said dryly, taking a sip from her own mug. "And then I told her to shut up, and then I brought the chicory anyway because I needed you both to stop screaming behind your forehead so I could finish mending the herring seine."

Esme lowered her mug and looked straight at my face.

"Let Ena back into the front," Esme said. "She's thirty feet back behind her own ears and she's terrified you're going to open that button tin on her arm."

Harumi stiffened. My shoulders came up around my neck, the jaw locking hard. "I'm not taking orders from a vessel who couldn't even hold onto her own neck when Galactix was done with her. Ena doesn't want the front. Ena's been trying to puke up her own ribs for three days while she stares at the water and thinks about the boy behind the dryers. When Ena has the body, we sit on a rock until we starve or somebody hits us with an axe."

*Let me out, Harumi,* I said, pushing forward against the long dark tunnel behind my pupils. The awl tapped three times, vicious and hot, right above my left eyebrow. Nausea rolled through our stomach, hers and mine at the same time, mixing the garlic oil with cold bile. *Please. Esme's right here. Just let me talk to her.*

"Oh, sure," Harumi sneered out loud, though her grip on our left bicep loosened by a fraction of an inch. "Talk to the mind anti-god. Ask her how to scrape the bad part out of your skull so you can go back to being the sweet little sister who cries at graves and says sorry every ten minutes. Go ahead. Ask her."

She shoved back.

It wasn't a clean switch. When you've been switching since you were seven years old in a kitchen that smelled like blood and burnt batter, and you don't have the corruption hum in your veins anymore to grease the hinges, getting dropped back into your own body feels like falling ten feet off a barn roof into wet mud.

My vision snapped forward with a physical *crack* behind my eyeballs.

Suddenly my arms were full-sized again. My knees ached with a deep, freezing throb from the sleet walk I hadn't taken. The garlic taste in my mouth was so strong I bent double over my knees and spat twice onto the zinc-dust floor, wiping my tongue on the back of my torn sleeve, my chest heaving as if I'd just swum up from the bottom of the sulfur trench.

And Harumi didn't leave.

She stayed right behind my right ear, leaning against the highway guardrail inside my skull, so close I could feel her rage hot against the back of my throat like heartburn.

"Esme," I gasped, wrapping both arms around my ribs to stop the shivering. "Esme, you can feel it. You fixed Pia when War was in her. You went into that little girl on the fourth floor. It's still in me. Even without the shards. When the tank broke in the Nursery and all of Twenty-two's gods poured through my mouth, something stayed behind. Or the residual corruption grew a brain of its own while I was using on Fifth Street. I keep losing days. I wake up with blood on my hands or garlic in my mouth or my boots tied wrong, and there's a little girl counting markers and a man watching the door, and Harumi keeps taking my hands..."

My voice broke. I pressed my scarred wrist hard against my teeth to bite the sob in half.

"Tell me how to cut them out," I whispered through my skin. "Please. Eri won't take her gloves off. You're the anti-god of the mind. Reach in and wall them off, or burn the hollow out, the way they pulled the corruption out of me after Galactix fell. If I don't get rid of her, she's going to put that last shard in my palm while I'm asleep, and we're going to kill somebody else's kid like we killed Dov."

Behind my right ear, Harumi went dead silent.

It wasn't a quiet silence. It was the silence of somebody who has just been slapped across the mouth in front of strangers.

In the corner on the cork floats, Eri stopped rubbing her temple. Her one yellow-brown eye fixed on Esme across the dim shed.

"Don't look at me," Eri said, her voice flat and raspy. "I tried to tell her on the beach two weeks ago and she wouldn't listen to me because I'm a liar from the Fourth Ring. Tell her what's in her skull, librarian."

Esme set her tin mug down on the floorboards between her boots. She folded her hands in her lap, over the grey seal patches on her oilskin coat, and looked at me with those tired, brown-and-smoke eyes.

"Ena," Esme said quietly. "I don't need to reach into your head to look. I've been listening to your mind since you were twenty-two years old at the hermitage."

I stared at her, my arms still locked around my stomach. "Back then it was Corrupt."

"Back then there was corruption on top of it," Esme said. "Like oil poured over a broken plate. Everybody looked at the oil because the oil was glowing red and killing cities. Nobody looked at the plate underneath, because we needed you to be a weapon, and as long as one part of you could swing a sword at Galactix and another part of you cried afterward so Tim could tell himself his sister was still in there, nobody asked how your mind was holding itself together."

Esme leaned forward on the coil of tarred rope.

"There is no monster in your head, Ena," Esme said. "There is no Horseman. There's no Nursery parasite, and there's no hollow from a master like the one in me. The corruption is gone. Every drop of it except the dead flake of glass you've got tied to your arm like an idiot."

"Then what are they?" I cried out. The awl behind my left temple felt like it was splitting the bone in half. "Why can't I remember last night? Why do I smell burnt pancakes when somebody raises a wrench at me?"

"Because you were seven years old when Himo climbed through your kitchen window and butchered your mother and father upstairs while you and Tim sat at the breakfast table," Esme said. Her voice wasn't cruel, and it wasn't soft; it was the exact, level voice she used when she mapped a nexus collapse. "A seven-year-old child's mind isn't finished growing together yet. It comes in pieces, and when a child lives carefully enough, the pieces knit into one room over time. When something comes through the window that is too big and too ugly for a seven-year-old to hold in one room without dying of it, the mind does the only clever thing it can do. It leaves the walls up between the rooms."

I couldn't breathe. The rain drummed on the zinc roof overhead: *clatter-clatter-clatter*.

"One seven-year-old stays at the Formica table counting colored pens and smelling the griddle," Esme said, "so the girl who goes upstairs and sees the blood on the wallpaper doesn't have to be the same girl who has to eat breakfast the next Tuesday. And twelve years later, when the brother who promised to keep you safe from everything puts a steel knife into your breastbone because your eyes turned red, the girl who loves him can't hold that knife and stay sane. So another wall goes up. And someone else stands on the highway guardrail and holds the rage and the knife and the blood, so Ena can keep loving Tim."

Behind my right ear, something flinched so hard my own right shoulder jerked against the zinc wall.

"And when they lock you in a flooded kiln for eleven days during the war," Eri put in quietly from the cork pile, her gloved fingers picking at a loose thread on her wool coat, "you build a mute bastard on the cellar stairs who knows how to count breaths and lace boots in the dark so neither the little kid nor the good sister has to learn how to slit a sentry's throat with a bucket handle."

"No," I whispered. I shook my head so hard my cropped silver hair slapped across my wet cheeks. "No. Harumi isn't me. Harumi likes it. When we were Corrupt, she laughed when the towers came down. When Dov saw me with the shards behind the dryers in the laundromat, I didn't want to hurt him, I was just scared he'd tell Marguerite, and my hand came up on its own and she turned him into black sugar..."

My right hand slapped flat against the side of my own jaw.

Hard. So hard my teeth clicked together on my tongue and my head snapped sideways toward the stove-pipe.

"You fucking coward!" my own mouth snarled.

Harumi didn't take the whole body this time. We were wedged in the doorway together, both of us jammed into the front behind my eyes at once, my left hand still clutching my ribs while my right hand gripped my own chin like a vise, my eyes watering from the blow.

"Say that again!" Harumi shouted through my teeth, her voice and mine scraping over each other in the same throat until it sounded like gravel in a bucket. "Say I killed Dov while you were just standing there being scared! Tell the mind-bitches who went down the stairs to the laundromat on Fifth Street at eleven at night because Marguerite said there were six hounds in the alley and a blue tin on the washing machine! Tell them whose feet walked down those steps, Ena!"

*You took the hand!* I screamed back at her inside our skull, while tears ran down both sides of our nose. *I only wanted to clear the hounds so Marguerite would say she was proud of me! I wasn't going to open the blue tin until I got back to the roof! And then Dov came out from behind the big dryer with his mop bucket and saw the red in my eyes, and I froze, I couldn't move, and you reached out and grabbed his shirt!*

"Because you dropped us on the floor!" Harumi roared out loud in the shed. Our right fist slammed down onto the rim of the herring barrel so hard the split skin across the middle knuckles burst open again, smearing bright red blood across the salted oak stave. "You always drop us on the floor when it goes bad! Every single fucking time since Tim stuck that butcher knife into our ribs in the kitchen! You look at somebody's sad face, and you go *oh no, I'm a bad person, I can't look, I can't be here,* and you pull the plug and fall three yards back into the dark and leave me holding a smoking body in a room full of broken glass and a nineteen-year-old kid screaming at the top of his lungs three feet from the street door!"

Esme didn't flinch. Eri didn't move on the cork floats. Both of them just watched us fight over one mouth.

"What was I supposed to do when you went dark in the laundromat, Ena?" Harumi's voice cracked on the name, high and ugly and shaking with ten thousand years of carrying the butcher's apron while the saint got the flowers on the monument steps. "Let him run out onto Fifth Street yelling that Corrupt had twenty-two shards in her pocket? Let Marguerite's dogs tear us apart while you sat in the basement with Sunny counting Sharpies? You handed me a loaded arm full of red grease and stepped out of the room so you wouldn't have to watch what it did, and then at three in the morning you came back out, got down on your knees on the tile, cried over the black sugar while you scraped him into the button tin, and told yourself *Harumi made me do it* so you could still be the girl who says sorry!"

I sat on the barrel with both hands open on my knees, shaking from my heels to my teeth.

Inside my head, the awl had stopped tapping. The wall between the barrel and the highway guardrail was thin as wet tissue paper, and through it, for the first time since the day Tim died at the monument, I felt what it actually felt like to be Harumi.

It didn't feel like the red hum.

It didn't feel like laughing while cities burned.

It felt like standing barefoot on broken glass in front of an unlocked kitchen door for thirty-four years with a bloody knife in your fist, freezing cold, waiting for the next thing to come through the window while the girl behind you wept into her hands and called you a devil for having blood on your sleeves.

"You hate me," I whispered out loud. My voice was very small.

For three seconds, neither of us moved the mouth. Then my head tilted half an inch to the right, and my shoulders dropped into Harumi's slouch, though she didn't push me back down the stairwell. She left me right there behind the left eye where I could feel the salt drying on our cheeks.

"I don't hate you, idiot," Harumi said roughly, staring down at the bloody knuckle print on the barrel stave. "I'm made out of your ribs. How the fuck am I supposed to hate you? I hate that you pretend you don't know why I'm here. I hate that every Tuesday on that roof, when Eri climbed the fire escape with her peaches, you shoved me behind the water tank with the mint tin like a dirty rag so you two could play best friends, and then the second her horns went down the ladder you whistled me back up to hold the shard so the burn wouldn't touch your clean hands."

From the cork pile in the corner, Eri let out a short, harsh breath that wasn't quite a laugh.

"She didn't fool me on Tuesdays because you were hidden behind the tank, Harumi," Eri said, looking at the zinc ceiling. "She fooled me because I didn't look. Same way I didn't look in Duke Vasselgrave's head before I sold Lute. If I looked behind the water tank, I wouldn't get to sit on the roof eating peaches with the only person in three dead worlds who still called me babe."

Eri rolled her head on the folded canvas coat and looked at us with her one yellow-brown eye.

"And for what it's worth," Eri added, "I always liked you better than her when we were drinking. Ena tips the cup over after two glasses and talks about her brother's handwriting. At least you know how to steal turnips without waking the dogs."

Our mouth twitched. My left side wanted to cry; Harumi's right side wanted to spit on the floor. It came out as a crooked, wet snort.

"They were cabbages," Harumi muttered. "Mostly."

Esme leaned down, picked up the second tin mug of warm chicory from by our boots, and held it out toward our hands.

"You cannot cut them out, Ena," Esme said, waiting until both of my hands, mine on the left, Harumi's raw-knuckled one on the right, closed around the warm tin together. "If I went into your mind and tried to wall off Harumi, or Kio, or the child at the table, I wouldn't be curing you. I'd be doing what Galactix did to me, and what Siren did to Eri and Wren. I'd be folding three quarters of your soul down under the floor in the dark so the quarter that looks polite in daylight could pretend nothing bad ever happened on Elm Street."

"Then how do I stop losing days?" I asked, staring down into the dark brown water in the mug. "How do I know I won't wake up tomorrow with that black shard melted into my palm?"

"You stop locking the door when you switch," Esme said simply. "Right now, every time you get scared or ashamed, you slam your eyes shut and throw yourself down the stairs so you don't have to watch what Harumi or Kio does to keep the body breathing. And every time Harumi takes the front, she shoves you back into the dark because she's sick of listening to you cry about how evil she is. That's why you lose fourteen hours. The wall is only opaque because both of you keep hanging blankets over it."

Esme tapped one finger against her own temple, right where the smoky blue ring sat around her brown iris.

"The thing that grew in Galactix's dent inside my head is still there," Esme said. "She's listening right now. Five minutes ago she suggested I smooth out the nerve behind your left eyebrow so the switch wouldn't hurt so much, and I told her if she touched your head without a signed paper I'd spend the next three hours thinking about salt pork until she got sick. We don't like each other. We will never be one neat, quiet mind the way I was when I was six. Yet I don't lose hours anymore, because I leave the reading-room door open an inch so I can see what book she's pulling off the shelf."

I looked down at my left arm.

With my right thumb, Harumi's split, serum-crusted thumb, and my own shaking left fingers working together, we rolled back the wet grey canvas cuff past the elbow.

The greasy leather cord had bitten two red grooves into the pale skin of our bicep. Wedged under the cord was the little round blue button tin from the laundromat on Fifth Street. The paint on the lid was chipped down to gray tin around the edges, scratched by Ena's fingernails on three hundred nights when she sat behind the water tank trying to decide whether to open it.

Inside the tin, wrapped in a piece of grey flannel to keep it from rattling while I rowed, was the last flake of black glass.

*If we throw it through the floor planks into the trench,* Sunny whispered from way back by the Formica table, very small, *will the dogs stop coming to the window?*

*No,* Kio answered from the cellar steps. A cold, flat certainty, measured in paces and wind direction. *The scent is already in the meat of the left arm. Three months of blood doesn't wash out in one tide. They chased the boat from the Reach.*

Harumi didn't say anything inside our head. She used our mouth.

"If we throw it in the trench," Harumi said, staring at the chipped blue lid on our bicep, "and something comes over that breakwater tonight that's bigger than a turnip crate, there is no big red hum coming to save us. There's no Corrupt. There's no wings of fire. There's just a thirty-four-year-old woman with bad knees, a butchered wrist, and a rusty gaff hook."

"Yeah," I said, with the exact same mouth, half a second later. "I know."

"You can't fight worth shit without the red, Ena. You freeze up when glass breaks."

"Then don't lock me in the basement when the glass breaks," I said, looking down at our right hand with its split knuckles. "Take the front. Hold the gaff hook. I'll stay behind the left eye and watch the door with Kio so you don't have to do it alone in the dark anymore."

For a long time, the only sound in the net-loft was the sleet tapping on the zinc roof and the water dripping into the five rusted lard tins: *plink, plok, plink*.

Harumi didn't untie the leather cord on our bicep. She wasn't ready to drop the tin into the sulfur trench yet; neither of us was, if we were telling the truth for once in our lives. Addicts don't throw the last shard off the bridge on day four just because somebody had a breakthrough over a mug of chicory.

Instead, Harumi pulled the wet canvas sleeve back down over the tin, buttoned the cuff at the wrist over the thirteen white scars, and picked up the mug with both hands.

We drank the chicory together. It was lukewarm and bitter as charred bark, and behind my right ear, for the first time in three days, the awl pulled back a quarter of an inch from the bone, and the highway guardrail didn't feel quite so far away from the barrel.



Chapter Six: The Woman Who Never Spoke

By late afternoon the sleet blew itself out toward the southern shoals, leaving a low, grease-colored sky that sat ten feet above the school-bus roof and smelled like dead dogfish.

Eri was out cold again after Wren came up the ladder at three o'clock, checked the linen wrappings on her shins without saying one word to either of us, and left two salted herrings and a tin of willow-bark paste on the barrel by the door. My knees were stiff from sitting on the staves, and inside my skull Kio had been tapping his boot heel against the cellar brick for two hours because he hadn't checked the windward breakwater in daylight.

So we went out.

All of us, in a loose, crooked sort of way that made my vision tilt slightly to the right whenever I stepped over a gap in the planks. I had the legs and the left hand; Harumi was sitting right behind my right shoulder, watching the rope rail and whistling silently between our back teeth; Kio was measuring the drop between the oil drums and counting the rusted chain bindings on the pilings.

To get to the outer breakwater of the Drift-Weir, you have to cross the narrowest catwalk on the structure: thirty feet of rusted highway guardrail bolted across six waterlogged cedar tree trunks that roll under your soles every time a swell hits the seaward wall. Beyond the catwalk lies the crescent reef made of broken concrete overpass slabs and the three sunken ore-hopper hulls. Nobody sleeps out there except the gulls. The spray comes straight over the rusted iron gunwales of the middle hopper at high tide, coating the concrete blocks in grey salt slime and tangles of torn brown kelp as thick as a man's thigh.

Except somebody did sleep out there.

Inside the rusted iron wheelhouse of the northernmost ore-hopper, where the glass had been blown out of the portholes years ago and replaced with tarred sailcloth stretched on driftwood frames, there was a coal-oil brazier burning dry kelp stalks.

A woman was sitting on a block of highway granite outside the wheelhouse door, scraping barnacles off an iron chain link with a flat cobbler's rasp.

She was nineteen, maybe twenty, though her shoulders had the thick, knotted slope of somebody who had spent the last two years hauling stone blocks in the cold. Her hair was dull brown, hacked off square at the jawline with a knife, and she wore an old brown wool coat that had been mended down the right ribcage with coarse white sail twine in big, clumsy cross-stitches. Across the front of her throat, just above the collar of her flannel shirt, the skin was puckered and shiny, crosshatched with tiny silver stretch-marks like a frost-split plum that had healed back together.

Resting across her knees, under the chain link, was a weapon.

It wasn't a knife, and it wasn't a sword like the long slim grey ones people in the restored world used to talk about when they told the story of the night the hounds came up out of the quarry in the narrow grey town. It looked like a butcher's cleaver that had been forged out of an iron anvil by someone who was blind and crying. The blade was fourteen inches long, four inches wide at the toe, black and pitted with salt rust, and three times heavier than any normal steel that size had a right to be. The handle was wrapped in grey oilcloth, and under the oilcloth, if you looked hard in the flat dull light, you could see the faint, raised outline of letters stamped straight into the iron tang.

"Pia," I said, stopping six feet back on the wet concrete slab so my shadow wouldn't fall across her knees.

She looked up from the rasp.

Her eyes were plain brown. No red at the edges. No ancient second voice rolling around behind the iris like cannon wheels on a stone road.

When I'd sat with her on the steps of Tim's monument two years ago, she'd been so crowded with War that every time she breathed, her coat seemed to ring like mail armor. Now her skull was as quiet as an empty barn after the hay has been hauled out for the winter.

Pia looked at my cropped silver hair, and at the sunken lattice of scars across the bridge of my nose, and then down at my left arm where the canvas sleeve was buttoned over my wrist.

She opened her mouth to answer.

What came out wasn't a voice. It was a dry, whistling scrape, like two pieces of pumice stone being rubbed together in an empty kettle.

"Ena," she whispered. It cost her three breaths to shape the two syllables. Her left hand came up and pressed two scarred fingers against the shiny, frost-split skin over her windpipe to hold the cartilage still while she spoke. "And. The other one."

Behind my right ear, Harumi tilted her head. *Well, shit,* Harumi thought, almost respectfully. *The quiet kid actually looks worse than us.*

"You don't have to talk if it hurts," I told her, walking over and sitting down on the wet granite lip of the overpass slab four feet to her left. The stone was freezing through my canvas trousers. Below our boots, on the seaward side of the ore-hopper hull, the grey chop slammed into the submerged concrete blocks with a dull, booming thud that vibrated up through my tailbone. "Esme told me you were on the Weir. What happened to your throat? Where's War?"

Pia set the cobbler's rasp down on the granite beside her hip. She kept her right hand flat on the salt-pitted blade of the heavy iron cleaver across her knees.

She pointed with two fingers of her left hand toward the grey horizon, where the sky met the sulfur trench in a smear of dirty pewter.

"Highway," Pia rasped. She spoke one or two words at a time, slow and flat, saving the air in her chest the way a man on a life raft saves rainwater. "When the sky broke. And the song came. War heard it."

I flinched. Under my left sleeve, the round edge of the blue button tin dug into my bicep.

When I punched the glass tank in Himo's Nursery and swallowed Twenty-two's throat, the song had gone out across every world and every pocket dimension like a tidal wave. *This is what you get.* It hadn't just pulled the Nursery babies like Siren back toward the drain; it had hit every god and anti-god and scrap of first-war divinity left in the universe like a hammer hitting cast iron.

"War was already thin," Pia whispered, her thumb tracing the oilcloth wrapping on the cleaver's hilt. "Two years of holding my stomach together from the quarry hound. When the song hit the town, the cracks opened under the square. The sky started falling into the sea. War tried to hold the roof up over my mother's house."

Pia paused, swallowed once with a dry click in her scarred neck, and looked down at the black iron blade.

"You can't hold a sky with a sword," she whispered. "She needed steel. More steel than we had on the new list. We only had eleven things left unsaid. Small ones. So she reached down into her own chest. She forged herself."

Harumi went completely still behind my right eye.

"She screamed through my mouth for nine minutes while the town went into the water," Pia rasped, her brown eyes dry and steady on the rusted hull plate in front of us. "Every battle she ever fought since the first two men quarreled over a piece of meat. Ten thousand years of horses and halberds and dead boys in ditches. All of it came up my throat at once to make a pillar big enough to keep the stone off the boats until the people got out into the bay. It burned my vocal cords out like fuse wire. And when the pillar broke and the water closed over the quarry, there wasn't enough of her left to say goodbye."

Pia lifted the ugly, fourteen-inch iron cleaver an inch off her knees and let it drop back onto the wool of her coat with a heavy *thump*.

"Just this," Pia whispered. "At the bottom of the boat. When I woke up."

"What sentence was it?" Harumi asked out loud, taking the mouth before I could stop her, though her voice wasn't mocking at all; it was low and rough and terribly quiet. "The cleaver. Every weapon she forged came from something somebody didn't say. What was that one?"

Pia looked at us. She knew it was Harumi talking by the drop in the shoulders and the flat slant of our jaw, and she didn't blink.

"Hers," Pia whispered, tapping the stamped letters under the oilcloth on the iron tang. "Not mine. The last thing she never said before she burned out."

"What does it say?" I asked.

Pia didn't look down to read it. She knew it by the shape of the metal under her calloused palm.

"*I don't want to die alone in a ditch again,*" Pia rasped.

None of us said anything for a long time.

The grey sea boomed against the outer concrete slabs: *whump... shhhhhh... whump*. Spray came over the rusted bow of the ore-hopper like cold spit, wetting my silver hair and beading on the black rust of Pia's cleaver.

*She's still got the scar on her stomach,* Kio noted quietly from the cellar stairs inside my head. *If the Horseman burned out, what's holding her guts together now?*

I didn't have to ask Pia out loud. Her brown wool coat hung open an inch at the bottom above her belt, and through the gap I could see a thick, clumsy corset of boiled horse-leather laced tight around her waist with tarred marline twine, holding the three-year-old quarry scar closed by brute mechanical pressure. Every time she took a deep breath, she winced slightly on the left side where the hound's teeth had gone in when she was seventeen.

She wasn't magic anymore. She wasn't a vessel for the apocalypse. She was just a twenty-year-old girl with a ruined throat, a belly held together by boiled leather and string, and twenty pounds of dead iron across her knees, sitting on the windward rock in the freezing spray because somebody had to watch the water.

"Why are you sleeping out here on the stone instead of in the sheds?" I asked her.
