Pia lifted her chin toward the dark trench beyond the reef.

"Smell," she rasped.

"The fish vats?"

"No," Pia said, resting two fingers on her throat. "You."

My stomach dropped three inches.

Behind my left temple, the awl gave one sharp, warning tap. Kio stood up on the cellar stairs inside my head, his weight shifting forward onto the balls of my boots before I even knew my legs were moving.

"They don't swim well," Pia whispered, her brown eyes tracking a floating mat of yellow bladderwrack thirty yards out beyond the concrete gap where the channel tide ran purple-black. "Their legs are wrong for deep water. They sink if they stop paddling. So they walk along the drowned highway concrete on the bottom until the pilings come up, and then they climb the anchor chains when the tide drops."

"Hounds," I said.

My voice didn't sound like a thirty-four-year-old woman's voice. It sounded thin and hollow, and right underneath it, rising up from the back of my sinuses like grease catching fire on a dirty stove, came the smell of burnt pancake batter and cheap floral detergent powder.

*Fifth Street,* Sunny whispered in the dark, her hands knocking the coffee can of Sharpies over onto the yellow daisies. *The big dog is by the soap machine.*

"Marguerite's hounds died when the city went under," Harumi snapped out loud, grabbing the front of our mouth with both hands to keep Sunny from pulling our eyes backward down the stairwell. Our right hand reached out and gripped the rusted iron rim of the ore-hopper porthole until the rust flaked under our fingernails. "When the highway collapsed, the residual pockets flooded. Nothing survived ten miles of salt water."

"Three days ago," Pia rasped, picking up the cobbler's rasp and sliding it into the pocket of her mended coat, "I killed a small one on the south chain at low tide. It had kelp growing out of its eye sockets and crab shells stuck in the meat of its ribs. It couldn't bark. Its lungs were full of salt mud. It wasn't looking for food. It was sniffing the tar barrels where the boats tie up from the Reach."

Pia looked down at my left bicep.

"You have a piece of it," Pia whispered. "Under your shirt. Even dead, it smells like the quarry to them. Tonight the tide drops four feet below the hopper keel at midnight. The concrete reef comes out of the surf. If they're down on the highway roadbed, tonight is when they walk up."

"Then throw the fucking tin in the water right now," Harumi snarled at me inside our skull, her own fingers twitching toward our left cuff.

*Kio said it's already in the meat of our arm!* I shouted back at her in the dark. *Three months of melting shards into our palm on Fifth Street doesn't wash off because you drop the tin! They aren't hunting the tin, Harumi, they're hunting our blood!*

And if we threw the tin into the forty-foot trench right now, and ten of Marguerite's hounds came crawling up out of the black surf over the breakwater blocks at midnight with crab shells in their ribs and red mud in their eight eyes, we would have nothing.

No red hum. No black hand that turned dogs into sugar with one touch. Just a rusty iron gaff hook and two bad knees.

We went back across the guardrail catwalk to the net-loft at dusk to get the gaff hook and a whale-oil lantern, and neither of us touched the leather cord under our left sleeve.

We didn't tell the seventy people sleeping in the tugboat shanties and the yellow school bus. If you yell *corruption hounds* on a floating raft of rotten planks at dark, half the people will jump into the skiffs and drown each other in the chop, and the other half will try to throw the silver-haired woman off the pier with fish-spears before the dogs arrive.

We told Wren.

She came out to the inner end of the guardrail catwalk at eleven o'clock at night with the fourteen-inch iron pipe wrench in her right fist, a whale-oil lantern shuttered down to a slit in her left, and a leather apron tied over her wool vest.

"Pip's locked in the wheelhouse with Esme and your one-eyed devil," Wren said quietly in the dark, the wind whipping the spray across her shaved skull. "If anything gets past this guardrail, Esme says she'll drop the chain bridge with the winch and let the whole outer hopper drift into the current with the three of us on it."

"Fair enough," Harumi said with our mouth, testing the weight of the four-foot ash-handled iron gaff hook we'd taken from the curing shed.

We crossed the rolling cedar trunks to the ore-hopper breakwater at half past eleven.

The tide had dropped further than I'd seen it since we left the Reach. The grey chop wasn't slapping against the iron hull plates anymore; it was sucking and gurgling four feet below, down among the slimed, jagged teeth of the broken highway slabs that formed the reef. The smell of the exposed bottom was overwhelming: rotten sea-lettuce, dead crabs, sulfur mud, and underneath it, faint and sweet and horribly familiar, the smell of an old copper penny that had been sucked in a feverish mouth.

Residual corruption.

Not bright like it used to be in the quarry or behind the laundromat on Fifth. Waterlogged. Half-dead. Turned to brine and rot at the bottom of the sea, with only one scrap of an old command left twitching in the spinal cord: *find the red, eat the red, bring the red home.*

The moon didn't come out, though the cloud cover thinned enough to let a sick, tallow-colored smear of light down across the wet concrete blocks.

Pia was standing on the wide flat slab of an overturned highway divider at the lowest lip of the breakwater, ten feet above the black surf. She had taken off her brown wool coat so it wouldn't catch on her elbows, standing in her grey flannel shirt with the stiff boiled-leather corset laced tight around her ribs and the fourteen-inch black iron cleaver held in both hands against her thigh.

Wren took the left gap between the hopper bow and a jagged hunk of brick masonry, bracing her boots wide in the kelp slime, the shuttered lantern set on a crate behind her heel so it threw our long crooked shadows out across the foam.

I stood in the middle gap, where a broken culvert pipe four feet wide sloped down into the surging black water.

Twelve o'clock came and went. Nothing moved except the kelp.

My hands were freezing on the ash shaft of the gaff hook. The wood was slick with salt grease. Inside my head, the four of us were stacked up behind my eyeballs like people crowding a narrow doorway in a thunderstorm: Kio counting the nine-second interval between the big swells; Harumi leaning hard against the right side of my jaw with her teeth bared; Sunny curled under the Formica table at the very bottom of my stomach with both hands clamped over her ears; and me in the front, staring down the dark culvert pipe while the thirteen scars on my left wrist throbbed in time with my pulse.

At twenty past twelve, a swell came in from the trench, lifted the black mats of bladderwrack in the culvert mouth, and didn't take all of them back out when it sucked down.

One of the mats stayed wedged between two pieces of rusted rebar at the bottom of the concrete slope.

Then the mat stood up on six legs.

It wasn't dry and grey like the hounds in the alley behind Fifth Street. The ocean had been eating it for six months. It was the size of a yearling calf, bloated and hairless, its hide the color of a drowned pig's belly, wrapped in tangled skeins of brown ribbon-kelp that trailed from the exposed white ribs on its left flank. Its head didn't sit straight on its neck; the cervical spine had twisted ninety degrees during the collapse, so its jaw opened sideways like a crab's claw, splitting all the way back to the shoulder blades.

Across the bloated ridge of its skull, nine eyes opened one after another in the dark.

Five of them were dead white, boiled milky by the salt water. The other four flickered with a dim, sputtering, greasy red light, like a wet match head being struck in a cellar.

And when its sideways jaw gaped open to drain a quart of black seawater onto the concrete, the sound that came out of its flooded windpipe wasn't a bark.

It was a wet, clicking stutter, the exact rhythm of the dead washing machines on Fifth Street when the power grid glitched during my relapse: *chk-chk-chk-chk.*

Behind that first one, down in the black foam at the lip of the reef, eleven more sets of dim red eyes blinked open in the surf.

My lungs locked shut.

I knew, somewhere in the part of my brain that belonged to Kio, that I was standing on a wet highway slab three miles out in Pelagia's sea with an iron gaff hook in my hands.

My eyes didn't care what Kio knew.

The dim red flicker in those four wet eyes hit my retinas, and the awl behind my left temple didn't tap; it drove straight through to the back of my skull like a railroad spike.

The concrete culvert vanished.

The smell of dead kelp vanished.

I was standing on cracked white-and-black vinyl floor tiles under a flickering fluorescent tube that smelled of scorching ballast wire and lemon bleach. Three feet to my left, the plate-glass front window of the Fifth Street laundromat had just blown inward in a shower of glittering diamonds across a row of folding metal chairs. Six grey hounds were coming through the frame with their mouths open back to their ears, and behind the big stainless-steel double dryer in the corner, nineteen-year-old Dov in his oversized green parka was dropping his mop bucket on the tiles, his mouth wide open, his eyes staring at the black smoke curling off my left hand, screaming: *Ena! Ena, you're using, oh God, you're using!*

"Ena!" Wren's voice screamed from ten miles away down a dark iron pipe. "Left! In the culvert!"

I couldn't lift the gaff hook.

My fingers opened on the ash handle. If I lifted my hand, black fire would come out of my palm and Dov would turn into black sugar on the linoleum and I would have to scrape him into a blue button tin on my knees at three in the morning so Eri wouldn't find him on Tuesday. My knees buckled on the slimed concrete. I went down on one hip in six inches of freezing tide-slop at the mouth of the culvert while the six-legged thing with the sideways jaw lunged up the wet slope straight at my throat.

*Move!* I tried to scream at my own legs, and nothing came out except a high, seven-year-old whimper from under the kitchen table. *Harumi! Harumi, take it! Take the front! Don't close the door!*

Harumi didn't shove me down the stairs into the dark this time.

She grabbed the body from the inside like a woman grabbing the wheel of a skidding truck from the passenger seat while the driver is still strapped in the chair.

My vision didn't go black. It slammed sideways by two inches and stayed wide open.

I felt Harumi's teeth bare in our mouth, a raw, vicious snarl that tore the dry skin of our lower lip open, and Kio snapped our left heel down against a rebar stub in the culvert floor before our back hit the stone.

"Hold the left eye, saint!" Harumi roared through our throat as the cold water splashed over our face. "Watch how we do this without the fucking glass!"

The first hound hit us chest-to-chest in the mouth of the culvert.

Before the collapse, when we had fourteen shards of residual corruption humming in our blood, a hound weighed nothing. You touched its snout with two black fingers and the meat came apart like wet cake.

Without the red hum, the thing weighed two hundred and forty pounds of wet, stinking gristle and waterlogged bone, and when it hit our left shoulder it felt like getting struck by a falling chimney.

The impact knocked the wind out of our lungs in a flat grunt. Our head cracked back against the concrete wall of the culvert pipe, stars bursting across both our eyes at once, mine on the left, Harumi's on the right. The hound's sideways jaw clamped shut an inch from our ear, its upper teeth raking deep across the top of our left trapezius, tearing through the wet canvas shirt and four inches of skin and muscle down to the collarbone.

The pain was blinding. Hot, bright, ordinary animal pain, with no red glow to turn it into righteousness.

Behind the left eye, I screamed inside our skull and tried to pull back down the dark stairwell toward the Formica table.

*Stay here!* Harumi bellowed inside our head, locking her fingers and mine around the ash shaft of the gaff hook in the water beside our hip. *Don't you fucking drop out on me now, Ena! Look at it! It's a dead dog in a ditch, not the boy behind the dryer! Pull with the left hand!*

I stayed.

For the first time in thirty-four years, I didn't close my eyes and fall into the dark when the blood started running down our chest. I grabbed the wet ash wood with my left hand right behind Harumi's raw-knuckled right fist, listened to Kio's cold voice in our ear saying *up under the jaw hinge, four inches behind the milky eye, use your knee for a fulcrum,* and we shoved the four-inch curved iron hook straight up through the soft green flesh under the hound's sideways throat.

The iron point crunched through the waterlogged roof of its mouth and locked behind the skull plate.

Harumi braced both our boots against the concrete lip of the culvert, arched our bleeding back out of the surf, and heaved sideways with every ounce of muscle we'd built rowing six miles from the Reach.

The hound's neck snapped with a sound like a green willow branch splitting in winter. Its four red eyes blinked twice, sputtered like drowned coals, and went gray.

"One!" Harumi barked, kicking the bloated carcass off the gaff hook back down into the foaming culvert mouth to plug the pipe against the two climbing up behind it.

Ten feet to our right, on the flat highway divider slab, Pia didn't make a sound.

Three hounds came over the seaward edge of her stone at once, their six legs scrabbling on the barnacles, sea-slime pouring from their split jaws. Pia didn't step back. She stood right in front of the gap where the catwalk led back to the sleeping shanties, the exact way the people of her narrow grey town said she'd stood in front of her uncle's door at two in the morning in her bare feet.

She swung the fourteen-inch black iron cleaver from the hip with both arms.

There was no flash of red light. No ancient voice calling weapons out of the air. Just twenty pounds of dead, salt-pitted steel forged out of a Horseman's last unspoken grief, driven by the thick shoulders of a girl who had spent four years holding fifteen hundred fights inside her ribs without dropping one.

The cleaver hit the lead hound square across the bridge of its nine-eyed skull. The heavy iron didn't slice; it crushed the head flat to the concrete with a ringing *clang* like a sledgehammer striking an anvil, spraying black brine and gray brain-grease across Pia's flannel sleeves. On the backswing, before the second dog could get its hind claws set in the kelp, Pia brought the flat iron butt of the oilcloth handle straight into its sideways windpipe, knocking it backward ten feet off the slab into the churning trough below.

To our left, Wren was knee-deep in a tide pool between two brick pilings, swinging the fourteen-inch pipe wrench with quick, brutal, economical strokes while a six-legged hound snapped at the leather apron over her thighs. She didn't fight like a soldier or a hero; she fought like a vet tech who knew where every joint in a dog's front leg bent and how hard you had to hit a collarbone with three pounds of forged iron to make it fold.

*Crack.* The hound's front left shoulder went out. It dropped its snout into the tide pool, and Wren brought the toothed jaw of the pipe wrench down twice on the base of its neck behind the ears until the legs stopped kicking.

Then two more came up through the culvert pipe over the dead one we'd wedged in the rebar.

The bigger one caught the hem of our canvas trousers in its lower jaw and yanked backward as a five-foot swell sucked out through the gap.

Our boots shot out from under us on the wet algae. We went down hard on our stomach in the concrete trough, freezing black salt water closing right over our head, filling our nose and our open mouth, dragging us waist-deep over the lip of the reef toward the forty-foot sulfur trench.

Under the water, the second hound drove its front claws into our left thigh and bit down on our forearm.

Right over the buttoned canvas cuff. Right over the thirteen white scars. Its teeth didn't tear the meat off only because the upper jaw caught on the round metal rim of the blue button tin strapped to our bicep, driving the sharp tin edge half an inch deep into our muscle.

The cold water burned in our torn shoulder like lye. Our lungs were empty. In the black surf around our face, four dim red eyes flared six inches from our nose.

And where the tin bit into our bleeding arm, right against the torn skin, the last black shard woke up.

I felt it through the flannel and the tin lid. Harumi felt it too.

It didn't need us to untie the cord. The hound's teeth had crushed the rusted blue lid half-open against our bicep, and our own blood was soaking through the grey cloth inside. All either of us had to do was flex our left fingers, reach six inches up our own torn sleeve in the dark water, and touch the black glass with one bleeding knuckle.

One touch.

The freezing water wouldn't be cold anymore. The torn trapezius wouldn't hurt. The twelve hounds in the surf would blow apart into black sugar in three seconds, and we wouldn't drown at the bottom of a sulfur trench with crab-eaten dogs chewing on our face.

*Do it!* Sunny screamed from under the kitchen table inside our head, terrified of the dark water and the teeth. *Turn the red light on! Make the dog go away!*

Inside our single skull, with salt water burning in our throat and the hound shaking our left arm like a rat in its teeth, Harumi and I looked at each other across the open doorway.

I didn't fall back into the dark.

And Harumi didn't reach for the tin.

*Fuck the red light,* Harumi thought, so loud and clear it rang off the inside of my teeth like iron. *The red light is how you end up on your knees behind the dryers with a dead kid in a bucket. Pull the hook, Ena. Pull it with me right now or we're both going in the ditch!*

We didn't touch our left sleeve.

With our right hand, hers and mine closing every finger over the ash shaft together while Kio locked our trailing right boot heel behind a jagged lip of highway rebar under the surf, we hauled our head and shoulders six inches out of the black boil.

We coughed once, spitting salt water and blood into the hound's nine eyes, and jammed the short iron point of the broken gaff handle straight into the sideways hinge of the jaw that was clamped on our left arm.

We didn't have leverage to pry it open.

Then a pair of waterlogged boots hit the concrete culvert lip above our head, and twenty pounds of black iron cleaver came down through the foam like a guillotine blade, severing the hound's twisted spine three inches behind its skull.

The jaws went slack on our arm.

Pia didn't try to speak over the boom of the surf. She hooked her left hand under the collar of our torn canvas shirt, Wren grabbed our right belt loop from the masonry pier with her free hand, and the two of them dragged us backward out of the culvert mouth onto the high granite slab while the receding wave sucked the two headless carcasses back down into the dark trench.

Down along the outer reef, the remaining five hounds clung to the barnacle-crusted concrete for ten seconds as the next big swell rolled in. Their milky eyes and their flickering red ones stared up at the three of us standing on the highway slab: Pia with her left hand braced against the boiled-leather corset over her stomach and the black cleaver dripping brine from her right fist; Wren with her pipe wrench cocked at her shoulder; and us on one knee between them, our left shoulder pouring bright red blood down our grey shirt, our chest heaving in huge, ragged, tearing gulps of cold night air.

Then the big wave broke over the lower reef, and when the white foam drained back down into the forty-foot trench, the concrete blocks were empty.

They had sunk back down to the drowned highway on the bottom.

For two full minutes, nobody on the breakwater moved.

I stayed on one knee on the granite slab. My whole body was shaking so violently that my teeth chattered together until my jaw ached. My left shoulder burned where the hound's teeth had laid the muscle open over the collarbone, and my left forearm was bruised purple from wrist to elbow, and my lungs felt as if somebody had scrubbed them out with a wire brush and cold vinegar.

I wasn't invincible.

There was no warm red hum in my chest telling me that weapons don't feel bad. I felt horrible. I felt terrified, and freezing cold, and small, and sick to my stomach from swallowing two mouthfuls of dead-dog seawater.

And I was still there.

I hadn't blacked out. I hadn't lost fourteen hours. I was sitting right behind my left eye, and Harumi was sitting right behind my right eye, and Kio was leaning against the cellar door behind our spine letting his breath out in slow twelve-counts, and down at the Formica table Sunny had taken her hands off her ears and was looking out through our pupils at the whale-oil lantern on the crate.

All four of us, in the same wet skin, hurting like hell and completely awake.

Slowly, our right hand came across our chest to the torn canvas sleeve over our left bicep.

The hound's teeth had crushed the blue button tin out of shape. The rusted lid was bent double, cutting into our skin, stained dark with our own blood. Wedged in the wet grey flannel inside the crumpled tin was the last fingernail-sized shard of black glass.

Harumi didn't ask me if I was sure. She didn't have to; the reading-room door was wide open between us, and we were holding the same breath.

Our raw-knuckled right fingers pinched the greasy leather cord where the hound's tooth had frayed it, snapped the twine with one sharp tug, and pulled the crushed blue tin, the blood-soaked flannel, and the last black shard off our arm in one piece.

We didn't look at it. You don't look at the glass when your shoulder is bleeding and you're shaking from the cold; if you look at it, you start doing the math again.

We leaned forward over the seaward edge of the highway slab, opened our fingers, and dropped the tin straight down into the black boil over the forty-foot sulfur trench.

It didn't hiss. It didn't flare red. A grey wave folded over it in the dark, and the sea swallowed it down into the mud with the rest of the dead world.

Beside us on the stone, Pia let out a long, whistling breath through her scarred throat and lowered the point of her iron cleaver to her boot toe.

Wren picked up the shuttered whale-oil lantern from the crate, shone the narrow yellow slit of light across our torn left shoulder and the crushed bite marks on our forearm, and let out a low grunt through her scarred upper lip.

"You're going to need seven stitches in that shoulder before the salt sets in," Wren said, turning back toward the rolling cedar catwalk that led to the net-loft. "And I'm out of willow spirits because I poured the last of them down your demon's throat at three. You're going to feel every needle, silver-hair."

Our mouth pulled up on the right side in Harumi's crooked, exhausted grin, while my left hand pressed a scrap of clean shirt tail against our bleeding collarbone to slow the drip.

"Good," we said, in one voice, and stood up on the wet stone to follow the light back across the water.



Chapter Seven: The Bag of Oranges

The boat smelled like dead kelp, wet wool, and the linseed oil Eri rubbed into her ironwood crutch every morning when the salt damp got inside the grain.

We had been three days coming south down Pelagia's jagged basalt chain, hugging the leeward shelf where the grey swells broke into long bands of green foam before they reached the black rock. The sky did not have clouds so much as it had a low, flat floor of fog that sat twenty feet above the mast and dripped cold brine down the back of my oilskin coat. Every time the hull lifted on a cross-swell, the hemp ledger inside my inner pocket shifted against my ribs, dry and stiff and heavy as a shingle of slate.

Eri sat in the bow with her bad legs stretched out across the coiled anchor line, her chin tucked into a raveling mutton-wool scarf, her one yellow-brown eye fixed on the mist ahead. The scarred hollow of her left socket, where the black linen patch was tied back tight over her brow, shone dull pink around the edges from the spray.

"Smells like sulfur," she said without turning around. "And dirt. Real dirt. Somebody hauled soil up these rocks on their back."

"Volcanic vents," I said, leaning my good right shoulder against the tiller to keep our nose off the submerged teeth of the reef. My left arm was wrapped in three layers of tarred linen from the wrist to the crook of the elbow, bound tight with leather twine so I would not have to look at the matte black skin underneath or feel the cold air catch on the cracked ridges where the thirteen shards and the blue button tin had cooked the meat down to charcoal. Even through three layers of tarred cloth, it had a smell when it got wet. Burnt hair and old copper pennies. "The water boils under the shelf. Keeps the frost off the terraces."

"Yeah, well, it smells like a wet chimney in hell," Eri muttered. She shifted her weight, wincing as her right shin knocked against the cedar rib of the skiff. Nobody can fix a shin once the Nursery babies have crushed the bone into gravel in their fat gray fists, and three years of salt air had turned both her legs into barometers that ached six hours before a squall hit. She tapped the brass ferrule of her crutch against the gunwale. "There. Look up through the break."

The fog parted for ten seconds where a hot draft came off the cliff face.

The basalt rose three hundred feet straight out of the surf, black and wet and ribbed like the flank of a butchered whale. Cut into the lower sixty feet of the stone were five narrow terraces walled with dry-laid lava rock, each shelf no wider than an alley, stepped back against the seam of a steaming fumarole. Stunted, crooked trees grew along the ledges, their trunks twisted like arthritic fingers by the gale, their waxed dark leaves shining with condensed sulfur steam. Hung among the branches, bright and violent against all that black stone and grey water, were hundreds of small, thick-skinned oranges the size of a child's fist.

And high above them, at the very lip of the three-hundred-foot precipice where the steam died out and the cold sea-wind took over, stood a small stone cottage whitewashed with burned lime. It sat so close to the edge that its seaward gable looked as if it were leaning out to spit into the surf.

"She built the orchard at the bottom," Eri said softly, her one eye narrowing as she tracked the steep, switchbacked mule path that zigzagged up the sheer wet rock between the steam vents and the white house. "And her sister lives at the top."

"Tsui likes the wind," I said. "And the view of the water."

"And Rui likes carrying forty pounds of fruit up three hundred feet of slick basalt in the rain," Eri said. "Gods and anti-gods. Every single one of you needs a keeper."

We brought the skiff into a narrow shingle cove at the foot of the lowest terrace, where an iron ring had been driven into a split boulder and wedged with lead. I dropped the lugsail, jumped over the side into thigh-deep surf that bit through my boots like broken glass, and hauled the bow up onto the black pebbles until the keel ground steady. My left arm throbbed in time with my pulse, a thick, greasy ache behind the bandages, and in the back of my skull Harumi gave a dry little cough, like somebody striking a match in a damp cellar.

Oh, look, Harumi said inside my head, her voice lazy and low as a grease fire. The other girls who helped save the world and got jack shit for it. Are we starting a club? Because I want a jacket.

I did not answer her. If you answer Harumi out loud when Eri is within ten feet of you, Eri gets that quiet, pinched look around her mouth where she is trying very hard to keep her mind inside her own skull and out of yours. I reached back over the gunwale, got my good arm under Eri's ribs, and lifted her clear of the wash onto dry shingle while she planted her crutch in the gravel and cursed my mother, my brother, and the rudder.

"Put me down, you big blonde ox, I've got the stick," she hissed, though she held on to the collar of my oilskin for three seconds longer than she needed to until her heels found flat stone. She looked up at the lowest terrace wall. "Hey! Fire girl! We brought salt pork and two sacks of barley meal from the north jetty!"

A head rose above the dry-laid lava stones on the second shelf.

Rui wore a sleeveless leather jerkin stained white at the armpits with sweat and salt, and coarse canvas trousers rolled to the knee. Her hair was hacked off above the collar, uneven and stiff with brine, the way she had kept it ever since she was eleven years old and chopped off her braid with kitchen scissors to match her sister's. Across the left side of her face, running from the hollow of her temple down the turn of her jaw and disappearing beneath the leather collar across her collarbone, was the scar. It was a raised, slick, shining print of melted flesh in the exact five-fingered shape of Galactix's hand. Where the master's palm had caught her during the nine minutes on the western edge before the collapse, the skin had no pores and no color at all; it caught the grey fog-light like wet tallow.

She was holding a pair of iron pruning shears in one hand and a woven hemp net bag half-full of small, scarred salt-oranges in the other.

She looked down at us for a long breath. Her eyes were the color of banked wood-ash, dull brown with a thin ring of dark amber around the pupil. When she looked at my silver-blonde hair, and the black tarred bandages on my left arm, her jaw tightened under the shining seam of the burn.

"Ena," Rui said. Her voice was hoarse, scraped raw by sulfur steam and sea air. "And Nine."

"It's Eri now, sweetheart," Eri called back, leaning both hands on the cross-piece of her crutch. "Nobody uses the numbers anymore. The accountant died on an old highway."

Rui climbed down the narrow stone steps built into the terrace wall, her bare feet calloused gray-black against the sharp scoria. She did not put down the net bag of oranges. She stopped four paces from us on the wet shingle, smelling of woodsmoke, crushed citrus peel, and the sour iron stink of the fumaroles.

"We heard the old world came down," Rui said, looking straight at me. "Three months ago. Before the sky went grey and the sea rose up around the rock. The nexus snapped in the red canyon. The white tower fell into the river."

My stomach contracted until my ribs hurt. "Yeah," I said. "It fell."

"Good," Rui said.

She said it flatly, with no heat in her mouth at all, the way a stone drops into a well.

"Four hundred feet of white marble," Rui said, shifting the heavy net bag to her left hip so she could wipe the rain off her scarred cheek with the back of her wrist. "And one fucking word at the bottom of it in letters six feet high. MODIFY. We went back there, you know. Two years after the canyon thawed. Somebody chiseled a second line underneath it in the dark. AND EVERYONE WHO STOOD BESIDE HIM. Small little crooked letters, three inches tall, right down in the dirt where the dogs pissed on the plinth. The city council didn't even clean the moss out of them. When the marigolds rotted after the festival, they shovelled the mulch right over the second line so you couldn't read it from the street."

She looked at my face, searching for the flinch. I did not give her one. I just stood there in my wet boots with the water draining down my shins, feeling the phantom weight of a piece of rebar in my fist and the leaning letters I had carved into a smaller stone on a hill above a dead town.

"He didn't build the tower, Rui," I said quietly. "He was dead before they laid the first block. Eri cut him in half with a portal in hell, and the virus finished what was left of him after the peace."

Eri did not look at me when I said it. Her thumb rubbed a slow circle over the brass screw at the top of her crutch.

"I know he didn't build it," Rui said. "That didn't make the letters any smaller when you walked past it on your way to buy bread." She looked down at the two canvas sacks of barley meal we had set on a dry boulder above the tide line. "You brought grain?"

"Barley and a side of smoked pork," I said. "The fisherman at the north jetty said the supply boat missed your cove last month because of the reefs."

"They didn't miss it," Rui said. "They don't like coming close to the shore when Tsui is having a bad day. When she forgets where she is, the water under the cliff pulls sideways. Wrecked a dory in the spring." She bent, hooked the lanyard of the salt-pork sack over her shoulder with her free hand, and jerked her chin toward the steep switchback path cut into the basalt cliff. "Leave the barley under the oilcloth by the lower vent. Come up if you want tea. Don't stay past dark if you can't handle the stairs. When the tide comes in, the bottom twenty feet go under."

"My favorite kind of staircase," Eri muttered. "Three hundred feet of wet soap."

We climbed slowly. Rui went ahead of us with the pork sack across her shoulder and the net bag of salt-oranges swinging against her thigh, moving up the notched stone steps with the mechanical, unthinking rhythm of an animal that has walked the same track ten thousand times in the dark. I walked two steps behind Eri, keeping my right hand an inch behind the small of her back without touching her, because if you grab Eri before she falls she will bite your thumb to the bone, and if you let her fall down three hundred feet of volcanic rock she will haunt you for the rest of your natural life.

Halfway up, the warm sulfur steam from the orchard vents dropped away below us, and the cold sea-rain hit our faces full on. Looking down, I could see the dwarf orange trees huddled in their stone troughs like green coals in a grate, and below them the black tidal shelf stretching out into the grey chop. The tide was going out, uncovering long, flat tables of basalt slicked with bladderwrack and barnacles.

At the top of the cliff, the wind came off the open ocean with nothing between it and the horizon to slow it down. The cottage was built of thick basalt blocks mortared with lime and horsehair, roofed with heavy grey flagstones weighted down by ropes and boulders so the gales could not peel it open. A tiny garden of sea-kale and salt-burned marigolds struggled inside a low wall by the door.

Rui stopped on the flagstone step.

Before she lifted the iron latch, she did something that made the hair on my arms stand up under my oilskin. She set the sack of salt pork down by the boot-scraper, took a deep breath through her nose, and smoothed her wet hair down with her palm. Then she pulled the collar of her leather jerkin up an inch on the left side, trying to cover the lower tail of Galactix's handprint across her collarbone, and fixed her mouth into a calm, patient, neighborly curve.

She knocked twice on her own sister's door before she pushed it open.

"Tsui?" Rui called out, her voice pitched softer, rounder, stripped of all the gravel she had used down on the beach. "I'm coming in. The step's wet."

The room inside was a single whitewashed chamber with an iron peat-stove in the corner, a scrubbed pine table, two Windsor chairs with cushions faded to the color of weak tea, and a wide casement window looking straight out over the grey void of the sea. The walls were bare except for three dried starfish nailed above the mantel and a blue wool shawl hanging from a wooden peg.

A woman sat in the chair by the window with her hands folded in her lap.

Tsui looked younger than Rui now, though she was three years older. That was what the emptiness did to a face; it smoothed out all the creases that worrying and remembering leave behind around the mouth and between the eyebrows. Her dark hair was braided neatly over one shoulder, tied at the end with a bit of frayed blue twine. Her skin had the cool, translucent pallor of cellar milk, and her eyes were a clear, pale, washed grey, staring out through the salt-streaked glass at the waves rolling in toward the cliff.

When the door opened, Tsui turned her head smoothly. She looked at Rui, then at Eri leaning on her crutch in the doorway, then at me standing behind them with rain dripping off my silver hair onto the braided rag rug.

A pleasant, polite, completely blank smile came onto Tsui's mouth.

"Hello," Tsui said. Her voice was clear and light, like water pouring out of a glass pitcher. "Can I help you? Did the boat come in from the north?"

Rui's shoulders did not drop. She had already braced them before she touched the latch.

"It's me," Rui said gently, stepping onto the rag rug and holding up the woven hemp net. "Rui. Your sister. And this is Ena, and Eri. They brought some barley down from the jetty. And I brought oranges."

For half a second, Tsui's pale grey eyes changed. A ring of deep, electric ocean-blue flared around her pupils, bright as phosphor in a night wake, the exact blue they had burned during the war when she stood back-to-back with Rui in the storm and dragged three million tons of river water out of its bed to drown Galactix's vanguard.

Then the blue went out, as cleanly as a candle snuffed between two wet fingers.

"Of course," Tsui said, standing up and smoothing the front of her grey wool skirt with both hands. "Of course. Come in out of the damp. I'm so sorry, I'm terrible with faces lately. The wind up here makes my head rattle. Oh, how kind of you. I love oranges. How did you know?"

"Lucky guess," Rui said.

She walked over to the pine table, dumped the oranges into a chipped earthenware bowl that already held three shriveled ones from the week before, and took an iron kettle off the cold side of the stove to fill it from the wooden water-butt by the wall. Every movement she made was practiced, quiet, and small, designed not to startle a woman who did not know who was standing in her kitchen.

Eri stumped over to the bench by the door and lowered herself onto it with a long, whistling breath through her teeth, propping her crutch against the whitewashed stone. I stayed standing by the threshold, my hands shoved deep into my oilskin pockets so Tsui would not look at the black bandages on my left forearm.

Tsui looked at me anyway. She tilted her head, a small, puzzled crease appearing between her smooth eyebrows.

"I know you," Tsui said politely. "Don't I? From the war. You had wings made of fire. Red fire. We fought the big shining thing together, near the river, right before the boy in the silver armor mended the sky."

My throat closed up so tight I tasted copper at the back of my tongue. "Yeah," I said. "That was me. Ena."

"Ena," Tsui repeated, smiling warmly. "How lovely to see you well. It was a terrible storm, wasn't it? All that water and all that fire together. I remember calling the water up until my arms went numb, and there was someone right behind my left shoulder pouring out such tremendous heat that the steam blinded the master's eyes for nine whole minutes. I never did see who was making that fire. I always thought it must have been one of the tall guards from the eastern citadel, or perhaps the wind caught a coal-barge."

By the stove, the iron lid of the kettle clicked once against the rim.

Rui did not turn around. Her bare heels stayed planted together on the stone hearth. Her back under the sweat-stained leather jerkin was rigid as an iron poker.

"You told me you remembered," I said quietly, before I could stop myself. I was looking at Rui's back, thinking of that day three years ago on the rim of the red canyon when Tsui had wept and caught Rui's wrist and talked about the kitchen scissors and the burned braid. "At the canyon. When Famine froze. You remembered the house with the red door."

Rui set the kettle over the grate, knelt, and shoved two blocks of dry black peat into the firebox. She did not use a tinderbox. She touched her right thumb and forefinger to the corner of the peat, and a small, tired orange flame leaked out from under her fingernail, caught on the dried moss, and went out.

"It doesn't stay," Rui said to the stove, her voice low enough that Tsui, who was already turning back toward the casement window to watch a gull ride the updraft, paid no attention. "The healers told me after the canyon. When Galactix chained her up as a vessel and ran all that master-power through her head, it burned the tracks out. Like a wagon wheel rutted too deep in mud. You can pull the wheel out into the grass for an afternoon if you push hard enough. If you tell the right story. If somebody's dying and the whole sky is cracking open. She'll remember the braid. She'll cry. She'll hold your hand all the way home."

Rui stood up, wiping the black peat dust from her knees onto her trousers.

"And then she goes to sleep," Rui said. "And in the morning the wheel drops right back into the rut. Every day. Or every ten minutes, when the weather's thick."

We sat at the scrubbed pine table while the peat caught and the kettle began to hum. Rui took a small brass knife from the drawer and peeled two of the dwarf oranges, her blunt, burned thumbs working under the bitter green-gold rind in one continuous spiral before she separated the pale segments and laid them on a saucer in front of her sister.

Tsui ate one segment slowly, watching the rain run down the outside of the glass. "There was a seal on the black rocks this morning," she said pleasantly. "A grey one, with a white scar across its nose. It stayed until the tide turned. I tried to call the water up to give it a deeper pool to fish in, the way I used to, you know, just a little lift of the swell, and my fingers went cold and nothing happened. I suppose the sea has forgotten my voice, now that the world is smaller."

She paused, holding the second piece of orange halfway to her mouth, and looked across the pine boards at Rui.

Her face was kind, open, and completely empty.

"I'm so sorry," Tsui said softly. "What did you say your name was?"

"Rui," said Rui.

"Rui," Tsui said, tasting the syllable as if she had never heard it in her life. "That's pretty. Like fire."

"Yeah," Rui said. Her right thumbnail pressed straight through the discarded rind on the table, splitting the bitter oil out onto the wood. "Like fire."

"Do you live near the jetty, Rui?"

"I live down the path," Rui said. "By the vents."

"Oh, the little stone hut by the orange trees," Tsui said brightly. "I always see the smoke from your chimney when I look over the wall. How nice to finally meet the neighbor who keeps the orchard. You must work very hard to keep them bearing in this salt."

Inside my head, the silence went very wide and very cold.

I looked down at my own boots on the rag rug. I looked at the tarred bandages on my left arm, and I felt the hemp ledger pressing against my breastbone, full of pages written in four different handwritings. Ena's slanting, cramped script. Harumi's big, heavy capitals that dug holes through the paper. Kio's tiny, measured columns of supply numbers and tide tables in the upper corners. Sunny's round, crooked drawings of cats and juice boxes and fat black markers at the bottoms of the margins.

I knew what it felt like to wake up on a mattress under a leaky tarp and find three days missing out of the middle of the week. I knew what it felt like to look down at your own hands and find fresh black glass dust under your fingernails, or blood on your knuckles, or a dead boy's name in a jar at a funeral while your own mouth stood at the front of the room and told everyone how brave he was. I knew what it was to have a room inside your own skull with the door locked from the other side.

Tsui was locked out from the other direction. She sat in the whitewashed room with the sun gone and all the furniture taken away, and every ten minutes the person who loved her most in the universe had to walk back through the door and introduce herself to a stranger wearing her sister's face.

Ten minutes later, when the tea had steeped bitter and brown in the earthenware pot, Rui stood up and said we should go down before the dusk squall blew the fog across the steps.

Tsui walked us to the door. On the wet flagstone step, with the salt wind blowing wisps of silver and dark hair across our faces, Tsui paused. She frowned slightly, looking at the left side of Rui's face, and reached out to lay one cool, milk-pale hand against the shining five-fingered seam of melted flesh on Rui's jaw.

"That looks like it hurt," Tsui said softly.

"It did," Rui said.

"How did you get it?"

Rui stood very still under her sister's cold palm. Her ash-brown eyes did not flicker.

"Standing next to you," Rui said.

Tsui's eyes flared blue. Deep, terrifying ocean-blue, like a trench opening under the hull of a boat. Her fingers twitched once against Rui's scarred cheekbone, and her breath hitched in her throat as if a fish had broken the surface under six inches of black ice.

Then the ice closed over it. The grey came back.

"Well," Tsui said kindly, dropping her hand to her skirt. "I hope it heals. Thank you for the oranges, neighbor. Mind the wet stones on your way down."

The wooden door clicked shut. The iron latch fell into its slot.

Nobody spoke on the way down the cliff.

Eri went first this time, sitting down on the steepest wet steps and hitching herself from ledge to ledge on her leather-gloved palms and her one good hip, her crutch clamped under her elbow, her jaw set so hard the muscle stood out like a walnut under her cheek. I walked behind her, carrying the empty barley sacks, and Rui came last, her bare feet slapping quietly against the streaming basalt, the empty hemp orange-net wadded into a ball so tight in her right fist that the cord cut white trenches across her knuckles.

When we reached the bottom terrace, the dusk had come down like wet wool over the sea. The tide was all the way out. Beyond the shingle cove, the long black basalt tables lay exposed for a hundred yards into the bay, pocked with shallow tidal pools and furred with dark, dripping kelp.

Rui did not stop at her stone hut by the fumarole.

She walked straight past the steaming orange trees, down the shingle bank, and out onto the farthest black rock shelf at the edge of the breaking surf, where the grey waves rolled in out of the fog and smashed themselves into spit against the stone.

"Leave her alone," Eri said from the door of the hut, where a dry overhang kept the rain off a bench of split driftwood. Eri's face was grey with pain from the three-hundred-foot descent; she was already reaching under her wool skirt to rub the knotted, crooked ridge of her left shinbone through her stocking. "Go inside and light the oil lamp, Ena. She wants to be by herself."

I stood in the rain on the gravel path. I looked at Eri, and then I looked out across the black tidal shelf, where Rui had sat down on the wet rock with her knees drawn up and her boots off and the empty net bag crushed under her heel.

Two dull orange lights had just bloomed in the grey fog at the water's edge.

She was not lighting a campfire. There was no driftwood on the outer table.

Rui had set both of her own hands on fire, all the way up to the wrist-bones.

I felt the click behind my eyes before I could brace for it. It was not the bad, violent slam of an ambush or a nightmare, where the floor drops out and I lose four hours in a blink. It was a sideways slide, like two people changing seats on a narrow bench in the dark because the one in front is too tired to drive anymore.

Let me talk to her, Harumi said.

No, I thought back, my fingers digging into the wet canvas of the empty grain sacks. You'll be mean to her.

I'm never mean to people who aren't full of shit, baby, Harumi said. Sit in the back and wrap your blanket around your shoulders. You're shaking anyway.

My chin came up an inch. My shoulders dropped out of Ena's perpetually hunched, apologetic stoop and squared off against the cold rain. The dull ache in my wrapped left arm sharpened into something hot and familiar that made the back of my teeth itch, and when I let go of the empty barley sacks, I just dropped them in the mud by the door instead of folding them neatly on the bench.

Eri looked up from rubbing her shin. Her one yellow-brown eye took in the angle of my jaw and the way my weight sat back on my heels.

"Oh, great," Eri said dryly. "Hello, Harumi."

"Hey, cyclops," I said, using Ena's mouth with the corners pulled flat and wide. "Go inside and put your ugly sticks by the vent before you whine all night. I'm taking a walk."

"Don't push her in the ocean," Eri said, reaching behind her to shove the hut door open with the butt of her crutch. "She's the only one down here who knows how to prune the fruit."

I walked down the shingle and out across the black tidal tables.

With the tide out, the stone was slick as greased iron under my boots, covered in popped bladderwrack and sharp little white barnacles that crunched like teeth when I stepped on them. The wind smelled of dead crabs and iodine and, as I got within twenty feet of the end of the shelf, the heavy, sweet-sour stench of scorching hair and blistered skin.

Rui sat on the very lip of the basalt ledge, her shins dangling over the surge channel where the incoming swells sucked and boomed six feet below. Both of her hands were held out palms-up on her knees.

The flames were not the big, roaring gold sheets she had thrown across the western front during the war. These were thick, slow, dark orange fire, clinging to her skin like burning pitch from the tips of her blunt fingers down to the blue veins on the insides of her wrists. In the bright glow, I could see the outer skin of her palms blackening, cracking along the lifelines, and peeling back in thin gray curls of ash while new, raw, weeping red meat tried to heal underneath and got burned all over again.

She did not make a sound. Her jaw was locked so tight the hand-shaped scar across her cheek looked like a piece of stretched tallow about to split.

I walked up behind her, kicked a wet clump of kelp off the rock, and sat down two feet to her left with my boots hanging over the same surge channel.

Rui did not turn her head. She stared down into her burning palms, the orange fire reflected in her ash-brown eyes.

"Go away, Ena," Rui said through her teeth.

"Ena's in the back room having a little cry about her dead brother," I said, leaning back on my good right elbow on the wet basalt and letting the rain hit my face. "It's Harumi. Keep burning yourself, though. Don't stop on my account. Smells like a barbecue at a leper colony."

Rui's head snapped toward me. The flames on her wrists flared three inches higher, hot enough to dry the rain on my left cheek before it hit my skin.

"I said get the fuck off my rock," Rui snarled.

"Make me," I said pleasantly. "Burn my other arm off. Then we'll match. You've got the five-finger discount on your face, I've got the charcoal briquette under the tar-rag, we can go up to the white house together and ask your sister for directions to the post office."

For a second I thought she was going to swing one of those burning fists straight into my teeth. The muscles in her bare shoulder bunched hard enough to pop. Then her breath broke in her chest, a ragged, wet hitch, and she looked back down at her blistered hands.

"Why do you let her hide behind you?" Rui asked, her voice shaking with the pain she was forcing down into her belly. "Every time something hurts too much, Ena ducks behind the curtain and sends you out to say the ugly part."

"Because that's what I was built for," I said, looking out at the grey rollers marching in out of the fog. "You think I rolled out of nowhere when the red stuff hit her at fifteen? Please. I was seven years old, sitting behind the drywall in our bedroom while the women in the grocery store stared at her hair like she was a rattlesnake in the produce aisle. Somebody had to hate them back. Ena couldn't do it. Ena wanted to be the sweet little sister who ate her tuna casserole and laughed at Timmy's marker drawings on her locker. So she shoved all the teeth and all the spite and all the memory of how good it felt to watch the kitchen window break into my side of the cupboard, and she shut the door."

I reached across with my right hand and tapped the tarred linen wrapping my left forearm.

"That's the joke, fire-girl," I said. "You sit down here every week and set your hands on fire because your sister looks right through you and remembers the war without you in it. You think you're the only one who got cut out of the story? Ena does that to me twelve times a day. I fought the six hounds in the laundromat. I was the one who took the thirteen shards out of the red-and-white mint tin so she wouldn't get her throat torn out by Tombstone's dogs behind the dryers. And I was the one who turned Dov into black sugar on the linoleum so he wouldn't go tattling to Eri and ruin her pathetic little forty-one-day sobriety badge."

Rui stared at me, the fire on her wrists guttering down to a low, pulsing red ring around her raw knuckles. "Who's Dov?"

"A nineteen-year-old kid with a patchy beard who thought Ena was a hero," I said, and laughed, though it scraped the inside of my throat like swallowed glass. "We buried him in a pickle jar mixed up with dog ashes. And Ena stood up in front of the chairs and cried real wet tears and let Marguerite hug her, and then ten minutes later she locked her own brain up so tight she couldn't even remember whose hand moved until Eri ripped her skull open on the Nursery floor. Everybody wants the monster to come out when the dogs break the window, Rui. Nobody wants to know the monster's name when they're carving the fucking statue."

The rain came down harder, hissing as it struck the hot skin of Rui's palms, sending up little grey threads of steam that smelled of salt and cooked meat.

"It isn't the statue," Rui whispered.

"Bullshit it isn't."

"It isn't," Rui said, and her voice cracked wide open, raw and small and fifteen years old again. "I told myself it was the white tower for two years. I walked around it three times looking for my name. And then Ena told me in the healers' tent that a name on a wall is nothing, and I went down into the Gray Abyss and found a statue of myself holding the western edge against Galactix, and I brought my own fire back out in my hand, and for one day at the red canyon Tsui remembered my braid. And I thought it was fixed. I thought: that's enough. One house on a cliff where my sister remembers me, and one Horseman in a canyon who knows what I tastes like, and I don't need the marble."

She held her raw, smoking hands up in front of her face. The skin on her palms was cracked open in deep red fissures from wrist to knuckle, blood and clear yellow serum oozing out to mix with the black char before the heat sealed it again.

"And then the world kept going," Rui said, a tear cutting a clean track down through the grey peat-ash on her right cheek while the left side, where Galactix had melted the ducts shut, stayed dry and shiny as grease. "That's the part none of the stories tell you. You have the big moment at the rim of the canyon. Your sister cries and says your name three times and remembers the kitchen scissors. And then Tuesday comes. And Wednesday. And next month. And the damage in her head didn't go away just because we loved each other hard enough for five minutes while the world was ending. The rot is still there. Every week I carry the bag up the hill, and every week she asks me if I'm the new neighbor who keeps the trees, and she touches this fucking handprint on my face and says she hopes it heals."

Rui dropped her hands onto her thighs with a wet slap.

"Do you know why I burn them?" she asked the surf. "Because the healer's fire inside me tries to fix my hands while I'm doing it. And as long as they're burning, I can feel them right now. Not three years ago on the western edge. Not when I was ten in the backyard. Right now. Before the tide comes in and puts them out and I have to go back up that path next Thursday and be nobody again."

I sat there in the rain with my boots swinging over the surge channel, listening to the black water suck at the underside of the basalt shelf.

Inside the quiet space behind my eyes, Ena was awake. She wasn't trying to take the mouth back. She was just standing there in the dark behind my shoulder, looking out through our eyes at Rui's blistered, bleeding palms, feeling every word Rui said land right on the soft, bruised place in our chest where we kept the ledger and the four handwritings and the morning confusion when the blanket was on the wrong side of the cot.

"Put it out," I said, softer than Harumi usually talks.

"The tide'll do it," Rui said, staring down as a white tongue of foam licked up within six inches of our hanging heels.

"Don't make the ocean do your chores," I said.

I leaned sideways, reached out with my unbandaged right hand, and took Rui by her burning left wrist.

The flame bit straight into my palm. It was real anti-god fire, hot enough to char dry oak in a second, and I smelled my own calluses singe yellow and crisp against her wet skin. In the back of our head Ena flinched hard, a hot needle of panic shooting up our arm, and for half a breath the red hum behind my collarbone woke up and hissed, wanting to eat the pain and turn it into that big, clean, empty certainty where nothing hurts because you are the thing that hurts everybody else.

I clamped my teeth down on my tongue until I tasted salt and blood, holding the red hum down in the cellar with both boots. I did not use a drop of residual corruption. I just held Rui's burning wrist with a plain human hand, skin charring against skin, and lowered her arm down between our knees into the cold black rise of the next wave.

The sea took her hands up to the elbows.

The water boiled white around our wrists with a sharp, violent hiss like a hot horseshoe plunged into a smithy barrel. Steam blew up into both our faces, thick with brine and the smell of our burned skin. Rui gasped, a ragged, shuddering sob tearing out of her chest as the freezing salt water bit into the open fissures of her palms, and she slumped sideways until her wet, scarred temple rested against my oilskin shoulder.

I did not let go of her wrist under the water. I held it there in the cold surge while three more waves washed over our knees, soaking my trousers to the hip, washing the grey ash off her fingers and the yellow blister-skin off my right palm.

"Hurts like a bitch, doesn't it?" I said into the top of her wet, chopped-off hair.

"Yeah," Rui whispered against my coat, her whole body trembling with the drop in heat. "Yeah. It does."

"Good," I said, and inside my chest Ena and I were holding the exact same breath at the exact same time, neither of us hiding behind the other on the narrow stone bench. "That means we're still here. Come on, fire-girl. Get up before the water goes over the boots. If Eri has to come out on this rock with her stick to fish us out, we'll never hear the end of it."


Chapter Eight: Midnight Hunger

I woke up at one in the morning with a mouthful of sour bile and my brother's headless silver breastplate resting across my windpipe.

It took me four seconds of clawing at the coarse wool blanket in the dark of Rui's orchard hut before my fingers found rough basalt mortar instead of the red dirt of hell, and another three seconds before my lungs remembered that Tim's body had not been lying in the pile by the eastern portal for three years.

The hut was hot and close, heated by an iron pipe that channeled steam from the lower fumarole under the flagstone floor. On the pallet across the room, Eri was asleep on her back with her mouth slightly open, one scarred shin propped on a folded flour sack and her bare right hand resting on the grip of her ironwood crutch even in her dreams. Two feet away, near the peat bucket, Rui lay curled on her side with both wrists wrapped in greased plantain leaves and clean linen strips I had tied for her before midnight.

My chest would not stop heaving. Every time I closed my eyes, I saw the bodies stacked twelve deep along the rim of the broken dimension, half-black and half-silver, their armor still twitching with residual red static while the sky fell in above them. And right at the bottom of the heap, where the portal had snapped shut like a pair of iron shears, the top half of a silver helmet with a dented H scratched into the cheek-guard, and nothing inside the collar but wet red meat and grey ash.

I rolled off the straw tick onto my knees, clamped my good right hand over my mouth so I wouldn't wake Eri, and crawled out the low wooden door into the rain.

I made it as far as the third dwarf orange tree on the lowest terrace before my stomach turned inside out. I dropped onto all fours in the warm, sulfur-smelling black mud under the leaves and vomited up bitter tea and yellow stomach acid until my ribs knotted and black spots swam across the dark. My left arm, inside its tarred bandages, was humming.

It always hummed after a night terror. When my heart hammered past a hundred and forty beats a minute and the sweat ran cold down my spine, the black charcoal flesh from my wrist to my elbow woke up like an old dog hearing a whistle. It wanted a shard. It knew there were no red-and-white mint tins or blue button tins hidden in the bricks of Pelagia, so it pulled at the residual marrow in my own bones instead, whispering in that low, soothing drain-voice that if I just let it open the door an inch, if I just let the red light come up behind my eyes for five minutes, the shaking would stop and I wouldn't see the silver helmet in the dirt anymore.

"Shut up," I whispered into the wet soil, pressing my blistered right palm flat against a sharp lava cobble until the stone bit deep enough to anchor me. "Shut up. It's day seventy-four. We're not doing it."

"You are very loud after midnight," a voice said out of the cliff face behind the trees.

I jerked my head up, wiping my chin on the sleeve of my wool shirt.

Ten yards past the end of the lowest orchard wall, where the volcanic vent steamed out of a split in the sheer basalt, the rock did not close. There was a narrow, triangular fissure thirty feet high, half-screened by hanging mats of salt-ivy and dead bracken, opening into a deep sea-cave carved out by the old tides before the shelf lifted.

A faint, smoky orange light flickered against the wet stone twenty paces inside the mouth.

Sitting on an upturned cedar driftwood log just inside the drip-line of the cave was a very small girl in a plain dark wool coat that came down to her ankles. She looked about twelve years old, or twelve thousand. Her face was pale and still, with calm eyes the color of deep winter water, and held open above her head was an old-fashioned black umbrella with a curved wooden handle. Three of its wire ribs were bent sideways from the ocean gales, and the black silk was patched with tarred canvas, yet from the iron tips of its spokes clear, dark drops fell at a slow, unhurried rhythm onto the basalt floor, ticking like a grandfather clock in an empty hallway.

"Death," I croaked, pushing myself up onto my boots with my back against the terrace wall.

"Hello, Ena," Death said mildly. She tilted the broken black umbrella back an inch so the brazier light from deeper in the cave caught her small chin. "Your stomach is making a great deal of noise. Go on inside by the fire. My sister has been awake since twelve-oh-one listening to you dream from through the rock."

I stepped over a trickle of hot, sulfur-yellow water that drained out of the cave mouth and walked inside.

The cavern smelled of burning pine-pitch, wet iron, and ancient salt. Thirty feet back from the entrance, where the basalt roof arched high and dry above the high-water mark, two iron braziers hammered out of old ship-buckets stood on three-legged stools, packed with resinous driftwood knots that burned with a dense, spitting golden flame.

Between the two braziers, sitting cross-legged on a pile of dry kelp-sacks with a heavy canvas sailcloth wrapped three times around her shoulders, was Famine.

Three years in the black glass hollow at the bottom of the red canyon had left marks on her that Rui's fire and Drake's forgiveness had never taken out of her hide. Her skin was still the pale, smoky blue of skimmed milk under ice, and across her bare cheekbones, the bridge of her nose, and the backs of her long, skeletal hands ran a web of fine white frost-cracks, like the crazing on an old porcelain cup that has been left out in a hard freeze and then filled with boiling water. She was thinner than any human body could be and stay upright; her collarbones stood out above the sailcloth like polished pewter handles, and her hair hung in stiff, frost-white ropes down to her waist.

Her eyes were open, black all the way from lid to lid, without white or iris, reflecting the two small squares of fire from the driftwood braziers.

"Corrupt," Famine said. Her voice sounded like dry corn husks rubbing together in an empty bin.

"It's Ena," I said, stopping six feet from her brazier, my knees still trembling from throwing up in the mud. "Nobody calls me that anymore."

"The dark calls you that," Famine said, looking at the tarred bandages on my left arm with her endless black eyes. "After midnight, everything goes back to its real name. Sit down. You are leaking cold all over my floor."

I lowered myself onto an overturned herring barrel beside the left-hand brazier and held my blistered right hand and my wrapped left arm out toward the driftwood coals. Even two feet from the iron bucket, the air around Famine had a peculiar, thin draw to it, like the draft in front of an open chimney flue that is always trying to pull the heat out of your shirt.

"I thought after the Gray Abyss," I said, staring into the spitting pine knots, "after we held the nexus for you and you went down and saw Drake, you didn't get the midnight thing anymore."

Famine looked down at her own long, blue-white fingers, cracked like old glaze across every knuckle.

"Drake forgave me," Famine said simply. "He held my hand in the grey dust, and he told me to eat, and Death took him under her umbrella to the river where your brother throws rocks. For four months after that, when midnight came, the hollow was quiet. And then the old world broke anyway. The nexus cracked under my feet, and the canyon fell in, and the ocean came up over the plains, and Rui brought me here in an open boat because I didn't have a world to hang from my shoulders anymore."

She pulled the stiff canvas sailcloth tighter under her chin.

"Do you know what happens to a stomach after you feed it once, Ena?" Famine asked quietly. "It wakes up. For three thousand years I never knew what being full felt like, so being starving was just the weather. Then Rui and Tsui fed me their fire and their water, and Dolly gave me grey jam in a jar, and Drake gave me his forgiveness. And now I know what food is. And when one o'clock in the morning comes, and there is no nexus running six million people's heartbeats through my chest to keep my hands busy, my mouth remembers what it did in hell."

My throat went dry as chalk. "Drake."

"I was trying to clean him," Famine whispered to the brazier. "You had filled him so full of red static and black iron that he couldn't even close his own eyelids. He was screaming behind his face while his hands swung a sword at Tim. So I reached into his chest with my hunger and I bit down on the red part, and I pulled. And for five seconds, Ena, for the first five seconds when the corruption came out of his veins and went down my throat, it was the greatest relief I had ever felt in three thousand years. I was doing something good. I was saving Tim's friend. And I was eating at the same time."

Her frost-cracked blue hands closed into fists on the kelp-sacks.

"And then the five seconds were over," Famine said, her voice dropping until it barely stirred the ash on the coals. "And the red part was gone. And the boy was right there underneath it, warm and alive and full of seventeen years of throwing rocks at a river and laughing at dinner tables. And my jaws were already open, and three thousand years of empty belly had hold of the back of my neck, and I could not stop pulling. I felt his neck-bones give under my teeth like dry reeds. I felt his thoughts go out all at once while I swallowed them."

I looked down at my wrapped left arm. Through the three layers of tarred linen, the black char was pulsing, hot and thick and needy, smelling of pennies.

"Five seconds," I said hoarsely. "That's how long a shard gives you when you press it into your palm before the lie starts. When the hounds broke into the laundromat on Fifth and had Dov by the ankle behind the dryers, I ran seven blocks back to my roof and dumped thirteen shards out of a mint tin into my hand. And while they were melting through my skin, for five seconds on the stairs, I wasn't an addict relapsing. I was the girl saving the meeting. I was forty-one days clean and I was going to rescue the kid who looked up to me."

Famine's black eyes turned from the fire to my face. She did not blink. Horsemen never blink.

"And then the dogs were dead on the tile," I said, my voice shaking so hard my teeth clicked together at the ends of the words. "And Dov stood up with his torn ankle and looked at my hand smoking and said, *You're using*. And the hum didn't even pause for breath. It just reached out and ate him too, right down to black sugar on the floor, because it felt so good to be sure that nobody was going to tell Eri."

I pressed both palms against my eye sockets until red and yellow stars burst behind my lids.

"Tim forgave you for Drake," I whispered into my hands. "Before he died. You told me he forgave you."

"He did," Famine said.

"And Drake forgave you in the Abyss."

"He did."

"Then why does it still make you want to claw your own throat out at one in the morning?"

From the mouth of the cave, where the sea-rain hissed into the hot yellow runoff from the fumarole, the black umbrella gave three slow, distinct ticks against the basalt.

"Because forgiveness is for the one who leaves," Death said quietly from her driftwood log, without turning around from the rain. "Not for the one who stays in the cave with the teeth."

Famine nodded once, her stiff white ropes of hair sliding over the coarse canvas of her wrap. "My teeth are still in my head, Ena. Your left hand is still on the end of your arm. You can wrap it in tar and linen, and I can sit between two buckets of burning pine, and when the sun is up we can help Rui pick oranges and pretend we are retired. Then the tide goes out at midnight, and the quiet comes in, and the hunger is still sitting in the cupboard where it has sat since the beginning of the world, asking what we are going to feed it tonight."

A gust of cold wind came off the black water outside, driving a sheet of freezing spray twenty feet past Death's umbrella straight into the throat of the cave.

The left-hand brazier sputtered. Half the coals went black in an instant as a heavy drop of roof-water splashed into the ash, and the shadows along the jagged basalt wall leaped ten feet toward my barrel like six multi-jointed black dogs rising up out of a shower of broken window-glass.

My chest locked up.

The smell of the wet charcoal in the brazier hit the back of my nose at the exact same second as the dark: burnt hair, wet ash, old pennies, and a silver helmet rolling across a kitchen floor with peanut butter on the handle of the bread knife.

I tried to stand up off the herring barrel and knocked it over backward. My spine hit the cold, slimy basalt wall of the cave. My lungs stopped taking in air; they only made short, high, whistling squeaks in the back of my throat while my right hand clawed at the tarred bandages on my left forearm, trying to rip the cloth off to see if the red light was coming through the cracks, trying to get away from the dogs, trying to get out of the kitchen before the knife went in.

"Ena," Famine said, leaning forward on her kelp-sacks, though she was smart enough not to reach for me with those frost-crazed blue hands.

I couldn't see her. The cave was full of grey statues and broken glass and the low drain-voice humming *baby, everybody's gonna watch*, and my heels hammered against the rock floor as I shoved myself backward into a corner where the ceiling came down low and wet over a pile of empty fish-baskets.

Too loud, Kio's voice snapped inside the dark of the skull, brittle as a dry ruler breaking. Heart rate one-sixty-eight. Oxygen dropping. Harumi is burned out from the tide shelf. I can't hold the door against a full panic spike, the hinges are wet.

Then drop the latch, Harumi snarled from way back in the smoke, exhausted and hoarse. Put her down in the basement before she rips the bandages off and bleeds out on the stone.

The cave went black.

It did not fade out. It snapped shut like a heavy wooden trapdoor dropping over a cellar stair, cutting off the roaring in my ears and the copper taste in my mouth between one heartbeat and the next.

When the eyes opened again, two seconds later, the ceiling of the cave looked three times higher than it had before.

Sunny sat curled up between two wicker fish-baskets with her knees pulled tight under her chin and her big oilskin coat pooling around her boots like a tent. She blinked twice at the smoky orange light of the brazier, wiped her wet nose on the cuff of her sleeve, and looked down at her hands.

Her right hand had a big yellow blister across the palm that hurt when she wiggled her fingers, and her left arm was wrapped up in smelly black tape that she wasn't supposed to touch because that was the grown-up arm that got sick.

She tucked the wrapped left arm carefully inside the front of the oversized coat so it could take a nap, reached into the big right-hand pocket where she kept her things, and checked with her fingers to make sure the bruised salt-orange Rui had dropped by the water-butt and the dry heel of kelp-bread from supper were still there inside her handkerchief. They were. Also three smooth black pebbles from the beach and a stub of carpenter's chalk.

Sunny let out a long, shaky breath that ended in a hiccup, crawled out from behind the fish-baskets on her hands and knees, and looked across the fire at the blue lady wrapped in the boat-blanket.

Famine had frozen halfway off her pile of kelp-sacks, her skeletal blue fingers braced against the stone floor, her endless black eyes wide as saucers as she stared at the woman in the oversized oilskin coat who had suddenly stopped screaming and was now sitting cross-legged by the knocked-over barrel, sucking on the side of her blistered thumb.

By the mouth of the cave, Death turned all the way around on her driftwood log. She lowered the broken black umbrella two inches and tilted her small pale head.

"Hi," Sunny said in a very small, polite voice. Her throat felt scratchy like somebody had been yelling with it, so she swallowed twice to make it smoother. "Did I knock the bucket over? I'm sorry. Sometimes when the dark gets noisy I fall down."

Famine did not move for three full seconds. She looked at Sunny's face, where all the hard lines around the jaw and the terrified narrowing around the blue eyes had vanished completely, leaving a seven-year-old girl's round, solemn stare in a twenty-something woman's battered skull.

"You are not Ena," Famine said slowly, her dry corn-husk voice dropping half an octave in astonishment. "And you are not the mean one who smells like iron."

"That's Harumi," Sunny said, nodding seriously as she righted the herring barrel with her good hand and patted the top of it to make sure it was steady. "She says bad words, but she only does it because the kids at school wrote mean things on the locker with marker. I'm Sunny. I'm seven. Well, Kio says our body is older than seven now because the world broke twice, so I'm seven-plus-extra, like when you get an extra nugget in the box by mistake."

She looked closely at Famine's face across the driftwood fire, at the pale skimmed-milk blue skin and the fine white cracks running over her cheekbones like a dropped teacup.

"Are you very cold?" Sunny asked, concerned. "Your face has little winter cracks in it like the birdbath in our yard in January."

Famine slowly sank back down onto her kelp-sacks, pulling the stiff canvas sailcloth up around her thin shoulders. She looked bewildered, like a wolf that had braced itself for a spear and been hit with a dandelion instead.

"I was cold for a very long time," Famine said carefully. "In a glass hole at the bottom of a canyon. I am warmer now."

"You still look super skinny," Sunny said, dragging the herring barrel two feet closer to Famine's side of the brazier and climbing up onto it so her boots swung three inches clear of the stone floor. "Like the stray kitty under the porch before Tim gave him the tuna can. Wait right there. Don't move."

Sunny reached deep into her right oilskin pocket with her good hand and pulled out the knotted red handkerchief. She spread it carefully on her knees, untied the four corners with her teeth and her one thumb, and revealed the small, thick-skinned green-gold salt-orange and the three-inch heel of dense dark kelp-bread she had saved from the hut.

"My left hand is sleeping inside my coat because it's got a boo-boo," Sunny explained, holding the orange pinned against her knee with her right palm while she dug her blunt thumbnail under the thick peel the way she had watched Rui do it in the white house. It took her a long time with only one hand, and the bitter oil squirted onto her wrist and smelled like Christmas, and her blister stung when the juice got on it, so she blew on the blister twice and kept peeling. "Usually Kio cuts the crust for me because he's really neat with knives, or Eri does it with her teeth while she pretends she's grumpy, though she isn't really grumpy, her legs just hurt from the bad babies."

Famine stared at the small green-gold fruit on the girl's knee. Her black, pupilless eyes tracked the juice dripping over Sunny's blistered palm and sizzling once on the hot iron rim of the brazier.

"What are you doing?" Famine whispered.

"Making snacks," Sunny said, pulling the peeled orange apart into two ragged halves with her fingers and laying the bigger half on top of the dark crust of kelp-bread. She held the bread and the dripping fruit out across the smoking driftwood coals in her blistered right hand. "Here. You can have the big half and the bread, because you're the guest in the cave and your tummy was growling so loud it shook the floor."

Famine did not reach out. She pulled her cracked blue hands back an inch against her chest, her thin shoulders hunching inside the canvas wrap.
