"You should not feed me with your hand, little one," Famine said, and for the first time in three thousand years the second Horseman of the Apocalypse sounded frightened of her own mouth. "I am Famine. I am the hunger at the end of every world. When I start eating, sometimes I do not know how to stop. I took a boy's head once when I was only trying to help him."

Sunny didn't pull her hand back. She kept the piece of kelp-bread and the wet orange segments held out steady over the warm air of the fire.

"I know," Sunny said softly. "You're Tim's friend who accidentally bit Drake too hard when he had the red bugs on him. Tim told Ena about you when they were in the dark place with the fire rocks. He said you tried really hard to be nice even though your job was eating things."

Famine's breath stopped in her throat.

"You aren't going to bite my hand," Sunny told her, shaking the bread a little so a drop of sweet orange juice fell onto the back of Famine's frost-cracked knuckle. "Because I'm giving you a snack on purpose. When you share a snack out of your pocket, that's rules. Nobody gets eaten during snack time."

Famine looked down at the single clear drop of juice shining on the white porcelain crack of her blue knuckle.

Slowly, moving as if her bones were made of dry glass that might snap if she breathed too hard, the Horseman of Famine lifted her two blue-white hands out of the sailcloth. She took the crust of dark kelp-bread and the five sticky segments of salt-orange off Sunny's blistered palm without touching her skin by so much as a hair.

Famine looked at the bread. Then she put one segment of the salt-orange into her mouth.

It was a bruised windfall orange from the bottom terrace, grown in volcanic sulfur-mud and watered with salty sea-mist, bitter at the pith and sharp as vinegar under the sugar. It wasn't a dead god's power from the Gray Abyss. It wasn't two anti-gods pouring their whole lives into her arms at the bottom of a canyon. It was half a piece of fruit warm from a seven-year-old girl's coat pocket.

Famine chewed it once.

Her narrow blue throat worked as she swallowed.

And then the white frost-cracks across Famine's cheekbones went dark, and water began to leak out of her black, pupilless eyes.

She did not know what the water was at first. She touched her own cheek with the fingers that were not holding the kelp-bread, staring at the wet salt smear on her blue skin in utter bewilderment, because even in the Gray Abyss when Drake had held her stone hand and her chest had shaken, her eyes had stayed as dry as desert flint. Now the water was pouring down both sides of her thin nose, dripping off her chin onto the canvas sailcloth, stinging the tiny frost-splits in her skin like warm rain.

"Oh," Famine whispered, her hand trembling around the crust of bread as a thick, wet sob tore out of her hollow ribs. "Oh, what is that? Sister, what is it doing?"

From the mouth of the cave, Death's small face softened in the orange firelight.

"You're crying, Famine," Death said gently. "Eat your bread before you get it too salty."

Famine bowed her frost-white head over her knees, holding the dark crust of kelp-bread in both hands against her mouth, and wept while she ate it, small, ragged, gasping sounds that echoed softly off the high wet roof of the sea-cave and mixed with the ticking of the black umbrella and the hiss of the driftwood coals.

Sunny ate her own three orange segments one by one, swinging her boots quietly against the side of the herring barrel so she wouldn't disturb the blue lady while she was having her cry. When she finished the last piece, she wiped her sticky fingers carefully on the red handkerchief, folded it into a neat square the way Kio liked it so he wouldn't get cross when he came back to the desk, and put it back in her pocket.

Then Sunny turned around on the barrel and looked toward the mouth of the cave, where the other girl in the long dark coat sat on the cedar log with her patched black umbrella open under the rock overhang.

"Excuse me," Sunny said politely. "Umbrella girl?"

"Yes, Sunny?" Death said.

"Are you the one who took my big brother Tim to the river where he skips the flat rocks with Drake?"

The dark drops falling from the iron spokes of the umbrella paused for the space of one breath, then ticked onto the basalt again, slow and steady.

"I am," Death said.

Sunny looked down at her boots, picking at a dried flake of mud on her knee with her good thumb. Her bottom lip wobbled once before she bit it still.

"Is he still mad at me?" Sunny asked in a whisper barely louder than the rain outside.

Death tilted her head. "Mad at you for what, little one?"

"Before the bad lady in the white coat climbed through the kitchen window on Sunday," Sunny said, her voice wobbling harder as she stared at her thumb. "When I was seven. The big kids at school wrote the bad poem on Ena's locker about how our hair was too shiny and she was going to break the whole world. And Tim had a shoebox under his bed with eleven fat black Sharpies in it that he used every morning before the bell rang to cross out the mean words and write BULLSHIT and draw a smiley face so we wouldn't cry."

In the quiet behind the cellar door in her head, Ena was pressing both hands against the wood, listening through the crack without daring to breathe.

"One afternoon when Tim was at baseball practice," Sunny whispered, two fat tears rolling down her cheeks and dropping onto the front of her oilskin coat, "I went under his bed to get the shoebox because I wanted to draw a picture of our house with the chimney smoking so we could tape it over the kitchen window and keep the bad lady out. And I tripped on his gym sneaker by the closet, and I dropped the shoebox, and the cap came off the brand-new fat Sharpie he just bought with his lawn-mowing money. And it rolled under his desk and leaked a big black puddle right into the carpet where Mom said we weren't allowed to have ink. And I got scared he wouldn't cross out the locker anymore if I broke his marker, so I put the cap back on real fast and hid it at the bottom of the box under the ten dead ones and I never told him."

Sunny wiped her eyes with the back of her blistered wrist, hiccuping once.

"And when he found it the next morning, it was all dried out and couldn't make the smiley face," Sunny said. "And then on Sunday Mom and Dad stopped working by the stove, and after that Tim stopped drawing smiley faces on the locker and started looking at our hands when we made peanut butter sandwiches, and then he put the big bread knife in Ena's chest. I always thought maybe if I didn't break his eleventh Sharpie and hide it under the dead ones, he wouldn't have run out of ink to cross the bad poem out."

Across the brazier, Famine had stopped crying. She was sitting very still with the last crumb of kelp-bread in her blue fingers, staring at the seven-year-old girl on the herring barrel.

At the mouth of the cave, Death closed her broken black umbrella halfway, so the patched silk folded around the wooden stick with a soft rustle like a bird settling its wings on a branch. She stood up from the cedar log and walked twenty paces into the cave, stopping right at the edge of the warm circle of brazier light. She did not step onto the dry stones by Sunny's boots, because Death never steps inside a circle she does not mean to finish, yet she stood close enough that Sunny could smell the clean river-water and wet willow leaves on her dark coat.

"Sunny," Death said softly.

Sunny looked up, her blue eyes swimming with tears.

"When I took your brother Tim out of the ruins after the virus finished him," Death said, her deep-water eyes steady and warm in the firelight, "he was carrying only two things in his pockets that were small enough to cross the water. People are not allowed to bring swords or silver armor or white towers under my umbrella. They can only bring what fits inside a closed hand."

Sunny sniffed. "What did he bring?"

"In his left pocket, he had a smooth flat river stone that Drake gave him when they were twelve," Death said. "And in his right pocket, he had a dried-out black plastic marker with a bite-mark on the cap and a stain on the side where somebody dropped it on a bedroom rug."

Sunny's mouth fell open.

"He knows you dropped it," Death said gently. "He knew that morning when he found it at the bottom of the shoebox, because you left a smudge of black ink on the nose of his gym sneaker when you tried to wipe it off with a sock. He didn't stop crossing out the locker because the marker dried up, little one. He kept that eleventh marker in his pocket through hell, and through the war, and all the way to the river. Whenever he misses you and Ena while he is waiting on the bank, he takes the cap off and draws a smiley face on a flat rock with the dry felt tip before he skips it across the water to Drake."

Sunny sat on the herring barrel with her hands in her lap, staring at the small pale girl with the black umbrella.

"It doesn't make any ink on the rock, though," Sunny whispered. "If it's dried out."

"It doesn't need ink at the river," Death said, and a very faint, quiet smile touched the corners of her mouth. "The water knows what it says. He is not mad at you, Sunny. He has never once been mad at you about the Sharpie."

Sunny let out a breath so big her whole coat deflated around her shoulders. She looked down at her blistered right palm, then over at Famine, who was watching her across the driftwood coals with wet black eyes and orange peel still sitting on her canvas knee.

"Okay," Sunny said, rubbing her nose with her wrist and smiling a small, tired, watery smile. "That's good. Because Kio said we aren't allowed to buy any more markers at the jetty store since we only have fourteen copper pence left in the leather pouch and we need salt-lard for the lamp." She yawned, her jaw cracking wide as the heavy, warm steam of the cave and the relief inside her chest pulled her eyelids halfway down. "I think I'm going to go back in the dark room and go to sleep now. Ena's stopped shaking behind the door. If I fall off the barrel when we switch back, don't let our head hit the iron bucket, okay?"

Famine reached across the edge of the fire with one blue, frost-crazed hand and took hold of the rough hem of the oversized oilskin coat to steady her on the barrel.

"I have you, little one," the Horseman of Famine said softly. "Go to sleep."


Chapter Nine: What We Did to Each Other

The pelagic typhoon hit the cliff at noon the next day, and by three o'clock the ocean was throwing green water eighty feet straight up the basalt wall into the lower orchard terraces.

Rui had gone up the cliff path at dawn with two coils of tarred hemp rope to lash down Tsui's slate roof before the barometer bottomed out, shouting back through the rising gale that she would stay in the white cottage until the wind shifted and that Famine and Death were sealed dry behind the high tide-wall in the deep cave. She told Eri and me to take the stone salt-storehouse cut into the second terrace shelf thirty feet above the surf line and drop the oak storm-bar across the iron brackets from the inside.

By nightfall of the first day, the world outside the storehouse had stopped existing.

The room was twelve feet long and eight feet wide, built of unmortared basalt blocks three feet thick wedged directly into a pocket of the volcanic cliff. There were no windows. Along the back wall stood four rows of cedar barrels packed with coarse grey sea-salt and dried lingcod; along the front wall sat two sacks of peat, a three-gallon stoneware jug of water, an iron grease-lamp hanging from a roof hook by a chain, and the two-inch-thick oak plank door, held shut against the vacuum of the cyclone by seven iron bolts and a squared beam of driftwood the thickness of a man's thigh.

The sound outside was not wind.

It was a continuous, physical hydraulic roar that lived inside the rock under our hipbones and inside the fillings of our teeth. Every forty seconds, a thirty-foot gray wall of the southern ocean slammed flat against the basalt shelf directly below our floor, and the compressed air forced up through the blowholes in the cliff face shrieked like five hundred steam whistles bursting at once, followed by a booming, hollow thud that made the hanging grease-lamp swing in a wild two-foot arc and rained black grit down from the ceiling slabs onto our hair.

By the evening of the second day, the lamp-grease was half gone, the air inside the eight-by-twelve stone box smelled of rancid cod-oil, wet wool, and our own sweat, and neither of us had slept for thirty-six hours.

I sat in the far corner behind the salt barrels with my knees jammed against my chest and my hands clamped tight over my ears, staring at the two-inch oak door.

Every time a wave hit the shelf below us and the whole cliff shuddered, the sound that came through the stone was not the ocean anymore.

It was glass.

It was a green glass cylinder forty feet tall going up into the dark of the Nursery vault like a skyscraper, cracking all at once from top to bottom under the knuckle of my blackened left fist, and the warm green brine coming out in a tidal wave that knocked Eri across the concrete floor while the song stopped across every world in the universe.

"Stop looking at the door like that," Eri said from the other end of the room.

She was sitting on a folded peat-sack with her back wedged into the angle of the stone wall, both of her broken, crooked shins stretched out straight on the flags in front of her. She had her heavy leather gloves on, even inside, and her bare thumbs were hooked nervously into the belt-loops of her skirt while the ironwood crutch lay across her lap like a rifle across a sentry's knees. In the yellow flicker of the swinging grease-lamp, the pink scar tissue around her empty left eye socket looked raw and tight, and her one remaining yellow-brown eye had dark purple hollows under it like thumbprints in dough.

"It sounds like the tank," I said. My voice came out thin and raspy over the thunder in the rock. "When the water hits the shelf. It sounds just like the tank breaking."

"It's seawater hitting basalt, Ena," Eri said sharply. "We're thirty feet above the surge line and the door has seven iron bolts on it. Stop winding yourself up or you're going to drop into Kio or Harumi and I don't feel like getting lectured or punched tonight."

"You're the one who has your mind sticking out six inches past your teeth," I said, pulling my hands down from my ears and wrapping my right fingers around my tarred left forearm because the char underneath was throbbing in time with the waves. "I can feel you pressing against the back of my head every ten minutes to see if I'm losing it."

"I haven't gone into your head since the Nursery!" Eri snapped, her gloved hands tightening on the shaft of the ironwood crutch until the leather creaked. "I promised you on the boat at the north jetty I wouldn't go in without asking, and I haven't!"

"You don't have to go in," I said, looking across the eight feet of stone floor at her one bright eye. "You sit there and stare at my arm like you're waiting for the red light to start leaking through the cloth so you can decide whether to push me off the cliff or tell me to punch another wall."

A massive cross-swell struck the cliff face outside. The whole storehouse jumped half an inch to the left. The oak door bowed inward against the driftwood beam with a crack like a pistol shot, and a spray of atomized salt water hissed straight through the seam around the iron hinges, wetting both our boots.

Eri flinched so hard her heels jerked against the stone floor, and she bit down on her bottom lip until a bead of bright red blood welled up on the dry skin. Her shins were killing her; I could see the involuntary tremor twitching in the muscle of her right calf above the crooked lump where the Nursery babies had snapped the tibia three years ago.

"Don't talk to me about the Nursery," Eri said, her voice dropping into a low, shaking register that I had not heard since hell. "You don't get to sit in that corner clutching your arm like a wounded virgin and act like I'm the one who ruined your life on that floor."

"You told me to break it!" I yelled over the roar of the receding wave, pushing myself up onto my knees behind the salt barrel. "I knelt down right next to your broken legs on that concrete floor, glowing red, full of twenty-two shards from the blue button tin, and I begged you! I said, *Tell me not to, Eri. Please. You're the only one I'd listen to. Tell me not to and I'll walk out.* And you looked right at me with your one eye and you said, *Break it. Take all of it. Go out to the highway and make her sorry.*"

Eri hauled herself upright by jamming the brass ferrule of her crutch into a crack between the flagstones and throwing her good shoulder against the basalt wall. She stood swaying in the middle of the eight-foot room as the hanging lamp swung past her head, throwing her one-eyed shadow huge and broken across the salt barrels.

"And why did I have one eye to look at you with, Ena?!" Eri screamed back, her voice tearing rough and ugly through the thunder of the storm.

She reached up with her left gloved hand, hooked two leather fingers under the black linen tie above her brow, and ripped the eyepatch down onto her cheek.

In the yellow tallow light, the left side of her face was a sunken, puckered crater of pale purple and white scar tissue where the lids had healed collapsed inward over nothing, with three jagged red tracks radiating out from the tear duct to the bridge of her nose and down her cheekbone where a blade had twisted on its way out.

"Let's go all the way back to the beginning if we're opening the books tonight!" Eri roared, leaning forward over her crutch so close I could smell the blood on her bitten lip and the old wet wool of her coat. "Since we're trapped in a rock at the end of the world and you want to talk about what I did to you in the Nursery! Where were we the first time we met in that vault, babe? Ten years ago! Book One, before the peace, before the roof, before the cans of peaches! When I was Nine, when I was just Himo's little mind-demon trying to hold the corridor against Corrupt while Siren was coming up the stairs!"

My back hit the salt barrels so hard the cedar staves groaned. My stomach dropped out from under my ribs.

"Eri, don't," I whispered.

"Oh, now it's *Eri, don't*!" she laughed, a ragged, barking sound with no humor in it anywhere. "You want to talk about the Nursery floor, let's talk about the Nursery floor! You caught me by the hair in the green hallway outside the baby tanks. You had your red wings out and your eyes had no pupils in them at all, and you pinned my head against the drainage grate with your boot on my throat while I was screaming for my mother, who didn't give a shit if I lived or died. And you didn't kill me. No, killing me would have been too fast for Corrupt. You took a broken piece of glass off the instrument panel, and you knelt on my chest, and you cut my left eye out of my skull while I was wide awake and inside your head feeling you enjoy it!"

"That was the corruption!" I screamed, tears hot as scalding water spilling over my lower lids while my hands came up in front of my face. "That was before they pulled it out of me! I wasn't in the front, the red had me all the way down."

"You were right there!" Eri shouted, jabbing a gloved finger four inches from my nose, her whole frail body shaking on the ironwood stick. "I'm a demon of the mind, Ena! Don't ever fucking lie to a mind-demon about who was in the room! The red was driving the hands, sure, and Harumi was laughing in the doorway, and right behind them, sitting on the floor watching through the glass and not lifting one single finger to pull the brake because she liked being too big to get stabbed in a kitchen anymore, was you! You carved my eye out on that grate, and then you threw me down the chute into the dark and left me for Siren to chew on while my face was still draining into my collar!"

The room spun sideways under the grease-lamp. I could hear the wet pop of the optic nerve giving way under a jagged shard of gauge-glass, a memory I had buried under ten years of grey ash and forty-one-day sobriety chips at the laundromat on Fifth, and it was inside my own right palm, the weight of the glass, the hot slick gush over my knuckles while a girl with two yellow-brown eyes screamed under my knee.

Inside my skull, the bench tipped over.

My breath caught in the middle of a sob and turned into a hard, dry rasp. My posture jerked straight up against the salt barrels, my chin lifting, my wet eyes narrowing into slits as Harumi slammed her boots down on the floorboards of my mouth.

"Yeah, we took your fucking eye out, Nine!" I snarled in Harumi's flat, saw-toothed voice, shoving myself off the barrels so hard my chest almost brushed the tip of Eri's finger. "And what did you do five days later when we died the first time and dropped down into the grey river?! You sat on your little three-legged stool by the water in Purgatory with your face wrapped up in dirty rags, pulling memory-pearls out of dead people's ears with iron tongs, and when Ena came washing up on the mud bank half-dead and begging for somewhere to hide, you opened the gate to the city of statues and shoved her in!"

Eri didn't back up an inch. Her one yellow-brown eye blazed in the dim light, fierce and wet and wide open.

"You bet your ass I did!" Eri yelled back. "And I watched you walk down the main square on the viewing screen from my booth by the ferry!"

"You laughed at her!" I roared, my right fist slamming down onto the lid of the salt barrel beside me hard enough to split the cedar board and drive a wooden splinter under my blistered palm. "We could hear your voice coming out of the speakers on the lampposts all the way across the square while we were standing in front of Tim's plinth looking at the stone bread knife sticking out of our own stone ribs! You got on the horn and you laughed until you choked and you told the whole city of the damned that Corrupt was too bad for hell! That even the pit wouldn't swallow her, so she had to walk around a museum of everybody she ever murdered until her feet wore off to the ankles! You kept her locked in that square with her dead brother's statue for four hundred cycles until she chewed her own tongue black!"

"She owed me an eye!" Eri screamed, bringing the ironwood crutch down against the basalt flagstones with a ringing crack that threw a spark off the stone. "She owed me an eye, and she owed my brother his head, and when your sweet little brother Tim came running through my portal in hell with his silver helmet on trying to finish her off or save her or whatever stupid boy-hero bullshit he was doing that week, I didn't even blink! I shut the portal door right across the middle of his chest while he was halfway through it, and I watched both halves of Modify drop into two different dimensions, because I wanted you to know what it felt like to have somebody take a pair of shears to the only thing you cared about!"

The name hung in the eight-by-twelve stone box between us while a wave boomed against the cliff below and shook the salt dust off the rafters.

In the back of my head, Ena made a sound like an animal stepped on in the dark, and Harumi's fingers went numb on the split lid of the barrel.

"And you knew," I whispered, the voice slipping back and forth between Ena's choke and Harumi's gravel in the same sentence, both of us standing in the doorway of the skull at once because neither of us could hold the weight of it alone. "For two years on that roof. Every Tuesday when you climbed up the seven flights of the fire escape with your bad stomach and your cans of peaches in a paper bag, and sat next to me with your legs hanging over the street, and listened to me talk about going to Tim's stone on the hill on Thursdays. You never told me you were the one who shut the portal on him. You let me carve I'M SORRY and ME TOO into that rock with a piece of rebar while you sat three feet away eating peaches out of a tin with my pocketknife!"

Eri's laugh came out broken in the middle, ending in a wet cough that doubled her over her crutch.

"Oh, we're talking about Tuesdays on the roof now?" Eri gasped, looking up at me with water running out of her one good eye and sliding down into the puckered scar of her empty left one. "Let's talk about Tuesdays on the fucking roof, Ena! Every Tuesday for fourteen months I dragged these two dead sticks up seven flights of rusty iron stairs because teleporting made me puke my guts out in the alley behind the pharmacy! Fourteen flights of steps on two shins that the Nursery babies turned into dry oatmeal, with a heavy sack of canned fruit knocking against my hip, because I thought: *Nobody else is going to sit with her. Everybody else is dead or staring at Modify's white monument, and Ena is up there under a leaky tarp with a one-eared orange cat trying to stay clean after ending the world!*"

Eri dropped her left hand from her crutch and slapped her own chest hard, right over the breastbone.

"I sat on that parapet with my legs hurting so bad I couldn't see straight," Eri cried, her voice cracking high and thin like a girl's, "and I kept my mind locked up inside my own skull with three iron padlocks so I wouldn't invade your privacy! I didn't peek once! Not one single time in fourteen months, because you were my best friend and I wanted you to have one person in the universe who trusted your word without digging around in your head with tongs! And every Tuesday I asked you how the count was going, and you looked right into my one good eye with your big clean blue eyes, and you said, *Day thirty-two, Eri. Day forty-one, Eri. Still going.*"

My knees buckled. I slid halfway down the curve of the salt barrel, my good hand catching the iron hoop to keep from pitching face-first onto the flags.

"And I bumped my shoulder into yours," Eri sobbed, her head shaking back and forth as the grease-lamp swung wildly above us, "and I said, *Proud of you, babe.* Like an idiot! Like a blind, crippled fucking clown climbing seven flights of stairs every week to bring peaches to a girl who had a red-and-white mint tin full of fourteen black glass shards hidden behind the water tank ten feet behind my back! You had a shard melted into your left palm while I was holding your hand at Dov's funeral! You stood up in front of those nine broken people at the laundromat and cried over a pickle jar full of dog ashes and a nineteen-year-old boy you disintegrated yourself because he caught you getting high behind the dryers, and you let me hold your hand and tell you that using your power to fight the hounds wasn't a relapse, it was a sacrifice!"

"I couldn't let you know!" I screamed, dropping all the way to my knees on the cold stone floor and tearing at the front of my hair with my blistered right hand while my wrapped left arm pounded once, twice against the salt barrel. "That was the only thought in my head when Dov stood up behind the dryers! Not the hounds, not Marguerite, not the prophecy! I looked at his face and I heard the hum say *He'll tell Eri*, and I couldn't bear for you to climb up those seven flights on Tuesday and look at me the way Tim looked at me in the kitchen before he took the bread knife out of the block! I killed a nineteen-year-old kid who stayed clean because of me just so you would keep bumping my shoulder on Tuesdays and saying you were proud of me!"

"And I saw it!" Eri roared back, leaning down over me on her crutch, tears pouring off her chin onto her leather gloves. "The second I went into your head on the Nursery floor by the big green tank! I reached in because I was scared, the way you grab a stair-rail in the dark, and the whole fourteen months hit me in the face all at once! The mint tin behind the brick! The blue button tin with twenty-two pieces of Dov and the dogs that you crawled around on the laundromat tile at three in the morning picking up in the dark! Every Tuesday a lie! Every single fucking peach can a joke!"

She hauled in a ragged, whistling breath, her one eye fixed on me with a terrible, naked clarity that stripped the skin off my face.

"And you want to know the truth about what I did next, Ena?" Eri whispered, her voice dropping so low that I had to read half of it off her bleeding lip over the thunder of the typhoon. "You want the real ugly part that I didn't even write down at the end of my own story?"

I stared up at her from my knees on the wet basalt floor.

"When you knelt there glowing red next to my broken shins," Eri said, "and you asked me to tell you not to break the tank. I wasn't just using you to get to Himo on the old highway so she'd turn around after ten thousand years and say *Sorry, Nine*. That was half of it. That was the excuse I told myself so I could live with it."

Her gloved hand shook on the cross-piece of the ironwood stick.

"The other half," Eri said, each word dropping like lead shot onto the stone between my knees, "was that I had just found out my best friend had been lying to my face every Tuesday for a year and had murdered a kid to cover it up, and I hated you so much in that second that I wanted you to punch the glass. I looked at you begging me to save you from the prophecy, and I thought: *You want to be a monster so bad you hide tins behind the water tank while I climb seven flights on broken legs? Fine. Choke on the whole tank. Eat Twenty-two and eat the song and go out to the highway and burn the universe down so you never have to lie to me about day forty-one again.* I could have reached into the soft part of your mind with one finger and turned you around and made you walk out that vault door clean. And I didn't push. Because I wanted my mother to say sorry to me, and I wanted to punish you for making me look like a fool on your roof."

The silence inside my head did not break like glass; it shattered like a basalt boulder hit with a sledgehammer at twenty below zero.

Four voices screamed inside the same three inches of bone behind my eyes at once.

*She knew,* Ena wept, curling down toward the cold stone flags. *She hated us. She sent us out to the highway to die.*

*I told you!* Harumi roared, lunging for the front teeth with both hands swinging, wanting to grab the hemp ledger out of our coat and smash Eri's one remaining eye out of her skull. *I told you nobody counts us when the bill comes due! Throw it at her! Break her stick!*

*Count the bolts,* Kio's voice shrieked, high and thin and cracking down the middle as the walls of the inner corridors collapsed into each other like wet cardboard. *Seven bolts on the oak door, four rows of salt barrels, six staves per barrel, pulse one-eighty-four, we are switching too fast, the joints are stripping, stop, stop, stop.*

*Timmy!* Sunny sobbed from under the cellar stairs, holding her hands over her ears in the dark while the water came through the ceiling. *Make them stop yelling in the kitchen!*

My body jerked on the floor like a marionette whose strings had all been tangled around a turning wheel.

My right hand ripped open the breast of my oilskin coat, tore out the heavy hemp-bound ledger with its four handwritings and its frayed twine ties, and in Harumi's blind, snarling swing I hurled the book straight at Eri's head.

The corner of the heavy ledger caught Eri across her unscarred right temple, knocking her sideways off the balance of her crutch. She did not cry out as she fell; her bad shins folded under her like wet straw, the ironwood crutch clattered across the stone floor into the peat sacks, and she went down hard on her hip and elbow against the basalt wall.

Before the book even hit the stones, Harumi lost the mouth.

My spine snapped backward toward the oak door. I scrambled on my knees and elbows across the wet grit into the corner by the iron hinges, moving with Kio's rigid, mechanical terror, my blistered right index finger stabbing into the dark air to point at the iron bolts one after another while my voice came out clipped, flat, and breathless:

"Bolt one, two inches, wrought iron, holding. Bolt two, two inches, holding. Bolt three, rust on the lower bracket, holding. Bolt four..."

A thirty-foot wave slammed the cliff outside. The oak plank jumped against my shoulder, showering my face with cold brine.

Kio shattered out of the front in half a second.

My knees pulled all the way up into my stomach, my chin tucked down between my shins, and Sunny's high, terrified wailing poured out of my mouth into the hem of my wet wool skirt while I rocked back and forth against the salt barrels: "I didn't mean to drop the marker, I put the cap back on, please don't get the bread knife out of the wood block, I'll eat the tuna fish, I'll be good..."

And then the red hum inside my wrapped left arm surged up like boiling tar to drown all four of us out, and Ena slammed back into the body with every nerve on fire.

I screamed, a raw, tearing sound that had no words in it at all, and my blistered right hand went straight to the leather twine binding the tarred linen around my left forearm.

I got my teeth under the knot, ripped the twine loose, and tore the three layers of tarred bandage off the black charcoal flesh underneath. The skin from my wrist to my elbow was cracked open in six places where the dry salt air and the old corruption scars pulled against the muscle, oozing thick, dark, penny-smelling blood. I dug the nails of my right hand straight into the deepest black fissure on my inner left forearm, trying to claw the dead, humming meat off my own radius bone, trying to dig the twenty-two shards of Dov and the hounds out of my veins with my bare fingernails while blood ran down my wrist and dripped onto the flagstones.

"Ena, stop!"

Across the eight-foot stone box, Eri did not reach for her ironwood crutch where it had rolled against the peat sacks.

She left the stick on the floor.

On her hands and her broken, knotted knees, dragging the crooked lumps of both shattered shins across the rough, wet basalt flagstones without a sound except the loud, sharp grinding of her teeth, Eri crawled across the storehouse floor through the salt dust and the cod-grease. Every three inches her left knee hit a stone edge and her whole frame shuddered with the white-hot stab of the unhealed tibia gravel, and she kept coming anyway, one hand in front of the other, just like she had crawled for six hours on her elbows through the drainage pipes of the Nursery to reach the green tank.

She reached my corner by the salt barrels.

She did not go into my mind.

I would have felt it in a microsecond if she had touched my head; after fourteen years I knew the exact cool, sliding weight of Eri's mind-demon fingers opening the back door of my skull to turn a dial or quiet a panic. The air inside my head stayed completely untouched, loud and ragged and entirely my own.

Instead, Eri sat back on her ruined heels in front of me, clamped the leather thumb of her right glove between her teeth, and yanked the glove off her hand, spitting it onto the wet stone. Then she stripped off the left glove with her bare right fingers and threw it aside too.

Her bare hands were small, scarred across every knuckle from the day she had pounded on the green glass of Twenty-two's tank until the skin split, with dirt under the short nails and a pale crease across her right palm where a memory-tong had burned her in Purgatory.

She reached out with both bare, warm, human hands and closed her fingers around my right wrist, pulling my bloodied fingernails out of the black, weeping flesh of my left forearm.

"Get off me!" I sobbed, thrashing my shoulders against the salt barrel, trying to wrench my wrist out of her grip so I could finish tearing the black skin off my arm, while Harumi bared our teeth and Kio tried to count the bolts over her shoulder. "Let go! I took your eye! I lied to you every Tuesday! I killed Dov! Look at it, it's black all the way to the bone, you wanted me to choke on the tank, let me dig it out!"

"Shut up," Eri panted, her bare hands locking around both of my wrists now, holding my blistered right hand and my bleeding, charcoal-black left arm pinned together between our chests while my dark, copper-smelling blood ran warm over her pale knuckles and down into the cuffs of her wool sleeves. "Shut up, Ena. Shut up, Harumi. Kio, stop counting the fucking door, it has seven bolts and it isn't going anywhere. Sunny, nobody has a knife."

I pulled back with all the strength in my shoulders, and Eri was half my size with two broken legs, so my jerk dragged her three inches forward across the sharp basalt flags on her scarred knees.

She let out a short, high gasp as her right shinbone ground straight into a ridge of stone, her face going grey as wet ash under the swinging lamp, and she did not let go of my wrists. She leaned her whole small weight forward until her wet forehead bumped hard against my collarbone, right over the pocket where the hemp ledger had been, and wrapped all ten of her bare fingers tighter around my bleeding hands.

"Stop pulling, you big idiot, you're going to rip my knees off," Eri choked out against my wet coat, her hot tears soaking through my wool shirt while her bare thumbs rubbed slow, fierce circles directly over the bleeding black cracks of my left wrist and the broken yellow blister on my right palm. "I'm not going into your head. Do you hear me? I'm right out here on the floor. I'm not pushing you and I'm not fixing you and I'm not telling you what to break."

My muscles went out all at once, like a severed staysail dropping off a mast in a gale.

I slumped down between the salt barrels until my forehead rested on top of Eri's narrow shoulder, my chest heaving in great, empty, rattling gulps that smelled of salt and blood and tallow smoke. Inside my head, the four doors did not slam shut; they stood wide open in the dark around a single room, Harumi sitting on the floor with her back against the wall, Kio resting his chin on his knees by the threshold, Sunny curled up with her thumb in her mouth by Ena's ankle, all four of us listening through the same pair of ears to the rapid, thudding beat of Eri's heart against our ribs.

"I'm not clean," I whispered into the wet wool of her collar, my fingers trembling inside her bare, bloody hands. "I told the cat day one every morning after the funeral, and then I used another shard out of the blue button tin just to go to sleep. And even now, seventy-four days on the boat with no shards anywhere in the ocean, five minutes ago when you looked at me with your patch off, the arm wanted to turn red so I wouldn't have to feel my chest hurting."

"I know," Eri said against my collarbone, her bare fingers warm and slick with my blood as she held both my hands between her breasts.

"And I wasn't fully gone when I cut your eye out on the drainage grate," I whispered, the bile rising hot in my throat as I forced the words past my teeth without letting Harumi say them for me. "You were right. I was sitting behind the glass watching my hand do it. And I didn't pull the brake because I was fifteen years old and my brother had put a bread knife through my ribs in our kitchen, and it felt so good to be the one holding the sharp thing while somebody else screamed."

Eri's bare hands tightened once around my fingers, hard enough to hurt my blister, and then stayed right where they were.

"I know," Eri said, her voice rough and steady in the dark. "I was in your head when you did it. I've known that for ten years, Ena. And when I shut the portal door across Tim's chest in hell and cut him in two, he was looking back over his shoulder to see if you were safe by the rocks, and I didn't wait half a second for him to clear the frame because I wanted him dead so you would hurt as bad as I did. And on the Nursery floor, when you were dying of twenty-two shards and begging me to be your friend and tell you to walk away, I threw you at my mother like a hand grenade because I wanted two words out of her mouth more than I wanted you alive."

She lifted her head an inch off my collarbone so she could look up into my face from three inches away with her one yellow-brown eye. The purple-white crater of her empty left socket was wet with tears and smeared with a streak of dark blood from my wrist where she had wiped her cheek.

"We're not good people, babe," Eri whispered over the roar of the sea outside the stone wall. "We never were. Not in Book One, not on the roof with the peaches, not in the Nursery, and not here. Everybody who was good died in the war or went under Death's umbrella with a dry marker in their pocket. You and me are what didn't wash off the rocks when the tide went out."

A wave broke against the cliff below us, booming through the basalt floor like a giant's heart, and the grease-lamp overhead guttered down to a tiny blue bead of flame on the wick, throwing the eight-by-twelve storehouse into deep, warm shadow.

"I'm not asking you to forgive me for your brother or the tank," Eri said, her bare, ungloved thumbs pressing steady and warm against the pulsing black veins of my left wrist, keeping me anchored to the stone floor without a single thread of mind-magic between us. "And don't you dare ask me to forgive you for my eye or for Dov or for lying to me on Tuesdays. Forgiveness is a story people carve on white towers so they don't have to look at the bodies in the ditch."

My right hand turned slowly inside her grip until my blistered palm lay flat against her scarred knuckles, and my blackened left hand closed gently around her bare left wrist.

"Then what are we doing?" I asked in a very small voice that belonged to me, and to Harumi, and to Kio, and to the seven-year-old girl behind the cellar door all at once.

Eri let out a long, tired breath that smelled of salt pork and blood, and leaned her head back down into the hollow between my neck and my shoulder, stretching her bad legs out across the wet flagstones alongside mine so her weight rested against my knees.

"We're sitting in a salt-shed waiting for the rain to stop," Eri said. "And when it stops on Thursday, I'm going to pick up my stick, and you're going to put your book back in your coat, and we're going to help Rui carry the bag of oranges up the cliff."



Chapter Ten: The Chalk on the Sea-Wall

On the second morning after we left the Salt-Spire, the sea stopped pretending it was only water.

Up until noon the swells had been deep Pelagian slate, heavy and green-bellied, rolling under the hull of our borrowed three-oared skiff with a low, steady thrum that put the orange cat to sleep on the coil of anchor line between my boots. Then the water began to shallow out over things that had no business lying at the bottom of an ocean.

"Rocks," Eri said from the bow, leaning her bad hip against the salt-bleached gunwale and resting her ironwood crutch across her thighs. Her good eye narrowed against the glare coming off the chop. "No. Wait. That's a roof."

I shipped the starboard oar, letting the ash blade trail in the froth, and leaned over the side.

Ten feet down, clear through the cold green brine, a black shingle roof sloped down into a bed of kelp. Below the eaves, a white wooden porch rail sat intact, crusted over with purple barnacles and pale anemones waving their tentacles in the current like fat little hands. Twenty yards past the porch, an iron grain-tower rose out of the silt at a thirty-degree tilt, its rusted cylinder split down the seam, its high steel catwalk poking four feet above the whitecaps where a row of pelicans sat drying their wings. Painted across the curved upper plates of the tank, faded by salt and green weed until the block letters looked like old bruises, was half a word:

HATTIEVIL

"The Glass Shoals," Kio said inside our head. His voice was right behind the bridge of our nose, calm and dry, the sound of a thumb running down the margin of a ruled page. *Get the ledger out, Ena. Don't let your fingers get wet before we write the heading.*

I reached inside my salt-stiffened wool coat, pulled the hemp ledger from the oilskin pouch against my ribs, and wedged the charcoal stub between my first two fingers. My left arm ached from rowing. The black burn scars running from my palm to my elbow always pulled tight when the salt spray dried on them, like shrink-wrapped leather shrunk two sizes too small over the bone.

*Tell me what to put,* I thought back.

*East of the Spire,* Kio dictated, watching through our eyes as the skiff drifted past the drowned brick bell-tower of a Methodist church whose stone steps came right up to the waterline. *The shoals where the flatland towns washed in after the page tore. Water depth: six feet to two fathoms. Visual confirmation of submerged masonry, asphalt causeways, and agricultural storage towers. Three alters present at the front. Ena holding the ink. Harumi asleep in the back. Kio on watch.*

I wrote it down in my small, cramped letters, pressing hard enough that the charcoal bit into the damp hemp fiber.

*She's not asleep,* Harumi murmured from the dark behind my ears, warm and thick as spoiled honey. *She's just bored, baby. Wake me up when somebody tries to bite us.*

I didn't write that part down. I snapped the oilskin shut around the book, tucked it back against my ribs, and took up the oar again.

For another mile we threaded a labyrinth of half-drowned country roads and broken limestone causeways. Fence posts stuck up out of the tide flats like rotten teeth, strung with rusted barbed wire that caught floating mats of bladderwrack and dead jellyfish. Here and there a whole section of brick storefront sat wedged between two basalt reefs, its plate-glass windows long gone, schools of silver minnows darting in and out over a tiled counter where an ice cream machine rusted in three feet of surf. Every piece of it smelled of wet iron, dead prairie mud, and deep-sea brine, two different worlds jammed together in the dark when the first sentence broke and left to rot in the tide.

At the center of the shoals, where three drowned highways met in a jagged ring of black basalt, stood the lighthouse.

It wasn't tall like the Salt-Spire. It was squat and thick-walled, built out of rough-hewn black stone mortared with burnt shell lime, rising forty feet above a broad stone sea-wall that held back the deeper swells. All around the outer parapet of the sea-wall, just above the shiny dark line of the high-tide mark, someone had drawn a band of white chalk six inches wide.

It wasn't a neat line. It was layered, circle inside circle, cross-hatched with dense, blocky letters that had been washed out by spray and redrawn so many times the black basalt underneath was caked with a crusty white paste. Every ten feet along the top of the wall sat an upside-down glass mason jar weighing down a scrap of heavy butcher paper covered in charcoal block script.

A girl was sitting on the wet stone jetty below the wall with a heap of green hemp crab nets spread across her knees.

She couldn't have been more than eleven or twelve. She wore a pair of rubber fisherman's waders tied up under her armpits with baling twine, a faded yellow slicker with the cuffs rolled back four times, and a crooked crown made of gold-painted cardboard that had gone soft at the points from sea fog and was held together with strips of grey duct tape. She had a flat cedar netting needle in her small red-chapped hand, and she was tying square knots in torn twine with the grim, methodical speed of an old dockhand.

When our bow scraped against the barnacled timber fender of the jetty, she didn't jump. She finished her knot, bit the tarred twine off with her side teeth, and looked up at us from under the duct-taped rim of her paper crown.

"You're inside the third circle," she said. Her voice was flat and reedy, an eleven-year-old's voice that had spent too many months giving orders in an empty street and hadn't quite forgotten the habit. "If you're humming, you have to turn your boat around and row back out past the grain tower. Rule four. Raven says if anybody hums inside the jetty she'll feed their tongue to the mud."

"Nobody's humming, kid," Eri said, hauling herself up onto the forward thwart and tossing our bow line onto the wet stone at the girl's rubber boots. "Tie that off around the iron cleat, will you? My leg's shit on wet steps."

The girl looked at Eri's leather eyepatch, then at the ironwood crutch, then at my scarred black left forearm resting on the oar loom. Slowly, she set her netting needle down on the dry middle of the pile, stood up in her oversized waders, and took a turn with our hemp painter around the rusted iron bollard. She tied a clean double half-hitch.

"I'm Hattie," she said, straightening her cardboard crown with two tar-stained fingers. "Hattie Lindqvist. I used to be the queen of that town out there." She pointed her chin toward the tilted iron cylinder of the grain elevator sticking out of the surf half a mile back. "Before it got wet. Now I fix the traps. If I don't fix the traps, the crabs get out through the bottom mesh and we just eat boiled seaweed and salt pork, and salt pork makes Brad throw up."

"I'm Ena," I said, picking up Tim by his middle. The cat let out a grumpy, rattling yowl and dug his back claws into the wool of my coat shoulder as I stepped up onto the slick basalt steps. "This is Eri. And the cat's Tim."

"Cats aren't against the rules," Hattie decided, watching Tim hop down onto the dry stone of the sea-wall and immediately begin sniffing a dead sand-flea near one of the chalk circles. "Don't let him step on the white line, though. Juniper gets really mad when the gulls smudge her letters, and when Juniper gets mad she cuts her thumb again, and we're almost out of clean rags."

Above us, the heavy plank door at the base of the black lighthouse swung open on greased iron straps.

Two women came out onto the stone courtyard behind the wall.

The first one was barely twenty, thin as a salt-marsh reed, wearing a huge knitted cardigan that had once been burgundy and was now bleached to the color of dried liver by brine and wind. The yarn at the elbows had rotted through, showing her bare skin underneath. Both of her forearms, from the hollows of her wrists all the way up into the frayed wool above her elbows, were covered in neat, parallel rows of tiny white burn scars, each one the exact size and shape of a candle flame pressed flat against the meat. Both of her thumbs were wrapped in bulky, grey-washed linen bandages spotted with fresh brown rust.

The second woman was younger, maybe seventeen or eighteen, dressed in black denim that had gone stiff with dried salt and chalk dust. A strip of stained linen was tied tightly around her left palm. Her dark hair was hacked off above her collar with a dull knife, and her eyes were deep-set and brown, ringed with purple hollows so dark they looked like thumbprints pressed into cold tallow.

What stopped me halfway up the jetty steps wasn't her eyes. It was her skin.

Every inch of her bare right arm, the back of her bandaged left hand, and the pale column of her throat above the collar of her black shirt was covered in dense, smudged columns of charcoal writing.

Some of the lines were fresh and black, written that morning in square capitals along the radius bone of her forearm. Some were three days old, faded to a greasy grey smear where sweat and sea spray had blurred the letters into the pores. As she walked down the courtyard flags toward us, she kept her right elbow turned slightly inward, reading her own wrist the way a clerk checks a bill of lading before opening a warehouse door.

*Look at her arm,* Kio whispered inside my head. Suddenly he was pressing right up against the back of my eyes, so close my vision blurred at the edges and my right hand twitched toward my coat pocket in his sharp, precise rhythm. *Ena. Look at the spacing on her wrist. She's indexing by day.*

The dark-haired girl stopped six feet from the top of the steps. She looked down at her right forearm, just below the bend of the elbow, where a thick black box had been drawn around three lines of charcoal print. Then she looked up at me, at my faded blonde hair blowing in the salt wind, and at my blackened left arm.

"Blonde hair," she read off her own skin, her voice low and raspy, like gravel turning over in an undertow. "Black left hand. One-eyed woman with a stick. Orange cat." She lowered her arm an inch, squinting at us. "Juniper wrote this on my table last night after the gulls came in from the west. You're the ones from the Spire. The prophecy girl."

"Ena," I said. "You're Raven."

Raven looked down at the inside of her left wrist, right above the grey bandage on her palm, where a single word was printed in thick charcoal block letters: RAVEN.

"Yeah," she said. She didn't smile. "That's what it says. Don't touch the chalk on the coping stone when you come over. The tide's turning, and the salt dampens the seal fast enough without people dragging their boots through it."

She stepped back to let Eri haul herself up onto the flat flagstones of the courtyard. The other woman, the one in the rotted cardigan with the bandaged thumbs, didn't step back. She stayed right by the parapet, staring at me with pale, fever-bright eyes that looked like they hadn't closed for longer than twenty minutes at a stretch in four years.

"You're the one who broke the glass," Juniper Vance said. Her voice was quick and jagged, the words tumbling over each other like somebody reading a thesis statement before the microphone gets cut off. "In the Nursery. I felt it from the bottom of the grain elevator in Halcyon when the big thread snapped. Everything went red for three minutes, and the whole town stopped smiling and sat down in the street and held their heads, and then the water came in over the county road." She held up her two bandaged thumbs, trembling slightly. "Nine hundred and fourteen times I burned her name to make that song stutter. Nine hundred and fourteen. And you just walked in and punched the tank."

"It wasn't a plan," I said quietly.

"Obviously it wasn't a plan," Juniper snapped, turning her head to spit over the sea-wall into the green chop. "If you'd had a plan, you wouldn't have drunk the whole thing down your own throat and turned the lights off on six worlds before everything washed into this big salt puddle. Do you know how long it took me to rebuild my string wall on wet stone with seagull feathers and crab twine? Half the receipts are soggy. You can't even read the dates from Switzerland anymore."

"Juniper," Raven said, without looking away from her own forearm. "Shut up. Bring the kettle out. If we don't eat before high water, the cellar grating starts rattling and nobody gets anything down."

We followed them inside the base of the lighthouse.

The ground-floor room was circular, twenty feet across, smelled of woodsmoke, boiled cabbage, wet wool, and tallow candles. A rusted cast-iron ship's stove sat in the center with its stovepipe crookedly jammed through a hole in the masonry wall. Around the curved black stone walls, somebody had hammered hundreds of square iron boat-nails into the mortar joints and strung tarred hemp twine from nail to nail in a massive, spiderweb tangle. Scraps of brown driftwood bark, dried fish skins, torn pages from a waterlogged Bible, and soggy index cards salvaged from a floating desk drawer hung from the strings by wooden clothespins, all pointing toward the center of the room.

In the center of the web, pinned to a split cedar shingle with an iron fishhook, was a water-stained photograph of a girl in a homecoming crown. Her face had been scratched out with a knife point until only the white paper fibers and the tin points of the crown showed.

Sitting on a wooden salt-barrel by the stove, peeling small grey potatoes into a tin bucket with a blunt paring knife, was a boy about seventeen.

He was tall and broad-shouldered, with dark brown skin and a faded blue hoodie whose sleeves were frayed to threads at the cuffs. When the door banged open, he flinched so hard he dropped the potato into the bucket with a wet splash. His left hand immediately came up and clamped around his own lower jaw, his fingers digging hard into the meat of his cheeks as if he were physically holding his mouth shut.

"Brad," Raven said, walking past him to set a fresh stick of white chalk on the shelf above the stove. "It's fine. They're not singing. It's the girl from the Spire."

Brad kept his hand clamped over his mouth for three full seconds, his chest heaving in a slow, counted breath through his nose. Then he forced his fingers open, one by one, lowering his left hand back to his knee while his right hand gripped the paring knife so tight his knuckles went ash-grey.

"H-hi," he said. His jaw locked on the vowel, trembling against an invisible groove in the air before he wrenched his chin sideways and bit the rest of the word off. "Sorry. I don't. I don't talk much when new people come in. My mouth still tries to do the. The old thing. If I don't hold it."

"You don't have to talk to us," Eri said, stumping over to an empty wooden crate by the warm stovepipe and easing herself down with a long, whistling grunt. She stretched her scarred left leg out toward the iron grate. "God knows I'm sick of everybody talking. You got anything in that pot besides cabbage?"

"Mussels," Hattie said, coming in behind us and hanging her cardboard crown carefully on a wooden peg by the door where the stove heat wouldn't warp the glue. "And three rock crabs from the morning low. Don't eat the little orange ones inside the mussel shells. Those are pea crabs. They taste like mud."

While Eri and Hattie argued over the iron pot on the stove, Raven sat down at a small driftwood table under the narrow lancet window. On the table lay a flat slab of smooth slate, three chunks of willow charcoal, a rag soaked in mutton tallow, and a heavy leather-bound Bible whose pages were swollen to twice their thickness from sea-damp.

Raven picked up a piece of charcoal, leaned her bandaged left hand flat on the table to steady the board, and began copying a line from the margin of the Bible onto the clear skin just above her right collarbone, twisting her chin to look down at her own chest as she wrote backward.

W-Y-N-N. O-C-T-O-B-E-R. B-A-R-N.

Behind my eyes, Kio didn't ask. He just stepped forward.

The switch wasn't violent the way it used to be when Harumi tore the door off its hinges in the ruins. It was a quiet click, like a brass latch dropping into its keeper. One second I was standing by the door with salt stiffening my knuckles; the next second I was sitting on the wooden bench two steps behind my own eyes, and Kio had our hands.

Our shoulders straightened an inch. Our chin tucked down. Kio walked across the flagstones with his neat, measured stride, pulled out the three-legged stool across from Raven's driftwood table, and sat down. He took the hemp ledger out of the oilskin pouch, laid it flat beside her Bible, and placed our charcoal stub parallel to the spine.

Raven stopped writing on her collarbone. Her dark, hollowed eyes moved from our face down to the open ledger on the table, where three weeks of Kio's square, tiny handwriting marched in ruled columns across the rough hemp paper.

"You write it down too," Raven said.

"Every four hours," Kio said. Our voice sounded different when he used it: flatter, clipped at the consonants, stripped of the shaky drag I always had in my throat. "And whenever the front changes. If we don't log the handoff before the next one comes up, Ena loses the afternoon and thinks she dropped a shard in her palm again. Or Harumi tells her we promised someone a fight we never agreed to."

Raven looked down at the inside of her right forearm. There was a square patch of pale skin near the wrist where the charcoal had been scrubbed off with tallow until the flesh was raw and red, and over the red patch someone had written in huge, jagged letters: STOP LOOKING FOR THE BARN. THE BARN IS UNDER EIGHTY FEET OF SALT WATER.

"The Tenant took the rest of the dirt when we fell through the gym floor in Westfield," Raven said quietly. She touched the raw red patch with one blackened fingertip. "When the world tore and we washed up on this rock, it couldn't stay in the stone. There's no dirt here. Just basalt and salt water. So before it went down into the deep trench off the shoals, it took back what it lent me. It didn't take it clean, the way Mark did after the first barn fight. Mark left static. A grey hole where his face was supposed to be. The Tenant just... chewed."

She turned her arm over, showing the back of her forearm. A list of six names was written there in descending order, each with a little word beside it in brackets.

MOM [COUCH, DRANK]
WYNN [SISTER, FOURTEEN, SHOES]
DEVON KRAUSE [SAINT AGNES, FOURTH FLOOR, MY FAULT]
BRAD [KITCHEN FLOOR, EYES]
JUNIPER [STRING, DON'T LET HER NEAR THE CELLAR ALONE]
MARK [OUTSIDE ON THE FLATS, DO NOT WIPE OFF]

"Every time I go to sleep," Raven said, her voice completely level, "something down in the trench still pulls on the thread through the rock. I wake up at five in the morning when the gulls start screaming, and for the first ten minutes I don't know where Kansas is. Three days ago I woke up and didn't know why my left hand was bandaged, so I unwrapped it and tried to draw a circle with the split palm, and bled all over the stove before Hattie woke up and read my arm to me."

She tapped the third line on the back of her wrist. DEVON KRAUSE [SAINT AGNES, FOURTH FLOOR, MY FAULT].

"That's the one that always fades first," she said. "My skin sweats it off in the night. Or maybe I rub it against the blanket while I'm asleep because I don't want to wake up and read it. Every morning I have to open the back of my grandmother's Bible, find the page where I wrote down what I did to him in the psych ward, and copy it back onto my arm before I let myself eat breakfast."

Kio looked at the line on her wrist. Then he turned three pages back in our hemp ledger and pointed one scarred finger at the bottom of a Tuesday column.

Written there, in Kio's heaviest, sharpest block print, underlined twice so the charcoal wouldn't rub out against the oilskin, was a single line:

DOV. NINETEEN. LAUNDROMAT ON FIFTH. SHARDS IN THE BLUE TIN. WE LIED TO MARGUERITE. DO NOT LET ENA FORGET WHICH ONES WERE DOGS.

Raven leaned across the driftwood table and read it. She didn't ask who Dov was. She didn't ask what the blue tin meant. She just looked at the double underline, then up into Kio's eyes, and gave one slow, stiff nod.

"If you don't write it down," Raven said, "the part of you that did it convinces you it was an accident."

"Or a sacrifice," Kio said. "Ena likes to call it a sacrifice when she gets tired enough. Harumi calls it winning. That's why I keep the book. Ink doesn't care how tired you are in the morning."

"Charcoal rubs off on the sheets," Raven said, picking up a sliver of mutton tallow and rubbing a protective smear of grease over the word WYNN on her collarbone so the damp air wouldn't blur it. "I thought about using a needle and lampblack. Tattooing it in so the trench can't pull it out while I'm under. Juniper offered to do it with her knife."

"Why didn't you?" Kio asked.

Raven looked down at DEVON KRAUSE on her wrist.

"Because if it's a tattoo, you stop reading it," she said. "It just becomes part of your arm, like a birthmark or an old scar. You look right past it while you're washing your hands. If you have to write it back on your own meat every single morning with your own hand before you get a cup of water, you have to mean it every day. You have to choose not to let yourself off the hook."

Behind the glass of our eyes, sitting on the bench in the dark with Harumi's chin resting lazily against my shoulder, I felt my throat close up.

"Come on," Juniper said from the open doorway, wrapping the rotted wool of her cardigan tight across her ribs as the grey wind blew mist into the room. She was looking straight at Kio. "You're not the sad one. You're the one who keeps the numbers. Come walk the wall with me before the tide hits the third step. Raven's hands shake too much in the afternoon to do the fine print around the drain holes."

Kio closed the ledger, tucked it back into the oilskin pouch, and stood up.

Outside, the sky over the Glass Shoals had gone the color of wet pewter. The tide was coming in fast across the drowned causeways; the porch rail of the submerged house we'd passed an hour ago was already under six feet of churning white water, and the waves were slapping hard against the iron cylinder of the Hattieville grain elevator with a hollow, booming clang that echoed across the lagoon like a cracked church bell.

Juniper walked along the stone parapet with her head down, carrying a canvas messenger bag dark with old bloodstains and salt crust. Every twelve paces she stopped by one of the upside-down mason jars, lifted the glass, checked the charcoal script on the butcher paper underneath, and weighted it back down.

Kio walked two paces behind her, his hands clasped behind our back to keep the wind from catching our coat.

"Nine hundred and fourteen," Kio said as Juniper knelt by a stone scupper where the sea spray was bubbling up through the wall.

Juniper didn't look up. She pulled a thick cylinder of white carpenter's chalk from her bag and began redrawing a washed-out triangle around the mouth of the drain hole. "Nine hundred and twenty-one now," she said. "Seven times since we washed up here. Whenever the fog gets thick and the water comes up into the cellar under the floor, she starts whispering through the grate, and Brad starts grabbing his face, and the old man out on the flats starts yelling louder. So I cut the thumb and I burn the paper on the stove lid."

She sat back on her heels, blowing chalk dust off her bandaged fingers. The wind pushed the frayed sleeves of her cardigan back to her elbows, laying bare the hundreds of tiny, regular white ovals marching up her flesh like tally marks on a cell wall.

"Everyone thinks I'm crazy," Juniper said. She didn't sound angry about it; she sounded like she was reciting the table of contents from a book she'd memorized four years ago in Ohio. "My roommate at Ohio State thought I was crazy. The detective in Cleveland after the ninth floor at Saint Columba's thought I was a serial killer. Even Hattie, when she locked me in that dark grain elevator for two weeks and slid crusted peanut butter sandwiches under the steel door, looked at me like I was the monster because I wanted to pull the strings and wake her mom up, even if waking her mom up killed her."

She turned her head and looked up at Kio. The salt wind whipped a wet strand of hair across her mouth; she didn't brush it away.

"You know how the song works," she said. "Calypso's song. Or the other thing, the little peeling one we've got locked in the cellar with her. Or whatever red furnace your girl Ena was carrying around before she blew the world out. It all works the same way. It looks for the soft spot. It looks for what you want so badly you'd die for it. A boy in a lemon tree. A dead brother. An apology from your mother. Another week without cancer pain. And once it finds what you want, it turns the wanting into a handle and steers you by it."

"Yes," Kio said. "That is accurate."

"So the only way to keep your own mouth," Juniper said, holding up her two bandaged, rust-spotted thumbs against the grey sky, "is to make sure that every single morning, before the voice wakes up, you give your body something it cannot possibly want. Nobody wants a knife in the ball of their thumb. Nobody wants a candle flame held against their wrist until the grease blisters. For thirty seconds, while the nerve is screaming, there is no room in your brain for wanting to be loved, or wanting to be special, or wanting your dead brother back. There's just the burn. And the voice hits the burn and slides right off like rain off oiled tin."

She looked at our blackened left forearm, where the skin was still fissured with grey tracks from the thirteen shards I'd pressed into my palm the night I killed Dov.

"She didn't burn herself to keep it out," Juniper said, nodding at our arm. "Your girl. She burned herself letting it in. So what did you use? To stay awake behind her eyes while she was glowing red on that highway?"

Kio looked out over the parapet at the churning water where the black roof of the drowned house had vanished completely under the surf.

"Lists," Kio said simply. "When Ena took the thirteen shards in the alley behind the roof, everything inside the head turned red and loud. Harumi took the mouth. The hum filled every room we had. It wanted to erase the math. It told Ena that forty-one days was the same as nine days if nobody found out. It told her that Dov was already dead when she got back to the laundromat."

Kio raised our right hand and touched the tip of our index finger to the bridge of our nose, right where the headache always lived when the three of us pressed too close to the front at once.

"I couldn't stop the hand," Kio said. "I don't have the muscles when Harumi is driving. All I had was the corner table in the back room. So while the red was loud, I counted bricks. Seven flights of stairs up to the roof: one hundred and twelve steps. Fourteen shards in the red-and-white mint tin; one used at eight in the morning; thirteen tipped into the left palm at seven-fifteen at night. Six hounds in the laundromat. One nineteen-year-old boy behind the third dryer. Twenty-two shards picked up off the tile afterward and put into a blue button tin. If you count the exact weight and number of every piece of glass, the hum cannot tell you it was a dream. Numbers don't hum."

Juniper stared at him for a long moment. Then, for the first time since we'd climbed out of the skiff, the tight, manic wire of her mouth relaxed by a fraction of an inch.

She reached into her bloodstained canvas bag, felt around at the bottom among the candle stubs and chalk butts, and pulled out a fresh, unburned stick of dense white gypsum chalk, wrapped in a twist of oiled brown paper to keep the salt damp out. She held it out to Kio.

"Take it," she said. "Charcoal washes off in salt water. That stuff's got tallow and lime ground into it. You write with that on stone or oilcloth, the tide can roll over it three times and you'll still be able to read the numbers when the water goes down."

Kio took the chalk, tested its weight between our thumb and first two fingers, and tucked it into the dry inner pocket of our coat beside the oilskin ledger. "Thank you, Juniper."

"Don't thank me," she muttered, turning back to the parapet and dropping to her knees by the next mason jar. "I still think your girl ruined four years of my research by eating the main subject without taking notes first. If you want to talk to the boy who actually knows what the song tastes like from the inside, you better send her back out before the tide covers the back causeway. He doesn't come inside the chalk line until dark, and his dad doesn't come inside at all."

She pointed her bandaged thumb toward the back side of the island, past the black masonry curve of the lighthouse tower, where a long spit of wet basalt and blue-black mussel beds stretched out into the rising eastern chop.

Inside our head, Kio stepped back from the glass, folded his hands neatly in his lap, and looked at me.

*Your turn, Ena,* he said gently. *Go talk to the boy.*



Chapter Eleven: The Boy With the Silver Bell

I came back to the front with the taste of salt fog in my mouth and Kio's chalk stick sitting heavy against my ribs.

For a second I stood by the eastern angle of the sea-wall, waiting for my balance to settle. Switching back from Kio always left my shoulders feeling strangely loose, like someone had been wearing my coat with better posture for an hour and handed it back warm. Down on the wet courtyard flags by the jetty, Tim had found a dried herring head under an overturned crab basket and was pinning it down with one orange paw, growling at the gulls from the back of his throat.

I stepped over the six-inch band of white chalk on the eastern coping stone, careful to keep my heels off Juniper's block letters, and walked down the narrow basalt steps onto the low-tide mussel flats.

The flats stretched out for sixty yards behind the lighthouse, a jagged apron of black volcanic rock carpeted thick with blue-black mussel clusters, green sea-lettuce, and shallow tide pools that reflected the pewter sky like broken mirrors. The smell out here was raw and cold: iodine, crushed shells, wet kelp, and the deep, metallic stink of low water.

Thirty yards out from the sea-wall, an old lapstrake rowboat with a stove-in bow lay upside down on a ridge of barnacled stone, wedged above the reach of the ordinary chop.

An old man in his late forties sat on the salt-bleached keel of the rowboat. He was wearing a faded blue mechanic's work shirt with a frayed oval patch over the left pocket where a name had once been stitched, and tied around his waist over his oil-stained trousers was a checked cotton kitchen apron bleached nearly white by sun and sea spray. He had a crooked chunk of grey driftwood braced against his thigh and was shaving long, curling pale ribbons off it with a short-bladed clasp knife, shaping what looked like the round breast and stubby bill of a wooden duck decoy.

Ten feet below him, where the last three basalt steps dropped straight into the rising green wash of the lagoon, a boy sat with his knees pulled up to his chest.

He looked about seventeen, though he was so thin his collarbones pushed up against the neck of his grey T-shirt like bird bones. Over the shirt he wore a brown leather jacket that had been soaked in salt water and dried in the wind so many times the cowhide was stiff as tree bark and rimed with white brine-crust along the seams. Both of his sleeves were pushed up to the elbows. Running up the pale undersides of his forearms, following the exact paths of the veins from wrist to crease, were branching tracks of faded, dark-blue scar tissue, like someone had injected blue ink under his skin and let it burn out from the inside.

Tied to the front left belt loop of his jeans with a piece of frayed tarred twine was a small, cracked silver shrine bell, the kind Pelagian fishermen hang from the bows of their dories to ward off fog-squalls. Someone had reached inside the bell with a pair of pliers and wrenched the iron clapper clean out by the root, so when the wind shifted his jacket hem against it, the silver rim tapped silently against his denim without making a sound.

As my boots crunched on the dead barnacle shells along the causeway, the man on the overturned rowboat looked up from his driftwood duck.

His face was weathered and lined, the face of a man who had spent twenty years working twelve-hour shifts under fluorescent lights and coming home to a six-pack in the refrigerator, except every hard line around his mouth had been ironed completely flat. His eyes were wide and soft, and around the outer rims of his brown irises ran a cloudy, pale blue ring that didn't move when he blinked.

"Morning!" he called out across the mussel beds. His voice was bright, loud, and cheerful, the exact pitch of a Saturday-morning television commercial echoing over the empty grey water. "Look at that sitting right there on the steps! That's my boy Mark! Love you, champ! Proud of you!"

Down on the wet basalt steps, the boy in the stiff leather jacket didn't turn around. His shoulders just pulled in half an inch closer to his ears.

A white gull wheeled low over the rowboat, tipping its wings in the salt wind. The man on the keel turned his smiling face up toward the bird, holding his half-carved driftwood duck out in one broad, calloused hand.

"That's my son down there!" Ray told the gull, nodding earnestly as his knife blade flashed in the dull light. "Smartest kid in Westfield! Always knew he was going to do something big! Love you, son! Proud of you!"

I walked past the rowboat, keeping five yards of wet kelp between me and the keel. Ray didn't look at me with curiosity or suspicion; his cloudy blue-ringed eyes slid right across my scarred black arm and my wool coat with the exact same flat, beaming warmth he'd just given the seagull.

"Morning!" Ray told me cheerfully, shaving another curl of grey wood onto his checked apron. "Fine day on the water! My boy Mark's right down there on the steps! Proud of him! Always been proud of him!"

"Morning," I said quietly, and walked down to the wet basalt steps.

The incoming tide was already lapping over the bottom step, dark green water swirling around the rubber soles of Mark's boots and floating the tendrils of rockweed that clung to the stone. I stopped on the third step up, wiped a patch of salt spray off the black basalt with my coat hem, and sat down four feet away from him.

Up close, you could see the dirt ground into the seams of his leather jacket, and another mark on his throat: a pale, rough ring of healed skin all the way around his neck and jaw where something wet and gritty, like roots and prairie sod, had closed over his head in the dark and squeezed before spitting him out into the water when the worlds broke.

For a full minute neither of us said anything. The water rose an inch over the bottom step, cold foam hissing into the cracks between the stones, then sucked back out with a low rattle of pebbles. Behind us, up on the rowboat keel, Ray began to whistle a flat, four-note tune between his teeth while his knife went *snick, snick, snick* against the driftwood.

"He does that for fourteen hours a day," Mark said. His voice was quiet and hoarse, scraped thin at the bottom like he'd spent a long time screaming underground and never got his full chest back. "Until Raven comes out with a lantern after dark and tells him it's bedtime. Then he walks inside, lies down on the pallet by the salt barrels, says it three more times to the ceiling, and shuts his eyes until sunrise."

"Brad said he tried to switch it to himself after your funeral," I said, looking out at the tilted iron cylinder of the Hattieville grain tower where the whitecaps were breaking across the submerged road. "Back in your town. Before the water came."

Mark let out a dry, humorless breath through his nose. He reached down with his right hand and touched the cracked, clapperless silver bell hanging from his belt loop, rolling the dead metal cylinder between his thumb and forefinger.

"Yeah," Mark said. "Brad told me when the sea washed us up on the reef three weeks ago. Actually, it took him two days to tell me, because every time he looked at my face his jaw locked up and he kept starting to say *we're still best friends*, and he had to punch himself in the chin until his teeth bled so he could get his own words out."

Mark looked down at his forearms, turning his wrists slightly so the pale grey light caught the faded dark-blue vein tracks branching under his skin.

"When the Tenant pulled me down through the crack in the gym floor," Mark said, "it didn't kill me. It wanted the song.
