Me and Calypso had just eaten four hundred and forty kids under the paper stars, and I was so full of blue I was shining through my jacket. The Tenant wrapped all those roots and elbows around me in the dark under Kansas and it just drank. It sucked every drop of those four hundred kids right back out through my pores. Took Devon's sorry. Took Brad's sentence. Took the little blue knot under my heart that I hid from Raven in the barn. By the time the highway tore open and the ocean came in and drowned the dirt, the Tenant was full, and it spat me up into the surf like a chewed cherry pit."

He tilted his head back slightly toward the overturned rowboat, where Ray was holding the driftwood decoy up at arm's length, squinting at its wooden bill with his big, empty grin.

"When we pulled Ray out of a floating garage roof two days later," Mark said softly, "Brad had him by the shirt collar so he wouldn't drown. And the second Ray saw me standing on the jetty, Brad's little push wore right off. Because Brad only had the brown ring at the bottom of the glass. I had the first batch. My voice cut the groove in my dad's head too deep. He looked right past Brad, walked up the steps dripping salt water in his kitchen apron, put his hand on the back of my neck, and said, *Morning, champ. Love you. Proud of you.*"

"Love you, son!" Ray called out immediately from the rowboat, catching the words on the wind like a dog catching its own name. "Proud of you! Smartest kid in town!"

Mark closed his eyes. His shoulders didn't shake; he just sat very still on the wet stone while the green water washed over the toes of his boots.

"Raven offered to try and pull it out of him," Mark said, opening his eyes again to stare at the froth swirling around his ankles. "With the chalk. She said she doesn't have the Tenant behind her anymore, and she doesn't have the glass vial because I stole it from her on Route 9 and lost it in the gym, so she'd have to do it raw. She said if she pulls a groove that deep out of a forty-six-year-old man who's been wearing it for months, it'll probably stroke him out right there on the floor. Like Juniper's cancer patients in Cleveland. Or if it doesn't kill him, he'll wake up on a rock in the middle of the ocean remembering the last real thing that happened between us."

"Which was him grabbing your arm in the kitchen," I said. "And throwing a beer can at the sink."

"Yeah," Mark said. "He'll wake up, and he'll look at me, and the blue will go out of his eyes, and he'll hate me worse than he ever hated me in his life. So I told Raven not to touch him. I told myself it was to keep him from having a stroke." He looked over at me, his brown eyes raw and hollow in the salt light. "You know why I really told her not to do it?"

*Because he still likes hearing it,* Harumi whispered inside my head. There was no mockery in her voice this time. She sounded almost gentle, leaning her elbows on the back of my bench in the dark. *Tell him, Ena. Don't make him sit out here and say it by himself.*

"Because even when you know it's fake," I said, looking down at my blackened left palm resting on my knee, "even when it sounds like a skipping record and makes you want to throw up every time he says it to a seagull, there's a half-second right when he starts the sentence where your chest still gets warm before your brain catches up."

Mark stared at me. His fingers stopped rolling the dead silver bell against his hip.

"Raven told me what you were," Mark said slowly. "She said you were the one from the prophecy. The girl with the red corruption who drank Calypso's tank in the vault and said the words on the guardrail."

"Day one," I said. "Yeah. That was me."

"We have meetings inside sometimes," Mark said, nodding his chin back toward the black stone tower of the lighthouse. "At night, when the tide's high and the two things in the cellar start banging on the iron grate. Juniper lights seven tallow candles around the stove, and Raven reads her arms out loud so she won't forget who she killed by morning, and Hattie sits on a salt barrel without her paper crown on and admits she liked making her sixth-grade math teacher cry, and Brad holds his jaw with both hands and tries to say five sentences that don't have the word *friends* in them."

He let out a short, ragged laugh that turned into a cough.

"It's like church for people who ruined everything," he said. "And you know the rule at those meetings? The one rule everybody in there agrees on, even Juniper and Hattie? You're supposed to talk about how horrible it was. You're supposed to say you were a monster, or you were tricked, or the song hijacked your brain, and every minute you had it was a nightmare you're so grateful to be awake from."

The tide surged up over the second step, cold green brine soaking through the bottoms of my trousers and filling my boots. Neither of us moved up a step. The cold felt clean against my ankles.

"I used to go to meetings before the highway," I told him. "In a ruined laundromat on Fifth Street, sitting on metal folding chairs in front of dead washing machines, drinking burnt barley and chicory out of styrofoam cups. Our sponsor was an old captain named Marguerite who used to breed glass hounds for Tombstone and had burn scars up both arms. Every Tuesday and Friday I'd stand up in my chair and say, *Hi, I'm Ena, I'm forty-one days clean,* and everybody would do that thin little clap, and I'd sit back down with a fresh black shard melted into my left palm under my sleeve, humming to myself while this nineteen-year-old kid named Dov looked at me like I was his hero."

Mark looked down at my left hand, where the grey-white fissures crisscrossed the black char of my skin.

"Why'd you keep putting it back in?" he asked, his voice dropping so low I could barely hear it over Ray's whistling and the slap of the waves. "After you already knew what it made you do to your brother?"

"Because when you don't have it," I said, and my voice didn't shake at all, because after you've ended six worlds and sat on a rock in the ocean, lying about it feels like too much work to lift, "you wake up on a wet mattress under a leaking blue tarp, and your body weighs three hundred pounds, and every single thing you ever ruined is sitting on your chest staring at you. You remember your brother's eyes turning blue right as the knife went in. You remember the look on Dov's face behind the third dryer when he saw the red light leaking out of my skin and realized I was lying to the whole group. You're just a small, dirty, shaking thing who got everybody killed."

I turned my left hand palm-up in my lap. Even now, bleached by Pelagian salt and scrubbed raw by Kio's accounting, the nerves under the black scar tissue gave a faint, phantom throb whenever I talked about the mint tin.

"And then you press a shard the size of a fingernail into your palm," I said softly. "It hisses like ice dropping into a hot skillet. And in three seconds, the three hundred pounds lifts right off your back. The cold goes away. The room stops being ugly. This huge, warm, bright red hum comes up through your arm into the base of your skull, and it doesn't just make the pain stop. It tells you that you're right. It tells you that weapons don't have to apologize for what they cut. When I tore those six glass hounds apart in the laundromat, Mark, I didn't feel like a relapse. I felt like a god walking through wet sugar."

Mark sat motionless beside me, the water swirling up to his shins now. His chest rose and fell once, ragged and deep, and when he looked at me again, his eyes were bright with water that wasn't sea spray.

"A backpack," he whispered.

"What?"

"A fifty-pound backpack," Mark said, his hands curling into tight fists on his knees so the blue vein-scars stretched white over the tendons. "That's what I called it when I tried to explain it to myself that first Saturday morning in my bedroom. My whole life before she said *Hi Mark* in B Hall, I was carrying this fifty-pound sack of wet gravel everywhere I went. Every time I walked past Devon Krause in the cafeteria and waited for my tray to get slapped out of my hands. Every time I sat in the library at lunch eating a squashed sandwich behind the reference shelves so nobody would see the bruise on my ribs where my dad's tallboy hit me the night before. Every single second of sixteen years, just flinching and waiting and being nobody."

He raised both hands in front of his face, staring at the faded blue tracks running down his wrists.

"And then I jumped off that trampoline into forty gallons of warm brown pudding, and she stood up on her chair and sang," Mark said. His voice broke right down the middle, raw and wet and terrible in the quiet of the tide flats. "And it went into my teeth and my spine, and the next morning I woke up and the ceiling of my room was breathing, in and out, slow and warm, and the backpack was gone. I didn't flinch once. I could hear the whole town wanting things through the floorboards like heat coming up from a radiator. When I walked into the cafeteria two weeks later with the piece I hid from Raven, and I looked at Devon Krause stepping on that freshman's hand, and I said *You want to say sorry to him, don't you, Devon?* and a two-hundred-pound senior dropped to his knees in the spaghetti and started crying because I told him to..."

Mark pressed the heels of his scarred palms hard against his eyes, his breath hitching in his throat.

"I told Brad in the bathroom it was justice," Mark choked out against his wrists. "I told him if somebody leaves a gun at your house, you don't just not use it. That was such bullshit, Ena. I didn't care about the freshman. I didn't even care about justice. When three hundred kids in that cafeteria went dead quiet and looked at me, not laughing at the pudding boy, not looking away from a bruise, just looking at me like I was the sun in the middle of the room, I closed my eyes and I breathed it in like air. And when I pinned Brad to my closet door the first Sunday, my best friend who gave me half his Lunchable in second grade, and I felt how easy it would be to crush him with one thought... I liked it. It tasted like being the one who throws the beer can."

Up on the overturned rowboat, Ray finished shaving the bill of the wooden duck, blew the sawdust off his checked apron, and looked down at the back of Mark's shaking head.

"Look at him down there!" Ray shouted happily to the empty grey sky. "Always working hard! Never gives up! Love you, champ! Proud of you!"

Mark lowered his hands from his wet face. He didn't wipe his cheeks. He looked down at the silent silver bell tied to his belt loop, picked it up between two fingers, and shook it hard.

The bell wagged back and forth in the salt wind. Zero sound came out of its cracked silver lip.

"I found this wedged in the timbers of the jetty the first day we washed up," Mark said, staring at the empty dark inside the bell where the iron clapper had been torn out. "It was ringing every time a wave hit the pilings. Just this clear, high little *ting, ting, ting*, right in the same key my locker used to hum when it wanted to open for me. I sat on the steps for three hours listening to it, and my mouth kept opening on its own, trying to match the note. Trying to see if there was one little drop of blue left down under my ribs if I just hummed hard enough."

He let the dead bell drop back against his wet jeans.

"So I took Brad's pliers out of the tackle box and I ripped the tongue out of it," Mark said. "And I tied it to my pants so every time I walk, I feel it tap against my leg and not make a sound. Because if I go inside that lighthouse and sit by the stove with Raven and Juniper and Brad, and I tell them the truth... if I tell Raven that after she fed Devon Krause to a hole in a hospital floor to save me, and after Brad spent eleven days locked behind his own eyes screaming for his mom because of me, I still sit out here at low tide and miss the way the ceiling breathed on Saturday morning... they'll look at me the exact way my dad would look at me if the blue came out of his eyes."

The water hit the third step then, a cold green swell that lifted the hem of my wool coat and soaked my thighs to the bone.

I didn't stand up. Instead, I reached across the two feet of wet basalt between us with my scarred, blackened left hand, and I laid my palm flat over the back of his cold right fist where his faded blue vein-tracks met the knuckles.

His hand flinched under mine, an old sixteen-year-old flinch, and then stayed still. His skin was freezing.

"I tore a nineteen-year-old boy into black glass behind a washing machine because I didn't want my best friend to find out I broke my forty-one-day chip," I told him, looking straight into his wet brown eyes. "And then I crawled around on the tile floor in the dark after his funeral and picked up the pieces of him and put them in a blue button tin behind my water tank, and I melted one of them into my hand that same night so I could go to sleep without shaking. And right now, sitting on this rock with you while my boots fill up with freezing salt water, Harumi is sitting in the back of my head remembering how warm that shard felt going in."

*It was a really good shard, baby,* Harumi said softly from the dark, without an ounce of shame. *We don't have to lie to the pudding boy. He gets it.*

Mark looked down at my black hand covering his blue-scarred wrist. For three or four seconds he just breathed, the ragged hitch in his chest slowly smoothing out into something tired and plain and human.

"How do you sit in the room with them?" Mark asked quietly. "With your friend with the one eye. Or with Brad. When you know you'd still reach for the tin if somebody left it open on the table in front of you?"

"I don't hold the book by myself anymore," I said. I tapped my breast pocket where the oilskin ledger and Juniper's tallow chalk sat dry against my ribs. "When my hand starts reaching for the tin, I give the front to Kio and let him count the bricks. And when Kio gets too tired to count, I tell Eri the truth before I have ten minutes alone to make up a better story. You don't get to stop wanting the hum, Mark. Nobody who ever had that much sky inside their mouth ever forgets what it tasted like. You just decide, five minutes at a time, not to put the clapper back in the bell."

Behind us, the heavy plank door at the base of the lighthouse creaked open against the wind.

Brad stepped out onto the top of the eastern sea-wall, carrying two tin mugs with thin grey steam curling off the rims. He stopped inside Juniper's chalk line, looking down over the parapet at the two of us sitting knee-deep in the rising green tide, and at Ray sitting on the rowboat keel ten feet above us still smiling at his wooden duck.

Brad stood there for a second with both hands full of hot mugs, so he couldn't grab his own jaw when his mouth opened. His chin trembled once, pulling hard to the left as his throat fought the old groove.

"T-tide's up to the fourth step," Brad called down, his voice cracking over the consonant and forcing its way through on sheer muscle. "Hattie boiled the. The crabs. Get your asses inside before the water gets in your pockets."

Mark looked up at Brad standing against the grey sky with the two tin mugs. Then he looked back at me, took a long breath of cold salt air, and pulled his legs up out of the wash.

"Come on," Mark said, reaching down with his blue-scarred hand to haul me up off the wet basalt. "If we don't eat Hattie's crabs while they're hot, she puts the paper crown back on and makes rules about who gets the claws."

We walked up the flooded steps together, my wet boots squelching beside his, the clapperless silver bell tapping silently against his hip with every step. As we passed the overturned rowboat, Mark reached out and gently took the clasp knife and the driftwood duck out of his father's hands.

"Time to go in, Dad," Mark said quietly. "Water's coming over the reef."

Ray stood up in his checked cotton apron, towering half a head above his thin son, and clapped one heavy mechanic's hand onto Mark's leather shoulder.

"You got it, champ!" Ray beamed, his cloudy blue-ringed eyes shining in the dusk. "Whatever you say! Love you! Proud of you!"

"Yeah," Mark said, not looking away from his father's flat, smiling face as he led him up the basalt steps and across the chalk line into the lantern light. "I know, Dad. Come eat some crab."



Chapter Twelve: The Jar on the Shelf

I waited until the kids were asleep around the stove before I took the lantern off the nail.

It wasn't hard to wait them out. Humans who spend all day hauling crab traps out of freezing surf and writing their sins on their own arms crash hard once the boiled shells are cleared away. By midnight the circular room at the base of the lighthouse sounded like a barracks after a forced march.

Brad was curled on a pile of dry sailcloth by the woodbox with his left hand still tucked defensively under his chin in his sleep. Ten feet away, Mark lay on his back with his salt-stiff leather jacket pulled over his chest like a blanket, while his father lay stretched out on the pallet beside him in his checked cotton apron, smiling straight up at the soot-blackened beams in the dark. Every twenty minutes or so Ray's mouth would open on an exhale and whisper, very softly, *Proud of you,* into the quiet room, and Mark's eyelids would twitch once and stay shut.

Hattie was asleep with her head on a coil of tarred net twine and the orange cat sprawled across her stomach, purring like a small rusty sawmill. Over by the driftwood table under the window, Juniper sat propped against the black stone wall beneath her forty-foot tangle of crab-twine and soggy index cards, her chin dropped onto her chest, her two bandaged thumbs twitching in her lap.

Only Ena and the goth girl with the charcoal arms were awake when I stood up from my crate and tucked my ironwood crutch under my left armpit.

Ena was sitting on the floor with her back against my crate, her knees drawn up, the oilskin ledger open across her thighs where Kio had left a note for the morning watch. She tilted her head back and looked up at me with those clear, exhausted eyes.

"You don't have to go down there alone," Ena whispered. "Harumi's awake. If the little one tries the eye thing through the bars..."

"The little one tried the eye thing on me when she was three days old and I was still dumb enough to feel sorry for a crying thing in a hallway," I said, keeping my voice low so I didn't wake the kid with the cardboard crown. I reached down and gave Ena's scarred left shoulder a single firm squeeze. "And Twenty-two already ate the only thing in my head worth chewing on. Stay by the stove, babe. You've had enough salt water in your boots for one day."

Across the room, Raven looked up from the slab of slate where she was sharpening a fresh piece of willow charcoal with a small paring knife. In the red glow from the stove grate, the block letters marching up her neck and arms looked like black iron bars holding her skin together.

"The tide's two hours off the top," Raven said quietly. "Water on the bottom floor's going to be right at your belt buckle. Don't take the lantern past the third step from the bottom, or the draft from the sea-grate will blow the wick out. And don't touch the iron bars with your bare skin. Juniper rubbed thumb-blood and tallow chalk into the rust this morning. If you smear it off, we have to listen to Twenty-two singing commercials until low water."

"I know how to keep my hands to myself around cages, kid," I said. "I used to work the Fourth Ring."

I lifted the heavy oak trapdoor set into the flagstones behind the stovepipe.

A wave of cold, rank air came up out of the hole right into my face. It smelled of dead low-tide mud, rusted iron, rotting kelp, and something else underneath that made the empty left socket behind my leather strap give a slow, dry throb: the sour, stagnant green smell of Nursery tank fluid that had never quite washed out of wet clothes.

I hooked two fingers through the brass ring of the whale-oil lantern, swung my ruined left leg over the granite lip of the hatch, and started down the steps.

There were fourteen steps, cut straight out of the black foundation basalt of the reef. They were slick with green sea-slime and wet chalk dust. I had to take them sideways, one at a time, planting the rubber-wrapped tip of my ironwood crutch on the step below, leaning my good right hip against the weeping stone wall, and dragging my stiff left shin down after it. Both of my shins had healed crooked after the Nursery babies dropped me in that drainage pipe outside the vault; every time cold damp hit the bone, the fractures ached like somebody had left an iron wedge driven between the fibula and the meat.

At the sixth step down, the sound of the stove above me vanished behind the curve of the masonry, replaced by the deep, rhythmic *slosh, thump, hiss* of the Pelagian swell pushing in through the submerged sea-arch at the base of the rock.

At the tenth step, my right boot went into the water.

It was freezing. The open chop out by the grain tower had been clean cold; this was cellar cold, thick with brine and trapped dark. I stopped on the eleventh step, where the black water swirled around my knees, and hooked the brass ring of the lantern over an iron spike driven into the mortar joint at shoulder height.

The yellow tallow flame pushed the dark back ten feet across the surface of the flooded cellar.

The room was a round stone cistern twenty feet wide, cut into the living rock underneath the lighthouse tower. On the far side, where an arched tunnel let the ocean in and out with every swell, a heavy portcullis of thick square iron bars had been wedged into the stone to keep the sea from washing the cellar out into the deep trench.

A second iron grating, made of salvaged jail bars and welded ship's railing painted thick with a paste of white tallow chalk and dried brown blood, split the cellar in half six feet in front of the bottom step.

Behind the bars, the stone floor sloped up slightly toward the back wall, forming a narrow ledge of black rock where the water at high tide only came up to a sitting person's waist.

There were two of them back there in the dark, sitting six feet apart in the cold slosh.

The one on the left was huddled in the corner where the iron bars met the curved stone wall, her knees pulled up out of the water onto a submerged ledge, her arms wrapped tightly around her shins.

She was humming.

It was a terrible sound. Not a siren song; there wasn't enough power left in it to make a minnow turn around. It was a flat, raspy, off-key croak, repeating the same four stumbling notes over and over while the sea water rose and fell against her ribs. Some old church song about a river and a far shore, sung by somebody who had never learned the words and couldn't carry the tune in a bucket.

Even in the dim yellow throw of the whale-oil lantern, I could see how badly her body was coming apart.

She wasn't wearing Wren's body anymore. Whatever happened when Ena said *Day one* on the guardrail and the six worlds tore down the middle and dumped their trash into Pelagia's ocean had knocked the parasite right out of the vet tech she stole in the parking garage and slammed her back into her original Nursery draft-mold.

And the draft-mold was three weeks past its expiration date.

Her skin was the color of wet newspaper left in a gutter, a pale, translucent teal-grey that was peeling off her shoulders and forearms in long, sodden ribbons that floated on the surface of the tide water around her waist before washing loose. Three of the fingers on her left hand had gone completely black and soft, curled inward against her palm like dead kelp pods. Her white hair hung in thin, patchy clumps over her face, showing pink, seam-scarred scalp underneath where whole handfuls had floated away on the water.

When the lantern light hit the water, she stopped humming the river song. Her head jerked up.

Both of her eyes were still glowing, the two blue replacements she'd grown in my skull after she made me cut my own eye out with my own hand in the Nursery hallway, except the blue had gone cloudy and filmed over like boiled fish eyes, leaking a faint, milky phosphorescence into the dark around her peeling cheeks.

"Wren?" she whispered. The voice was wet and whistling, catching on a throat whose cartilage was going soft. "Wren, is that you? Did you bring the gauze? My hands came unwrapped again in the water. I tried to keep them out of the salt, I swear I tried, I held them up for hours, and then I got tired..."

I didn't stay on the eleventh step. I stumped down the last three steps into the basin until the freezing black water rose up over my thighs and closed around my waist, soaking the heavy leather belt under my coat. I planted my ironwood crutch firmly on the drowned flagstones three feet from the chalk-smeared iron grating and looked through the bars at her.

"Not Wren," I said.

The thing in the corner flinched back so hard her peeling spine slammed against the wet basalt. A strip of grey skin two inches wide tore loose from her left shoulder and drifted out across the dark water toward the grating.

"Oh," Siren whispered. Her milky blue eyes widened, straining through the cataracts to make out the leather strap across my face and the ironwood crutch under my arm. "Oh. No. You. You're dead. You were on the floor of the vault with your legs broken when the blonde girl broke the world. I felt you go out."

"Almost," I said. "Turns out when you're at the very bottom of the floor already emptied out, you fall straight through the crack before the ceiling hits you. Look at you, parasite. Back in your own meat."

Siren looked down at her black, soft fingers floating in the lap of her torn shift. Her lower lip started to tremble, and right as a sob came up her whistling throat, her face twisted and something else came out instead: a high, hideous, giggling laugh that echoed off the wet stone dome of the cellar and made my stomach turn over.

"Stop laughing!" she screamed at her own mouth, slapping her good right hand over her peeling lips while tears leaked out of her cloudy blue eyes. "I'm not laughing! I hate when it does that! I'm scared, Eri, I'm so scared, look at my fingers, look at them, the middle one came off yesterday when the tide went out and it washed right through the sea-grate and a crab took it!"

She scrambled forward a foot on the submerged ledge, not touching the chalked iron bars, her ruined hands held up in front of her chest like a beggar showing her stumps.

"It's taking longer down here because of the salt," she babbled, the tears and the giggles fighting each other in her wet throat. "In the air it only takes three days to rot, the machines said three days, *sapient but not sentient*, remember? Remember the speakers in the ceiling? Down here the cold brine pickles it a little, like salt pork, so I keep not dying, I keep almost dying every night when the water comes up to my chin, and every time I close my eyes I can see the hallway! The grey hallway with the plastic chairs along the wall and the fluorescent light that buzzes and nobody ever calling your name! It's right behind the stone, Eri, I can see it through the wall whenever the lantern goes out!"

I stood in three feet of freezing salt water, leaning my weight on my crutch, and looked at her.

For weeks, back when I was locked under the floor of my own skull in the parking garage while she wore my skin and watched my fingers rot off one by one, I had practiced what I was going to do when I finally got my hands on her. I had promised her I would claw my way up through her throat and make her watch while I put her in the ground. I had imagined taking my old pliers from the cave on the Fourth Ring, reaching into that three-day-old patchwork brain of hers, and pulling out every stolen memory of Wren's lullaby and Pip's charcoal dinosaurs until there was nothing left inside her head except the grey hallway she was so terrified of.

I reached out with my mind now, just a millimeter, slipping past the chalked iron bars into the front of her peeling skull.

There was nothing in there to pull.

It wasn't even a whole mind. It was a handful of broken scraps from six different dead anti-gods Himo had boiled down in a vat and poured into a bad mold without checking the temperature first. An old soldier's terror of an operating table. A drowned sailor's memory of cold water. A stolen half-verse of a vet tech's church hymn about a river. And underneath all of it, filling ninety percent of the space between her ears, just a raw, blind, three-day-old infant's shriek: *I don't want to stop being here.*

I pulled my mind back out of her and let my hand drop to the crosspiece of my crutch.

"Where's Wren?" I asked her.

Siren went very quiet. She pulled her peeling knees back up to her chest, hiding her black fingers in the wet folds of her shift.

"When the sky broke," she whispered, "and the water knocked me out of her... she woke up in the waves. She found the little boy. Pip. They were holding onto a floating tire from the parking garage. She looked right at me floating next to her in my real skin, all grey and falling apart, and I tried to tell her *I held the door, remember, Wren, I held the door when the city fell,* and my voice didn't work anymore."

Siren's cloudy blue eyes stared down at the black water lapping against her chin.

"The blue was gone out of her eyes," Siren said softly. "She looked at me, and she knew I never held any door. She knew I'd been wearing her for two weeks while she screamed in the dark for her little brother. She didn't hit me with the wrench. She just pulled Pip closer to the tire, kicked her feet in the water, and swam away into the fog without saying a single word to me. And then the girl with the red string caught me in a crab net and dragged me in here."

She looked back up at me through the bars, shivering so hard her teeth clicked together.

"Are you going to push me through the door now?" she whispered. "Into the grey? Because I took your eye?"

I didn't answer her. Because six feet to her right, on the higher dry corner of the basalt ledge where the incoming tide only lapped at the heels of a pair of ruined white satin pumps, the second thing in the cellar made a small, scraping noise.

*Scrape. Scrape. Plink.*

I turned my head and angled my good right eye toward the far corner of the cage.

She looked about sixteen, if you didn't look too closely at the way the wet light sat on her skin.

She was wearing a pink tulle homecoming dress from Westfield High, except four weeks in a tidal sea-cellar had rotted the crinoline petticoats into grey strings and stiffened the pink satin bodice with a dirty white crust of dried sea salt. On her matted, brine-stiff blonde hair sat a five-pointed tin dime-store tiara that had been bent flat down the middle like somebody had stepped on it in an alley, tied under her chin with a piece of green fishing line so it wouldn't fall off when she shivered.

Sitting between her bare, blue-veined knees on the wet black stone was an empty, quart-sized glass mason jar with no lid.

In her right hand she held the broken half of an old mussel shell. She was using the sharp purple edge of the shell to scrape tiny white acorn barnacles off the wet basalt wall beside her hip, picking each crushed little crustacean off the rock with her thumbnail and dropping it into the empty glass jar.

*Plink.*

"That's mine," she whispered to the glass jar. Her voice was tiny, high, and thin, the size of a cricket rubbing its legs together at the bottom of an empty well. There was zero gold in it. There was zero blue. There wasn't even an echo. "Whatever. Next."

She scraped another barnacle off the wet stone, pinched its dead little shell between her thumb and forefinger, and dropped it into the jar.

*Plink.*

"Hi, Twenty-two," I said.

The girl in the rotted pink homecoming dress stopped scraping. Slowly, she turned her head on her thin neck and looked at me through the chalk-and-blood-smeared iron bars.

In the yellow light of the whale-oil lantern, her face was the plainest, smallest thing I had ever seen.

Back in the tank in the Nursery, and in every vision I'd ever caught of her across the worlds, Calypso's face had been hard to look at directly because there were four hundred other faces sitting behind it, forty thousand years of dead gods and Swiss girls and drowned sailors and high school gymnasiums all shining through her teeth like stage lights.

Now the stage lights were out.

When Ena broke the tank and drank the song, and when Ena said *Day one* on the guardrail and let Twenty-two eat the very last want in the dark at the end of everything, Twenty-two had gotten her wish. She had eaten the last slice. She had swallowed every candle puff in the universe, and the cat on the roof, and Himo's frozen little pea of regret, and Ena's tired wish to be told it wasn't her fault, and even the reader turning the last page.

And when you eat the end of everything until there is nobody left in the theater to watch you eat it, the meal has nowhere to go. The stomach splits open in the dark, and forty thousand years of stolen wants leak right back out into the sea, leaving nothing behind on the wet rocks except the container Himo built on a steel table before she ever put the first drop of hunger inside it.

Her eyes weren't blue anymore. They weren't even brown. They were the pale, watery grey of washed glass.

"Oh," Calypso said in her tiny, hollow jar-voice, tilting her crooked tin crown to squint at my leather eyepatch. "Hi, Nine. You got a stick. Cute. It's giving pirate. I'm honestly kind of obsessed for you."

She didn't smile when she said it. Her mouth just moved around the words out of forty thousand years of muscle memory, the way Ray's mouth up above moved around *Proud of you*, while her watery grey eyes stayed completely flat and cold.

"Look at you," I said, gripping the ironwood crutch as a swell rolled in through the sea-arch behind her, lifting the rotted pink tulle hem of her dress and floating it around her shins like dead jellyfish membrane. "The main character of every story that ever got told. Sitting in a hole under a rock eating barnacles out of a pickle jar."

"I'm not eating them, babe," Calypso said softly, tapping one broken fingernail against the side of the glass mason jar. "You can't eat barnacles. Barnacles don't want anything. They just stick to rocks and wait for water. If you put one in your mouth it just tastes like wet lime and dead crab shell. I tried on Tuesday. Very zero-stars. Do not recommend."

She looked back down at the jar between her knees, where maybe thirty crushed white barnacle shells sat in an inch of cloudy brine.

"I'm just putting them in here so they're in here," she whispered, scraping her thumb along the chipped glass rim of the jar. "Because otherwise the jar's empty. And when the jar's empty, it makes this sound when the wind blows through the sea-grate. This hollow little *whoooo* sound. Like the shelf."

My left hand tightened on the grip of my crutch until my knuckles popped.

"What shelf, Twenty-two?" I asked quietly.

Calypso didn't look up from the mason jar. Her thumb went round and round the glass rim, slow and mechanical, while next to her in the dark corner Siren started humming the four broken notes of Wren's river song again under her breath to keep from seeing the grey hallway behind the stone.

"The shelf above the sink in the first lab," Calypso said in her tiny cricket voice. "Before you. Or after you. Whatever. Numbers are boring. I was very small. I didn't have teeth yet, I was just a little grey lump of hunger in green water that wanted the light to stay on, and the jar was about this big, with a zinc screw-lid with holes punched in the top with an icepick so the gas could get out."

She lifted the broken mussel shell and scraped another white barnacle off the black basalt wall. *Scrape. Scrape. Plink.*

"She left me on the third shelf up, between number nineteen that kept eating its own fingers and twenty-one that only screamed when the lights went off," Calypso said. "And she sat at the steel table with her white coat on and her pen going *scratch, scratch, scratch* in that bark notebook of hers. For three hundred years. I didn't have eyes yet, so I couldn't see her, I could just taste the room through the icepick holes in the lid. I could taste the metal sink. I could taste nineteen's fingers. And I could taste the back of her coat. Cold. Neat. Humming like a refrigerator."

Calypso's thumb stopped moving on the rim of the glass jar.

"Every day for three hundred years," she whispered, "I made myself a little brighter inside the glass. I learned how to glow blue so it would reflect on the steel wall in front of her desk. I learned how to hum a little note that sounded just like the bird Chimera made her out of the dead star, the off-key one she kept in her pocket. I thought: if I hum loud enough, if I get bright enough, if I become the biggest, prettiest, most interesting thing in this whole room, she'll stop writing in the notebook and she'll turn her chair around and she'll look at my jar."

She dropped the broken mussel shell into the water between her feet. It sank onto the black stone without a sound.

"And you know what happened when I finally got big enough to crack the glass?" Calypso asked, lifting her pale grey eyes to look at me through the bars. "She didn't turn around. She didn't even stop writing the sentence she was on. She just reached up without looking, opened the chute at the back of the sink with her left hand, and swept the broken glass and me right down into the pipe that emptied into the first war. Like brushing eraser crumbs off a desk."

The tide surged in through the outer portcullis, a heavy green roller that lifted the water in the cellar up past my waist, soaking the bottom of my wool ribs and slapping cold foam against the blood-and-chalk paste on the iron bars. Behind the grating, the swell rose right up to Siren's chin in the low corner, making her gasp and tilt her peeling chin back against the stone, while on the higher ledge the water swirled over Calypso's knees and flooded the glass mason jar in her lap, floating the dead white barnacle shells up to the top like little bits of bone.

"I ate every god in ten thousand skies, Nine," Calypso said softly, watching the dead barnacles float out of her jar and drift away across the dark cellar water toward the iron bars. "I ate the whole hospital in Cleveland. I ate Halcyon. I ate four hundred kids under paper stars while a boy in pudding cried next to me. And then on the highway, right at the end, I ate yours."

She looked straight into my one good eye.

"I ate your *Sorry, Nine* right off the floor of the Nursery while your legs were broken," Calypso said. "Ten thousand years you spent growing that thing in your chest like a tree in a basement. And right after I ate yours, right before Mom went out on the road, I asked her if she wanted anything. And she gave me one tiny little pea of ice. *I would like to have turned around.* And I ate that too."

I stood there in the freezing dark with the salt water pushing against my chest, waiting for the rage to come up.

I waited for the red heat that had driven me for ten millennia: the thing that made me sell Lute's forty years of songs to Duke Vasselgrave for a fake tooth-key, the thing that made me carve Tim's body in half with a closing portal, the thing that made me crawl six hours on broken shins through the rubble of the Nursery to hand my best friend Ena a tank full of the end of the world just so I could make our mother bleed.

Nothing came up.

Not because Twenty-two had eaten my want on the vault floor and left me smiling and empty. The smile had worn off the first day Ena and I washed up on the Pelagian shingle and I had to drag myself out of the surf on two broken legs while an orange cat yowled at me from a piece of driftwood. My head was completely mine again. Every scar in it was right where I'd left it.

The rage didn't come up because I was looking through the rusted iron bars at the two monsters who had ruined my life: the three-day-old parasite who had scooped my eye out with my own fingers and worn my body like a stolen winter coat, and the forty-thousand-year-old cosmic hunger who had licked the last hope of my soul off a wet floor like spilled frosting.

And neither of them was a monster.

One of them was a rotting three-day-old mistake shivering in salt water, humming a stolen lullaby wrong in the dark because she had been poured out of a vat knowing what death was and didn't want to sit in an empty waiting room by herself.

And the other one was a small, cold thing in a dirty pink dress and a tin dime-store crown, holding an empty glass jar between her knees, who had eaten six entire universes trying to get a woman in a white coat to look up from her desk for five seconds.

And standing on this side of the bars, leaning on an ironwood stick in three feet of freezing bilge with one eye gone and Lute's blood still on my ledger, was Experiment Number Nine. Who had spent ten thousand years setting fire to everyone who ever trusted her for the exact same five seconds from the exact same woman.

Three broken jars from the same shelf, bobbing around in the basement of a drowned world while the woman who made us was already gone, having spent her very last breath wishing she'd turned around for somebody else entirely.

I let out a breath. In the cold air of the cellar it came out as a thin white puff of steam, and then it turned into a laugh. Not Siren's broken reflex-giggle. Just a low, dry bark from the bottom of my chest.

"What?" Calypso asked, frowning slightly under her crooked tin crown as she tipped the seawater out of her empty mason jar. "What's funny? I'm literally having a moment here. You're ruining the lighting."

"Nothing, Twenty-two," I said, reaching inside my wool coat above the waterline. My fingers brushed past the small black tooth-key I still wore on a string around my neck, found the dry inner pocket where I'd tucked one of Hattie's leftover boiled rock-crabs wrapped in a scrap of oilcloth, and pulled it out.

I reached through the gap between two chalk-smeared iron bars, careful not to brush Juniper's blood-paste with my sleeve, and tossed the wrapped crab onto the wet stone ledge between the two of them.

Siren flinched at the splash, then stared at the oilcloth bundle with huge, milky blue eyes. Calypso looked down at it, poked the red crab shell with one bare, blue-veined toe, and looked back up at me.

"What's that?" Calypso asked.

"Crab," I said. "Don't eat the little orange ones inside if there are any. The kid upstairs says they taste like mud."

Calypso stared at the crab, then at me. For a second, the forty-thousand-year-old homecoming queen habit tried to wake up behind her flat grey eyes; she tilted her chin, touched one hand to the bent points of her tin crown, and opened her mouth to say something cute.

Then her shoulders sagged half an inch under the salt-stiff pink straps of her dress, and she just picked up the oilcloth bundle with both hands, broke off one of the small side legs, and handed it across the wet rock to the peeling grey thing huddled in the corner.

Siren took the crab leg in her two good right fingers, her cloudy eyes fixed on my face in total, bewildered silence.

"Are you coming back down tomorrow?" Calypso asked in her tiny jar-voice, holding the rest of the crab in her lap beside the empty mason jar. "When the tide goes out?"

"No," I said, turning my back on the iron grating and planting the rubber tip of my crutch on the fourth step up. "We're sailing west with the morning ebb. Me and Ena have a ledger to finish."

"Nobody's going to watch us if you leave," Calypso said behind me, so small I almost lost the words in the slap of the waves against the outer bars. "The goth girl and the string girl don't even look through the grate when they change the chalk. They just look at their own arms."

I paused on the sixth step, one hand on the brass ring of the whale-oil lantern, my wet trousers draining salt water down the black basalt behind me. I looked back over my shoulder once at the two of them sitting in the dark behind the white-smeared bars: a peeling grey ghost and a girl in a bent tin crown, sharing a cold boiled crab in the rising tide.

"Good," I told her. "Maybe if nobody's watching you, Twenty-two, you can finally shut the fuck up and eat your dinner."

I lifted the lantern off the iron spike, hauled my crooked left leg up the remaining eight steps one at a time, and pulled the heavy oak trapdoor shut over the smell of the tank, dropping the iron bar into its bracket with a solid, final thud.



Chapter Thirteen: Eleven Thousand Threads

The ocean did not go out gently during the Spring Ebb. It peeled back from the shelf of Pelagia like skin coming off a scald, sucking down into the center of the world with a long, wet, grinding roar that lasted half the morning.

Where the gray swells had rolled thirty fathoms deep the night before, there was only the causeway: six miles of glistening black abyssal mud stretching out toward the smoking seam at the middle of the sea. The mud smelled of old iron, burst bladders of kelp, and dead things that had never seen the light until the water dropped away and left them twitching in the cold wind. Scattered across the flats were the ribs of drowned cities, rusted highway signposts carried down from older collapses, and the pale, bloated bellies of blind trench-eels gasping themselves dry in the potholes.

Ena walked with her boots tied tight around the ankles with strips of greased canvas, though the black silt still found its way inside, cold and slick between her toes.

Ahead of her, Eri moved at a stiff, swinging hitch. Her shins had healed crooked after the six gray ushers in the Nursery dropped her in the drainage pipe. Neither bone had ever been set right. When the air turned damp and cold off the trench, Eri walked by locking her left knee and dragging her right boot in a shallow half-circle through the slime, her jaw clamped so hard the muscle jumped below her ear. She had refused Ena's arm at the high-water mark, and she had refused it again a mile further out when she slipped on a tangle of bladder-wrack and went down on one elbow in the muck.

"Don't," Eri had snarled, before Ena could even reach down. Her single dark eye was fixed on the smoking horizon where the seam sent up a greasy yellow plume against the low clouds. The hollow socket on the other side of her nose was covered with a folded square of tarred linen tied behind her skull with twine. "I've got two feet. Use yours."

So Ena kept her hands to herself. She kept her left hand shoved deep into the pocket of her oilskin coat, where nobody could see the black glass under the nails or the way the gray ash-skin up to her elbow still steamed when the cold salt spray hit it.

Inside her head, the house was crowded and noisy, all the doors banging in the draft off the exposed sea floor.

*It smells like the sink drain when the pipes backed up at the church basement,* Sunny whimpered from somewhere behind Ena's ribs, her small hands pressed over her face. *And the fish are looking at us. Their eyes popped out when the water went away. Ena, tell them to stop looking.*

*They're dead, kid,* Kio said, his presence heavy and square against the back of Ena's neck, watching the ridges of black mud on either side of the causeway for anything moving on four legs. *Dead fish don't look at shit. Keep your eyes on the flats. Anything that sank when the highway came down could be dug into this silt waiting for the tide to turn.*

And lower down, warm and thick as warm gin in an empty stomach, Harumi shifted her weight and trailed her fingers along the inside of Ena's bad wrist. *Look at that smoke out there, baby. Smells like the old days, doesn't it? Smells like the floor under the big green jar. We're walking right back toward the tap, and you're pretending your throat isn't dry.*

"Shut up," Ena muttered under her breath, her teeth chattering from the wind and from the forty-ninth day of nothing in her veins except salt air and bad water. Her tongue felt huge and furry against the roof of her mouth. Behind her eyes, a thin, hot headache pulsed in time with the distant rumble of the trench. "All of you. Just give me five minutes."

"Didn't say a fucking word," Eri rasped over her shoulder, not stopping.

"Wasn't talking to you."

"Good. Save your breath. Mud's getting deeper past that ridge."

Halfway across the flats, three miles out from the tideline and three miles short of the smoking fault, the skeleton of a leviathan rose out of the black silt like the hull of a wrecked cathedral.

The beast had died before the water ever finished rising over Pelagia, or maybe it had fallen through from one of the drowned worlds when the Seam first cracked open. Its curving white ribs stood forty feet high, encrusted to the middle with barnacles that popped and hissed as they dried in the open air. Between the great arches of bone, someone had lashed driftwood planks, sheets of corrugated tin fished out of the abyss, and tarred sailcloth to make a high platform above the high-tide mark. Steep wooden ladders hung down from the platform into the mud, weighted at the bottom with net-bags full of basalt stones so the currents would not carry them off when the water came back.

A thin ribbon of gray peat smoke rose from a stovepipe poked through the ribs near the beast's breastbone.

Eri stopped at the foot of the ladder, breathing hard through her nose, gray sweat beading along her upper lip from the three-mile haul on her ruined shins. She looked up at the tarred platform, then back across the six-mile expanse of black grease behind them.

"Tide turns in four hours," Eri said, leaning her hip against the lowest bone arch to take the weight off her right leg. "We don't climb up into the high rocks before the water comes back, we're crab food. Why are we stopping at a squatters' rib?"

"Because Teodor's cliff is on the other side of the east channel," Ena said, looking up at the stovepipe. "And the east channel doesn't drain all the way unless the wind shifts south. Whoever lives up there knows where the footing holds around the sinkholes."

She reached for the wet hemp rung of the ladder with her right hand, keeping the left tucked against her ribs, and hauled herself up. Below her, Eri cursed under her breath, a bitter string of filth about knees and ladders and dead sea-cows, before grabbing the ropes and pulling herself up by her arms, one stiff, grunting rung at a time.

At the top of the ladder, a hatch in the driftwood planking stood open to a long room built inside the curve of the whale's spine.

It was warmer inside than out on the flats, smelling of burning peat moss, boiled bladder-kelp, and wet wool. Three women sat around a low table made from an old painted wooden door laid flat across two salt-barrels.

The door had been nailed to the barrels upside down, so that its faded house-paint faced the ceiling, scarred by boot-heels and hot kettle bottoms. Across the middle of the wood, a peeling red sticker had been sealed over with clear fish-glue so the damp would not take it off, and beneath the sticker, in thick black marker written by a child's heavy hand, were the words:

`KEEP OUT. THIS MEANS NOOR.`

A nineteen-year-old girl with dark curly hair tied back in a grease-stained rag was kneeling by the peat stove, pouring boiling brown water from a dented tin pot into three chipped mugs. She had a soft, round face that had gone hollow under the cheekbones since the worlds fell into the sea, and dark eyes that looked as if she had not slept all the way down into a dream in six months.

Across the upside-down door sat a young woman in a gray wool dress frayed at the cuffs, scraping dried salt out of the seams of a net with a bone needle. She had pale blonde hair tucked behind her ears and a thick, triple-folded strip of coarse gray salt-cloth tied tight across her eyes and knotted at the back of her skull. The cloth was stained dark at the temples where sweat and old tears had soaked into the weave and dried stiff.

And in the corner nearest the whale's curving seventh rib, sitting on an upturned crate with her knees drawn tight together and her hands locked in her lap, was a woman in a plain white blouse that had turned the color of old tallow from sea-fog and peat smoke.

On her knees, held under both palms as if someone might snatch it out of the room, sat a small square metal lunchbox. The green paint had rusted off the corners in scaly brown patches, leaving only the faded belly and yellow bulged eyes of a cartoon frog stamped into the tin lid.

When Ena's boots hit the planking, the woman with the frog lunchbox flinched so hard her shoulders struck the whalebone behind her. Her lips parted, and then she clamped them shut so violently that the skin around her mouth went white.

The blindfolded girl with the bone needle tilted her head toward the hatchway.

"Two of them," she said. Her voice had the bright, flat rhythm of a girl who had spent years talking to crowds from a balcony and had not yet figured out how to make it small enough for a room made of bones. "One drags her right foot. One smells like burnt copper and wet ash. Come all the way in or go back down the rope, ladies, you're letting the wind in on the stove."

Eri hauled herself through the hatch, rolled onto her good hip on the planks, and sat up with her back against a timber brace, panting through her teeth. Her hand went automatically to the tarred rag over her empty socket to make sure the knot had not slipped.

"Friendly fucking tollbooth," Eri muttered.

"It's not a tollbooth," the dark-haired girl by the stove said quietly. She did not look up from the mugs. She picked up a wooden spoon and stirred the steeping kelp leaves until the bitter, iodine steam rose to the rafters. "Nobody pays to cross the mud. You just drown if you don't watch the gray patches where the gas comes up."

Ena stood by the hatch, her oilskin dripping black silt onto the dry planks. She looked at the upside-down door serving as a table. She looked at the child's black marker under the dried fish-glue.

"Noor," Ena said.

The girl by the stove went very still. The wooden spoon stopped clinking against the tin pot. Slowly, Noor lifted her head and looked at Ena: at the hacked-off, dirty blonde hair, at the raw red burn-scars around her collarbones, at the left hand hidden inside the oilskin pocket.

"You know my name," Noor said. Her voice was flat and careful, the voice of someone walking across thin pond ice in the dark. "Were you on the news? Before the lights went out?"

"No," Ena said. "I was on the highway."

Noor's hand tightened on the handle of the tin pot until her knuckles went the color of lard. For three seconds nobody in the rib-house breathed. Outside, four miles away across the grease, a pocket of marsh gas blew through the silt with a low, wet thump, and the distant trench hissed like a kettle on an iron range.

"You're the one who sang it back," Noor said. She did not scream. She did not throw the boiling water. She simply looked down at the upside-down door between the salt-barrels, right at the black marker letters she had varnished with fish-glue after she carried the door out of the drift-wreckage on the beach. "The girl with the blonde hair. You took the song out of the radio and you made everything stop."

"Yes," Ena said.

*Oh, look at her,* Harumi murmured behind Ena's eyes, leaning forward inside the skull to get a better view through Ena's pupils. *She's one of Twenty-two's snacks. You can still smell the blue spit on her. Hollowed right out like a gourd and filled up with warm dishwater. Tell her, baby. Tell her what her little brother tasted like when you swallowed the tank.*

*Shut your mouth, Harumi,* Kio snapped inside the dark. *Don't give her the front, Ena. Keep your feet flat on the boards.*

Ena swallowed hard against the coppery bile rising in her throat. She took two steps toward the table and stopped, keeping six feet between herself and the three women.

"When the seam dropped everything down here," Ena said, her voice rough and scraped thin, "what she took out of you came loose. Didn't it?"

Noor set the tin pot down on the iron grate of the peat stove. Her hands were trembling so badly that the lid rattled like teeth in a cold wind.

"It came back in the water," Noor said softly. She sat down on a crate beside the upside-down door and laid her palm flat over the words `THIS MEANS NOOR`. "When the sky went dark in Pittsburgh, I had a cup of tea in my hand. Theo was asleep in the other room. I was happy. I didn't hurt anywhere. And then I woke up in the surf on the black rocks up there, coughing up sea-water, and I remembered the crosswalk on Forbes Avenue."

She pressed her thumb hard against the black marker letters, rubbing them as if trying to feel the grain of the wood underneath.

"I remembered all nine months of it at once," Noor whispered. "Like Juniper said I would. I remembered buying the bus tickets at three in the morning. I remembered the sound the bumper made when it hit his backpack. I remembered his sixth-grade math workbook with the fractions done wrong in pencil. And the worst part..." Her throat clicked as she swallowed. "The worst part wasn't even the car. The worst part was remembering that a girl came to my apartment with seven black candles and offered to give him back to me, and I told her no. I told my own mother no over baklava because I liked having a clean kitchen and a boyfriend who drew cats in the foam."

Noor looked up at Ena then, her dark eyes wide and wet and utterly dry of any excuse.

"I found his door floating in the kelp beds three days after we washed up," Noor said. "Just the door. Nothing else from Brookline came through. Not my mom. Not my dad. Not the lemon tree. Just the door I walked past so I wouldn't have to remember I had a little brother. So I nailed it here where I have to eat my dinner off it every night. Because the song's still in my ribs sometimes, when it gets quiet before the tide comes back. I can still feel how easy it would be to hum."

Behind Ena's breastbone, Harumi let out a low, shivering laugh that made Ena's own ribs ache.

*God, I like her,* Harumi purred, pressing her forehead against the back of Ena's eyes until Ena's vision blurred red at the edges. *Let me talk to her, Ena. You don't know what to say to a girl like that. You still want to be good. You still want to sit on your little folding chair and count days like a girl scout. She knows what the good stuff feels like. She knows you'd trade your own brother's bones for ten minutes of not hurting.*

Ena dug her right fingernails into her thigh through the stiff oilskin, hard enough to pinch the skin until it burned.

"You don't hum, though," Ena said to Noor. Her voice shook, pitched halfway between her own low rasp and Harumi's warm, wet contralto.

"No," Noor said, looking down at her chipped mug of bitter brown kelp-water. "I drink this shit instead. It tastes like rust and dead crab, and it burns going down, and as long as my mouth tastes like this, I know I didn't let her eat him twice."

In the corner by the seventh rib, the woman in the tallow-colored blouse shifted on her crate. The rusted tin lunchbox gave a tiny, hollow squeak under her palms.

She looked at Ena with wide, gray, exhausted eyes, then looked at the blindfolded girl across the table, then looked down at the cartoon frog between her thumbs. She opened her mouth, closed it, swallowed, and finally spoke in a whisper so thin and flat she had stripped every bit of warmth out of her own throat on purpose.

"Where are you going?" the woman asked.

Ena looked at her. "Ivy Calder."

Ivy flinched again, her chin tucking down into her collar. She did not nod. Nodding was too close to yes, and yes was the word that lived in the house like a loaded gun.

"She only asks questions now," said the blindfolded girl, setting her bone needle down on the upside-down door and reaching for one of the hot tin mugs by smell alone. Her fingers had thick white calluses on the pads and old flour ground permanently into the crescents of her nails, though there had been no wheat in Pelagia since the drowning. "Don't take it personal, corrupt girl. Ivy hasn't made a declarative sentence since we crawled out of the canyon. Have you, honey?"

Ivy kept her eyes on the frog lunchbox. "How far down the trench do you mean to go?" she whispered.

"All the way to the sluice under Teodor's rock," Ena said.

Across the room by the hatch, Eri let out a short, harsh bark of a laugh, stretching her crooked right leg out across the planks and rubbing the knotted shinbone through her wet trousers. "She thinks we're going down into the old vault before the water comes back. Because the green tank-slop is leaking out of the fissure and turning the trench into a soup."

Dolly Adore tilted her blindfolded face toward Eri's voice. Below the gray salt-cloth, her mouth twisted into something small and sharp and almost amused.

"You're the mind demon," Dolly said. "Juno told me about one of those. No, wait. That was War talking inside Pia's head. Different kind of passenger." She took a sip of the boiling kelp tea, winced at the foul brine of it, and set the mug back down on the red `KEEP OUT` sticker. "You've got a hole in your face and a hole in your head where somebody took your favorite toy away, don't you? I can smell it from here. Smells like my old waiting room."

Eri's hand came off her shin. Her single dark eye narrowed to a slit. "Keep talking, pink girl. Let's see how fast you can find the ladder with a rag over your face when I kick this crate out from under you."

"Oh, please," Dolly said, leaning her elbows on Sami Haddad's bedroom door. "I spent two years listening to forty thousand people tell me they'd walk into a furnace if I smiled at them, and then I spent eight months in a gray bakery getting yelled at by an old man with flour in his eyebrows because my crusts were too thick. You don't scare me, one-eye. Nothing scares me except taking this rag off and watching three more idiots fall in love with my eyelashes while we're all starving to death in a dead whale."

She reached up with one flour-scarred thumb and tugged the bottom edge of the salt-cloth down half an inch to scratch her cheek, never coming near the lid of her eye.

"Do you know what happened when the sea came up over the gray towns?" Dolly asked, to the room, to the whale ribs, to nobody. "The water was coming down the main street like a wall. Black water, full of broken glass and roof tiles. And this boy from the quarry, eighteen years old, had a rope tied around a chimney on the bakery roof. He could've pulled himself up. He had both hands on it. And then the wind blew my hair out of my hood and he looked down and saw my eyes in the lightning."

Dolly took another swallow of the bitter tea. Her throat worked.

"He let go of the rope," Dolly said. "To climb down and give me his coat. Because I looked cold. The water took him through the front window of the hardware store before he hit the third rung. He was still smiling at me under the water while the glass cut his throat open. So the first thing I did when I washed up on this mud-bar was tear the hem off my petticoat and tie it around my head until the knot bled. Nobody else gets to die because I have strawberry eyes."

In the corner, Ivy Calder's knuckles went white around the rusted edges of the frog lunchbox.

"Did anyone ask you to come down into the mud?" Ivy whispered to Ena. Her voice was flat as a ruler laid on stone. Not a shred of persuasion in it. No tilt of the head. No softening around the mouth. Every word was checked at the teeth before she let it out, to make sure it could not hook into anyone's chest. "Who told you that you had to go down into the sluice?"

Ena looked at the former Undersecretary for Public Calm. She remembered Ivy standing at the edge of Famine's black glass hollow in the red canyon, twenty thousand people standing silent and empty-eyed behind her on the path while Ivy shook and cried because one girl with a dragon book in her bag had walked out of her apartment.

"Nobody told me," Ena said. "The tank broke when I hit it. Now the bottom of the Nursery is wedged in the fault line under the cliff, and every time the water warms up around the vents, the green brine boils up and carries the black glass with it. We passed three hounds buried to their necks in the silt a mile back. Their eyes were still red. They were chewing their own forelegs off trying to get loose to reach the water."

Ivy looked down at the frog on the tin lid. With the tip of her right forefinger, very slowly, she traced the rusted outline of its yellow eye.

"Where is Juno?" Ena asked quietly.

Ivy's finger stopped. Her shoulders came up toward her ears, a small, involuntary hunch against a blow.

"Why would you ask me that?" Ivy whispered.

"She doesn't know," Dolly said from the table, not unkindly, though her voice stayed dry and hard as salt-tack. "None of us know where anybody went after the highway went dark. One minute Juno was in the back of the bakery arguing with Pia about whether dragons in books should have four legs or six, and then everyone set their bread paddles down on the flour troughs and stopped wanting the bread to finish baking. And when the water dropped us here, it was just me, and Ivy fifty yards down the tide-line holding that empty tin box she dug out of her old cupboard before the flood."

Ivy lifted her eyes. They were red-rimmed from the peat smoke, terrified, and fiercely, desperately plain.

"What if I called her?" Ivy asked, her voice cracking on the middle word so she had to stop and flatten it out again before she finished. "What if she is alive somewhere on the high islands, and she hears that I am out here on the mud, and she comes looking for me, and she only comes because some little piece of the string never came all the way out of her heart when she was six?"

The rib-house went quiet except for the hiss of the kettle on the peat block.

Ena felt the floor tilt under her boots.

It was not the whale shifting in the mud. It was the inside of her own skull pulling sideways, the way the black silt outside pulled at a boot heel when you stood in one spot too long.

*Listen to her,* Harumi whispered, so close to the front now that Ena's left hand twitched inside her oilskin pocket, the blackened nails scraping against the greased lining. *She's asking the same thing you ask every night when you wake up choking on your own spit, baby. What if there's no you under the string? What if Himo wrote the whole thing down before you were seven, and Tim only loved you because you were the shiny little blonde bait on the hook, and the minute the line goes slack there's nothing left in this skin but four broken voices fighting over a dead girl's coat?*

*Back off, Harumi,* Kio growled, though he sounded further away now, muffled behind a thick door while the hot copper taste flooded the back of Ena's tongue. *Ena. Look at Eri. Count the nails in the door.*

Ena tried to look at the nails in Sami Haddad's bedroom door. There were eight of them, square iron boat-nails driven through the hollow-core pine into the salt-barrels. Seven. Eight.

Her chest would not take a full breath. The air inside the whale ribs smelled too much like boiled greens and old salt water, and boiled greens and salt water smelled too much like the floor of the vault when she had gone down on her knees by the broken glass of the skyscraper tank and opened her mouth to the flood coming out of Calypso's throat.

She had not held one thread. She had held all of them. For twenty minutes on the old highway, with the gray ruins falling away to nothing on either side of the guardrail, every world in every story had been inside her ribs like ten thousand radio stations screaming on the same dial. She had felt Noor's hand let go of the teacup in Squirrel Hill. She had felt the orange cat with half an ear sitting on the wet mattress under the blue tarp on the roof, waiting for a door to open at the top of the stairs. She had felt Chimera's scars turn to gray powder between Himo's fingers.

And she had liked it.

That was the filthy, unforgivable truth that Harumi kept tucked under her tongue like a shard of glass from the mint tin. When Ena stood on that guardrail and opened her mouth and said *Day one*, she had not been thinking about saving Eri's broken legs or stopping the gods from folding solar systems in half. She had been so full of red heat and golden song that her skin felt ten miles wide, and the relief of letting the whole universe break at once had felt better than the first cut after six months dry, better than the red tide rising over Terra while her brother's blood cooled on her boots.

Before Ena could stop the switch, her posture loosened. Her shoulders dropped an inch. Her chin came up, and the corner of her chapped mouth hooked sideways in a slow, tired, wet smile that did not belong to Ena at all.

Across the room, Eri's head snapped up. Her single dark eye fixed instantly on Ena's mouth.

"Ah, fuck," Eri muttered, pushing her spine harder against the timber brace. "Not here. Put her back in the cellar."

Harumi did not look at Eri. She took Ena's left hand out of the oilskin pocket and let it hang loose at her side, the black glass fingers catching the orange glow of the peat stove. She walked two slow, unhurried steps to the crate across from Ivy Calder and sat down, resting her good elbow on the upside-down `KEEP OUT` sign and her chin in her palm.

"You're asking the wrong girl about strings, sweetheart," Harumi said. Her voice was lower than Ena's, silkier even through a throat raw from salt wind, with a lazy drag at the ends of the words like honey poured over broken bottles. "She doesn't know what's hers and what's the worm's. Never has. She's been crying about a piece of paper since she was in second grade, and the first time somebody gave her the match to burn the whole house down, she struck it on her own teeth and asked me to hold her hair back while she puked."

Ivy stared at her, pressing the frog lunchbox harder against her stomach. "Who are you?"

"I'm the part that doesn't lie at the church basement meetings," Harumi said pleasantly. She reached out with one blackened fingernail and tapped the tin edge of the frog's yellow eye. A tiny flake of brown rust fell onto Ivy's white skirt. "You didn't cut twenty thousand leashes because you wanted your little friend to have opinions about dragon books, Undersecretary. You cut them because she caught you. And now you sit out here in a dead fish biting your own tongue in half so nobody can blame you next time somebody agrees with you. Right?"

Ivy's face went the color of wet chalk. Her lips trembled, shaping a word, choking it back, shaping another.

"Stop it," Noor said from the stove, her knuckles white on the wooden spoon. "Leave her alone. She barely sleeps. When she talks in her sleep she wakes up and scratches her own throat until it bleeds so the words don't get out into the room."

Harumi turned her head and looked at the dark red fingernail gouges along the base of Ivy Calder's neck, half-hidden under the frayed collar of the tallow blouse. Five parallel scabs, crusted black, where Ivy had clawed at her own windpipe in the dark.

Harumi's smile softened into something horribly tender.

"Oh, baby," Harumi murmured, leaning across Sami Haddad's bedroom door. "We know all about that, don't we? Keeping a little tin box full of sharp things behind the water tank so you have somewhere to put the noise when your head gets too loud. Look at the four of us. One won't open her eyes, one won't use a period at the end of a sentence, one eats off her dead brother's door, and Ena's hiding in the back closet behind a seven-year-old and a grumpy bodyguard because she can't stand the way her own skin feels when the red goes out."

She turned her face toward the open hatchway, where the cold wind off the mudflats carried the faint, sweet, rotten-apple stink of boiling green tank-fluid from three miles down the trench. Her nostrils flared, drawing it in deep.

"You can smell it from here, can't you?" Harumi whispered, her eyelids fluttering half-shut as a shiver ran all the way down Ena's spine and made her toes curl inside her muddy boots. "Mommy's basement broke open at the bottom of the sea. All that forty-thousand-year soup bubbling up through the rocks. You could take one sip, Undersecretary, and nobody would ever leave your apartment again. You could take one sip, Noor, and you'd be right back on the mattress in Pittsburgh with the cat foam in your cup and no little voice calling your name from the bottom of the well."

"Harumi," Eri said from the floor. Her voice was flat and cold as an iron bar. "Shut your fucking trap or I swear to Christ I will crawl inside your frontal lobe and play your father's kitchen floor on loop until you throw up your own stomach lining."

Harumi turned her head toward Eri on the planks. The red spark flickered deep in the centers of Ena's pupils, small as a dying coal in wet ash, then guttered out because there was nothing left in the blood to feed it.

"You wouldn't do that, Nine," Harumi said softly, though the lazy silk had gone out of her throat, leaving only a thin, brittle tremor. "Your legs hurt too much. And besides, Twenty-two licked all the teeth out of your mouth before the lights went out. We're both running on empty."

In the corner, Ivy Calder lifted one hand from the frog lunchbox.

Her fingers were shaking so hard they knocked against the tin lid with a hollow rattle. She did not reach for Harumi's black left hand. She reached across the upside-down door and touched the back of Ena's clean right wrist, where a pale, raised white line from a shard of brown bottle glass crossed the blue vein below the thumb.

Ivy's skin was freezing cold and smelled of salt-grease and peat.

"Does she hate you?" Ivy whispered.

Harumi blinked. The smirk froze on her chapped mouth.

"What?" Harumi said.

"The girl inside," Ivy whispered, her gray eyes fixed on Harumi's face with terrible, starving intensity. "When you hold the threads for her because she is too frightened to hold them herself. When you do the ugly thing so she can stay alive in the dark. Once the noise comes back, and she wakes up and sees what is in your hands... does she hate you for it?"

Harumi did not answer.

Inside the head, the loud, drafty room went dead silent. Even Kio stopped pacing behind the ribs. Even Sunny stopped crying by the cellar door.

Because nobody had ever asked Harumi that.

At the church basement meetings, when Ena talked about the voice in her head that wanted the glass or the red heat, the people on the folding chairs always nodded with tight, sympathetic mouths and talked about *fighting the urge* and *starving the monster*. Nobody ever asked the monster what it had been like to stand in a kitchen at seven years old while two people turned into red-eyed ash next to a plate of burned pancakes, and realize that if somebody did not grow teeth right then, in that second, the little girl in the yellow pajamas was going to lie down on the linoleum and never get up again.

Harumi looked down at Ivy's cold fingers resting on the white bottle-glass scar on Ena's right wrist.

"Every single morning," Harumi said, very quietly. "Like I broke into her house."

Ivy closed her eyes. A single tear squeezed out from under her pale lashes, ran down the crease beside her mouth, and dropped onto the rusted frog's back. She nodded once, very small, to herself.

And in the silence that followed, the floor of the skull shifted again. The warm gin-haze drained back down into the dark behind the breastbone, leaving Ena sitting at the upside-down door with her throat raw, her right wrist cold under a stranger's fingers, and her knees shaking under the table.

Ena let out a long, ragged breath that shuddered all the way up from her heels. She pulled her black left hand back into her lap and covered it with her oilskin hem.

"I don't hate her," Ena whispered, her own voice back, small and cracked and exhausted. She looked at Ivy, and then down at her own covered hand. "I'm just scared that when we get down to the bottom of the cliff, where the green water is, I won't know where I stop and she starts."

Ivy slowly drew her cold fingers back from Ena's wrist and laid both palms flat over the frog lunchbox again.

"How can anyone know," Ivy whispered to the tin lid, "until you stand next to the glass and see which hand reaches out?"

By the hatchway, Eri dragged her crooked leg in and braced both hands against the timber post to haul herself upright. Bone ground against bone in her right shin with a dull, wet click that made Noor wince by the stove.

"Two hours till the turn," Eri said, wiping cold sweat off her forehead with the back of her sleeve. "Drink the fucking kelp water if you want it, Ena. The mud doesn't wait for therapy."

Noor picked up the two remaining chipped mugs from the stove grate and carried them to the upside-down door. She set one in front of Ena and held the other out toward Eri, keeping her eyes carefully on the tarred cloth over Eri's socket rather than the crooked shins below.

"Stay on the gray shell-ridges past the third mile-post," Noor said quietly. "Don't walk where the silt looks smooth like butter. There's a hole there where an apartment house from some city went down roof-first, and the eels live in the stairwells. Follow the whale-vertebrae markers up to the basalt steps. The old man who doesn't talk keeps a fire burning on the top shelf. You can see the smoke once you clear the sulfur fog."

Ena picked up the hot tin mug with her good hand. The tea smelled awful, like low tide in a rust bucket, and burned her chapped lips on the first swallow. She drank all of it anyway, right down to the gritty black kelp dregs at the bottom, because Noor was watching her with Sami's dark eyes, and because the bitter salt-iron taste scraped the sweet green tank-smell out of the back of her throat.

"Thank you," Ena said, setting the empty mug down on the unpainted pine beside the `KEEP OUT` sticker.

Dolly Adore lifted her chin behind her triple-folded blindfold, her flour-rough fingers finding the bone needle on the wood again.

"If you see any of the old ones up there by the copyist's fire," Dolly said brightly, "tell them the pink girl is living in a dead fish with an ex-bureaucrat and a barista, and we still haven't drowned yet. They love hearing that mortals are tough. Makes them feel less bad about dropping the sky on our heads."

Ena looked once more at Ivy Calder in the corner. Ivy did not look up from the rusted frog, and she did not say goodbye, because goodbye had too many hooks in it. She only tapped her thumb once, very lightly, against the metal clasp of Juno Pell's six-year-old lunchbox.
