Ena turned and went down the wet hemp ladder into the black mud, with Eri descending heavy and stiff behind her, toward the yellow smoke at the center of the world.


Chapter Fourteen: The Copyist's Fire

The basalt cliff rose three hundred feet out of the smoking trench like a black tooth broken off at the gum.

Cut into its seaward face was a narrow staircase of wet volcanic stone, no wider than a coffin lid, slick with yellow sulfur crust and green weed that had died when the ocean sucked down into the fault. Halfway up the stairs, the air grew hot and foul. Below them, in the split black belly of the abyssal trench, the drowned ruins of the old highway and the shattered steel ribs of the Nursery vault lay wedged between two grinding shelves of rock. Great lazy bubbles of luminescent green tank-brine, the size of wagon wheels, swelled up through the black sludge at the bottom of the gorge and burst with slow, wet slaps, sending up clouds of steam that smelled of copper pennies, pickled meat, and forty thousand years of dead gods cooked down into soup.

Eri went up the stairs on her hands and knees for the last hundred feet.

She hated doing it. Every time her broken right shin scraped across the sharp edge of a basalt riser, a white line of spit showed between her teeth and her single eye went flat with fury, but her crooked legs would not hold her weight on the grease-slick stone. Ena climbed two steps behind her, keeping her body between Eri and the three-hundred-foot drop into the boiling green fissure, never touching her, never offering a hand, just standing solid on the wet rock whenever Eri had to stop and lean her forehead against the step above to breathe through the nausea.

At the top of the cliff, the sulfur smoke parted in a steady cold draft that did not come from Pelagia's sky.

The shelf of rock at the summit was flat and wide, barren of kelp or sea-silt and covered instead in sparse, tough gray grass that grew out of dry dust the color of very old paper. In the middle of the grass stood a small round hut built of unmortared fieldstones, with a weathered wooden door and a single window looking out over the end of the world.

Before the door burned a fire of pale gray driftwood. The wood did not turn to ash as it burned; the orange flames licked steadily around the salt-crusted branches without consuming them, throwing out a dry, quiet heat that smelled of cedar shelves and old bindings.

Four people sat on flat stones around the fire.

Nearest the door sat an old man in a threadbare gray coat with ink stains ground so deep into the wool cuffs that they looked like birthmarks. His hair was thin and white, his face lined like a dry riverbed, and across his knees lay a flat square of dark roofing slate and a piece of white tallow-chalk.

To his left, sitting so close together on a single wide boulder that their shoulders and hips pressed flat along one side from collarbone to knee, were two women.

One was very small and dark, wearing a plain tunic of dull gray leather, with a pale scar cutting through her left eyebrow and gray eyes that held no light at all, not even the reflection of the driftwood fire. The other was taller, though she had gone terribly thin since the old days, her cheekbones sharp under dark skin, and wrapped around the base of her throat was a stiff, crusted bandage of gray linen where an ancient puncture wound still wept a thin, dry shadow when she turned her head. Every few minutes, the thin woman would shiver in the damp draft off the trench, and the small dark woman with the scarred eyebrow would reach over without looking and pull the corner of a shared wool blanket higher around her neck.

And on the fourth stone, across the fire from the two of them, sat a tall blonde woman in a dirt-stained shirt with the sleeves rolled up to the elbows, her forearms scarred by rose-briars and framing nails. She had no white coat, no golden megaphone, and no horse. On her head, tilted slightly crooked over one ear and tied together at the back with a bit of brown parcel twine where the paper had split in the damp, sat a small yellow crown cut into uneven points, covered in smudged stars drawn in purple crayon.

When Ena hauled herself over the top lip of the basalt stair and stood waiting while Eri dragged her right leg up onto the gray grass and collapsed onto a dry rock by the cliff edge, nobody around the fire stood up. Nobody drew a weapon.

The tall blonde woman adjusted the yellow paper crown on her head with two scarred fingers so the salt breeze would not catch its bent front point.

"Well," said Conquest. Her voice was still large enough to carry across an army, though she kept it low, rough and plain as a carpenter's rasp. "You look like shit, little sister."

Ena stood at the edge of the gray grass, her chest heaving, the wet hem of her oilskin dripping black trench-mud onto the pale dust. She looked at Conquest, then across the fire at the small dark woman with the scarred eyebrow, and then at the old man with the slate on his knees.

"Teodor," Ena said.

The old copyist looked up at her. His eyes were pale blue, watery with great age, and filled with a quiet, bottomless sorrow that had no fear in it at all. He had seen her at seven years old on a street in a gray city on Terra, laughing at something her twelve-year-old brother had said while people across the road stared at her hair and whispered about a knife. He had seen her when the red tide took her eyes and Tombstone stood behind her shoulder. He saw her now, with her hair hacked off ragged around her ears, her left arm black glass to the elbow, and forty-nine days of shaking sobriety holding her upright like splints on a broken bone.

Teodor lifted the piece of tallow-chalk and wrote on the dark slate in a small, neat, steady hand that had copied ten thousand forgotten books before the stars went out. He turned the slate around so she could read it.

`Welcome in. You are shaking. Come by the fire.`

"I'm fine where I am," Ena said, her teeth clicking together on the words. Over by the cliff edge, Eri had pulled her knees up against her chest and was pressing the heel of her palm hard against her crooked right shin, her single dark eye glaring across the grass at Kaina and Cosmi.

"She's not going to sit down with you lot," Eri spat, her voice raw from the sulfur steam. "Look at this fucking campfire. The butcher who shot the hole in the sky, the space-folder who leaked the black shit out of her neck, a Horseman wearing a kindergartener's arts-and-crafts project, and the librarian who published the hit piece. All that's missing is my mother with a clipboard."

The thin woman with the bandage around her throat flinched. Her hand went up to the gray linen at her collarbone, her fingers pressing hard against the spot where Kaina's small, precise ending had struck her ten thousand years ago at the Seam.

Beside her, Kaina did not flinch. The anti-god of endings looked at Eri with her flat gray eyes.

"Your mother isn't coming," Kaina said. Her voice was small and dry and completely steady. "I watched her ending on the road from up here. Before the light went out. It was very small. The greedy little one with the bent crown ate it right out of her mouth, and then there was only an empty white coat on the asphalt."

Eri went rigid on her rock. Her single eye did not blink.

"She's dead," Eri said.

"Not dead," Kaina said simply. "Ended. There's a difference. A dead thing leaves bones in the dirt or a ghost in the water. An ended thing just stops having a next part. Your mother has no next part, Nine. Neither does the scarred one who held her hand on the guardrail. Neither does the hungry little queen who ate the last crumb and sat down alone in the dark with nobody left to watch her."

Eri stared at the driftwood fire. Her hand slid slowly off her broken shin and dropped into the gray grass beside her boot.

For ten thousand years, at the bottom of everything Eri had ever done, under the pearl shop by the river in hell, under the lies she had fed Ena to keep her burning, under the six hours of dragging her shattered legs through a drainage pipe on her elbows while her teeth bled, there had been one fixed picture: a woman in a white lab coat standing at the edge of a pit, and the moment when that woman would finally turn her head all the way around and look down and say *Sorry, Nine.*

Calypso had eaten the want out of Eri on the vault floor, leaving a flat, cold puddle where the tree had grown. Now Kaina had just told her that the woman in the white coat did not even exist in the dark anymore to know that the puddle was there.

Eri let out a sound that was half a laugh and half a dry retch, and leaned her head back against the black basalt boulder, staring up at the pale paper-colored mist above the cliff.

"Good," Eri whispered to the empty sky. "Fucking good. Saved me a trip."

Cosmi looked across the flames at Ena. When Cosmi spoke, her voice had a faint, dry rattle in it from the old scar in her throat, though the corners of her mouth still held the ghost of the startled, bright laugh she had carried before the first war.

"How did anything fall down here at all?" Ena asked, looking at the three ancient things by the fire. "I said the words on the guardrail. I felt the lights go out. I felt Pittsburgh stop. I felt my cat on the roof stop waiting for me. Twenty-two ate it out of my face until there was nothing left."

Teodor wiped his slate clean with the sleeve of his ink-stained coat and wrote again, his chalk tapping softly in the quiet. He held it up.

`A margin sits outside the page. When a page is burned, or eaten, or closed, whatever was dropped in the margin stays on the table.`

"Teodor's hill has been sitting off the edge of the story since the Prophecist built this hut," Conquest said, reaching down to poke a salt-crusted branch back into the flames with a dry stick. "When Kaina pulled the Seam shut ten thousand years ago, the rubble fell down into the crack between the worlds and made the Abyss. And when you and that hungry little monster on the highway turned out the lights on all the told worlds, everything that had already fallen through the cracks, or got washed off the edges before the song finished, slid down the drain into the bottom of the margin. One big cold puddle at the edge of the desk. Your brother's ocean, your mother's basement vault, sixty people from my town who were standing in my schoolhouse when the ground gave way, and every poor bastard who washed up on the rocks before the door shut."

Conquest looked up from the fire, her sharp blue eyes meeting Ena's under the crooked rim of the yellow paper crown.

"Nell didn't come through," Conquest said quietly. "The girl who gave me this. I went back down into the mud three times during the first ebb, looking for the schoolhouse roof. Half the town came through on the north island. The old man with the cane. The two carpenters. Not her. The song got her while she was holding the chalk at the blackboard."

Conquest touched the brown parcel twine holding the back of her paper crown together.

"So I come up here when the tide goes out," the first Horseman said, "and I sit by the old man's fire and make sure the sulfur damp doesn't rot the paper. Because there's nobody left in ten thousand worlds to give me another one."

Teodor set his slate down on the grass beside his boots.

He reached inside the breast of his threadbare copyist's coat, where the wool had kept things dry against his ribs through the rising of the sea, and drew out two objects.

The first was a small, thick book bound in brown leather that had warped and stiffened from salt spray. The pages inside were brown bark-paper, brittle at the corners, covered in small, cold, slanting handwriting in dark sap-ink that had gone the color of dried blood.

The second was a single square leaf of thin gray material, older than paper, older than leather, folded into quarters so many times over ten thousand years that the creases were held together only by a backing of clear library linen that Teodor had pasted behind it centuries ago with his own careful hands.

Teodor rose from his stone, walked across the gray grass with the slow, stiff gait of a very old librarian, and held both objects out to Ena.

Ena did not take her left hand out of her pocket. She looked down at the brown leather notebook and the folded gray leaf in Teodor's ink-stained palms, and a cold sweat broke out across the back of her neck, wetter and colder than the fog coming off the abyssal trench.

*Don't touch it,* Sunny cried out inside her head, scrabbling backward into the dark behind Ena's eyes like a child trying to crawl under a bed. *That's the lady's book! The lady who came through the kitchen window when Daddy was making the batter! Ena, don't touch it, it has the gray dust on it!*

*Take it,* Kio said, his voice tight and hard. *Know what's on the paper. You can't fight a setup you haven't read.*

Ena reached out with her clean right hand. Her fingers were trembling so violently that when she took the brown leather notebook and the folded gray sheet from Teodor, the dry bark-pages rustled against each other like dead beetles in a wall.

"Where did you get her book?" Ena whispered.

Teodor took his slate from the grass, balanced it on his forearm, and wrote three words.

`The highway fell.`

"When the road broke under the trench," Kaina said from her boulder beside Cosmi, "the white coat and the notebook came down through the seam with the rest of the concrete. The water hadn't come in over the fault yet. Teodor walked down the stairs before the first high tide and picked it out of the mud."

Ena stood by the driftwood fire with the grey bark leaf tucked under her thumb and opened Himo's four hundred and twelfth notebook with one hand.

The stiff leather cover cracked. The pages smelled of dead tree sap, cold iron, and the dry, sterile wool of Himo's coat when Ena had driven five seconds of stolen time and a red blade into the center of her chest in the Nursery.

She did not start at the front. The book fell open where the spine had been broken and folded back on the highway asphalt before the end: to the middle pages, where Himo had copied forward her own foundational logs from the older books so she could review the data while she waited on the guardrail.

Ena looked down at the dried-blood ink.

`LOG 1,181. Observation, small world, sector 4, low-density residential street. Two-story frame house, peeling yellow paint on the south eaves. Kitchen window faces an alley behind a greengrocer. Sightline unobstructed from the ash-tree by the fence.`

`Subjects: Male parent, thirty-four, carpenter, bad left knee from a scaffold fall, burns the first two pancakes of every batch because he turns the iron skillet too high while talking with his hands. Female parent, thirty-two, seamstress, keeps a jar of dried mint by the sink, sings a three-verse folk song about a river every evening at eight while braiding the female child's hair. Male child, Tim, age eleven: loud, poor impulse control, stands between the female child and large dogs in the street. Female child, Ena, age six: shining blonde hair, high empathy response, cries when the male parent pretends to burn his thumb on the skillet.`

`Assessment: High domestic cohesion. Zero prior exposure to divine or anti-divine interference. Grief yield upon sudden termination of parents in full view of both children projected at 99.4th percentile.`

`Note: Wait until the female child turns seven. Seventh birthday increases episodic memory retention in mortal cortex by forty percent. We need her to remember the smell of the batter on the floor.`

`Note: Do not get attached.`

Ena's breath stopped in her throat.

The driftwood fire in front of her went blurry, the orange flames stretching out into long, wobbling needles of light.

She turned the stiff brown page with her thumb.

`LOG 1,182. Month seven of observation. Sunday, 08:14. Rain on the alley roof. The male parent dropped an eggshell into the bowl and the female child laughed so hard she spilled her milk across the oilcloth table cover. Instead of scolding, the female parent wiped it up with a blue dish towel and sang the second verse of the river song out of tune on purpose to make the male child groan.`

`I stood under the ash-tree in the rain for four hours after they went upstairs. I have calculated the exact dose of trace corruption required to turn both parents within twelve seconds of dermal contact while leaving the female child's nervous system intact enough to carry the dormant seed until adolescence.`

`Five months remaining until the seventh birthday.`

`Note: The blue dish towel has a hole scorched in the corner from the iron skillet.`

`Note: Do not get attached.`

Ena could not feel her legs.

She knew about the blue dish towel.

Nobody in the world knew about the blue dish towel with the black iron-skillet burn in the corner except Ena and Tim. When Ena was fourteen, sitting on the roof under the blue tarp eating stale chips with Tim after a bad day when somebody had written `CORRUPT WHORE` in black marker across her school locker, Tim had tried to make her laugh by reminding her of the time Mom had chased Dad around the kitchen table snapping that burned blue dish towel at his backside because he used it to take a hot pan off the burner. Ena had held onto that memory for fifteen years like a smooth stone in her pocket. It was hers. It was the proof that before the prophecy, before the knife, before the red tide and the Nursery and the folding chairs in the church basement, there had been a tiny square of yellow light where nobody was watching them and nobody owned them.

And Himo had been standing outside in the alley rain under the ash-tree with a notebook, timing the laugh with a stopwatch to calculate when Ena's brain would be old enough to hold the trauma without forgetting the smell of the grease.

"Ena," Eri's voice came from very far away, sharp and alarmed over by the cliff edge. "Ena, put the fucking book down. Your eyes are rolling."

Inside Ena's head, the doors did not just bang open; the hinges tore out of the plaster.

*Mommy!* Sunny screamed, and it was not a voice in the back of the skull anymore; it was out in the cold air on top of the basalt cliff, high and thin and seven years old, tearing raw through Ena's vocal cords as Ena's knees buckled under her and hit the gray dust. *She saw the blue towel! Timmy, she was looking in the window when I spilled my milk! Close the curtains! Daddy, close the curtains, there's a lady by the garbage cans!*

Himo's notebook fell out of her hand into the gray grass.

Sunny was in the front, all the way in the front, her hands flying up to cover her ears, her muddy boots kicking blindly against the dirt as if she were sliding backward across wet kitchen linoleum away from two things with red eyes and backward voices that used to be her mother and father. Because Sunny had never left the kitchen. That was why Sunny existed. When the woman in the white coat climbed back out the window over the sink and said *Soi Kijo*, seven-year-old Ena had stayed behind on the floor next to the burned pancakes so the rest of the body could get up and run out the front door after Tim.

"Daddy!" Sunny sobbed, curling sideways into a ball in the gray dust in front of the fire, clutching her stomach with her clean right hand while the black glass left arm lay dead and heavy as a log in the dirt beside her. "It's burning on the stove! Turn the pan down, it's making smoke!"

Across the grass by the cliff edge, Eri threw her weight forward off the rock, tried to stand on her crooked legs, and went down hard on one knee with a sickening crack. She did not stop. She dragged herself across the gray dust on her knees and one elbow, fast as a crab, until she reached Ena's curled body and grabbed Ena's clean right shoulder with a grip that bruised the bone.

"Get back here!" Eri barked, shaking her once, hard, while refusing to go inside Ena's head with her mind-demon claws because she had sworn after the vault she would never dig in Ena's skull again. "Kid! Sunny! Look at me! You're not in the kitchen! Look at the rag on my face!"

Before Sunny could open her wet eyes, the body jerked like a fish on a gaff.

The terror snapped shut behind an iron door, and Kio slammed into the front so hard that Ena's teeth came together on the inside of her lip and drew blood.

Her body rolled off its side, came up onto one knee and one boot in a fighter's crouch, and Kio's right hand snatched a jagged chunk of black basalt the size of a brick off the ground, holding it cocked at his shoulder toward Teodor and the three immortals by the fire. His chest was heaving, blood running down his chin from the bitten lip, his eyes wild and flat and sweeping the cliff edge for an exit.

"Back up!" Kio snarled in Ena's raw throat, his voice pitched low and grating, every muscle in her thin shoulders bunched to throw the stone or dive off the three-hundred-foot cliff if anybody moved an inch. "Get the fuck away from her! All of you! Keep that old bastard and his paper back or I'll cave his skull in!"

Teodor did not step back. He stood perfectly still three feet away, his empty hands hanging at the sides of his ink-stained coat, looking down at the folded square of gray bark-paper that had fallen beside Himo's open notebook in the dust.

Conquest did not move on her stone either. Neither did Kaina or Cosmi. They had lived through ten thousand years of gods tearing minds apart, and they looked at the girl crouching in the dust with a rock in her hand and blood on her chin with complete, unblinking stillness.

"I got her," Eri panted, kneeling in the dirt right beside Kio's boot, her hand still flat and firm on the center of Ena's shoulder blade between the torn oilskin seams. "Kio. Look at me. It's Nine. Nobody's touching the kid. Nobody's climbing through the window. Look at the fire. Just drift-wood and three old washed-up bags of bone."

Kio breathed hard through his nose, the basalt rock trembling in his white-knuckled fist.

Inside the skull, behind Kio's raised shield, Harumi was not laughing anymore.

Harumi was down on her knees in the dark by the cellar door, holding seven-year-old Sunny's head against her chest with both hands so the child would not see the last pages of the notebook lying open in the grass. For the first time since the day in the alley when Ena was fourteen and found a broken brown beer bottle behind the water tank, Harumi had no joke to make. She had no warm silk whisper about how good the red felt. Because even the red, even the worm, even the craving that Harumi had worn like a velvet dress to keep Ena from feeling the cold, had been measured out in milliliters on a Tuesday in the rain by a woman who never once looked back at her own daughters.

*She wrote us down,* Harumi whispered in the dark, her voice shaking against Sunny's yellow hair. *Kio, she wrote all of us down like rats in a maze. Even the glass in the mint tin. Even the highway.*

Kio's fingers slowly uncurled around the chunk of basalt. The rock dropped into the gray grass with a dull thud.

His shoulders sagged forward, the rigid iron going out of the spine all at once, and Ena came back into the front the hard way, gasping for air as if she had been held under green brine, and immediately dry-heaved onto the dust beside the fire, coughing up a string of bitter brown kelp tea and blood from her bitten lip.

She stayed on her hands and knees for a long minute, shivering from head to foot, while Eri crouched beside her with one hand pressed steady and heavy between her shoulder blades.

"Still here," Eri muttered, her voice rough and low. "Whole fucking clown car's still here. Breathe through your nose, dumbass."

Ena wiped her mouth with the back of her clean wrist. Her eyes fell on the open back pages of Himo's brown notebook lying six inches from her knee, where the salt breeze had flipped the bark-leaves forward to the final entries written on the old highway right before the lights went out.

She read Entry Eight, and Entry Nine, and Entry Eleven, upside down in the firelight.

`I had the pen and the page, and I unfolded the page, and I held the pen over the word everything, and I thought about crossing it out.`

`And then I thought about a world where the wind blows and the tea gets made and the cat waits on the mattress and there is no ugly old woman anywhere in it saying "That's a fucking mess, Himo."`

`And I folded the page back up.`

`Please did not mean stop. Please meant hurry.`

Ena stared at the ink on the inside of the back cover.

`Observations. Subject Ena: fueled by grief. Subject Himo: also. Yield: everything. Result: as predicted. As written. As I wrote it, in my own hand, years ago, without once thinking it would be about me. Final note. I got attached.`

A laugh came out of Ena's throat.

It was a terrible sound, ragged and wet and raw, tearing up out of her belly while the tears finally spilled over her lower lids and cut clean tracks through the sulfur grime on her cheeks.

"She could've crossed it out," Ena said to the dirt, her right hand gripping a handful of gray grass until the roots tore. "She had the pen in her hand on the road while I was standing on the guardrail. She could've crossed out the word *everything* and Tim's cat would still be sitting on the mattress on the roof. And she didn't do it because her girlfriend died and she wanted me to shoot her in the head along with the rest of the universe."

"Yes," said Kaina quietly from her boulder. "That is what gods do when they learn how to grieve late. They assume the sky should come down with them because they never learned how to carry a dead thing in their arms and keep walking."

Kaina looked down at her own small dark hands resting on her knees, and then sideways at Cosmi's bandaged throat.

"We had to learn," Kaina said. "It took ten thousand years in the cold after I closed the Seam. Watching twenty-seven survivors go mad one by one while I saw the shape of every single ending and had to put them down like sick dogs before they tore the rest of us apart. Your mother never learned. She spent forty thousand years writing *do not get attached* at the bottom of a page, and the very first time she lost one face, she handed her own gun to a seven-year-old girl from a yellow kitchen and begged her to pull the trigger."

Beside Ena's knee, Teodor knelt down in the gray grass.

His old knees popped in the quiet. He did not touch Ena, and he did not touch Himo's brown leather notebook. With extreme care, using both ink-stained hands, he picked up the other object she had dropped: the single square sheet of thin gray bark-paper backed with library linen.

He unfolded the quarters on the flat stone between them, smoothing the ancient creases with the side of his thumb.

"That's it," Ena whispered, staring at the faded writing on the gray bark. "The original. The one from the library on Terra."

Teodor nodded once.

Ena leaned over the stone, the heat of the driftwood fire drying the salt tears on her face, and looked at the words that had been whispered behind her back in grocery stores since she was seven, written across her school locker in marker, and screamed at her by terrified crowds while her twelve-year-old brother stood in front of her with his fists balled up shouting *How can people resort to being this crazy?*

On the original gray bark, the writing was not all in one print.

Most of the lines were written in a tiny, neat, trembling script that slanted slightly to the left, the ink pale and watery: the hand of an old woman in a stone hut outside of time who had spent her life recording futures gently so people might step out of their way.

And right across the middle of the sheet, cutting through the neat script like a butcher's cleaver through small bones, were four lines written in a wide, savage, upright hand that had pressed the nib down so hard it had gouged deep troughs through the bark fibers:

`When the end begins, corruption will fall, and there shall be one with shining blonde hair, the beloved sister, fueled by grief to harbor this corruption and end everything in a wasted attempt at redemption. It will only start when a man plunges a knife into his sister.`

It was Himo's handwriting. The exact same cold, upright, slanting strokes from Log 1,181 in the brown leather notebook lying two feet away in the grass.

Teodor reached down with his crooked forefinger and tapped the very top edge of the gray bark sheet, above the prophecy, where the copyists who came after him had stopped bothering to copy because it made the doom sound less certain.

In the small, neat, watery script of the old woman who had owned the stone hut before Teodor, the hand shook so badly that the letters bumped into each other like frightened sheep:

`Note From The Master Writer: The Prophecist was Corrupted for a Second While Writing This Prophecy.`

And then Teodor moved his ink-stained finger down past the bottom of the page, to the very lowest margin of the gray bark, where the linen backing held a flake of crumbling corner together.

There, scratched so faintly with a dry nib after the ink had run out that Teodor had needed to hold it up to a cold library window ten thousand years ago even to see the indentation in the fibers, were four words in the Prophecist's own tiny, terrified hand.

`Please don't hurt her.`

Ena stared at the four scratched words at the bottom of the bark.

She reached out with one clean finger and touched the dry groove in the paper where the old woman's pen had dug in after Himo let go of her wrist and walked out of this very stone hut.

The old woman had not known Ena's name. She had only seen a flash of a little girl with shining blonde hair through the dirty window of Himo's one-second corruption, and the first thing she had done when she got her own hand back was scratch a note to every world that would ever read the page, begging them not to hurt the child in the kitchen.

And Teodor Ashe, twenty-three years old in a cold library on Terra, had copied those four words onto his first clean page before anyone else threw them away, and had spent the next ten thousand years sitting on a hill outside of time trying to pay for putting the paper on the shelf.

Teodor picked up his slate and his tallow-chalk. His hand was very steady as he wrote. He set the slate down beside the gray bark leaf.

`She let go of the pen after she wrote that. She carved I LET GO into the table inside and walked down into the mist so nobody could ever use her hand to write a girl's life again.`

Teodor looked into Ena's eyes, and then he wrote one more line beneath it, pressing the white chalk firm against the dark stone.

`The document is finished, Ena. Both hands that wrote it are gone. Nobody is writing what you do next.`

Below the cliff, from three hundred feet down in the split basalt gorge, a deep, hollow boom echoed up through the rock, shaking a shower of gray dust off the stones of the hut.

A plume of scalding green steam shot fifty feet above the lip of the trench, carrying the stench of boiled tank-fluid and charred copper across the gray grass. Down in the dark mud three miles back toward the whale ribs, something answered the boom: a long, wet, ragged howl that was not a dog and was not a man, followed by another a mile closer along the causeway.

Eri pushed herself up onto her good knee by the cliff edge and looked down into the yellow fog boiling out of the crack.

"The tide just turned at the outer shelf," Eri said, her single dark eye hard and bright in the firelight. "When the cold salt water hits that hot magma vent under the vault floor, the whole main tank-sump is going to blow up the chimney and wash straight across the flats into the open sea. Every fish, every sleeper, every poor bastard living on a rib or an island from here to the north shelf is going to be swimming in your mother's green bathwater by morning."

Ena looked down at the original sheet of the prophecy on the flat stone, and then at Himo's water-warped brown notebook lying open in the grass.

Inside her skull, the four of them were very quiet. Sunny was sitting by the cellar stairs with her thumb in her mouth, watching through Ena's eyes. Kio stood behind Ena's right shoulder, waiting. Harumi sat on the floor with her arms wrapped around her knees, looking at the four faint words scratched at the bottom of the gray bark.

*Please don't hurt her.*

Ena reached down with her black glass left hand.

She did not pick up the original prophecy; she left the old woman's four words lying on Teodor's stone in the warm light of the copyist's fire. Instead, her black glass fingers closed around Himo's four hundred and twelfth notebook, where four hundred thousand years of math and forty-two logs about a yellow kitchen and a burned blue dish towel were written in tree-sap blood.

She stood up, walked two steps to the driftwood fire, and dropped Himo's notebook into the center of the orange flames.

The driftwood did not burn away, because it belonged to the margin outside of time, but the bark-paper from the world Himo had ended caught instantly. The brown pages curled black at the corners, hissed as the salt-damp boiled out of the leather spine, and went up in a tall, hot column of greasy red smoke that vanished into the pale paper sky within ten seconds, leaving not even a fleck of ash among the cedar coals.

"Come on," Ena said to Eri, turning back toward the wet black stone stairs leading down into the fissure. "Let's go shut the drain."


Chapter Fifteen: The Drowning of the Tank

Conquest came down the basalt stairs with them as far as the lower ledge, carrying fifty feet of tarred hemp anchor-line coiled over one scarred forearm and an iron wrecking-bar she had pulled from the ruins of the highway bridge.

She did not offer to go inside the catacombs. Even forty feet above the fissure's mouth, where the yellow steam boiled out between the cracked basalt pillars of the old sluice-works, Conquest had to stop on the slick stone step and press the back of her hand hard against her nose and mouth. Beneath the crooked yellow paper crown, her eyes watered and her skin had gone gray around the jaw.

"I can't go down in that," Conquest said, her voice thick and muffled behind her knuckles. "That's the stuff she cooked us in. If I breathe that green steam down in the dark, I'll start hearing the megaphone in my head again. I can feel it trying to get into my teeth from here."

"Stay on the ledge," Ena said, taking the iron wrecking-bar with her black left hand while Eri slung the coil of tarred hemp rope over her narrow shoulder. "If we don't come back out before the tide reaches the third stair, take Teodor and the others down the north ridge to the boats."

Conquest looked down at Ena's bare, hacked-off blonde head, and then at the paper crown on her own.

"Don't die down there in the dark, little sister," the first Horseman said roughly. "We just got the noise back."

Ena and Eri stepped off the bottom stair onto the flooded basalt sill of the catacombs.

There was no grand archway. When the Gray Abyss of Shattered Ruins and the lower sub-basements of Himo's Nursery had dropped through the floor of the dying multiverse into Pelagia's volcanic trench, the concrete vaults and steel tank-walkways had sheared in half against the old sea-king's stone drainage tunnels. The entrance was a jagged three-foot split where a rusted steel fire door from the Nursery had been crushed sideways into a Roman-style basalt sluice arch, half-choked with black volcanic slag and broken six-inch glass from the skyscraper tank.

Out of the crack poured a foot-deep stream of warm, glowing green brine.

The smell hit them square in the face like a wet wool blanket soaked in ether and slaughterhouse offal. It was the exact smell of the Nursery floor: amniotic fluid, ozone, copper filings, and the sickly-sweet rotting-fruit reek of Corruption-027 steeped in tank preservative for forty thousand years.

Beside Ena, Eri stopped dead on the stone sill.

Her left boot was an inch above the green water pouring over the threshold. She did not put it down. Her chest locked up mid-breath, her single dark eye going wide and white-rimmed around the iris, staring down at the faintly glowing green stream swirling around the broken concrete chunks at her feet.

"Eri," Ena said over the hiss of the steam.

Eri did not answer. She could not hear her.

In Eri's single eye, the basalt archway was gone. The wet black volcanic rock had turned back into the mile-high concrete ceiling of the Nursery vault, and the rushing water in the trench was the low, sweet, forty-thousand-year hum of the big glass tank going up into the dark like a skyscraper. Down in the green reflection on the water's surface, six gray faces were looking up at her, hairless, identical, with wide empty mouths curved into gentle, peaceful smiles as their hands reached down to pick her up by the ankles and carry her out to the drainage pipe.

And worse than the six gray ushers: a smaller shape, soaking wet in a ruined homecoming dress with a bent tin crown on her wet hair, crawling across the green puddle on her hands and knees with blue light leaking between her teeth, whispering in a cute high-school voice: *Hold still, babe. Just gonna lick the spoon. You don't even want her to say sorry anymore, do you?*

Eri's right hand clawed at the tarred linen patch over her empty left eye socket, where Siren's fingers had gone in behind the eyeball on the vault floor before the tank broke. Her crooked right leg gave out completely, and she slammed sideways against the wet basalt wall, sliding down until her hip hit the dry lip of the step, her breath coming in shallow, whistling squeaks through a throat that had closed to the size of a pinhole.

"Get off me," Eri gasped at the empty green water, kicking her good left heel spasmodically against the rock, her fingernails digging bloody crescents into her own cheek below the eye patch. "Get your fucking mouth off my head, Twenty-two! I'm empty! There's nothing left in there, get off!"

Ena dropped the iron wrecking-bar onto the stone with a loud clang.

She did not grab Eri from behind, because grabbing someone who was back on the vault floor was how you got an eye torn out. She stepped directly in front of Eri, blocking her view of the glowing green water with her own muddy oilskin coat, and knelt down on both knees on the dry sill so her face was level with Eri's single wild eye.

"Nine!" Ena shouted over the roar of the incoming tide out in the gorge. She held up her black glass left hand six inches from Eri's nose, where the cold salt air made the gray ash-skin hiss. "Look at my hand! Look at the black! You're not in the pipe! The tank is broken! I punched a hole in it, remember? Twenty-two is gone!"

Eri's chest heaved, a long string of spit hanging from her chin as her single eye jerked from the black glass hand to Ena's scarred face.

"My legs," Eri choked out, her teeth chattering so hard she bit her own tongue. "They picked me up by my legs, Ena, they broke both my fucking shins..."

"Your legs are crooked and they hurt like a bitch because they healed six months ago!" Ena yelled back, taking Eri's icy right hand in her warm, clean right palm and pressing Eri's fingers flat against the rough, barnacle-crusted basalt of the cliff wall. "Feel the shells! That's sea-rock! That's barnacles! The six gray bastards are gone! Himo's notebook just burned up in Teodor's fire! Nobody's in the tunnel except us and a rusted iron chain!"

Eri's fingers scraped across the sharp white lime of the dead barnacles on the wall. The edge of a shell cut the pad of her index finger, drawing a bright bead of red blood.

Eri looked at the blood on her finger. Then she looked at the tarred hemp rope coiled over her shoulder.

Slowly, her whistling breath deepened into a harsh, rattling cough. She spat a mouthful of bile and blood onto the dry rock beside her boot, wiped her mouth on her shoulder, and glared at Ena with pure, shaking hatred.

"If you ever tell anybody I sat down like a little bitch at the door," Eri rasped, "I'll tell the whole island what you used to think about your youth pastor in eighth grade."

"Deal," Ena said.

Eri did not stand up yet. With shaking, viciously deliberate hands, she pulled the coil of tarred hemp anchor-line off her shoulder. She took one end of the thick rope, wrapped it twice around her own narrow waist over her torn wool coat, and tied a square bowline knot right over her hipbone, pulling it so tight with her teeth and both fists that the hemp bit three inches into her belly.

Then she took the other end of the line, measured out eight feet of slack, and reached across to wrap it twice around Ena's leather belt over the oilskin coat, knotting it just as hard.

"What are you doing?" Ena asked, looking down at the eight-foot tether connecting their waists.

"The floor inside is broken concrete over volcanic vents," Eri said flatly, hauling herself upright against the barnacled wall while her right shin popped. "And your head's got four drivers fighting over the steering wheel, and my legs don't bend right when the water gets past my knees. One of us steps in a crack or goes under the green slop in the dark, the other one hauls her out or drowns with her. Nobody walks out of this fucking basement alone today. Pick up your crowbar."

Ena looked at the tarred hemp rope tied to her belt. She gave the knot one hard tug with her good hand to make sure it would hold, picked up the iron wrecking-bar with her black left fist, and stepped down into the glowing green water.

The catacomb tunnel sloped downward at a steep angle toward the main fault chamber two hundred yards below the cliff face.

Within twenty paces, the grey daylight from the gorge behind them vanished around a jagged bend where a massive concrete pillar from the Nursery's sub-floor had crashed through the basalt roof. There was no light at all except the sickly, yellow-green phosphorescence of the tank-brine itself, which swirled knee-deep around their legs and lit the wet black rock walls from below like a nightlight in a slaughterhouse.

The heat inside was suffocating. Out on the mudflats the wind had been near freezing, but down here the volcanic fault was cooking the sunken remnants of the Nursery like a stewpot on a low burner. Thick, oily green steam hung in layers against the low stone ceiling, dripping down the back of Ena's neck and plastering her short blonde hair to her skull.

And floating in the knee-deep water, washing sluggishly back and forth against their shins with every surge of the rising sea outside, was the wreckage of Himo's forty thousand years of work.

Shattered curves of two-inch-thick curved safety glass from the rows of lesser incubator tanks crunched under Ena's boots like rotten ice. Bundles of rubber tubing, rusted steel shackles, and waterlogged pages of numbered laboratory charts swirled past in the glowing eddies. Thirty yards down the slope, wedged upright in a cleft of split basalt where the ceiling dropped to five feet, a cracked cylinder of glass still held a gray, hairless Nursery baby the size of a grown man, pickled and dead in stagnant brine, its eyes closed and its grey mouth stretched in the permanent, empty smile that Calypso's song had left on its face before the multiverse went dark.

Ena kept her eyes off the glass cylinder and her shoulder angled in front of Eri, breaking the current as the green water rose from their knees to mid-thigh.

The brine was soaking through her greased trousers now. Where it touched the bare skin of her legs, it did not feel like water. It felt warm and slick and alive, tingling against the old white razor-scars on her thighs and setting off a deep, rhythmic throb in the black glass of her left forearm.

Her bad arm was waking up.

For forty-nine days since the highway, the black glass from her left palm to her elbow had been cold and heavy as an iron skillet, a dead scar left over from burning out the world. Now, submerged to the wrist in Himo's glowing tank-fluid, thin hair-lines of dull red light were beginning to pulse deep beneath the black surface in time with her heartbeat.

*Oh, God,* Harumi groaned inside the skull, and there was no theatrical smirk in her voice now; it was the raw, shaking groan of a junkie with five years clean whose face had just been pushed into a bowl of warm gin. *Ena. Baby, get it out of the water. Take your left arm out of the water right now. It's going up the veins. I can taste it in the back of my teeth.*

Ena lifted her left arm out of the thigh-deep brine, cradling the iron wrecking-bar high against her chest. Even out of the liquid, the green steam was condensing on the black glass fingers, and the dull red veins under the ash-skin kept pulsing, sending a warm, sweet, terrifyingly familiar hum straight up her left shoulder and into the base of her neck.

*You're right,* the hum whispered in her blood, tiny and warm and reasonable as a painkiller dissolving on the tongue. *You don't have to be tired anymore, Ena. Your knees don't have to shake. The little girl doesn't have to cry about the kitchen. Open your mouth and drink an inch of it off the rock and you can tear this whole cliff in half with one hand.*

"No," Ena said out loud, her teeth clamped so tight her jaw ached. "Day forty-nine. Day forty-nine."

Behind her on the eight-foot rope, Eri stumbled over a submerged steel pipe and went down to her chin in the green water before the hemp line snapped taut against Ena's belt.

Ena planted her boots wide on the broken glass floor, leaned forward against the pull of her waist, and hauled back on the wet rope with her right hand until Eri got her good left foot under her again and came up spitting glowing green brine out of her mouth.

"Don't swallow it!" Ena shouted over the deep, grinding roar coming from the fault chamber ahead.

"I didn't swallow it, you fucking idiot, it went up my nose!" Eri coughed, clinging to the wet rope with both hands while her chest heaved and her single eye watered violently from the caustic chemical burn of the preservative fluid. She shook her head like a wet dog, leaning half her weight against the taut hemp line between them as she dragged her useless right leg through the rising current. "Keep moving! Look at the wall! The salt-water's already coming in under the green!"

Ena looked down at the glowing surface around her waist.

Dark, cold ribbons of black ocean water were beginning to twist through the warm green tank-brine like ink poured into soup, swirling faster as the incoming Spring Flood pushed six miles of sea back into the mouth of the abyssal trench outside. With every thirty seconds that passed, the water level in the sloped tunnel rose another two inches, climbing from their hips toward their ribs, and the air trapped under the low basalt roof grew denser, hotter, and harder to draw into the lungs.

Fifty yards ahead, the tunnel opened into the main sluice-vault directly over the volcanic fault.

It was a round chamber cut out of black stone by whatever ancient marine civilization had ruled Pelagia before the drowning, forty feet across, with a domed roof held up by four massive pillars of squared basalt. Directly through the center of the stone floor, a raw fissure ten feet wide had split open down into the magma shelf below the seabed.

Wedged deep inside that glowing crack, crushed like a tin can in a doorjamb, was the bottom third of Himo's skyscraper tank: the huge steel base-ring and the broken brass pipes of the throat-apparatus where Calypso had been wired into the multiverse. Every time a wave of cold ocean water surged through the lower sea-culvert and hit the red-hot volcanic rock below the tank base, a geyser of boiling green tank-sludge and liquid black corruption-slag erupted ten feet into the chamber, coating the basalt pillars in shiny, tar-like black glass that hummed like a struck tuning fork.

Above the seaward culvert hung the ancient sluice-gate: a six-ton slab of iron-banded basalt suspended on two thick chains of hand-forged iron links that passed over a stone drum in the ceiling. A massive iron ratchet-wheel, eight feet tall and crusted half an inch thick with salt-rust and black slag, held the chains locked against the wall on the far side of the chamber.

If that gate dropped before the full weight of the tide came in, the six-ton slab would seal the fault chamber off from the open ocean, trapping the boiling tank-sump and the black glass beneath three hundred feet of dead rock. If the tide filled the chamber first, the current would blow the green sludge straight back up the stairs and out through the lower culverts across the whole floor of the sea.

Between the tunnel mouth and the iron ratchet-wheel lay thirty feet of chest-deep, churning green and black water.

And floating in the middle of that water, bumping gently against the stone pillars as the geysers surged, were four of the drowned hounds from the old highway and two gray Nursery ushers whose bodies had not completely dissolved when the lights went out.

They were not alive. Kaina had told the truth: what had ended on the highway had no next part of its own.

Yet the liquid black corruption-slag boiling out of the broken throat-apparatus was crusting over their dead gray skin and packing their empty eye sockets like wet tar, and Corruption-027 had been built by Himo to self-replicate in any vessel made of god-flesh, living or dead. As the red-veined black glass cooled on the floating corpses, their jaws clicked open and shut in the water with a rhythmic, mechanical snap, like broken toys wound too tight, and from the two dead gray ushers came a skipping, two-note stutter of a noise that was not a voice at all, only air forced through dead vocal cords by the boiling gas in their chests:

*Day. One. Day. One. Day. One.*

At the sound of those two words echoing off the low stone dome in the green dark, Ena's brain split down the middle like a dropped plate.

She did not switch once. The water was at her collarbones now, hot and freezing in alternating bands as the fault-steam hit the incoming sea, and the black slag crusted along the basalt wall two inches from her shoulder was humming straight into her teeth, and the terror in the small stone drum of the chamber was so huge that the walls inside her head simply collapsed.

*Mommy, they're in the kitchen!* Sunny screamed, taking the throat and the right arm all at once, her voice shrill and ragged above the roar of the geyser as she splashed blindly backward against the taut rope tied to Eri's waist, dropping the iron wrecking-bar under the water where it caught solely by the leather wrist-loop around her black left arm. *Their eyes are red! Timmy, don't let them touch the table, they're talking backward! Mommy, wake up!*

"Sunny, no!" Eri bellowed from eight feet back in the chest-deep water, bracing her crooked legs against the doorway arch and hauling on the hemp rope with both bleeding hands to keep the panicked seven-year-old from backing straight into the boiling fissure in the center of the floor. "Kio! Get the fuck out here! The kid's got the body! Kio!"

The head jerked sideways so hard the neck vertebrae popped.

Sunny vanished into the howling dark, and Harumi slammed into the front with her mouth wide open and her eyes rolled half back, gasping in a huge lungful of the sweet green tank-steam.

Her clean right hand shot out and grabbed the wet basalt wall where a thick blister of black corruption-glass had cooled over the stone. Her fingers closed over the sharp black slag, slicing her palm open without feeling it, and she pulled her head toward the wall with her tongue out, starving, frantic, forty-nine days of shaking misery burning up in one blind animal lunge to lick the black tar off the rock and let the red tide back in.

"Oh, fuck you, no you don't!" Eri screamed, throwing all ninety pounds of her bruised weight backward on the tarred hemp rope in the chest-deep current.

The bowline knot around Ena's waist jerked her hips backward two feet in the water, tearing Harumi's bleeding right hand off the crusted wall an inch before her mouth touched the black glass.

Inside the mouth, Harumi caught herself for one horrifying, lucid half-second.

She saw Sunny's yellow hair down in the dark water inside their head. She remembered the four small words scratched at the bottom of the gray bark in the copyist's firelight: *Please don't hurt her.*

Before the craving could pull her head back toward the black slag on the stone, Harumi clamped Ena's own teeth down across the tip of her tongue.

She bit down with every ounce of jaw strength in the body until the cartilage crunched and hot, bright salt blood flooded her mouth, drowning out the sweet rotten-apple smell of the green steam with raw mortal iron.

*Kio!* Harumi screamed inside the skull, spitting a mouthful of red blood into the glowing green water around her chin. *Take the hands! I can't hold the mouth, take the fucking hands!*

The spine went rigid as an iron bar.

Kio took the body from the neck down, while Ena and Harumi held the bleeding mouth shut from the inside together, both of them lock-jawed on the bitten tongue while tears and green brine ran down their cheeks.

Kio did not look at the dead gray usher drifting past his shoulder with its jaw clicking *Day. One. Day. One.* He reached down under the glowing water with the black glass left hand, grabbed the iron wrecking-bar by its leather wrist-loop, and lunged forward through the chest-deep surge toward the eight-foot ratchet-wheel on the far wall.

Behind him, Eri was up to her neck now. She was five inches shorter than Ena, and her right leg was dragging uselessly behind her like an anchor over the broken tank-glass on the floor. Every wave of cold black sea water coming through the culvert lifted Eri off her good left foot and slammed her back against the basalt pillar.

She did not let go of the rope. She wrapped the wet hemp twice around her bleeding left forearm and pulled herself forward hand over hand along Ena's wake, coughing up green foam, until both of them reached the high stone plinth under the iron ratchet-wheel.

Kio hauled himself up onto the submerged stone step so the water dropped back to his waist, then turned and grabbed Eri by the front of her oilskin collar with one hand, dragging her bodily up onto the step beside him before the dead hound circling the fissure could drift into her legs.

"The pin's rusted shut!" Kio roared in Ena's voice, blood spraying from her swollen tongue as he jammed the wedge tip of the iron wrecking-bar into the dog-latch of the giant ratchet-wheel.

The iron latch had been sitting in salt steam and cooling black corruption-slag since the vault fell. A crust of shiny black glass as thick as a man's wrist had fused the iron pawl to the stone wall, holding the six-ton sluice-gate suspended above the culvert while the incoming ocean thundered through the gap below like a freight train.

Kio threw his full weight onto the end of the four-foot iron bar.

The black glass left arm was burning hot now, the red lines under the ash-skin glowing bright as stove-coals from soaking in the brine, and the clean right palm was already sliced open from the slag on the wall. When Kio bore down on the rough iron bar, the torn skin of the right palm split all the way across the lifeline, greasing the metal with red blood.

The iron latch did not budge. Instead, the four-foot wrecking-bar bent three inches in the middle and slipped out of the frozen notch with a screech of sparks, slamming Kio's right knuckles against the basalt wall.

Down in the center of the chamber, the ten-foot volcanic fissure gave a terrible, grinding shudder as a three-foot wall of cold flood-tide poured through the culvert and hit the red-hot steel base of Himo's tank.

The geyser that blew up from the crack nearly hit the stone dome forty feet above. Boiling green water and chunks of molten black slag rained down across the chamber. One of the dead gray ushers was tossed sideways by the wave and washed directly against the stone plinth at their knees, its cold, waterlogged gray fingers catching in the hem of Eri's trousers as its dead jaw clicked against her crooked right shin:

*Day. One. Day. One.*

Eri let out a scream that tore her throat raw. It was pure animal terror, ten thousand years of the pit and six hours of the drainage pipe boiling up in one white-hot spasm, and she raised her good left boot and stamped the heel down into the dead usher's smiling gray face once, twice, three times, until the pickled skull caved in like a wet gourd and the corpse rolled back into the deep water.

"The chain!" Eri screamed at Kio, pointing a shaking, bloody finger up at the heavy iron links running from the top of the ratchet-wheel to the stone ceiling drum. "Don't pry the dog, the dog's fused! Hook the bar through the master link above the wheel and pull straight down! Break the pin out of the stone!"

The master link was seven feet off the submerged plinth, two feet above their heads, where the main hoisting chain met the ratchet axle.

Kio didn't hesitate. He shoved the bent iron wrecking-bar upward with both hands, jamming the crow-foot through the six-inch iron loop of the master link until it wedged hard against the axle bolt.

He hung his full weight from the end of the bar with both hands.

A hundred and ten pounds of wet, starving twenty-two-year-old girl was not enough to shear a two-inch rusted iron axle bolt out of solid basalt. The bar groaned, flakes of brown rust and black slag snowing down into Kio's upturned face, and the rising green water surged up over the plinth to his hips, then his ribs, threatening to float his boots right off the stone step and take all his leverage away.

"Get on me!" Ena's voice broke through Kio's teeth, both of them fronting together in the roaring dark as the green brine washed over their shoulders. "Eri, climb onto my belt and hang! We need the weight!"

Eri looked at the boiling fissure six feet behind them, then up at Ena's two hands locked white-knuckled on the iron bar overhead: the right hand dripping bright red mortal blood down the iron, the left hand glowing dull red beneath its black glass crust as the heat in the stone tried to climb past her elbow toward her heart.

Eri grabbed the taut hemp tether between their waists, hauled herself flush against Ena's back in the chest-deep water, locked both arms around Ena's ribs right over the bowline knot, and lifted her crooked legs completely off the stone step, dropping all her dead weight straight down onto Ena's hips.

The sudden jerk nearly tore Ena's shoulders out of their sockets.

Inside her skull, nobody was hiding in the cellar anymore.

Kio locked the elbows and the spine so the joints would not pop. Harumi took the fire burning in the black glass left arm and held it confined below the elbow by sheer spite, refusing to let a single drop of the red heat climb into the chest while using the dead glass fist like a welded iron clamp on the bar. Sunny held her eyes wide open in the dark, looking right at the green water without screaming anymore, counting the links in the chain out loud inside their head the way Tim used to make her count fence-posts when she had a nightmare: *one, two, three, four.* And Ena held the torn, bleeding right hand shut around the wet iron beside the black one, her feet wedged into a crack in the submerged basalt step to keep both of them from floating as the cold flood-tide hit her chin.

Above their heads, the black slag encasing the iron axle bolt let out a high, singing crack, like a guitar string snapping in an empty room.

"Pull!" Eri shrieked into the wet back of Ena's neck, kicking her good leg downward under the water to bounce their combined weight against the bar.

Ena let out a roar that tasted of bitten tongue and bitter kelp tea, and hauled down with everything left in all four of them.

The two-inch iron bolt sheared clean through at the wall.

With a sound like a cannon firing inside an iron drum, the eight-foot ratchet-wheel tore loose from its masonry anchors, sparked wildly across the basalt pillar, and plunged into the boiling fissure below.

Overhead, the liberated iron chain shrieked across the stone ceiling drum at forty miles an hour in a shower of orange friction-sparks.

Six tons of iron-banded basalt sluice-gate dropped out of the roof dome and slammed down across the seaward culvert mouth.

The impact shook the entire mountain. The stone slab sheared straight through the incoming wall of ocean water, bit four inches deep into the volcanic bedrock of the sill, and sealed the chamber shut with a boom that slammed against their eardrums like a physical blow.

Instantly, the roaring freight-train current stopped.

The churning water inside the chamber, cut off from the incoming tide and trapped at chest height, rebounding off the dropped gate in one violent cross-wave that tore Ena's boots out from under her on the slick plinth and threw both women backward into the dark water at the foot of the tunnel stairs.

The eight-foot hemp rope held them together as they rolled over the broken glass on the sloped floor. Ena got her black left elbow jammed behind a fallen concrete block, stopped their slide ten feet above the flooded chamber, and reached back with her bleeding right hand to haul Eri's head up out of the knee-deep wash by the back of her coat.

For a full minute neither of them moved. They lay half-submerged on the sloped concrete rubble in the pitch dark, coughing up green foam and salt water, listening to the chamber below them go quiet.

On the other side of the six-ton stone slab, they could hear the massive, muffled thump of Pelagia's ocean piling thirty fathoms deep against the outside of the cliff wall, locked out. Inside the chamber below their boots, starved of the cold seawater that had been feeding the steam explosions, the geysers in the volcanic fault subsided to a low, bubbling hiss, cooling and crusting over under its own dead weight in the sealed dark.

The glowing green light in the water around their legs slowly faded to a dull, muddy gray as the broken tank base settled deeper into the volcanic ash and choked itself out.

"Is it shut?" Eri wheezed, her cheek pressed flat against the wet concrete beside Ena's knee, her single eye closed.

Ena lifted her head in the dark and looked down the slope toward the six-ton stone door holding back the sea.

She lifted her left hand out of the puddle. The dull red lines under the black glass had gone dark again. Her right palm was bleeding sluggishly from a three-inch tear across the skin, and her tongue was swollen to twice its size where Harumi had bitten it, and every muscle in her back and shoulders felt as if it had been beaten with an iron pipe.

Inside her head, the house was quiet. Sunny was curled up asleep on the top step by the front door, her thumb in her mouth. Kio had sat down against the wall and let his hands drop onto his knees. And Harumi was sitting right beside Ena in the front, leaning her tired head against Ena's shoulder in the dark, neither of them asking who owned the coat.

"It's shut," Ena whispered around her swollen tongue.

She reached down with her torn right hand, wrapped it around the wet hemp rope tying her waist to Eri's, and pulled until Eri rolled onto her side and got one elbow under her chest.

Together, tied by eight feet of tarred anchor-line, dragging one broken shin and one dead glass arm up the three hundred wet basalt risers through the clearing sulfur mist, they climbed out of the basement toward the driftwood fire at the top of the rock.



Chapter Sixteen: The Statue in the Tide

At the very bottom of the six-mile basin, where the black salt mud was knee-deep and smelled of dead kelp and old iron, the main square of Purgatory had come to rest against the base of the great basalt sluice-pillars.

When the city had crumbled like a sandcastle in the tide at the end of the world, most of the little statues on the street corners had gone to powder. The man kicking the dog, the woman reading her sister's diary, the kid with a lighter by a shed: the grey sea had ground them into silt over the months since the highway fell. Only the heavy stone from the center of the square had survived the drop into the abyssal fault. It had slid down through the collapsed ceiling of the Nursery vault and wedged itself between the two central pilings of the drowned gate, canted ten degrees to the left, half-buried in dark weed and white barnacles.

I stopped twenty paces out in the suck of the mud. My breath came in ragged, whistling tears through my teeth.

My left arm was shaking so hard I had to clamp my right hand around my wrist to keep my fingers from clawing at the scarred black skin of my palm. The withdrawal had been eating up from my elbow since midnight, cold and greasy, like someone dragging a rusted fishhook back and forth through the marrow of my ulna. Every vein from my knuckles to my collarbone felt packed with crushed glass. Without the hum, the air in the trench was too loud. The drip of seawater off the basalt gate sixty feet above our heads sounded like hammers on sheet tin. My chest scar, the deep puckered seam right over my sternum where the bread knife had gone in when I was fifteen, pulsed in time with my heartbeat.

And there it was, rising out of the low tide water in front of me.

The plinth was cracked down the middle, crusted with purple mussels and green bladderwrack that hung in wet ribbons down the grey stone. Above the weed stood two life-size figures carved from the same dense, dead granite.

A boy of nineteen in a kitchen. A girl of fifteen at a counter.

Even with the salt eating at the edges of the stone, the carving had kept every cruel little detail. My stone hair was pulled up in a plastic clip at the back of my head. My stone left hand was holding a butter knife down at my side, unlifted, with a smear of stone peanut butter over the knuckle of my thumb. My stone mouth was open on a three-letter name.

Across from her stood Tim.

His right arm was broken off at the elbow. It had snapped when the square fell, just the way he had felt it snap when the song went out on the highway. The broken stone forearm lay three feet away in the black mud, fingers still curled around the handle of the long serrated bread knife, the blade buried to the hilt in the silt. Between his stone boots, set carefully on the flat of the plinth so the current would not wash them off, sat a stoneware casserole dish full of petrified tuna and noodles, a cardboard shoebox with the lid rotted away to show eleven dried-out fat black Sharpies bundled together with decaying twine, and a battered silver helmet with a rough *H* scratched into the brow.

Barnacles had grown in a white crust across the toe of his left boot and up his shin. Dark kelp was wrapped around his ankle like a leash.

I looked up at his stone face.

In all the years I had spent fighting him when he wore the helmet and called himself Modify, and in all the Thursdays I had spent sitting in the dead grass on the hill above our town with my back against the rebar-scratched rock I called his grave, I had never seen the statue from Purgatory. I had only heard the damned souls talk about it by the river.

They had carved his face on that Tuesday afternoon in the kitchen.

He did not look like an anti-god of creation. He did not look like a monster. He was nineteen years old, skinny in the shoulders, wearing the flannel shirt our dad had left in the hall closet, and his stone face was locked in the single most terrifying expression I had ever seen on a human being.

He looked sure. He looked relieved. And right under the relief, caught in the gray grain of the rock around his eyes at the exact half-second after my eyes had turned from red back to blue, he looked so fucking scared it made my stomach turn over and empty nothing but sour bile into the back of my throat.

Below the barnacles on the base of the plinth, the brass plaque was green with verdigris, half-covered in grey slime. I could still read the stamped letters from twenty feet away.

FULFILLED A PROPHECY HE ONCE CALLED BULLSHIT.

"He kept the markers," a tiny voice whispered inside my head.

I did not black out. My knees did not buckle and drop me into the dark at the back of my skull while somebody else took the body. For the first time since the Sunday our parents stopped breathing between the stove and the table, the doors inside my head did not slam shut.

Sunny was right behind my eyes, looking out through the wet blur of my vision at the rotted shoebox by Tim's boots. I could feel her small hands pressing against the inside of my ribs, warm and trembling, the eight-year-old part of us who still remembered the smell of pancake batter and the sound of our mother singing about a river while Dad cursed at the cast-iron skillet.

*He kept all eleven,* Sunny said. Her voice was clear as rain in the quiet behind my temples. *Look, Ena. Under his shoe. The fat black ones that smelled like vinegar when he took the cap off. He told me he threw them in the trash by the auto shop so the principal wouldn't search his locker, except he didn't throw them out. He put them in the sneaker box under his bed.*

"I see them," I whispered out loud. My voice cracked on the salt air.

*Breathe through your nose, Ena,* Kio said.

He was here too. Usually, when Kio stepped to the front to count the mortar cracks or check the latches on the shutters or inventory the salted fish in the pantry, the rest of us went dim, tucked away behind thick gray wool while he handled the things that needed a steady hand and a flat pulse. Now I could feel him standing right at my left shoulder inside the skin we shared. He was taking the measure of the six-mile trench: the sixty-foot drop of the iron-bound basalt sluice-gate above us, the rusted iron winch-pin sticking out of the high stone shelf on the east wall, the low gurgle of the sea sucking back through the seaward cavern three miles out before the Spring Tide turned and came back down the throat like an avalanche.

*In for four,* Kio said, steady and low, pressing his palm over the racing hammer of our heart from the inside. *Hold for four. Out for four. Your left arm is cramping from the cold and the drop in residue, Ena. It is only nerve pain. The bone is whole. Keep your boots wide on the shale so the mud does not pull your heels under.*

And then I felt the heat on my right side.

It was not the corruption hum. The red hum was gone, burned down to a cold, sick smear of gray ash in the hollows of my bones. This was Harumi herself.

For years I had thought she and the hum were the same thing. I had treated her like a tumor or a demon or a curse that lived in the red light behind my eyes and came out when the tins were empty or full. Now, with no black glass in our blood and the dark salt wind blowing off the kelp beds, she was still here. She felt tall and ragged and furious, leaning against the inside of my ribs with her arms crossed, staring out through our blue eyes at the grey stone boy on the plinth.

*Look at him,* Harumi said. Her voice did not sound like a drain anymore. It sounded like a girl who had been locked in a burning kitchen at fifteen and had taken the knife into her own chest so the rest of us would not have to feel the iron scrape the ribs. *Nineteen years old and he listened to a woman with a fucking tuna casserole. Look at his stupid flannel shirt. He didn't even roll the sleeves up.*

Something moved in the shadows behind the left side of the plinth.

A wet cough rattled off the sluice-pillars. Then came the faint, musical chink of small glass pieces shifting inside thin tin.

Marguerite dragged herself around the base of the stone square.

She looked ten years older than she had on the night the laundromat windows came in on Fifth. Her gray buzz cut was grown out into dirty white tufts matted with dried blood and grey sea-clay. The heavy canvas coat she always wore had been ripped open from the right knee to the hip; beneath the torn cloth, her calf was a raw, purple-black mess of punctured muscle where one of her own three-jointed glass hounds had bitten down to the bone before the pack went into the fault. The old burn scars running up both of her forearms looked white and puckered in the cold dawn light.

In her left hand she held an empty revolver with the cylinder hanging open, rusted solid by the salt spray.

In her right hand, held tight against her ribs like a Bible, was her mother's blue button tin.

"I knew you'd come down when they drained the weir," Marguerite rasped. She couldn't stand straight on the bitten leg; she leaned her hip against the barnacle-crusted flank of the plinth, right below my stone fifteen-year-old bare feet, and panted through gray lips. "I watched from the cliff pipe for three days while your little one-eyed demon friend and the weir women worked the chains. I told myself, she'll come down to clear the channel before the gate drops. She has to. She can't leave the stone standing in the middle of the track."

I didn't take a step closer. My left palm twitched against my right wrist. Even from fifteen feet away, through the smell of rot and low-tide sulfur, my skin knew what was inside that blue tin.

My teeth started to chatter. Saliva flooded my mouth, bitter and metallic, the disgusting animal reflex of a body that had spent months melting black shards into its own flesh behind a rooftop water tank.

"What are you doing down here, Marguerite?" I asked.

"Waiting for my girl," Marguerite said. She tried to smile, the old Tuesday-and-Friday-night folding-chair smile, the drill-sergeant nod that said *thank you for sharing* while nine broken people clapped with thin hands in front of dead washing machines. On her grey, hollowed face, it looked like a skull trying on a hat. "The pack took my leg two miles back up the dry ravine. Tess's third litter. Seven of them. They smelled the tin in my coat. They drove me down into the fault when the water ran out. They're still down in the fissure under the Nursery slab, Ena. Dozens of them. Licking the green silt where the big tank broke. Blending with what's left of the god-jelly down in the dark."

She shifted her weight, wincing as dark blood oozed from the tooth-holes in her calf and ran down over her boot into the salt mud.

"You look awful, kid," she said softly. "Look at your arm. You're shaking like a wet dog. How long has it been since the highway? Four days? Five? Cold turkey off a full charge of twenty-two shards and a throat full of dead gods. Jesus Christ, Ena, your heart's going to stop in your chest if you don't taper."

*She's got twenty-two more in that box,* Kio said quietly. *I can hear the weight of them when her thumb moves on the lid. She harvested the kennel beds before she left the city.*

*Don't look at her thumb,* Harumi snapped. *Look at her leg. Her own grandkids chewed her hamstring in half and she's still holding the leash out.*

Marguerite pried the lid off the blue button tin with her thumb.

The sound it made, that tiny flat *pop* of painted tin letting go, went straight down my spine like hot oil.

Inside the tin, nested on a scrap of gray rag to keep them from chipping against one another, lay twenty-two pieces of black glass. Some were the size of a thumbnail, curved and oily, taken from the skull-plates of her dead hounds. Some were smaller, sharp-edged splinters like broken bottle necks.

In the grey light at the bottom of the sea-trench, they hummed.

It was a low, sweet vibration that didn't travel through the air. It traveled through the wet mud into the soles of my boots, up my shins, into my teeth, and right into the raw black scar tissue of my left palm.

*You're tired,* the hum whispered from the open tin. *Your chest hurts. Your arm is screaming. Your brother is made of stone and he meant to kill you. Take one. Just one to steady your hands. You don't have to end the world today. Just take the edge off so you can breathe.*

"Look at this rock," Marguerite said, gesturing with her chin toward the statue above her head. Her voice went low and warm, the after-meeting voice she used when we were stacking the metal chairs against the dead dryers on Fifth and she put her hand on my shoulder. "It's wedged right between the main gate-shoes, Ena. Six tons of Purgatory granite jammed in the track. If your friends up top knock the winch-pin out when the tide turns, that basalt drop-gate is going to hit this plinth and hang up six feet above the mud. The sea will flood the trench, sure, and four hours later when the tide goes out again, the gap under the gate will still be open. The fissure to the Nursery will still be open. And every hound left in those tunnels is going to crawl right up the weir steps into your little stilt-village and eat those fisherwomen in their beds."

She held the open blue tin out over the mud between us. Her burn-scarred forearm trembled with the effort of keeping it level.

"You can't move six tons of stone with a crowbar and two clean hands," Marguerite said. "You're just a ninety-pound girl with bad lungs and a hole in her sternum right now. You couldn't lift that broken stone arm out of the mud by yourself. Take the tin, Ena. Put four shards in. Just four. Shove this statue out of the gate track, smash the plinth to gravel so the drop-stone seals flat on the bedrock, and burn the rest of the pack down in the fissure while you're at it. Nobody up on the cliff has to see the red in your eyes through this fog. Your demon won't know. We'll reset your count tomorrow morning. Day one. Together. That's what a sponsor's for."

I stood in the mud. My boots were sinking half an inch deeper with every breath.

My left hand was pulling against my right fingers like a live animal on a chain. I could feel the exact shape of those black splinters against my palm before I even touched them: the cold sting first, like dry ice on a burn, and then the hiss as the glass melted through the skin, and then the huge, warm, crimson quiet rolling up through my veins and turning off the pain in my arm, turning off the cold rain, turning off the sight of my brother's terrified nineteen-year-old stone eyes and the smear of stone peanut butter on my fifteen-year-old thumb.

"It's a sacrifice," I heard myself whisper. The words tasted like copper and dead water. "What Eri said after the laundromat. If you use to save people, it doesn't count as a relapse."

*That was a lie when Eri said it,* Kio said, his voice sharp and steady inside my skull. *And look at the gate track, Ena. Look with my eyes, not hers.*

I blinked the sweat out of my lashes. Kio tilted our chin up two inches, sighting along the massive iron-bound grooves cut into the vertical basalt pilings on either side of us.

The heavy stone drop-channel didn't run where the plinth was wedged. The main iron shoes ran four feet behind the statue, flush against the raw bedrock wall over the Nursery fault. When the eighty-ton slab of basalt came down from the chains above, it wasn't going to hit the plinth at all.
