It was going to shear right down behind it, sealing the fissure shut and crushing the stone square forward into the tide-race as the ocean came behind it.

*She's lying about the gate track,* Kio said calmly. *The plinth is clear of the drop-line by forty-eight inches. She doesn't care if the gate seals. Her leg is gangrenous and she knows she cannot climb the iron rungs out of the basin before the Spring Tide comes in. She wants you to put the glass in your hand so you will go down into the fissure and clean out her kennels for her before she dies.*

*And because she can't stand dropping the leash while the dog can still walk,* Harumi said.

I felt Harumi step forward inside our chest.

In the old days, when Harumi came to the front, there was a tearing sound in my ears like wet canvas ripping, and then red light, and then I would wake up hours later on a roof or a highway or a tile floor with my left arm smoking and black glass in the seams of my boots.

There was no tearing sound. There was no red light.

I felt my spine straighten in the wet canvas jacket. I felt my jaw set in the exact hard, crooked line Harumi wore when she laughed at the meetings. And I was still right there inside my own skin with her. Sunny was holding onto the hem of our ribs on the left, watching the box of eleven Sharpies. Kio was holding our knees steady in the shale mud. All four of us were looking out of the same blue eyes at the old woman leaning against our brother's monument.

"You told the packs where the laundromat was," Harumi said out loud with our mouth.

Our voice didn't drop into the old backward prophecy rasp. It was just a woman's voice, rough from salt air and four days of screaming vomit up on the cliff, flat and clear against the wet rock.

Marguerite's thumb froze on the edge of the blue button tin.

"You left pinches of black glass in the alley behind the dryers on Fifth," we said. "Eri saw it when we pulled the rubble down around the Nursery vault. And before that, when I was on my hands and knees in the dark at three in the morning picking up the pieces of a nineteen-year-old boy who brought me the good chair, you'd already been there. You swept up Dov and your grandkids off the tile and put them in your mother's button tin and left it on a pile of bricks two blocks from my roof so the sun would hit it when I walked to Tim's grave."

Marguerite stared at us. The drill-sergeant warmth drained out of her face, leaving only the gray skin and the white burn scars and the smell of her rotting leg.

"You needed a reason," Marguerite said, and for the first time her voice had no meeting in it at all. It was just the Kennel Master under Tombstone, counting inventory. "You were going to use anyway, Ena. I watched you go behind that water tank on day thirty-two. Nobody made you climb those stairs. Nobody made you tip thirteen shards into your hand on a Friday night. And nobody made you move your hand when Dov opened his mouth behind the dryers. You did that because you didn't want your little one-eyed friend to stop clapping for you."

"Yeah," I said. That was me, Ena, speaking right alongside Harumi without taking a breath between us. "I did. I killed Dov because I was high and I was a coward and I didn't want Eri to know I'd been lying to her every Tuesday for a month. That one's mine. It'll be mine for the rest of my life."

"And the rest of it is yours, bitch," Harumi said, taking a step forward through the black mud.

Marguerite flinched back against the stone plinth, her rusted revolver coming up half an inch before she remembered the cylinder was empty.

"You sat in a circle of people who were trying not to die," Harumi said, stopping four feet from the open blue tin. "You made them drink burnt barley out of styrofoam and say their names while you kept a map on your wall and bred glass dogs in the ruins. You fed Petrosyan and the sisters and Dov to your grandkids so you could see if your gun still fired. And now your leg's eaten through and the sea's coming back in twenty minutes and you're still standing here holding out a tin of dead boy and dog shit, hoping I'll put my collar back on for you."

"They're under the slab!" Marguerite shouted, saliva flying from her gray lips as she shook the open tin at us. Two black shards bounced over the rim and dropped into the mud by her boot, hissing faintly as the salt water touched them. "Do you hear me? There's forty of them down in the dark under the Nursery floor! If you don't take the glass and burn them out before the water comes, they'll swim up the weir channels! You're a weapon, Ena! That's all you ever were! Your brother knew it in the kitchen! Tombstone knew it! Himo knew it! You can't sit on a wooden porch with a cat and pretend you're a person while my dogs are still breathing under the rock!"

"Watch us," Harumi said.

She didn't reach for the tin. She didn't slap it out of Marguerite's hand or smash Marguerite's face into the granite the way the red hum would have done.

Instead, Kio reached down to the base of the plinth, where a three-foot length of rusted iron tie-rod from the collapsed Nursery roof lay half-submerged in the kelp beside Tim's broken stone forearm. Our right hand closed around the heavy iron spike. Our left hand, black-scarred and trembling in the cold air, stayed curled tight against our ribs, empty.

"Go climb the maintenance ladder by the west pillar if you want to live, Marguerite," I said. "The iron rungs go all the way to the cliff road. If you start dragging yourself up now, you might make the top ledge before the gate drops and the water hits."

"I can't climb sixty feet on one leg!" Marguerite snarled, clutching the blue tin to her chest with both hands now, her knuckles white over her burn scars. "Take the shards! Carry me up! That's what you do! You save people and then we reset the count!"

"The meeting's over," I told her. "Stack your own fucking chairs."

Marguerite stared at my empty black left hand, then up at my blue eyes. Whatever she saw in them, four people looking out of one face without a drop of red light anywhere in the iris, made her breath catch in her throat.

Slowly, cursing under her breath in a thin, whistling monotone, she shoved the lid back onto the blue button tin. Wait-time was running out; a deeper boom echoed from the seaward mouth of the three-mile cavern as the outer shelf began to fill. Clutching the tin inside her torn coat, Marguerite turned away from the plinth and began dragging herself on her elbows and one good knee through the black mud toward the low stone rubble-slope at the west wall. Halfway to the iron ladder, the tin slipped from her greasy fingers through the tear in her coat and landed face-up in a shallow puddle of green sea-foam near the base of the east winch-steps. She didn't even notice. She was already hauling herself up the bottom iron rung with both burn-scarred arms, grunting as her torn calf trailed blood down the basalt.

We turned our back on her.

Now there was only the statue in the tide.

I stepped up onto the lower shelf of the cracked granite plinth, my wet boots slipping a little on the purple mussels before Kio braced our heels against a seam in the rock.

Up close, the stone boy and the stone girl were exactly our height.

I reached out with my scarred left hand and touched the cheek of the fifteen-year-old stone girl. The granite was freezing cold and wet with salt spray. I traced the small plastic hair clip at the back of her stone head, then moved my fingers down to her left thumb, where the sculptor of Purgatory had carved the tiny raised smear of peanut butter above the unlifted butter knife.

*She didn't fight him,* Sunny whispered inside us, and her tears were already hot on my real cheeks, mixing with the cold mist dropping from the sluice-gate above. *We had the butter knife right in our hand and we didn't even pick it up. We were just making lunch because there were no groceries left except the jar in the back of the cupboard and the bread was going stale.*

"I know, little one," I whispered.

I turned from the stone girl and looked across the narrow gap of granite into my brother's stone face.

For so long, every time I had thought about that Tuesday in the kitchen, my mind had split into pieces to keep from looking at his eyes. I had given the terror to Sunny and locked her in the dark with the Sunday pancakes. I had given the cold counting of days and rules to Kio. I had given the rage and the knife in our sternum and the red hum to Harumi. And I had sat on a mattress under a leaky tarp with an orange cat missing half an ear, telling lies to a piece of rebar-scratched rock on a hill because a rock on a hill didn't have a face.

This rock had a face.

I looked straight at Tim's stone eyes. I saw the certainty in them, the terrible one-second relief of a nineteen-year-old boy who had carried the whole whispering town on his back since our parents died on the kitchen floor and had finally broken under the weight of the casserole woman's fingers on his wrist.

And right beside the certainty, I looked down at the shoebox of eleven dead black Sharpies wedged between his barnacle-crusted boots, and the silver helmet with the scratched *H* sitting beside the cold tuna casserole.

*He meant it,* Harumi said. Her voice was shaking inside our chest. I had never felt Harumi shake before. Not when Tombstone fell, not when Galactix burned, not when we punched the sixty-foot glass tank in the Nursery vault. *For one second he wanted us dead because he believed what they wrote on the locker. Both things are true, Ena. He crossed it out every morning for five years until his markers dried up, and then on Tuesday he picked up the bread knife and meant every fucking inch of it.*

*And the second after that, when the red went out of our eyes, he wanted to take it back,* Kio said softly. *And you cannot stop a knife halfway.*

Harumi let out a sound through our throat that tore my vocal cords raw.

It wasn't a laugh. It wasn't the prophecy drain-voice. It was a long, ragged, ugly sob that doubled us over against the wet granite between the stone boy and the stone girl.

I pressed my forehead against Tim's stone chest, right over the pocket of our dad's carved flannel shirt, while Harumi wept through my eyes. She cried with her whole chest, five years of dry, burning rage breaking open all at once into hot salt water that ran down the cold grey stone of my brother's shirt and dripped onto the shoebox of dried-out Sharpies at his feet. Sunny cried with her, holding onto the memory of the little smiley faces he used to draw under the word BULLSHIT before first bell so we would laugh when we opened our locker. Kio stood quiet inside our trembling legs, keeping our boots anchored on the mussel-shell ledge while the deep cavern out to sea boomed a second time, closer now, the first heavy groundswell of the incoming Spring Tide beginning to push white foam around the base of the sluice-pillars.

"You idiot," Harumi sobbed into the cold stone of Tim's chest, our left hand flat against his granite collarbone while our right hand gripped the rusted iron spike. "You stupid, scared nineteen-year-old idiot. You should have thrown the casserole in the yard. You should have asked me who I was."

"He didn't know how," I wept alongside her, my cheek scraping the barnacles on his lapel. "Nobody knew how. We were fifteen and he was nineteen and Himo climbed in the window and broke the whole house."

I looked down at the green brass plaque bolted to the base of the plinth, where the seawater was already lapping over the bottom edge of the metal.

FULFILLED A PROPHECY HE ONCE CALLED BULLSHIT.

"We're not leaving that on him," Harumi said fiercely, wiping our streaming nose and eyes on the wet sleeve of our jacket. "Not down here under the mud. If the ocean's going to bury this square forever, it's not burying him with Purgatory's plaque on his shoes."

*We have four minutes before the lower basin floods past the east steps,* Kio said. He didn't tell us to hurry or drop the iron rod. Instead, he shifted his grip inside our right hand, locking our thumb and forefinger around the rusted tie-rod at the exact angle to chisel wet granite without snapping the wrist. *The brass bolts are rotten. Wedge the point under the left rivet first.*

Together, all four of us leaned our weight into the iron spike.

We jammed the chisel-end of the tie-rod behind the green brass plaque and wrenched backward with both hands. The corroded bronze bolts shrieked once and sheared off in the wet stone. The plaque popped loose and splashed face-down into the black mud, sinking out of sight under the dark kelp.

Beneath where the brass had been, the granite of the plinth was smooth and dark gray, untouched by barnacles.

On the hill above our ruined town, I had spent three days carving my brother's headstone alone, blacking out whenever my hand shook too hard, letting Harumi hate me from the dark while I carved his half of the apology into the rock and pretended it made us clean.

Now my scarred left hand held the shaft of the iron spike steady against the stone, and my right hand hammered the butt of the rod with a heavy cobble from the mud. Kio aimed the point. Harumi drove the weight of our shoulder behind every blow. Sunny counted the letters out loud so we wouldn't run out of room before the edge of the rock. Ena held them all together in the cold salt spray.

Sparks and wet gray granite dust flew against our knuckles. The skin over our right thumb split open and bled down the rusted iron, mixing with the stone grit, and none of us flinched.

First we drove a deep horizontal groove straight across the ghost-outline where the Purgatory plaque had been, crossing the phantom words out from edge to edge the way a fat black marker crosses out a sentence on a school locker door.

Underneath that deep scar, in leaning, jagged capitals half an inch deep in the granite, we carved the four lines before the foaming green tide-water rose over the top of our boots:

TIM.
BROTHER.
I'M SORRY.
ME TOO.

And down in the bottom right corner, just before the cold swirl of the incoming sea washed over the stone shelf and filled the fresh-cut grooves with white foam, Sunny guided our raw, bleeding hands to scratch one tiny circle with two dots and a crooked curved line underneath.

A little smiley face in the stone, six fathoms under the world, where the tide was coming in.

"Come on," Kio said, his voice ringing clear as an iron bell as water surged to our knees around the plinth. "Up the east steps. The sea is in the throat."



Chapter Seventeen: Two Words in the Rain

The Spring Tide did not come into the six-mile trench like waves on a beach. It came like a wall of black quarry stone falling down a stairwell.

Up on the high cliff rim above the east sluice-pillar, the rain had turned from coastal mist to a blinding horizontal downpour that smelled of lightning and churned kelp. I lay flat on my belly on the wet basalt shelf at the top of the ravine, my chin hanging over the sixty-foot drop, my one good eye squinting through the sheets of gray water pouring off my hair.

Both of my shins were strapped into heavy whalebone-and-canvas splints that Wren and the weir-doctor had laced tight from my ankles to my kneecaps after they hauled me out of the Nursery drainage pipe. Every time I shifted my weight on the cold rock, the unhealed fractures inside my tibias ground together like broken crockery inside a sack.

I didn't care about my legs. I had spent six hours crawling on my elbows through broken glass and green tank-slime when Himo's dead-eyed babies threw me out like trash; a couple of splinted shins in the rain was nothing.

What I cared about was the girl climbing the narrow stone winch-steps forty feet below me.

"Move your ass, Ena!" I screamed down into the roar of the gorge. The wind tore the words right out of my teeth and threw them backward over the mudflats. "The outer shelf is gone! It's in the chute!"

Three miles out to sea, where the great subterranean fault opened into the deep cold water beyond the weir-pilings, a column of white spray shot two hundred feet into the dark sky. The sound hit us a second later: a low, chest-crushing concussion as the first bore of the tide slammed into the narrow rock throat and raced inland toward the drained Nursery basin.

Down in the boiling green swirl at the base of the statue, the water was already six feet deep. I saw Tim's stone head and the plastic clip in fifteen-year-old Ena's granite hair vanish under a crest of white foam and torn bladderwrack. Far across the trench, on the west maintenance ladder, Marguerite had made it thirty feet up the iron rungs before her bitten leg gave out; she was lashed to the ladder by her canvas coat-belt now, clinging to the wet iron with her burn-scarred arms while the rising surge hammered the rock ten feet beneath her boots.

And on the east side, climbing the steep, slick basalt steps toward the massive iron winch-platform that held up the eighty-ton drop-gate, was Ena.

She didn't have the red glow anymore.

After the old highway, after I had dragged myself out of the ruined Nursery vault behind Pia's mule-cart and found Ena lying face-down in the gray dust by the guardrail with the song burned out of her throat and Himo gone into the ruins, the red light had died out of her skin completely. Now, in the pouring gray rain, she looked terrifyingly small. Her blonde hair, hacked short with a dull knife months ago, was plastered dark and flat to her skull. Her left arm hung stiffly from the shoulder, black-scarred from knuckles to elbow, twitching in jagged involuntary jerks every three steps as withdrawal tore at her nerves.

Twenty feet below my cliff rim, the narrow stone stair ended at the winch-ledge.

It was a shelf of raw, wet basalt no wider than a tabletop, jutting out directly beside the main suspension chains of the sluice-gate. An iron locking-pin as thick as a man's thigh ran through the master link of the chain, holding the eighty-ton slab of iron-bound rock suspended above the mouth of the Nursery fissure. Once that pin was driven backward out of the collar, the gate would drop sixty feet straight down into the bedrock seat, sealing the vault and the hounds and the broken god-tank under ten thousand tons of stone and sea-mud forever.

Wait-chains had been rigged to pull the pin from the top of the cliff with the mule-team, except the salt-rust had welded the iron collar shut and the haul-rope had snapped clean in two ten minutes ago.

Somebody had to hammer the pin out by hand on the ledge.

Ena pulled herself up onto the winch-shelf on her knees, gasping for air, her right hand still gripping the three-foot rusted iron tie-rod she had carried up from the statue.

And right there on the bottom step of the ledge, washed into a shallow depression of the rock by the rising spray where Marguerite had dropped it on her crawl across the basin, lay the blue button tin.

The lid had come halfway off in the wash.

From twenty feet above, through the driving rain and my own shaking wet fingers on the cliff edge, I felt the hum coming out of that little blue box.

I am a demon of the mind. Even waterlogged, one-eyed, and crippled in both legs, I can feel what residue does to the air around a person's skull. Those twenty-two shards of black glass, half hound-bone and half the nineteen-year-old boy from the laundromat on Fifth, were singing straight up into Ena's face as she knelt on the stone step.

I watched her freeze.

Her knees hit the wet rock six inches from the tin. Her blackened left hand came up off the stone and hovered over the open rim, her fingers curling and uncurling like spider legs over a stove.

I didn't think twice. I dropped my forehead onto the wet basalt of the cliff rim and threw my mind down into hers, the way I had done on the floor of the Nursery vault, the way I had done to ten thousand damned souls by the river in hell when I needed to turn somebody around at the soft part.

*Ena!* I shouted inside her skull, reaching for the steering wheel behind her eyes to yank her hand back from the glass.

I hit a wall.

It wasn't a locked door like the ones Marguerite built in her head, and it wasn't the huge, roaring crimson furnace of the corruption song that had knocked me flat on the vault floor.

It was four people standing shoulder to shoulder in a dry room with the windows wide open.

As soon as my mind touched hers, I felt all four of them look back at me at once. There was no hiding behind the water tank. There was no secret mattress count.

I felt Ena's terrible, wrenching physical sickness: the bile in her throat, the fishhook spasm in her left ulna, the screaming biological hunger of an addict looking down at twenty-two hits of pure relief while her muscles cramped in the cold rain.

Right next to Ena, I felt Harumi, raw-eyed from weeping on her brother's stone chest, her jaw set like iron, staring down at the black glass with pure, cold hatred.

Behind them both, I felt Sunny holding onto a mental picture of a little smiley face carved in wet granite under the foam, and Kio counting the seconds between the roar of the tidal bore in the cavern and the tremor in the basalt beneath our knees.

*Get out of my head, Eri,* Harumi said to me.

She didn't say it mean. She didn't push me out with powers. She just stood there inside Ena's skin, holding Ena's shaking left wrist from the inside while Kio held the right, and looked up at me on the cliff ledge with Ena's wet blue eyes.

*Don't push me,* Ena thought back at me, and her own voice inside her head was trembling like a plucked wire, naked and ashamed and completely steady all at once. *Please, Eri. Don't use the demon trick on me. If you make me drop it with your head, I'll never know if I put it down myself.*

I pulled my mind back out of her so fast my breath caught in my chest. My fingernails dug into the wet lichen on the cliff rim until the nails tore.

"Then put it down, babe!" I yelled down through the rain with my own raw throat, no mind-push, no demon fingers, just a wet, crippled idiot screaming over a sixty-foot drop. "Look at me! Don't you fucking touch it!"

Down on the winch-shelf, the first surging crest of the tidal bore exploded out of the cavern mouth at the south end of the trench.

It was twenty feet high, black and white and tumbling with ripped kelp, broken timber from the outer weirs, and drowned glass hounds swept up from the lower fault. The wave hit the statue of Purgatory first. Six tons of gray granite tilted, lifted off its cracked base in the boiling foam, and rolled over into the deep channel with a dull underwater boom that shook the cliff under my belly.

Green water shot ten feet up the east wall, drenching Ena to the waist on the winch-ledge.

She reached down with her trembling black left hand and picked up her mother's sponsor's blue button tin.

My heart stopped in my throat. For one horrible second I thought of the Nursery floor: me telling her *Break it*, me handing my best friend the end of the world because I wanted Himo to turn around, me setting this whole nightmare in motion because I was too selfish to tell her *Don't*.

Ena held the open blue tin in her scarred left palm. She looked straight down at the twenty-two black shards humming in the wet rag.

"Dov," she said out loud. I couldn't hear the word over the thunder of the water, yet I read the shape of it on her wet lips from twenty feet up.

She didn't pick out a shard. She didn't close the lid and slip it into her jacket pocket for Tuesday.

She turned her left wrist over and tipped the blue button tin upside down over the edge of the winch-ledge.

All twenty-two shards of black glass poured out into the empty air, glinting once like drops of oil in the grey rain, and fell sixty feet straight down into the roaring black mouth of the Nursery fissure just as the thirty-foot wall of the main Spring Tide slammed into the basin below. Then she opened her black fingers and dropped the empty blue tin in after them.

"Pull your hands back!" I screamed as she spun toward the iron winch-collar.

She couldn't use the chain-pull. With both hands wrapped around the three-foot rusted iron tie-rod she had used to carve her brother's stone, Ena braced her boots against the slick basalt wall and drove the heavy butt of the iron spike straight into the jammed locking-pin of the sluice-gate.

*Clang.*

The first blow jarred her whole frame so hard I saw her left shoulder buckle from the nerve cramps. Her bare right palm, already split from chiseling the plinth, painted the wet iron red.

*Clang.*

The rusted pin shifted half an inch in the collar, screeching against eight decades of salt-scale. Below the ledge, the rising sea was boiling twenty feet below her boots now, slamming drowned hound carcasses and broken Nursery beams against the vertical gate-tracks.

*Clang.*

On the third blow, Ena threw every ounce of her weight and Harumi's fury and Kio's leverage into the rod.

The thick iron locking-pin shot backward out of the master link and sparked off the cliff wall into the abyss.

Freed all at once from its sixty-foot chains, the eighty-ton slab of iron-bound basalt dropped like a guillotine blade.

The noise tore the sky in half. Huge links of iron chain whipped upward through the wooden winch-housing in a shower of orange sparks and shattered oak beams. The massive stone gate sheared down through the grooved pilings, smashed straight through the rising foam at the bottom of the gorge, and drove its wedge-cut foot eight feet deep into the soft shale roof of the Nursery vault.

The entire cliff shuddered from the impact. A geyser of dirty green water and pulverized rock shot fifty feet into the rain, burying the fault, the vault, the tanks, the hounds, and the twenty-two black shards under an avalanche of collapsed sea-floor and roaring gray ocean.

And the recoil of the whipping master-chain tore the stone winch-ledge right out from under Ena's boots.

I saw the crack open across the wet basalt behind her heels like a black lightning bolt. The shelf tilted outward toward the foaming pit below.

Ena dropped the iron tie-rod and scrambled forward on her belly as the rock gave way beneath her waist. Her boots kicked at empty air over a forty-foot drop into churning white water and jagged slate. Her right hand caught a wet projection of shale on the vertical cliff face just below my overhang, five feet down from where I lay, and then the shale sheared off in her bleeding fingers.

She was going down.

In the Nursery, when the glass tank broke and she walked out glowing red toward the highway, I had lain on the floor in the warm green water because my shins were in pieces and I couldn't stand up to stop her. I had watched her walk away without turning around, and I had told myself there was nothing a mind demon with broken legs could do about physical weight.

"Fuck that," I snarled into the mud.

I didn't use my mind. Mind demons crack heads, and you can't hold up ninety pounds of wet human girl with a psychic push.

I dragged myself forward over the jagged lip of the cliff overhang until my ribs and hips were hanging out over the forty-foot drop into the boiling sluice-basin. There was a narrow, ragged fissure in the basalt three feet back from the edge. I jammed both of my splinted shins straight down into the stone crack, hooking my boot-heels under an outcrop of granite to anchor my weight, and lunged down over the drop with both ungloved hands just as the last shelf gave way under Ena's chest.

My hands clamped onto her left wrist.

Her scarred, blackened, shaking left wrist.

The full dead-drop weight of her body hit my shoulders like a falling mule.

Behind me, wedged in the basalt crack, one of the heavy whalebone splints on my left shin snapped in half with a wet, sickening *crack* like a pistol shot. The broken bone inside my leg shifted under the canvas wrappings. A white-hot bolt of agony shot up my thigh and burst behind my single eye in a spray of yellow stars, so bright and vicious I bit clean through my own lower lip and tasted hot blood pouring down my chin with the rain.

My wet palms slid two inches down Ena's slick black forearm toward her wrist bones.

Below her dangling boots, the trapped Spring Tide was slamming against the newly dropped sluice-gate like a herd of crazed whales, throwing white spray and splintered timber thirty feet up the rock walls.

Ena looked up at me.

Her face was two feet below mine in the driving rain. Her short blonde hair was plastered across her forehead. Her eyes were wide, terrifyingly blue, with zero red at the edges. She saw my splinted legs wedged into the stone crevice behind me; she saw the fresh blood soaking rapidly through the torn canvas trousers over my left shin; she saw the veins standing out like cords in my neck as my shoulders threatened to tear out of their sockets.

"Eri," she choked out, hanging over the abyss by the exact black left hand she had used to melt shards and kill Dov and punch the tank. "Your legs. Let go. You'll break them again, let go."

"Shut the fuck up!" I screamed down into her face, blood and rainwater spraying from my split lip. "If you let go of my wrist I'll follow you down to hell and read your diary out loud to every demon on the river! Get your right hand on my belt! Climb, you stupid blonde bitch, climb!"

I locked my fingers around the scarred black skin of her left wrist until my fingernails cut into my own palms. I didn't let go of the bad hand. I held onto the part of her she hated most, the black-stained skin where every lie and every relapse lived, and I braced my bleeding shins deeper into the jagged rock crack, screaming through my clenched teeth as the fractured tibia ground against the whalebone splint, and I hauled backward with every muscle in my back and belly.

Ena's right hand came up and clawed into the wet leather of my coat belt.

Kio took her feet, finding a quarter-inch lip of basalt on the vertical cliff face with the toe of her right boot and kicking upward at the exact second I heaved with my shoulders. Harumi threw their left elbow over the wet granite lip of the overhang, scraping the skin off their forearm to the raw pink meat.

With one last wrenching heave that made the sky go black around the edges of my one eye, I dragged her torso over the cliff rim.

We collapsed together in a tangled, muddy heap three feet back from the drop, rolling over and over in the black peat mud and wet heather until we hit the low stone wall of the weir-track.

Below us, the six-mile trench filled to the high-water mark with a deafening, hollow thunder. The sea buried the sluice-gate. It buried the Nursery fault. It buried the gray stone boy and the gray stone girl at the bottom of the dark water, forty fathoms down, where no damned soul would ever walk past and laugh at their plaque again.

Up on the cliff rim, there was only the pouring gray rain and the sound of the two of us trying to breathe.

I lay on my back in two inches of mud. My left leg was throbbing so hard from ankle to hip that every heartbeat made my stomach heave, and dark red blood was washing out through the canvas lacings of my broken splint and turning the rainwater pink around my knee. My right eye was full of mud and salt. My lower lip was split to the gum.

Right beside me, close enough that her wet shoulder was pressed hard against mine in the dirt, Ena lay curled on her side.

She was shaking from head to toe in violent, jerking tremors as the cold rain and the withdrawal crash and the adrenaline hit her all at once. Her right hand was bleeding from the split thumb where she had hammered the spike. Her left arm, black to the elbow, lay palm-up in the mud between us, empty.

For a long time neither of us moved. The rain beat down on our faces, washing the gray granite dust off her knuckles and the blood off my chin.

Finally, I turned my head in the mud and looked at her with my one eye.

She was looking back at me.

I didn't have to go into her head to know who was looking at me. I could see it in the way her eyes held mine without blinking, in the way her mouth was trembling at the corners while her jaw stayed square. Ena was there. Harumi was there. Little Sunny was tucked right behind the wet blue irises, and Kio was counting our breaths in the rain.

"You dropped the tin," I croaked. My voice sounded like a crow swallowing gravel.

"Yeah," Ena whispered. Her teeth chattered so hard she bit the inside of her cheek. "All twenty-two."

"And your arm hurts like a motherfucker."

"Like somebody's skinning it with an oyster knife," Harumi said through Ena's chattering teeth, and then Ena's own voice came right behind it without a hitch: "It's going to hurt tomorrow too. And Tuesday."

I let out a wet, broken noise that was half a laugh and half a sob, and reached out with my mud-caked right hand to lay my palm flat over her shaking black left forearm in the dirt. Her skin wasn't hot like a stove anymore. It was freezing cold, goose-pimpled and scarred and completely human.

"Look at my shin," I told her, nodding my chin toward my mangled whalebone splint in the mud. "Wren's going to beat me to death with my own crutch when we get back to the pier. She spent four hours lacing that thing."

Ena looked at the blood soaking through my trousers. Her face crumpled.

"You stood on them," Ena whispered, tears spilling over her wet eyelashes into the mud on her temples. "In the vault your legs were broken in six places and you wedged them into a rock crack for me. Eri, after I left you on the floor in the green water. After I lied to you every Tuesday for months. Day forty-one when it was day nine. Dov. The tins. All of it."

"Shut up," I said softly, my thumb rubbing the cold black scar tissue on the back of her wrist. "Don't you start doing the martyr thing where you act like you were the only liar on the roof."

I looked up at the gray sky pouring water down on us. My chest felt hollow and raw, as if the sixty-foot sluice-gate had dropped right through my own ribs and pinned the thing I had been carrying for ten thousand years under the sea-mud.

"Did she say it?" I asked the rain.

Ena went very still beside me.

I already knew the answer. I had seen the highway in her head when Pia's mule-cart found her in the ruins four days ago, though we hadn't spoken a single word about it out loud while we worked the weir chains to drain the basin. I wanted to hear her say it with her mouth in the rain.

"She said she was sorry," Ena said quietly, her wet shoulder pressed tight against mine in the mud. "On her knees in the road, with her stitches torn open and her hands full of gray dust where Chimera went out. I told her to say it for you. For Nine. For your legs on the floor of the Nursery and ten thousand years at the edge of the pit."

"And she didn't," I said.

"She said it to the dust," Ena said. Her voice didn't waver. She wasn't lying to me to make me feel better, the way she used to lie on Thursdays and Tuesdays. "She said it to Chimera. She didn't even hear your name when I said it, Eri. She's never going to turn around for you. Or for me. Or for my parents on the kitchen floor, or for Tim, or for the girl in the jar. She doesn't have it in her. Nobody who broke us is ever coming back down that highway to apologize."

The rain ran into my empty left eye socket and down over the old scar on my cheek.

Ten thousand years.

Since the day Himo stood at the edge of the pit with her hand on my back and said *Don't get attached* and pushed me in, I had built my whole crooked, spiteful life around two words. I had pulled people's worst days out of their skulls like splinters and sold them by a river in hell. I had lain on the wet tile of the Nursery next to six dead smiling ushers, looking up at my best friend glowing red with twenty-two shards of a dead kid in her blood, and when she begged me to tell her *Don't*, when she said *You're the only one I'd listen to, tell me not to and I'll walk out*, I had looked at her and said *Break it*.

I had handed Ena the glass tank and the voice of every dead god and sent her out to finish a prophecy that would burn her alive, all because I wanted a woman in a white coat to turn around and say *Sorry, Nine*.

And the woman in the white coat had cried over somebody else's dust and walked off into the ruins without ever once thinking my name.

"We're a pair of assholes, aren't we," I whispered, staring up at the clouds until the rainwater blurred my one good eye.

"Yeah," Harumi said softly from Ena's mouth. "World-class."

I turned my head on the muddy grass so I was looking straight into Ena's face again, three inches away.

"You asked me to tell you not to," I said, and my voice broke right down the middle, ugly and thick with ten thousand years of waiting on a floor for somebody to turn around. "In the Nursery. You knelt down by my legs and you looked at me with red in your eyes and you said *Tell me not to, Eri, please, you're the only one I'd listen to*. And I knew you were relapsing. I saw Dov in your head, and the blue tin, and Harumi laughing at the song, and I could have reached in and turned you around with one push, or I could have just held your hand and told you to come home to the roof."

My right hand tightened on her cold black wrist in the mud. I couldn't stop shaking now either. It wasn't just the broken splint on my shin; it was the truth coming up out of the back of my throat like salt water.

"I told you to punch the glass," I sobbed, pressing my wet forehead against her temple in the dirt. "I used you, Ena. You came to save me and I used your addiction like a crowbar because I wanted Himo to say two fucking words to me, and I didn't care if the song ate you alive as long as I got to hear them. I'm no better than Marguerite leaving tins on a brick pile. I handed you the needle and told you to go kill my mother for me."

Ena didn't pull her wrist away.

Instead, her bleeding right hand came up out of the mud and caught the back of my soaked neck, pulling my forehead hard against hers until our noses bumped in the rain.

"I'm sorry, Ena," I wept against her cold skin, saying the two words I had spent my whole existence waiting to hear from a creator who didn't care if I lived or died, giving them to the only person in any universe who had ever come back through a door when I called. "I'm sorry, Harumi. I'm sorry, Sunny. I'm sorry, Kio. I shouldn't have told you to break it. I should have told you to come home."

For three heartbeats, there was only the roar of the sea in the filled trench below us and the drum of rain on our backs.

Then I felt all four of them breathe out at once against my cheek.

"I'm sorry too, Eri," Ena whispered into my mouth, her tears warm against the cold mud on my face. "For every Tuesday on the roof. For lying to you when you bumped my shoulder and said you were proud of me. For walking out of the vault and not looking back."

"Me too, one-eye," Harumi rasped right behind her, and her black left hand turned over in the mud and laced its scarred fingers tight through mine. "You still owe me a can of peaches for climbing that fucking ladder."

*And you dropped your splint in the crack,* Kio added gently, using Ena's bleeding right thumb to wipe the blood from my split lower lip. *If we do not bind your left tibia before the rain chills the muscle, you will not walk for six weeks.*

*We can ride in Pia's cart with the goat,* Sunny said through Ena's wet, crooked smile.

I lay there in the black mud on the cliff edge with my broken leg throbbing and my split lip bleeding and my fingers locked tight through Ena's scarred black hand, and for the first time in ten thousand years, the jar on the shelf at the back of my head stopped humming.

Nobody had come back down the old highway to apologize to Nine.

Nobody needed to.

She was right here in the dirt with me, holding my hand in the rain, and she had turned all the way around.



Chapter Eighteen: Day One on the Water

Thirty-one days after the sluice-gate dropped into the fault, the morning tide came up under the cedar pilings of our house at Salt-Reach with a soft, sleepy slap like a dog licking a bowl.

I woke up before the sun cleared the eastern mudflats.

For a few seconds, lying on the wide ticking mattress under two wool blankets that smelled of cedar smoke and salt hay, my body did the old panic check before my eyes were even open.

My left hand went straight to the center of my chest.

Through the faded flannel of my sleep-shirt, my fingers found the thick, puckered ridge of scar tissue over my sternum where the bread knife had gone in when I was fifteen. Because it had rained out beyond the sandbar around three in the morning, the deep cartilage under the scar ached with a dull, familiar toothache throb. My left forearm, lying across my stomach, was stiff from the wrist to the elbow; the skin there would stay black as coal tar until the day I died, criss-crossed with pale silver lines where fourteen shards and then twenty-two shards had burned their way into the veins and never quite come out.

I waited for the hum.

I waited for that cold, greasy fishhook in my ulna to start whispering about loose bricks behind the water tank, or for the tearing sound in my ears that meant I had lost six hours of the night and woken up in a strange alley with glass under my fingernails.

There was no tearing sound. There was only the slow, rhythmic creak of the hemp mooring-lines down at the boat landing, and the warmth of the small room around me.

*It is five-fifteen,* Kio said softly from the quiet parlor behind my forehead. There were no locked iron doors between us anymore, no thick gray wool stuffing the hallways of our skull. I could feel him waking up the way you hear a housemate turning the pages of an almanac in the next room over. *Low slack turns to flood in twenty minutes. The wind is south-southwest off the kelp beds. Your pulse is sixty-four. The ache in the sternum is only the drop in barometric pressure from the squall at three.*

*Is the cat still on our feet?* Sunny asked, warm and sleepy behind my ribs.

I lifted my head an inch off the goose-feather pillow and looked down the length of the gray wool blanket.

Curled squarely across my ankles like an eighteen-pound loaf of burnt sourdough bread sat Tim. He was bright orange, missing half of his left ear from an argument with a wharf rat back in the city ruins, and currently purring like a rusty sawmill while digging one hind claw gently into my shin through the quilt to remind me that breakfast was five minutes overdue.

"He's here," I whispered out loud.

Across the four feet of scrubbed pine floorboards between our two low cots, a mass of tangled dark hair poked out from under a patchwork quilt.

Eri was asleep on her stomach with her face mashed sideways into her pillow. Her black leather eye-patch was hanging from the bedpost by its strap, alongside her heavy ironwood crutch with the brass ferrule that Wren had turned for her on the village lathe.

Even in the dim gray dawn coming through the oiled-paper window, I saw Eri's shoulders twitch.

Her breath hitched twice in her throat. Her right hand clawed at the edge of the mattress, her bruised knuckles tightening on the ticking the way they had tightened on the wet shale of the drainage pipe when she was crawling away from the Nursery vault on her elbows. A bead of sweat rolled down her temple past the sunken hollow of her empty left socket.

"Jar," Eri muttered in her sleep, her voice tiny and raw. "Wait. Hold on. Turn around."

I swung my legs out from under the blanket, careful not to dump the orange cat onto the floor, and reached across the narrow gap between our beds.

I didn't go into her head. I don't have demon powers, and even if I did, we had a household rule about skulls now. Instead, I reached out with my scarred black left hand and gave her bare shoulder two firm taps with my knuckles.

"Hey," I said quietly. "Nine. Wake up. You're on the water."

Eri's single brown eye snapped open.

For half a second her pupil was blown wide, staring back into the sixty-foot green glass tank in the dark under the earth. Then she smelled the salt marsh and the drying net-twine outside the window, saw my black hand resting on her shoulder, and saw the orange cat glaring at her from the foot of my cot.

She let out a long, shuddering breath that deflated her whole ribcage into the mattress.

"Jesus fucking Christ," Eri rasped, rubbing her good eye with the heel of her palm until the wet lashes stuck together. "It was the shelf dream again. The little girl with the bent crown sitting in the jar tapping on the glass with a spoon."

"You want water?" I asked.

"I want someone to shoot me in the bad leg so I stop dreaming about drainage pipes," she grumbled, reaching up blindly to snag her leather eye-patch off the bedpost. She tied the cord around her skull with practiced, one-handed jerks, settled the patch over her left socket, and then reached for the ironwood crutch leaning against the wall. "How's your arm?"

*Stiff in the wrist,* Harumi answered through our mouth before I could even shrug, rolling our left shoulder twice until the tendon popped. *Feels like we slept with it in a bucket of bait-salt. Nothing a mug of terrible mud won't fix.*

"Good," Eri said, grinning crookedly with her split lower lip, where a thin white line still showed from the morning she had hauled us out of the trench. "Because it's your turn to light the stove, Harumi. I carried the peat basket up from the landing yesterday and my left shin still sounds like a maraca if I pivot on it."

I stood up on the cool pine planks, pulled a thick gray fisherman's sweater over my sleep-shirt, and padded barefoot out into the main room of the stilt-house while Tim trotted ahead of me with his tail held up like a boat mast.

The house had been an abandoned net-loft at the far end of the Drift-Weir pier before Pia and Wren and the rest of the village women helped us roof it with split cedar shakes three weeks ago. It stood twelve feet above the tide-flats on eight tarred fir pilings as thick as hogsheads. When the high water came in around noon, you could look straight down through the cracks in the porch planks and watch the silver mullet and green crabs moving through the eelgrass directly beneath your chair.

On the east wall of the kitchen, right beside the little cast-iron ship's stove where anybody cooking breakfast had to look at it, hung two things.

The top one was a wide ledger board made of planed driftwood, with six sheets of heavy hemp paper tacked to it by brass nails and a grease-pencil hanging from a piece of tarred twine.

Back in the ruined city, when I lived on the roof behind the water tank, losing time had felt like falling down an elevator shaft in the dark. I would blink on a Tuesday afternoon and wake up on a Thursday morning with blood on my shoes or three shards missing from a mint tin, terrified to ask anyone what I had done or said while I was gone, hating Harumi for taking the body, while Harumi hated me for locking her in the basement whenever people clapped at the meetings.

We didn't lock doors anymore. And on the mornings or nights when the stress of a sudden loud noise on the pier or a bad dream about the kitchen made one of us step back behind the curtain for a couple of hours while somebody else took the wheel, we wrote it down on the hemp board in plain black grease-pencil so nobody ever had to wake up scared again.

I stood in front of the stove, waiting for the kindling to catch under the tin kettle, and read the bottom three lines from yesterday afternoon:

*3:00 PM (Kio): Mended four fathom of gill-net with Pia on the lower deck. Barometer falling. Put the extra weight-stone on the crab trap by the third piling. Left arm tremor mild (grade 2).*

*6:30 PM (Harumi): The red-haired kid from the salt-pans dropped a crate of tin plates on the boardwalk while Ena was slicing turnips. Ena flinched and nicked her right index finger on the paring knife. I took over for forty minutes, wrapped the finger in linen with turpentine, told the kid to watch where he dumps his ironware, and ate the crust off the skillet bread. Don't bitch at me about the crust, Ena, you weren't going to eat it.*

*9:15 PM (Ena): Thank you for wrapping the finger. You could have left me half the crust. Goodnight.*

Below the hemp ledger board, set three feet off the floor so you could reach it comfortably while sitting cross-legged on the rag rug by the hearth, was a three-foot plank of smooth birch that Wren had painted with three coats of dark gray slate-wash. A little wooden cup nailed to the corner held six sticks of thick white tallow-chalk.

The slate-plank belonged to Sunny.

Across the dark gray wood, in bright white chalk that never got washed off until she asked to start a new picture, was a drawing of a wide river with seven curlicue waves, a stick-figure cat with one pointed ear and one flat ear sitting on top of a square cabin, and four stick-figure girls holding hands on a dock next to a girl with one big eye and a crutch. At the top corner, where the sun went, Sunny had drawn a fat black marker with the cap off and a huge smiley face in the middle of the rays.

While the water heated on the iron plate, Sunny reached out with our right hand, picked up a stub of white chalk, and added three little chalk fish under the dock pilings so the stick-figure cat would have something to look at today.

Behind me, the heavy thump-tap-thump of Eri's ironwood crutch crossed the threshold from the bedroom.

She swung herself over to the rough pine table by the open water-door, easing down onto the bench with a low grunt and stretching her splinted left leg out straight across an overturned herring crate we kept there for a footstool. The heavy whalebone splints were gone from her right leg now, replaced by a tight leather ankle-brace, though the left tibia that she had broken a second time in the cliff crevice while holding my wrist over the gorge was still bound in light cedar slats from knee to heel.

I spooned three heaps of roasted chicory and parched dandelion root into the tin pot, poured the boiling water over the grounds, and gave the orange cat a saucer of chopped salt-herring heads by the woodbox before carrying two steaming tin mugs over to the porch doorway.

Before we could sit down, the little silver bell hanging above the lintel of the pier door gave a soft, hollow *tock* against the cedar frame.

It was Mark's bell, the one we had carried down from the high pass weeks ago. Kio had taken the iron clapper out of the inside with a pair of pliers the day we nailed it over the threshold, because sharp metal ringing unexpectedly behind my head still made my vision go white around the edges and put my hand on the nearest knife handle. Without the clapper, the silver bell only made a gentle, woody chime when a strong gust off the water swung the bell-skirt directly against the cedar post, or when somebody reached up and tapped it once with a knuckle to let us know they were coming up the gangplank.

"Morning down there!" a voice called from the water below the porch.

I walked out onto the open stilt-porch with the two tin mugs in my hands.

The sky over the six-mile estuary was the color of the inside of an oyster shell, pale silver and rose at the horizon where the night fog was burning off the weirs. The tide was flooding fast across the channels now, clear green sea-water swirling over the mudbanks and lifting the long lines of cork floats on the drift-nets.

Down at the bottom of our wooden ladder, tied off to the barnacle-crusted third piling, floated a broad-beamed thirty-foot trading skiff with a patched red lugsail furled against the mast.

Rui stood in the stern with the tiller-rope looped around his elbow, his dark coat buttoned up against the dawn chill, while Tsui sat on the forward thwart with a woven willow basket between her boots, carefully tying oilcloth covers over three earthenware jars of salt-marsh honey.

"Catch!" Rui called up, lifting a smaller rush basket from the deck beside his knee.

I set the two tin mugs down on the flat porch rail and leaned out over the water with both hands open. Rui tossed the basket ten feet straight up with an easy swing of his shoulder; Kio timed the arc in our eyes and my hands caught the braided willow rim clean without spilling a single piece of fruit.

Inside the basket, nested in dried sea-lavender, were a dozen small, thick-skinned coast oranges from the glasshouses down at South Point, their rinds mottled green and bright gold, smelling so sharply of sunlight and bitter oil that Sunny made a little humming sound of pure delight inside my throat.

"Tsui said you two had gone through the last of the dried apples by Wednesday," Rui said, looking up at me where I leaned over the rail in my gray sweater with the black scars showing below my pushed-up left cuff. He didn't stare at the black arm, and he didn't look at me like I was a loaded gun on a table or a prophecy in a grocery store. He looked at me the way you look at a neighbor whose roof you helped tar last month. "We're running the morning load down to the low-water market at Point Halcyon. You need coal oil or flour before Friday?"

"Ten pounds of coarse rye and two bricks of salt if the factor's got them," Kio answered through my mouth, automatically checking the mental pantry list we kept behind our left temple. "And tell him the four gill-nets from our loft are ready on the drying-rack whenever his boy comes by with the cart."

"Done," Tsui called up from the bow, lifting two fingers in salute before shoving the skiff's nose off the cedar piling with a boat-hook. "Don't let Eri talk you into eating those oranges with the peel on, Ena! She's a barbarian!"

"I heard that, you old crow!" Eri yelled out through the open doorway from her seat by the herring crate. "The peel's where the medicine is! Prevents scurvy and mind-rot!"

Tsui laughed, the sound carrying bright and clean across the widening green water as the current caught the skiff's keel and swung them out into the main channel toward the sea-reach.

Before their red sail had cleared the outer weir-poles, the planks of the long village boardwalk clattered at the landward end of our pier.

Wren and Pia came walking down the timber causeway from the high cottages, carrying a rolled six-fathom seine net between them on a cedar pole and smelling strongly of Stockholm tar and fried bacon. Wren had a brass carpenter's rule sticking out of the breast pocket of her leather apron; Pia had her sleeves rolled up to the elbows over forearms as thick and scarred as anchor cables.

They stopped by the porch steps just long enough to drop a two-pound parcel of smoked skate-wing wrapped in dock leaves onto the bench beside our door.

"Gate-pilings held flat through the spring surge last night," Wren said, squinting past our porch roof toward the northern curve of the cliffs three miles up the bay, where the great basalt sluice-gate sat sixty feet deep under the grey water at the mouth of the drowned gorge. "Not a bubble coming up from the fault, and not a glass hound track anywhere on the north mud since the full moon. The silt's already banked nine feet deep against the lower stone."

She looked down at Eri's outstretched left leg on the herring crate inside the door.

"Let me see that cedar binding before you get chicory all over it, demon," Wren ordered, stepping onto the porch and kneeling by the crate with the brisk, merciless efficiency of a woman who repaired boats and shinbones with the exact same draw-knife. She tested the rawhide lacings around Eri's calf with two calloused thumbs, grunted once, and gave the bottom knot a quarter-inch tighten that made Eri hiss through her teeth. "Bone's knitting straight. Stay off the wet shale for two more weeks and quit swinging from cliff ledges by your ankles, and you'll be walking without the stick by the herring run."

"You hear that, babe?" Eri told me as Wren and Pia picked up their net-pole and tramped off down the lower pier toward the weir-boats. "Two weeks and I'll be climbing seven flights of stairs again just to annoy you."

"We don't have seven flights of stairs," I said, picking up the two steaming tin mugs from the rail and setting the basket of salt-oranges between us. "We have four porch steps and a boat ladder."

"Then I'll climb them seventeen times every Tuesday," Eri said, reaching for her ironwood crutch and hauling herself up off the bench.

She thumped across the threshold onto the open cedar porch and lowered herself down onto the top step at the very edge of the water-landing, letting her good right leg dangle out over the twelve-foot drop to the green tide while resting her splinted left heel on the cross-beam of the piling below.

I sat down on the rough cedar plank right beside her, my bare feet hanging out over the quiet grey water alongside hers, the orange cat coming out through the open door to curl himself around my right hip and sniff suspiciously at the basket of fruit.

I handed Eri her tin mug.

We both took a sip at the same time, and we both made the exact same face.

It was still the worst coffee left in the universe: bitter roasted chicory and burnt barley with a pinch of marsh-salt at the bottom of the tin, hot enough to take the skin off the roof of your mouth.

Down below our dangling feet, the tide had covered the dark mudbanks completely now. Four miles up the drowned ravine to the north, six fathoms under the clean green sea and nine feet of drifted river silt, the grey stone boy and the grey stone girl from the main square of Purgatory stood together in the quiet dark where no one would ever see them again, with a shoebox of eleven markers at their feet and four leaning lines and a little smiley face carved deep into the granite underneath them.

Somewhere out on the old inland highway, Himo was gone into the gray ruins with empty hands, and neither of us knew where she was, and neither of us cared.

My chest scar still hurt when the rain came in off the bar. Tomorrow afternoon, if somebody dropped an iron kettle behind me on the boardwalk, my heart would still jump into my throat and Kio or Harumi would still have to catch the paring knife before my hands shook. There would still be mornings when my black left palm itched in the damp air and remembered the hiss of black glass melting into the skin, and there would still be nights when Eri woke up sweating in the dark, reaching across the four feet of pine floor between our beds to make sure nobody had left her on the tile floor of the vault.

We weren't fixed. You don't fix a kitchen on a Tuesday or ten thousand years at the edge of a pit.

You build a house on eight cedar pilings over the water, and you hang a hemp board by the stove so everybody inside your skin gets to write their name down in the morning, and you take the clapper out of the silver bell over the door so it only chimes when the wind comes in off the sea.

Eri took one of the small green-and-gold oranges out of the willow basket, dug her thumbnail into the thick, fragrant rind, and peeled it in one long spiral before breaking the fruit cleanly in half. She handed four dripping segments to me, popped one into her own mouth, and looked out over the six-mile reach of silver water with her one good eye as the sun finally broke clear of the eastern headlands and turned the wet weir-nets to gold.

*Look at the fish under the third piling,* Sunny whispered happily behind my eyes, pointing with our chin at a school of tiny silver minnows darting through the sunlit kelp right where she had drawn them in white chalk on the birch plank inside.

*The flood tide is three inches higher than yesterday,* Kio noted, calm and content, warming our hands around the hot tin mug. *The crab trap will be full by noon.*

*And the orange isn't half bad,* Harumi said inside our chest, leaning back against the warm cedar post of the porch with our scarred black left arm resting easily in the sun across our knee, no sleeve pulled down to hide it, no tin behind the water tank, no count to fake for a circle of folding chairs.

Eri leaned sideways on the wooden step and bumped her shoulder hard against mine.

"How you doing, babe?" she asked, the morning breeze ruffling her dark hair around the strap of her leather eye-patch.

I looked down at my scarred left hand holding the tin mug, then at the orange cat asleep against my hip, then out across the bright, open water of the reach. All four of us breathed in the smell of salt and pine smoke and bitter orange peel together, one deep, clear lungful without a single lie in it.

"Day one," I told the tide.

Eri grinned with her whole crooked face, bumped her shoulder into mine a second time, and lifted her tin mug toward the rising sun.

"Day one," she said. "I'll be here for day two."

[END]
